                 We Congratulate You, Pioneers

WE feel deeply grateful to the pioneers, who for the past fifty years have
toiled unceasingly for the betterment of our community.  Your faith in the
future of this section was well founded, and, the reward, made possible by your
untiring service to Mankind, will be enjoyed for generations to come.

Your labors are now near an end. It is but for you to watch the progress of
those who have chosen to carry on. Your hardships were many, but, during the
course of years, you have proven your ability to cope with the elements in
quest of worldly possessions.

And now, it is for those who have taken the reins, to "drive hard" to keep the
pace which has been maintained in the past. They are inspired by your fruitful
efforts and as individuals, possessing the courage and undying spirit of their
forefathers, will ever strive for the betterment of our community.

City of Eureka

A. W. SCHUMACHER, Mayor.

           To those Noble Pioneers whose courage, determination
            and foresight permit us to celebrate our Fiftieth
                  Anniversary, this book is dedicated.

FOREWORD

This year, the city of Eureka is celebrating the fiftieth anniversary of its
birth; fifty years of continued growth, from a mere one room shack to a
thriving city of more than fourteen hundred people. As a permanent record of
the founding of this city and the building up of surrounding territory, the
committees in charge of the Eureka Golden Jubilee celebration are sponsoring
the publication of this book. Their object is to put in print the stories and
biographies of the pioneers who built up this community; the stories of various
business institutions which have played a prominent part in the city reaching
its present industrial status; and the stories of various organizations which
have done much in promoting the social life of the city. In this manner we hope
to be able to help those noble pioneers re-live their experiences; and preserve
for future generations a record of their hardships, their joys, and their
accomplishments.

The sources of information for this book, as in all books of this nature, were
meager. In the early days of the city's founding, the business of making a
living was paramount, and the keeping of records was of secondary importance.
The material for this book is based largely on hearsay matter gathered from
pioneers who recall the stories themselves, or have heard them told by others.
Other material has been gathered from old, musty records, or has been
volunteered by people who have heard that we are putting out a book of this
nature and have responded to our solicitation for historical data. True, no
doubt, this book is not complete. Historical sketches of many early settlers
have been omitted, but the editorial staff feels that the biographies contained
herein, represent a true cross-section of the life of the average pioneer. In
making their selection, the editorial staff has been guided by the following
factors: time of arrival, accuracy and vividness with which the subject
recalled his pioneer days, the important part which the life of the subject
played in the development of the community, and the general interest with which
his biography would be read by the average reader. We feel sure that much
material of really historical interest has been omitted, but with this in mind
and with what facilities were available, we did the "best we could".

The composition of this book was left largely to a group of amateur
journalists, and no doubt it will soon become apparent to the average reader,
that there are mistakes in grammatical construction, which could have been
remedied by professional writers. For this we ask your apology. No doubt, too,
mistakes will appear in some of the material itself; but as stated before, it
is based largely on hearsay, and the historical committee cannot vouch for the
authenticity of the same.

At this time, too, the Eureka Jubilee Committees, and more in particular, the
historical committee and editorial staff of this book, wish to express their
sincere appreciation to those who have so whole-heartedly helped us with the
publication of this book. The advertising committee, too, wishes to express its
deepest appreciation to those advertisers who by their monetary contributions
have made it possible to finance the cost of this publication. Without the help
and cooperation of all of you, the publication of this book would have remained
only a dream.

                                                F. J. HOMEYER, Editor-in-Chief.


                        DR. O. H. GERDES MEMORIAL

                               In Memoriam
                         DR. O. H. GERDES, M. D.
                                1868-1936
                           "He Gave His Best"

                      43 Years of Faithful Service

The erection of the Dr. O. H. Gerdes Memorial in 1937 was sponsored by the
Eureka Civic Club. The committee selected to complete the erection of the
memorial was: Theo. Straub, Emanuel Heilman and Walter Schonbrod.

Great care was taken in the selection of a suitable memorial for the late Dr.
O. H. Gerdes. The boulder known to Eureka for many years as "Big Stone", has
been a spot for school picnics, and a resting place for hikers. It was chosen
for this sentiment, and its close association with the community.

The dimensions of the boulder are ten feet high, eight feet wide and six feet
thick. The approximate weight of this huge stone is estimated at 80,000 pounds.
The Memorial was erected within the Lake Park grounds on the south side of
Eureka Lake.

This boulder is a true representation of the character of the man who gave to
the last for the betterment and well-being of the people he loved. The
Memorial, standing as it does, tenderly watching, as if to guide with the
unforeseen hand, the destinies of its people.

Fond memories of Dr. O. H. Gerdes will remain forever in the hearts of Eureka
and community.

            HONORABLE GOVERNOR LESLIE JENSEN, 1937-1938

                     GOVERNOR LESLIE JENSEN

Eureka's Golden Jubilee brings to mind the many obstacles overcome and
hardships endured by the pioneers who came to South Dakota to build their homes
and establish a government of free people.

We still have need for the fortitude and spirit of these hardy pioneers in
order to meet the problems of our present day.

Your undertaking of this Jubilee Celebration and the publication of this
historical book in itself shows that this pioneer spirit is still alive and
will carry us on to a new day of prosperity in our great State.

                                                LESLIE JENSEN, Governor.

                History of The City of Eureka

In the spring and summer of 1887, the Chicago, Milaukee and St. Paul Railway
Company extended the James River division of their system, north and south from
Roscoe, with the apparent object in view of eventually connecting the two
capital cities of Pierre to the southwest, and Bismarck to the northwest. In
June of that year, grading was discontinued eight miles north of the present of
Hillsview, and it was generally understood that a terminal station would be
established near the end of the grade.

A little town was started on the school section, number 36, which adjoins the
present site of the City of Eureka to the east. This town was known as St.
Petersburg, and consisted of the following business places: Jacob Liedle,
saloon; J. W. Brockbank, general merchandise; M. H. Smith, meat market, and a
machinery shed owned by Pfeffer and Bramaier and operated by William Bramaier.
This shed also served as a lodging place. A roomer once described the place as
follows: "Along one side were constructed bunks, and the casual visitor as well
as the resident population of St. Petersburg considered himself in luck to get
a lower berth, as the uppers served as a sort of protection from the summer
storms and rains."

At this time, but one building had been erected on the west side of the
railroad track. It was located near the present site of the Jacob Bieber, Jr.
home, and was occupied by A. G. Bernard, who homesteaded the Southwest Quarter
of Section 35, in Township 127, North Range 73, on which the main portion of
the City of Eureka is now situated. It was from this building that Eureka's
first newspaper was edited by Mr. Bernard, under the name, "The Eureka
Magnate." Mr. Bernard proved up title on his homestead, and then conveyed the
same to the Milwaukee Land Company, a corporation, who platted the land as an
original townsite, and attributed to it the name of "Eureka." No particular
reason has ever been given for the town being called "Eureka," but rumor has it
that the surveyors gave it that name for no apparent reason at all. Before the
town was platted, the town site was own on the map as "the end of the track,"
with no name.

The first train arrived in Eureka on the 27th day of July, 1887, but the
original plat of the city was not platted until October 3, 1887. In the
meantime, Charles Pfeffer, the local townsite agent, was besieged daily by
people desirous of purchasing lots and establishing business places and
residences, but because the city had not yet been platted this could not be
done.

The first hotel in Eureka was started on the 12th of August, 1887, by Mr. and
Mrs. Geo. C. Knickerbocker. The first quarters were remodeled from an old
section house, and were located on the railroad company's right of way,
directly east of the Dierenfeldt Meat Market of today. They soon desired to
have more commodious surroundings, but because the townsite had not yet been
laid out, they erected a structure on the Milwaukee Railroad's right of way, in
opposition to both the railroad company and their agent, Mr. Pfeffer. Their
example was soon followed by others, and in a very few days, a string of
buildings was extending from the present site of the City Hall over to the
depot, all on the Milwaukee Company's right of way.

However, the town was finally platted and the lots were placed on the market.
As these were the first lots, and sought by business men who intended to
establish their businesses here, they were soon disposed of. So great was the
demand for lots, that first, second and third Milwaukee Land Company Additions
were platted, and filed for record on the respective dates: August 16, 1888;
May 6, 1889; and November 1, 1890. Reagan's Addition, located on the Southwest
Quarter of Section 35, Township 127, North Range 73, and homesteaded by John E.
Reagan, was also plotted and added to the city. The present park and lake are
all included on the land originally homesteaded by Mr. Regan. Boettcher's
Addition was also platted and added to the city on May 6, 1889. This addition
was a part of Bauer Township, and is located south of present state highway No.
10. It was originally homesteaded by Orville A. Vermilya, who conveyed his
interests to John E. Boettcher, who conveyed to Fred W. Boettcher, before he
proved up title to the same. This property was described as the Northwest
Quarter of Section 2, Township 126, Range 73, and although the patent to the
same was issued right away, it was not put on record until January 30, 1923,
when a photographic copy of same was taken from the records in the Department
of Interior at Washington, D. C., to clear up apparent defects in the title to
certain lots in the said addition.

After the original townsite had been surveyed, the progress of the town was
remarkable. More and better homes, and business establishments were erected;
the main part of town being located between what is known today as Dakota
Street on the south, and McPherson Street on the north.

        Early Day Transportation

Concerning the original set-up of the town of Eureka, your writer has been able
to gather the following material from various sources. The first hotel was
erected by Mr. and Mrs. Geo. C. Knickerbocker. The first section foreman was a
man by the name of James Mundy. J. W. Guhin w as the first depot agent.
Lightner & Bickelhaupt owned the first grain house. J. S. Beaver was the first
grain buyer. Chas. Pfeffer was the first townsite agent. Pfeffer & Bramaier
owned the first Eureka flour mill. E. G. Kennedy operated the first farm
implement establishment. Hezel, Hepperle & Sauter owned one of the first
general merchandise stores. Frank Stockton was the first postmaster, and had as
his first deputy John E. Reagan, who succeeded Mr. Stockton as postmaster. John
Stickel was the first blacksmith. John G. Parrott was the first barber. Paul
Peterson and Jos. Worthy operated the first livery stable. A certain Mrs.
McKeever was the first public school teacher. D. B. Strait was the first
principal of schools. Miss S. Mulvey was the first milliner. B. W. Haggard
operated the first dray line. Edgar Sharp was the first lawyer. Moulton &
Hepperle operated a general merchandise store. Jos. and Gott. Werre operated a
store. Reagan & Hooper operated a drug store and sold hardware near the present
site of the Eureka Bazaar. James Gibson ran the first saloon. E. L. Hopkins ran
the first bank exchange.

Miss Edith Worthy has generally been conceded to be the first child born in
Eureka. The first male child born in Eureka was one C. W. Whitney, born
November 10, 1887. He is now residing at Mobridge, South Dakota. Mr. Whitney's
father and brother ran a livery barn and dray business in Eureka. One of their
chief duties in this regard, was to haul supplies from the railroad to the old
army post located at Fort Yates, North Dakota. The first marriage was that of
Mr. and Mrs. J. W. Guhin on October 4, 1887. On January 20, 1888, occurred the
first death, it being that of Daniel M. Smith, who then resided in the section
house. The first congregation was a Presbyterian faith and the Reverend Geo.
Holden acted as pastor. The first services for this church were held in the
school house.

In the spring and summer of 1888, business interests in Eureka were greatly
stimulated by the tide of immigration which poured in from Iowa, Illinois,
Minnesota, and southern South Dakota. Special trains from twenty to thirty cars
of immigrant movables were of a common occurrence. In addition to this local
movement, many extra passenger coaches were needed to transport the immigration
coming direct from Russia and Germany. The great majority of these newcomers
were a thrifty, hard-working people in good financial standings, and their
advent added much to the welfare and prosperity of Eureka.

        Main Street in Early Days

The effect of their immigration was more pronounced, when, in later years, they
proved themselves so instrumental in gaining for Eureka the reputation of being
the greatest inland wheat market in the world. The statistics for the year
1892, show the following remarkable receipts and shipments of Grain: Number of
cars of grain shipped from Eureka in 1892, was 3,300 cars or a train of cars 30
miles in length, or 165 trains of 20 cars each. Thirty-five warehouses and
elevators were in active operation. Number of men employed in operation of
these elevators and warehouses was 200. Total receipts of grain for year ending
December 31, 1892, was 3,000,000 to 4,000,000 bushels.

Day and night during the fall of this year, the streets were thronged with
teams and vehicles laden with grain and waiting for their turn to unload. Many
ox teams were pressed into service, some coming a distance of from 60 to 70
miles, from as far west as the river and as far north as Linton, Kulm,
Streeter, and Lehr, in what is now North Dakota. Storage capacity was often
over-taxed and car loads of wheat piled on the company's right of way was a
familiar sight.

The railroad earnings of the Eureka station in the months of October and
November of this year, exceeded those of any other station on the entire
Milwaukee system, reaching the enormous figure of $100,000.00 a month.

In the year 1890, the question of organizing Eureka under a special charter was
first agitated. An election was called and the question voted upon. The result
showed a tie vote. In January of 1892, another election was called, and the
question of incorporation was carried by a large majority.

Your writer has been unable to find much information as to the early form of
government of Eureka, prior to incorporation. No permanent records were kept,
but hearsay has it that the town was more or less governed by the county, which
had already been organized. The commissioner from this district more or less
represented the town, and all official acts were done through the county board
of commissioners, who appointed various sub-agents in the village to conduct
governmental functions.

The first special election of town officials of Eureka was held in January of
1892, and resulted in F. W. Boettcher being elected mayor, and G. C.
Knickerbocker, J. E. Reagan, F. W. Schamber, J. Pietz, Jos, Werre, and Peter
Wittmayer, as aldermen. The first meeting of the council was called for January
16, 1892, and L. T. Boucher was appointed city auditor; G. K. Tiffany, city
attorney; Joseph Worthy, police justice; J. F. Wilson, city justice; and Chas.
Peffer, city treasurer. The auditor's bond was set at $300.00, and he was
instructed to purchase the necessary books, stationery and supplies to begin
the transaction of business for the new city.

The next meeting was held on the 2Oth day of January, 1892, and the main
business there consisted of organizing the council. Geo. Knickerbocker was
elected president of the council, and F. W. Schamber, vice president. The bond
of the city treasurer was set at $1,000.00. M. W. Cowley was appointed the
first chief of police, and his salary was fixed at $50.00 per month, with the
further stipulation that he collect and report all fees to the city auditor,
and that in the event that in any month the amount of such fees fell short of
the amount of his salary, that he was then to receive a warrant from the city
for the deficiency. The salary of the city auditor and city attorney was set at
$200.00 per year for each office, and the salary of the city treasurer was
fixed at $50.00 per year. The Eureka Independent was designated as the first
official newspaper of the city. At this meeting, too, the salary of the city
mayor was set at $50.00 per year, and the aldermen voted unanimously not to
accept any salary for their services. The first committees appointed by the
mayor were: Water and Fire, with Aldermen Reagan, Knickerbocker, and Werre
serving on same; and Streets and Alleys, with Aldermen Wittmayer, Schamber, and
Pietz as committeemen.

The next early meetings of the city were confined largely to drafting
ordinances, rules and regulations for the government of the new city. The first
ordinance passed by the city was entitled: "An ordinance to license and
regulate places of public entertainment." It provided among other things that
all applicants for permission to run places of public entertainment must
furnish a bond to the city and fixed the license fee at not less than $300.00,
and not more than $600.00 for any one year. It also provided that every
licensee should keep a quiet and orderly house, and should close the same at
12:00 o'clock on every night. This ordinance, during the history of the town,
probably underwent more amendments than any other ordinance. The degree of the
fee paid and the length of hours seemed to be some of the questions that
vitally concerned the early politics of the town. The length of business hours
varied from 22 hours a day, and paying $1,000.0Q a year license fee, down to
the limits prescribed when the original ordinance was drafted.

The first reading of the entire compiled list of city ordinances was held on
the 2nd day of February, 1892. The second reading was held on the 9th day of
February, 1892, and the same were then adopted in toto. At the meeting held on
the 16th day of February, 1892, the first applications for permission to run
houses of public entertainment were received. At this meeting, the same were
approved for John Smith, Philip Martell, and Imanuel Reinvolt. The applications
of Jacob Liedle and J. King were likewise approved, conditioned upon them
furnishing an adequate bond. A committee was also appointed to investigate the
necessity of building a city jail, and this was done a short time afterwards.
Jos. Stontil was also appointed the first city assessor at this meeting.

In April, 1892, the city was divided into wards by ordinance. The original
wards of the city were bounded as follows: First ward consisted of all that
portion of the city north of a line drawn through the center of Main Street
extending east and west to the boundaries of the said city. Second ward
consisted of all that portion south of the first ward, and extending to the
center of Pfeffer Street.  The third ward consisted of all that portion
extending south of the second ward.

The polling places for the first official general election were as follows:
First ward, the office of the E. Bach Lumber Company, with the following men as
judges: Walter Bach, J. F. Wilson and James Finlay; second ward, the store of
Schamber & Son, with the following men as judges: M. Lutgen, Gottfried Werre
and Philip Schamber; third ward, the office of the St. Croix Lumber Company,
with the following men as judges: C. J. Curtis, Philip Martell and John Smith.

The result of the first general election of the city, held on April 19, 1892,
was as follows: Chas. Pfeffer was elected mayor without opposition; Wm.
Bramaier was elected city treasurer; J. F. Wilson, city justice; Jos. Worthy,
police justice; John Pietz and August Pein, alderman from the third ward were
also elected without opposition. B. J. Koch and Christ Hezel were elected
aldermen from the first ward; and Jos. Werre and Mike Lutgen were elected
aldermen from the second ward. This being the first election of the new city,
it was necessary to decide who should hold the long and short terms as
aldermen, by drawing lots. This was done and Alderman B. J. Koch, John Pietz
and Mike Lutgen drew the long terms. James W. Brown was appointed city auditor
and Emil Schamber was appointed chief of police, retaining the office until
June 21, 1892, when he resigned, and John Wittmayer was appointed to fill the
vacancy.

Upon a petition of voters of the city being presented to the new council, a
special election was called, and the question of issuing bonds on the credit of
the municipality in the amount of $2,000.00 for the purpose of constructing a
city hall, for the 24th day of August, 1892. The result of the election showed
104 people voting for the issuance of bonds and four voting against the same.
Bid for construction of the building were received soon afterwards, and the
contract for building the new structure was let to Walker & Israel at a cost of
$2,300.00. The building was completed in June of 1893, and Alderman Lutgen was
appointed as the first superintendent of the same.

The first levy for the new city was made at the meeting held on September 5,
1892, and the auditor's estimate of expenses for the ensuing year were as
follows: Salaries, $1,300.00; Interest and Sinking Fund, $240.00; Streets and
Alleys, $600.00; Miscellaneous Fund, $1,060.00, or a total estimated expense of
$3,200.00. The receipts were estimated as follows: Licenses and receipts from
all sources, $1,800.00. The amount to be raised by tax was $1,400.00, and the
levy for that year was set at seven mills on the dollar.

        Eureka -- 1894

In the fall of 1892, a petition of the legal voters of Eureka had been
presented to the council, asking that the new city hall building, when
completed, should be leased to McPherson County for a term of 99 years, at an
annual rental of one dollar per year to be used in the event of the location of
the county seat in Eureka, the city, however, reserving two rooms in the
northwest corner of said building for themselves, except during terms of court.
However, the county seat was not to be moved. The result of the general
election was that Leola won the right to retain the court house by the narrow
margin of four votes. Rumor has it that some of the more enthusiastic
supporters of the court house for the east end of the county, drove by team and
bob-sled to the village of Hillsview, and told the voting population there that
the election had been postponed because of a big snow storm, thereby assuring
them that they would retain the county seat.

The hazard of fire was very great in Eureka during those early years, as is
shown from the early records of the city. In early 1893, an ordinance was
passed by the city council requiring all owners of business places to have
barrels of water handy as a protection against fire. Soon afterwards, bonds in
the amount of $6,000.00 were voted to provide the city with a water system, the
question being carried by a vote of fifty-nine to nine.

The Milwaukee Land Company donated to the city Lot Six of Block Two of their
second addition to the city for the purpose of erecting a water tower. The
Solomon Isaak residence now stands on this site, but for some reason, the same
was never used. The tower was finally constructed on the present site of the
city pump house, and the first water main extended from there down to the
intersection of Main and Market Streets, and possessed two hydrants. The new
tower consisted of a 2,500 gallon wooden barrel tank, built on a seventy foot
tower, and a forty foot windmill provided the power to fill the tank. The
contract for building the first waterworks for the city was let to Fairbanks,
Morse & Co. As an added protection, the city also purchased a chemical engine
to combat fire.

In 1895, the Milwaukee Land Company donated certain lots in Block thirteen of
their third addition of the city for the purpose of digging a well to supply
the city with water. The present city well is located on the approximate site
of the first well.

The election of 1893 resulted in J. W. Guhin being elected police justice
without opposition. C. J. Hezel was re-elected alderman from the first ward
without opposition, and Lyman T. Boucher was elected alderman from the third
ward without opposition. Alderman Lutgen having resigned, Frank Ell was elected
to fill the vacancy from the second ward for the short term. David Isaak was
elected for the long term.

The early city elections and school elections were conducted concurrently, and
for many years, the names of the candidates for city offices and school offices
appeared on the same ballot. This material is covered elsewhere in this book,
so your writer will not dwell on the same. However, in passing, it might be
interesting to note that in the early days of education in the city, the
finances of the schools were especially poor. It was during these times that
the aldermen of the city, recognizing this fact, donated the revenue which the
city got from the houses of public entertainment to the schools for two months,
and granted to them the free use of the city hall for a period of nine months,
to help them out of their poor financial status.

The year 1893 also saw the Milwaukee Land Company add its fourth addition to
the City of Eureka, and Wm. Bramaier plot a certain portion of land south of
present state highway No. 10, and add the same to the city under the name of
Bramaier's addition. This tract of land was originally a part of the Northeast
Quarter of Section 2, in Township 126 North, Range 73, and was homesteaded by
Heinrich Bitz. He and his wife, Rosena Bitz, who is today living in Herreid,
South Dakota, soon after taking out a receiver's receipt on this land, and
before a patent was issued to them, conveyed the same to Wm. Bramaier, who
platted the land, as hereinbefore stated.

The election held in the spring of 1894 resulted in Chas. Pfeffer being
re-elected as mayor; Wm. Bramaier, as city treasurer, and Jas. W. Falihee,
Jacob Renz and John Pietz, as aldermen from the first, second, and third wards,
respectively.

Wm. Bramaier died soon after his re-election as city treasurer, and the council
called a special election to fill the vacancy. Fred. W. Schamber was then
elected as his successor. The activities of the council during this interim
were more or less the general ministerial duties of such an organization. One
now seemingly curious act of theirs during this time was the passage of an
ordinance forbidding the fast driving and running of horses on the streets of
the city, and imposing a fine for violation of this ordinance.

In 1896, John E. Reagan was elected mayor. Chris. Vorlander was elected city
treasurer, and Chris Wentz, Frank Ell and Julius Schamber were elected as
aldermen from the first, second, and third wards respectively. Soon thereafter,
the incumbent alderman, Matt. Mergenns, resigned, and Jos. Werre was appointed
in his place.

An interesting fact during this time was the drafting of a resolution by the
council wherein they offered a reward of five dollars to the first person
arriving with a team of horses to hitch to the hose cart when the fire alarm
was sounded. The person arriving second was given a reward of $2.50.

The election of 1897 resulted in Rudolph Koch being elected alderman from the
first ward; Matt. Mergens was elected alderman from the second ward; and Chris,
Doering, alderman from the third ward. The question of issuing bonds to the
extent of $2,000.00 for the purpose of purchasing additional fire equipment,
hose carts, fire hose, etc., was also voted upon and was carried. James W.
Brown was appointed city attorney for the first time, and Geo. N. Williamson,
later affiliated with the firm of Herried & Williamson, attorneys, was
appointed city auditor. The Northwest Blade was designated as the official
newspaper of the city for the first time.

The bone of much contention during the Reagan administration was the opening
and extending of Pfeffer Street across the Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul
Railroad Company's right of way to the east. Various ordinances were passed and
condemnation proceedings instituted, but the city was unsuccessful in having
the street opened in any way. The street today is closed, where the same
crosses the Milwaukee Railroad's right of way, south of the Philip Oster
elevator.

One of the closing acts of the Reagan administration was the granting to
certain individuals, including J. W. Zietlow, C. L. Parkhurst, El. Kennedy, C.
N. Herried, Fred W. Boettcher, Walter Boehler, J. W. Guhin, F. H. Hooper, and
Wm. Robb, the privilege of using the streets and alleys of the city for
telephone lines, poles and other electrical devices. This group of individuals
included in its number many persons who later established the Dakota Central
Telephone Company, which is today operating in Eureka, and throughout northern
South Dakota, in general.

This administration also sponsored the building of a new pump house to be used
by the fire department for housing their equipment and other purposes.

In 1898, after having been absent from the mayor's chair for two years, Chas.
Pfeffer was again elected mayor, and he is the only individual in the history
of the city who has occupied that honored office for more than two terms. The
new aldermen elected were Frank Ell, Julius Schamber, and Steve Fischer, from
the first, second, and third wards respectively. W. O. Irwine was appointed
city auditor.

On June 29, 1898, the Milwaukee filed the plat in the offices of the Register
of Deeds of McPherson County, adding its Fifth Addition to the said city.

Toward the latter part of this year, the question of extending the city water
mains was again agitated. This action was approved by the council, and the
contract to do work was let to Fairbank, Morse & Company of St. Paul. The mains
were then extended from Main Street at the water tank, south to the corner of
Fourth and Dakota Streets, and from the center of Market Street, east across
the railroad tracks to the corner of the S. H. Bowerman Lumber Company
warehouse.

While in office, Alderman Frank Ell from the second ward, removed from the
state, and the mayor appointed R. F. Puckett to fill the vacancy until the next
general election. In January of 1899, W. O. Irwine also tendered his
resignation as city auditor, and the mayor appointed James W. Brown as auditor
in his place.

In 1899, John Liedle was honored by being elected alderman from the first ward.
M. Mergens v. as elected alderman from the second ward for the long term, and
B. F. Puckett for the short term. John Pietz was elected alderman from the
third ward.

It was during this time that the city purchased, at a cost of $150.00, fifteen
blocks in the city cemetery from the Eureka Cemetery Association, for the
purpose of burying the indigent poor. There was also a considerable fire in the
town and the building of the Eureka Brewing Company burned down. The cause of
the fire looked very much like a case of arson and the city offered a reward of
$100.00 for the apprehension of the guilty one.

The city, by way of adding modern improvements, at this time also sponsored the
purchasing of twenty-four gasoline street lamps.

The election for the year marking the turn of the century saw F. W. Schamber
elected mayor, and the incumbent aldermen, Stoller, Puckett and Doering were
re-elected from their various wards. C. Vorlander was also re-elected city
treasurer. The mayor appointed Philip Schamber assessor for the coming year,
and I. P. Johnson as city auditor, which appointments were unanimously affirmed
by the council.

Chas. Pfeffer, the mayor during the previous term offered to the city, free of
charge, certain land to be used as a dumping ground. It was also during this
term that the city purchased its present curfew bell, which for many years was
also used as the fire bell. The new bell weighed approximately 700 pounds, and
was supposed to be "a metal bell of the highest tone."

While in office, Alderman Puckett removed from his ward, and upon tendering his
resignation, the mayor appointed Gottfried Werre, Sr., to fill the vacancy.

In 1900, the question of establishing a new location for the court house for
McPherson County was again submitted to a vote. Prior to the election, the city
council again drafted a resolution conveying the city hall property to the
county to be used in the event that the county seat should be moved to Eureka.
A thousand dollars was also appropriated out of the general fund to get the
building in shape for court house purposes, but the desired majority could not
be attained by Eureka, so the court house remained in its old location.

When the city hall building was constructed, it was at least partially built on
the right of way of the Milwaukee Railroad Company. In 1900, the city acquired
permanent possession of the Land on which the building is now setting, by
receiving a quit claim deed from the railroad company to the land.

It was during this time that Company A, of the First South Dakota guards was
organized. This company was also known as the Herreid Guards, and included
among its officers were Captain Kierbow, Lieutenant Chas. Mix, Lieutenant A. J.
Hezel, and many others. Through the help of donations from the city and from
private individuals, this company produced a band that was famous throughout
the state. During the year 1901, this company, consisting of men from both
Eureka and Leola, went to the Watertown state encampment, and received
noteworthy praise for its excellent military condition.

September 19, 1901, was set aside by Mayor Schamber as a day of meditation in
honor of President William McKinley, who had recently been assassinated.

While in office Alderman Renz removed from the city and the mayor appointed A.
A. Miller to fill the vacancy. Lewis P. Johnson also tendered his resignation
as city auditor, and John N. Brundage was appointed in his place.

The election of 1902 resulted in Fred Hepperle being elected mayor, and C.
Vorlander was again elected city treasurer. Albert N. Junge was elected
alderman from the first ward; Gottfried Werre, Sr., from the second ward; Jacob
Schall, alderman from the second ward for the short term; and John Doering as
alderman from the third ward. John N. Brundage was appointed city auditor, and
Geo. N. Williamson was appointed city attorney.

The ordinance governing the regulation of houses of public entertainment was
also amended. The license fee was set at from $30.00 to $40.00 per month. The
amendment also required that all saloons be closed between the hours of 11:00
o'clock p.m. and 5 :00 o'clock a.m., and stated that if it was known that the
owner or bartender was in the saloon during these hours, it was presumptive
evidence that there was a violation of this ordinance.

It was during this time, too, that the Milwaukee Railroad extended its lines
north to Linton. The Soo Line had also been constructed to our north a few
years previous, and this did much to lessen the importance of Eureka as a
primary grain shipping center. However, the process of adjustment, though slow,
finally turned out satisfactorily, and the city still developed, though with
more limited trade territory.

In 1903, O. C. Gibson was re-elected alderman from the first ward; Jacob Schall
as alderman from the second ward; and John Pietz as alderman from the third
ward.

As a forerunner of many municipal law suits that v. ere to follow, Dyer B.
Strait brought an action against the City of Eureka during this time. He
received a verdict in his favor in Circuit Court, but when the case was
appealed to the South Dakota Supreme Court, it was reversed, the higher court
holding in favor of the city.

Geo. N. Williamson, who carried the above case successfully to the Supreme
Court, then resigned as city attorney, and Jas. W. Brown was appointed in his
place. Alderman Junge also resigned and the mayor appointed R. J. Koch in his
place. Koch failed to qualify, so the mayor appointed Solomon Isaak in his
place.

The elections of 1904 and 1905 saw Fred Hepperle being re-elected as mayor and
C. Vorlander as city treasurer. The aldermen from the first ward were Henry
Kusler, Sr., and Christian Wentz. The aldermen from the second ward were Frank
Blessner and Jacob Schall. The aldermen from the third ward were J. M. Serr and
Henry Straub. J. N. Brundage served as city auditor for these two years, and J.
M. Brown served as city attorney.

It was during this time that the city purchased its first sewer tile,
thirty-three hundred feet, and the tiles were laid along the main streets of
the city. A special election for the school board was also called, this being
necessary because the two organizations, the city and the school district,
still held concurrent elections. The question was upon a $10,000.00 bond issue
which was voted for the purpose of constructing a school house and acquiring
grounds therefor. The present grade school is a result of that election.

The election of 1906 resulted in C. Vorlander being elected mayor for the first
time. Edward Maag was elected city treasurer, and John Keim, John N. Brundage
and John Pietz, were elected aldermen from the first, second and third wards
respectively. The mayor appointed Theo. J. P. Giedt as city auditor, and James
W. Brown as city attorney. He also appointed Christian Kuszmaul as city
marshal, who failed to qualify, and so he appointed John Oster in his place.

A committee consisting of Messrs. Warner and Klein appeared before the council,
on behalf of the Eureka Cornet Band, and the council allowed them the sum of
$100.00 for the purpose of erecting a band stand in some suitable location. The
location was finally selected at a point directly southeast of the city hall
and adjacent to the walk on the north side of Main Street.

The spring of 1907 brought to the city a period of extensive building. It was
during this time that the present Farmers Investment building was constructed
by the old German Bank, of which C. Vorlander was president. The Farmers and
Merchants State Bank also put up a bank building, and the Eureka State Bank is
today located in that structure. The Kennedy Implement Co. during this time
built the building in which Hieb & Opp today conduct their implement business.

        Main Street Looking West from Tracks

The election of 1907 resulted in John Stoller being elected alderman from the
first ward; M. Hammerschmidt, alderman from the second ward; and H. Straub,
alderman from the third ward. At the same election, the question of prohibiting
the "Sale of Intoxicating Liquors at Retail" was submitted to the voters of the
city for the first time, and was defeated by a vote of 104 to 11. James W.
Brown was again re-appointed city attorney, and Theo. J. P Giedt, city auditor.

On May 14, 1907, the Farmers Mutual Telephone Exchange was granted a franchise
to use the streets and alleys of the city for telephone purposes.

It was during this time, that the city was divided into sewage districts, the
boundaries of which today remain the same. District No. 1: "Embracing all of
that part of the city Lying west of the depot grounds of the Chicago, Milwaukee
and St. Paul Railway Company, north of the center line of Pfeffer Street to the
center line of Fourth Street; thence north along the center of Fourth Street,
to the north line of lot two of block ten of the Milwaukee Land Company's Third
Addition to Eureka; thence north along the quarter section line to the
intersection of First Avenue north, and thence along said First Avenue north to
the intersection of the aforesaid depot grounds." District No. 2: "Embracing
all that part of the city of Eureka lying west of the depot grounds of the
Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul Railway Company and south of sewage District
No. 1." District No. 3: "Embracing all that part of the city of Eureka lying
west of the depot grounds of the Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul Railway
Company."

The first sewer in Eureka was by resolution of the city council dated the 17th
of June, 1907, authorized to be dug along the following lines: "To commence at
the intersection of the center line of Main and Market Streets and running
thence west to the intersection of Second Street; thence north along the center
line of Second Street to the center of the alley in Block Two of the Original
Plat, thence west along the said alley line to an outlet at or near the point
where the said alley line intersects Fourth Street. Also along the following
lateral lines to connect with the main sewer as follows, to-wit: Commencing at
a point where the center line of Pfeffer Street intersects the center line of
Market Street, and running thence north along the said center line to the
intersection of the main sewer at the center of Main Street. Also lateral sewer
commencing at the point where the curb line of McPherson Street intersects the
center line of Market Street, running thence south to the intersection of the
main sewer at the intersection of Main and Market Streets."

The bid for the construction of this sewer was let to L. E. Smith, for the sum
of $3,291.00, and the cost of tapping the sewer was fixed at $1.00 per foot,
depending on the number of feet in the frontage of the lot where tap was made.
The outlet for the said sewer was at first to the present city lake and a
perpetual easement was secured from Michael E. Wenzel, to run the sewer line
over his premises. The city paid the sum of $25.00 for this easement, and
agreed to open a street near the south line of out-lot No. 3 of Milwaukee Land
Company's Fifth Addition.

The first sewer was paid largely out of private contributions, and those that
made such were allowed to tap the same free of charge. The others were forced
to pay the tapping charges hereinbefore stated.

The spring election of 1909 saw John Stoller elected alderman from the first
ward; Matt. Hammerschmidt, alderman from the second ward; and Joseph M. Schatz,
alderman from the third ward. The question of forbidding the sale of
intoxicating liquors was again presented to the people on the ballot and was
again defeated, this time by a vote of 103 to 20.

It was also in the year 1909 that Emil Dierenfeldt caused a certain portion of
his land to be surveyed and platted, and had the same added to the city under
the name of College Addition. Wilhelm Schnuerle, at the same time, caused a
certain portion of the city south of present highway No. 10 to be surveyed and
platted and added the same to the city under the name of Schnuerle's Addition.

With the introduction of automobiles to the general public, the council thought
it necessary to regulate those "self-moving creatures," by ordinance.
Consequently, at this time they passed an ordinance limiting the speed of
automobiles operating on the city streets, to eight miles per hour. The
ordinance further provided that the limit be set at five miles per hour at
crossings, and that the drivers of such vehicles must stop upon the signal of
anyone driving horses.  Rumor has it that the first automobile was owned by a
person named Marrow. The second one was owned by C. B. Foncannon; and the third
one by the late Dr. Gerdes.

The election of 1910 resulted in Fred W. Homeyer being elected mayor, defeating
Wm. J. P. Giedt for that office by a small majority. Fred Strobel was elected
alderman from the first ward; John P. Kiesz and Gottfried G. Werre, aldermen
from the second ward; and Hugo Hillmer, as alderman from the third ward. The
question of prohibiting the sale of intoxicating liquor was again submitted to
vote, but was again defeated by a vote of 147 to 45. James W. Brown was again
appointed city attorney, and John N. Brundage was appointed city auditor.

In November, 1910, an ordinance was passed granting to G. A. McLaughlin of the
Aberdeen Engineering Company of Aberdeen, South Dakota, a franchise to operate
an electric light plant within the city of Eureka, for a period of twenty
years.

The election of 1911 resulted in John Stoller being elected alderman from the
first ward; J. H. Hennecke, alderman from the second ward; and Henry Straub, as
alderman from the third ward. The intoxicating liquor question was again voted
upon and defeated, this time by a vote of 138 to 52. Theo. J. P. Giedt was
appointed city attorney for the first time, and John N. Brundage was
re-appointed city auditor. Soon afterwards, Alderman Hillmer resigned his post,
and Andrew Ottenbacher was unanimously appointed in his place.

In March of 1911, August Martell caused to be platted certain of his lands, and
added the same to the city under the name of Martell's First Addition to the
City of Eureka.

The election of 1912 resulted in Wm. J. P. Giedt being elected mayor. John
Liedle was elected alderman from the first ward; John P Kiesz, alderman from
the second ward; and Andrew Ottenbacher, as alderman from the third ward. The
perennial liquor question was again submitted to the people and was again
defeated, this time by a vote of 115 to 33. The question of issuing bonds in
the amount of $4,000.00 for the purpose of extending the city water mains was
also submitted to the people and was carried by a large majority. The bonds
were purchased by the German Bank of Eureka, South Dakota, and the contract for
extending the water mains was let to Johannes J. Klein, plumber and
steam-fitter.

At the first meeting of the new council, E. Heilman was appointed city assessor
for the first time. Theo. J. P. Giedt was appointed city attorney, and F. G.
Schaeffer was appointed city auditor. While in office, Alderman Hennecke
tendered his resignation and the mayor appointed Albert Isaak in his place.

The election of 1913 saw Phil Schamber elected alderman from the first ward; I.
Papendick, as alderman from the second ward; and J. M. Schatz, as alderman from
the third ward. E. Heilman, Theo. J. P. Giedt, and F. G. Schaeffer, were
re-appointed city assessor, city attorney, and city auditor respectively.

In an effort to curb the number of saloons in the city, the council passed an
ordinance during this time, fixing the annual license fee at $1,000.00, the
same being payable in monthly installments, in advance on the first day of each
month.

While in office, Aldermen L. Papendick and A. Ottenbacher tendered their
resignations, and the mayor thereupon appointed Albert Isaak and Jacob Strobel
to fill the vacancies. The year 1913 probably saw the lowest tax levy on real
and personal property during the city's history. The same for that year was set
at two mills on the dollar. In December of 1913, the first contract with the
Norbeck & Nicholson well drilling company of Redfield, South Dakota, was
entered into, wherein the said company contracted to dig a well for the city
from six to eight inches in width, for the purpose of giving the city an
adequate supply of water.

In January, 1914, the wooden water tank of the city burned down, and the
council at its first February meeting in 1914, unanimously decided to install
an air compression water works system.

The election of 1914 resulted in John N. Brundage being elected mayor, and H.
I. Bauer and J. Keim as aldermen from the first ward; A. F. Isaak and C.
Pleissner as aldermen from the second ward; and F. G. Mehlhaff, as alderman
from the third ward. The notice of said election having failed to specify who
was running for the long and short terms in the first and second wards, this
matter was decided by drawing lots, and Aldermen Keim and Pleissner were
successful in drawing the long terms. The question of forbidding the sale of
intoxicating liquors at retail was again submitted and was again defeated by a
vote of 108 to 21. The question of issuing bonds on the credit of the city to
the amount of $6,000.00 for the purpose of supplying the city with an adequate
supply of water for fire protection and for domestic uses was also voted upon,
and carried by a large majority. These bonds were purchased by the State of
South Dakota, out of funds belonging to the Permanent School Fund.

        Market Street -- 1922

M. J. Pufahl was appointed city auditor, and Theo. J. P. Giedt was re-appointed
city attorney.

It was during this time that the boundaries of the second and third wards were
changed to their present location; that is the old boundary line from Pfeffer
Street was moved south to Dakota Street.

The election of 1915 resulted in John Liedle being reelected alderman from the
first ward; Henry Straub, as alderman from the second ward; and Geo. G.
Neuharth, as alderman from the third ward. The liquor question was again
defeated by a vote of 104 to 45. The incumbent city attorney and city auditor
were again re-appointed.

During this regime, the water mains were again extended; this time from the
northeast corner of block 102 of Bramaier's Addition along the south line of
Harrison Avenue to the northeast corner of block two of Boettcher's Addition,
and from the northeast corner of block twenty-one, Milwaukee Land Company's
Fourth Addition, north along the east line of St. Peter Street, the northeast
corner of block seven, College Addition, and from the southwest corner of block
two, Milwaukee Land Company's Second Addition, west along the north line of
McPherson Street, to the corner of out-lot four of Milwaukee Land Company's
Fifth Addition. The contract for excavating was let to Jacob Kundert at a price
of twenty-five cents per lineal foot. The contract for doing the plumbing work
was let to the Eureka Plumbing & Heating Company.

In June of 1916, August Martell caused another portion of his lands to be
platted, and this time the same was added to the city under the name of
Martell's Second Addition to the City of Eureka. In October of the same year,
Philip Schamber and John Pietz caused certain of their lands in the northern
part of the city to be platted and the same were added to the city under the
name of Schamber & Pietz Addition to the City of Eureka.

In December, 1915, the city passed an ordinance requiring circuses and
menageries to pay a license fee of $25.00 per day when conducting their
entertainment in the city. Upon a petition of ninety-seven electors of the
city, the council was required to submit this question to a vote of the people
at a special election. The election results showed 130 votes for the ordinance
and 94 against the same, and the ordinance went into effect.

The election of 1916 saw C. Vorlander elected mayor. H. D. Opp was elected
alderman from the first ward; John P. Keisz, alderman from the second ward; and
F. W. Homeyer was elected alderman from the third ward. Upon petition of John
J. Pietz and fifty-seven other electors, the question of levying a tax of one
mill for free band concerts was voted upon, and was defeated by a vote of 92 to
118. The question of forbidding the sale of intoxicating liquors at retail was
again voted upon and the city was voted dry for the first time in its history,
the result of the vote being 117 for prohibition and 98 against same. E. A.
Warner was appointed city auditor, and Thomas Ringsrud was appointed city
attorney, by the new mayor.

It was during this term, too, that an ordinance was passed granting to O. H.
Gerdes, F. W. Homeyer, Philip Schamber, E. E. Snow and E. A. Warner, a
franchise to use the streets of Eureka for electrical purposes. This group of
individuals for many years operated this business under the name of Dakota
Utilities Co.

The first action of August Martell against the city of Eureka was instituted in
1916, and resulted in the plaintiff securing a verdict against the city for the
sum of $233.30 damages. This judgment was finally settled by the city paying
the amount of the money judgment, and purchasing from him sixty-six acres of
his land lying adjacent to the city limits at a cost of $60.00 per acre, and
granting to the said August Martell the use of the roads across these premises
to the ball park, and also giving him permission to keep the buildings located
on these premises for a term of five years.

The election of 1917 resulted in John Kallenberger being elected alderman from
the first ward, Fred G. Mehlhaff, as alderman from the second ward; and John J.
Pietz as alderman from the third ward. Alderman Kallenberger failed to qualify
and the mayor appointed Solomon Isaak to fill the vacancy. He also appointed
Thos. Ringsrud as city attorney and M. J. Pufahl as city auditor.

The elections for the years 1917 and 1918 resulted in Fred W. Homeyer being
elected mayor for the second time. H. D. Opp, Karl Kafer and John Kallenberger
as aldermen from the first ward; Alderman Kafer serving only for the short
term; J. A. Feneis and Jacob Strobel as aldermen from the second ward; and
Julius Schamber and George G. Neuharth as aldermen from the third ward. Theo.
J. P. Giedt and Thomas Ringsrud were appointed city attorney and city auditor
for these two years.

On August 12, 1919, a special election was called and the question of bonding
the city in the amount of $40,000.00 for the purpose of installing a proper
water works system was voted upon and carried by a vote of 114 to 27. On the
same ballot was also the following question: Shall the new system be an
artesian well system, or a soft water system? The artesian system was the one
favored by a vote of 97 to 44. The plans and specifications for the new system
were drafted by Cory and Lerocq. The bonds were purchased by the Bankers Trust
and Savings Bank of Minneapolis, Minnesota. The contract for digging the well
and equipping and lining the same with galvanized pipe was let to the Norbeck &
Nicholson Company of Redfield, for $18,200.00. The contract for construction of
the concrete reservoir was let to Fred E. Peterson, at the actual cost of
material, plus a profit of fifteen per cent. The contract further provided that
he should receive a bonus of $10.00 per day for every day that the job is
finished before December 1, of the current year, and he was to be penalized
$10.00 per day for every day necessary after the said day, to finish the work.
The contract for the machinery, pumps and piping necessary was let to the
Handalen Plumbing Company for the sum of $6,863.69.

The election of 1920 saw Theo. J. P. Giedt elected as city mayor. H. D. Opp was
elected alderman from the first ward; I,. Papendick as alderman from the second
ward; and Lucas Balliet as alderman from the third ward. The mayor appointed H.
O. Hepperle as city auditor, and John J. Harr as city assessor. While in
office, H. O. Hepperle resigned as city auditor, and the vacancy was filled by
appointing Gust. Mauser in his place.

It was during the Theo. Giedt administration that the sewer was extended to the
following places: "Commencing at the manhole east of the northeast corner of
Block One, Milwaukee Land Company's First Addition to Eureka, thence running in
a northeasterly direction across the railroad right of way to the west line of
Railway Street, and the intersection of Second Avenue and McPherson Street;
thence north to the intersection of College Avenue and Second Street, east
along First Avenue to the center of College Block and intersection of St. Peter
Street, and commencing at a point in the center of Fourth Street, and east of
lot four of block twenty-six, north to a point east of lot eight of block two
of Schamber & Pietz Addition to Eureka."

The water main was also extended as follows: "Commencing at the present main at
the intersection of St. Peter Street with First Avenue, and running thence west
along First Avenue to its intersection with Second Avenue. Also commencing at
the hydrant on the corner of Third Street, and First Avenue, and thence running
east to the intersection of Second Street; thence one block north and south
along Second Street."

The contract for both of these extensions was let to the Phelps-Drake Co. of
Minneapolis, Minnesota, at a cost of $11,570.00.

In 1920, Daniel Mettler caused to be platted a certain portion of his lands and
the same was added to the City of Eureka under the name of Mettler's First
Addition to the City of Eureka. Soon afterwards, Fred W. Homeyer platted a
portion of his lands and the same was added to the city under the name of
Homeyer's Addition. The city also platted a certain portion of its lands along
the lake shore and had it added to the city under the name of Lakeside
Addition.

John Doering tendered his resignation while in office and his vacancy was
filled by Jacob I,. Klein being appointed. The city also bought many trees to
be planted around the lake, at a cost of $700.00. Various projects were also
submitted for construction of a sewage disposal plant at the lake, but all
proposed plans were rejected.

The sewer was also further extended from the intersection of Dakota Street,
north on Fourth Street to the intersection of McPherson Street, and special
assessments were levied against abutting property owners to pay for the cost of
the sewer.

In April of 1921, H. D. Opp resigned as alderman from the first ward.

The election of 1921 resulted in Jacob Weixel being elected alderman from the
first ward for the short term. Henry Pietz was elected for the long term. Jacob
L. Klein was elected alderman from the second ward for the short term, and Fred
G. Mehlhaff for the long term. Casper Vetter was elected as alderman from the
third ward. Casper Vetter was appointed chief of police, after resigning as
alderman, and Jacob J. Klein was appointed in his place. Gust Mauser was
appointed city auditor.

In September of 1921, Henry F. Preszler caused certain of his land Lying
adjacent to the city to be platted and the same was added to the city under the
name of Preszler's Addition to the City of Eureka. This was the last addition
to the city up to the time of this writing.

The election of 1922 saw Karl G. Kafer elected mayor. P. W. Treick was elected
alderman from the first ward; Jacob L. Klein, as alderman from the second ward;
and J. Fred Orthwein alderman from the third ward for the short term, and
George G. Neuharth as alderman from the third ward for the long term. The
question of levying a tax of one mill for free band concerts was also carried
by a vote of 169 to 119. The mayor appointed M. W. Weber, incumbent city
auditor for the first time, and he has held that office continuously down to
the present time. Theo. J. P. Giedt was appointed city attorney.

The city facing an apparent shortage of coal for the Winter of 1922, the
council went on record as asking the Governor of the State and the Fuel
Administrator to look after the needs of the city for the coming winter.

Permission was also granted to C. Wittmayer, C. Kuszmaul, J. J. Keim and J. J.
Pietz, to extend the sewer from the southeast corner of lot four, block 26, of
Milwaukee Land Company's Fifth Addition on Fourth Street, north to about the
center of lot one, block 26, Milwaukee Land Company's Fifth Addition, at their
expense.

The city also purchased water pipe to extend the water mains from the following
places: Commencing on Fifth Street on the west corner, and running west along
Harrison Street to the corner of Sixth Street. And from the corner on Fourth
Street, where Fourth Street intersects with Harrison Street, south one block to
the corner of Schnuerle's Street, and the balance of mains were to be used in
various places where necessary."

The election of 1923 resulted in Jacob J. Weixel being elected alderman from
the first ward for the short term; and J. D. Harr, alderman from the first ward
for the long term. P. W. Treick was elected alderman from the second ward.
Henry Hoehn was elected alderman from the third ward. The question of levying a
one mill tax for free musical concerts was also carried by a vote of 149 to
125.

The water mains of the city were again extended to the following places:
"Commencing at the northeast corner of block 104, Bramaier's Addition, thence
running south along Fourth Street, to the southeast corner of block three of
Schneurle's addition, where Fourth Street intersects Schneurle's Street; and
commencing on the northeast corner of block two of Boettcher's Addition running
west along Harrison Street, to the northwest corner of block two of Boettcher's
Addition, thence commencing on the northeast corner of block three of Reagan's
Addition and running south along Fifth Street and crossing Harrison Street to
the northeast corner of block two of Boettcher's Addition, constituting 1,032
lineal feet." The bid for this extension was let to the Home Plumbing & Heating
Co. of Eureka, South Dakota, at a price of $0.68 per lineal foot.

It was during this time that the city was subjected to perhaps one of its most
trying times. It was besieged on all sides by claims and law suits instituted
by various property owners who claimed damages to themselves and their property
by reason of the overflow from the city's artesian well and sewer. Many of
these claims such as the claims of J. J. Harr, J. F. Harr, J. J. Stock and
others were settled without court action. August Martell, perhaps the one who
suffered the most damage, brought action against the city for his damages, and
employed as his counsel H. O. Hepperle of Aberdeen. The city retained James W.
Brown as their attorney. Upon trial in the Circuit Court of McPherson County, a
judgment was obtained by the plaintiff against the city for $4,230.00. There
was much talk about appealing the case, but finally after several mass meetings
of the citizens of the town and various actions by the city council, it was
decided that the city purchase all of Mr. Martell's lands and settle the case
for the sum of $6,500.00. Mr. Martell conveyed all his property to the city.
The final arrangements were made by the council together with a committee of
tax payers, and Mr. Martell and his counsel.

There were also various other property owners who claimed damage by reason of
the overflow and their claims were finally settled by the city purchasing from
them their property at a price of 40 per cent of the original cost. Certain
premises were also purchased from J. J. Harr at a cost of $5.00 per acre.

The election of 1924 resulted in K. G. Kafer being re-elected mayor. J. J.
Weixel was elected alderman from the first ward; J. L. Klein, alderman from the
second ward; and G. G. Neuharth, alderman from the third ward.

The city also donated the sum of $300.00 for the purpose of constructing a
fence around the race track located on the McPherson-Campbell County fair
grounds.

Mayor Kafer died while in office during his second term, and P. W. Treick, the
president of the council, assumed the duties of acting mayor.

The election of 1925 resulted in P. W. Treick being elected alderman from the
first ward; John Stock, alderman from the second ward; and Fred Netzer,
alderman from the third ward. Jacob J. Weixel was elected president of the
council, and therefor assumed the duties of acting mayor for the ensuing year.
Theo. J. P. Giedt was appointed city attorney.

The franchise of the Dakota Utilities Co. was assigned to the Northern Power
and Light Company, and the said assignment was approved by the city council.

The city having purchased various lands around the lake from various parties, a
portion of said lands were sold to Fred Strobel and J. C. Mutschler, at a cost
of $100.00 per acre. However, in making the conveyances to these parties, the
city reserved a perpetual easement for the purposes of any overflow from the
artesian well, and the said parties covenanted to absolve the city from all
damages resulting from such overflow.

        Airplane View of Eureka -- 1931

The election of 1926 resulted in H. I. Bauer being elected mayor. J. J. Hieb
was elected alderman from the first ward; F. G. Mehlhaff, alderman from the
second ward; and J. G. G. Neuharth, alderman from the third ward. The mayor
appointed A. Martell as city treasurer, who failed to qualify, and H. D. Opp
was appointed in his place. This was the first time that this office was filled
by appointment. Previous to this, the same was always voted upon by the people
at the city elections.

It was during this time that the city water mains were Again extended, this
time to the following places: "Commencing at the northwest corner of block six
of Original Plat, thence running west along Pfeffer Street to the northwest
corner of block 11, Milwaukee Land Company's Third Addition. And commencing at
the northwest corner of block two of Boettcher's Addition, thence due south
along Sixth Street to the southwest corner of block two of Boettcher's
Addition, constituting 666 feet."

Dave Bauer appeared before the council and tendered to them $300.00 which had
been gathered by donations, for the purpose of at least in part paying for the
construction of a public toilet for the city. This tender was accepted and the
city let the contract for constructing the present toilet and jail house to J.
H. Dalke at a price of $1,542.00. It was during this time that curbings were
put around the light posts in the center of the streets, but since that time
both the lights and curbings have been removed and placed next to the
sidewalks.

A representative of the Northern Power & Light Company appeared before the
council and asked that an election be called for the purpose of granting a
franchise to the said company to use the streets and allays of the city for
electrical purposes for a term of twenty years. Upon the same being submitted
to the vote of the people, it was defeated by a vote of 114 to 81. The
ordinance vas then amended and was again submitted to the people at a general
election called in November of 1926. This time it received the required
majority and the light company received a franchise for the term of twenty
years.

The election of 1927 resulted in Henry Ottmar being elected alderman from the
first ward; Christ Becker as alderman from the second ward; and C. Vetter as
alderman from the third ward. The question of granting to the Dakota Central
Telephone Company a franchise to use the streets and alleys of the city for
telephone purpose was voted upon and carried by a vote of 254 to 101.

This council was very much in favor of having a city band again organized, and
during the year 1927, made two appropriations of $150.00 each to be used for
city band purposes. It also donated the sum of $700.00 to be used for graveling
the streets. It also allowed Fred Beck the sum of $150.00 for injuries
sustained while acting as night police for the city.

The election of 1928 resulted in Julius Schamber being elected mayor without
opposition. John Liedle was elected alderman from the first ward; Joseph Bender
was elected alderman from the second ward; and John Wolff, alderman from the
third ward. H. D. Opp was appointed city treasurer.

The first question that was propounded to the new council was presented on a
petition of freeholders of the city demanding that measures be taken to supply
the city with an adequate storm sewer. A mass meeting had been called, and the
people felt that the matter should be left to the city council to decide. They
immediately employed Edward K. Mather of Mitchell, South Dakota, to prepare the
necessary plans and specifications. This was done, and the contract to build a
storm sewer up to the corner of Adam Weller's lot on Second Street, was let to
Tuttle & Ferguson at a price of $1,512. A right of way over the Obenauer,
Mutschler and Oster lots was received free of charge. Because of insufficient
funds in the city treasury, construction was postponed until the fall of 1929,
when the work was done under the supervision of the city council and local
labor employed.

The election of 1929 resulted in Henry Ottmar being elected alderman from the
first ward; Christ Becker, alderman from the second ward; and C. Vetter,
alderman from the third ward. The question of levying a tax of one mill on city
property for the benefit of the Eureka Community Hospital was carried by a vote
of 124 to 96. On May 6, 1929, Mayor Schamber and Aldermen Ottmar and Liedle
tendered their resignations. The president of the council, Joseph Bender, then
assumed the duties of acting mayor and appointed G. G. Human and A. F. Obenauer
to fill the vacancies existing in the offices of aldermen from the first ward.
M. W. Weber was appointed city auditor and Thomas Ringsrud, city attorney.

As an aftermath of heavy rains in the spring and summer of 1928, a lawsuit was
brought by Adolph Ehrman against the city for damages to his property. The city
employed R. F. Williamson as their special counsel to defend the action. Upon
trial in the Circuit Court a verdict was returned in favor of Ehrmann.

In 1930, Dr. O. H. Gerdes, pioneer physician of Eureka, was honored by being
elected to the office of mayor. Robert Jahraus was elected to fill the office
of alderman from the first ward for the short term, and Fred Schanzenbach from
the same ward for the long term. Julius Bender was elected alderman from the
second ward, and Philip Oster, alderman from the third ward. M. W. Weber, Theo.
J. P. Giedt, and E. Heilmann were appointed to the offices of auditor,
attorney, and assessor respectively. In 1931, the appointive officers were the
same, and the elective offices were filled by Andrew Stoebner being elected
alderman from the first ward; Geo. Koelle, Jr., alderman from the second ward;
and C. Vetter, alderman from the third ward. It was during this term that the
city purchased a certain plot of land known as "West Lake" from Phillip Oster
at a cost of $566.80. The deed to this property also provided that the grantor
waives all claims against the city for damages. However, he retained the right
to use this property for pasturage purposes.

In 1932, J. J. Weixel was elected mayor; Christ Fiechtner, alderman from the
first ward; M. M. Smith, alderman from the second ward; and Phil. Oster,
alderman from the third ward. The mayor re-appointed H. D. Opp, city treasurer,
and M. W. Weber, city auditor. Paul Kretschmar was appointed city attorney.

The election of 1933 resulted in R. F. Bockenheim being elected alderman from
the first ward; J. H. Schatz, alderman from the second ward; and John J.
Schaeffer, alderman from the third ward. The question of having the electric
light rate in Eureka reduced, was also submitted to the vote of the people and
was defeated; the vote being 476 to 96. All officers of the council were
re-appointed for another year.

The council for this year permitted the experiment farm to be incorporated
within the city limits. Friederich Schumacher also presented a petition asking
that certain of his lands be excluded from the city limits. The petition was
denied by the council, and Schumacher appealed to the Circuit Court, where the
decision of the council was upheld.

It was also during this time that beer was legalized in South Dakota, and
duties of passing upon beer licenses was conferred upon the council. At the
same time, various governmental projects were started to help the needy and
unemployed in Eureka. A question of voting bonds in the amount of $18,000.00 on
the credit of the city for the purpose of establishing additional sewer lines,
and building a new storm sewer was presented to the voters of the city at a
special election, and was carried by a vote of 333 to 132. It was during this
time that Alderman Phil. Oster tendered his resignation from the council.

In 1934, George Nagel was elected mayor of the city. J. D. Harr was elected
alderman from the first ward; Christ Flemmer, alderman from the second ward;
and Geo. Adam, alderman from the third ward. The new mayor re-appointed M. W.
Weber, city auditor, and H. D. Opp, city treasurer. Theo. J. P. Giedt and Ruben
Beck were appointed city attorney and city assessor respectively.

It was during the Nagel administration that water meters were installed in all
places where city water was being used. A new airport was also established, six
miles north of Eureka on the Jacob Dais land. The contract for the construction
of the sewer authorized by the special election was let to the Starbuck
Construction Company of Starbuck, Minnesota, for a bid of $7,800.00. The
question of having a municipally owned liquor store was voted upon at a special
election and was carried by a vote of 347 to 20.

The election of 1935 resulted in Dan F. Strobel being elected alderman from the
first ward; J. H. Schatz and John J. Schaeffer were re-elected aldermen from
the second and third wards respectively. The mayor appointed Paul Kretschmar
city attorney. This administration sponsored the building of additional sewers
and colored lights were installed for the Christmas season.

In 1936, A. W. Schumacher was elected mayor; A. F. Obenauer, alderman from the
first ward; M. M. Smith, alderman from the second ward; and Geo. Adam, alderman
from the third ward. M. W. Weber and H. D. Opp were appointed city auditor and
city treasurer respectively. F. J. Homeyer was appointed city attorney.

The first assessment of the new town of Eureka was made in 1888, when it was a
part of Gluckstahl Township. The real estate valuation at that time was set at
$9,962.00; the personal assessed valuation was set at $29,276.00; and the levy
was set at thirteen mills. The highest assessed valuation of the city was
reached in 1920, when the totals of real estate and personal property amounted
to $932,000.00, and the mill levy for that year was 35.23. Since that time
there has been a general decline, until the present assessed valuation is
$549,352.00, and the mill levy is set at 52.75.

The 1935 census shows the population of Eureka to be 1,430. This likewise has
shown a gradual increase over previous years. The 1930 census showed the
population to be 1,308; the 1925 census showed it to be 1,228; and the 1920
census showed the population to have been an even 1,200.

                        Eureka's Most Famous Citizen

                            CHARLES N. HERREID

On the occasion of its Golden Jubilee, Eureka pays tribute to its most famous
man. He was Charles Nelson Herreid, who went forth from this city to become
Governor of the State. Previous to that time, 1901, he had been actively
connected with the public life of the county since its inception. He was, in
fact, a prime factor in the organization of the county in 1884, as appears in
another story in this volume.

Governor Herreid was a native of Dane County, Wisconsin, being born there
October 20, 1857, the son of S. N. and Tena (Kytle) Herreid. Prior to receiving
his degree in law from Wisconsin University law school, he had taken his
preliminary training in Galesville University in that same state.

He often related in after life that his coming to Dakota Territory was quite by
accident. He had started the practice of law and came to Aberdeen on business
for a client residing in Winona, Minnesota. The country was then very new,
Aberdeen being little more than a collection of frame shacks, optimistically
called a city. But there was a spirit about the region, primitive though it
was, that appealed to him and he decided to cast his lot with the frontier
prairie.

Accordingly, he and his young wife (he had married Jeanette E. Slye of Mindoro,
Wisconsin, soon after receiving his law degree) came to Leola, shortly after it
was established as county seat. The young attorney had been named clerk and
register of deeds of the county at its organization. The young couple
maintained their office and residence in the same building, for there was, as
yet, no provision for housing the county offices. And there they worked
together, sometimes until the wee hours of the morning on county records, and
perhaps, matters of law as well.

As time went on, he served as county judge, and continuing his interest in
state politics was named lieutenant governor in 1892, holding the office two
terms. Commenting on his record in that body, a historian says, no appeals were
ever taken from his decisions in the sessions. Following his term as lieutenant
governor, he served as National committeeman from South Dakota, 1898-1900.
Other places of honor and trust held by him during this period include service
on the board of trustees of the State University, 1897, and on the board of
regents, 1898-1900.

While he moved to Aberdeen following his term as governor and there played an
important part in not only the legal profession but in the business life of the
city as well, his affection for the scenes of his early life as a professional
man remained steadfast.

When it appeared that the fortunes of Leola would fall short of their earlier
promise the ambitious young attorney moved to Eureka, where he felt his
abilities in law might be exercised to better advantage. Leola had a disastrous
fire in 1889, and as its hopes of acquiring railroad facilities faded into an
indefinite "sometime," there seemed to be nothing else to do. The subsequent
history of his career brings out the accuracy of his judgment. And so it became
Eureka and not Leola that was to claim him as a citizen when he was chosen
governor. He had built a home here at that time and established himself as a
power in his profession as well as a citizen of the municipality who was ever
active for its welfare.

The venerable dictum, that the lieutenant governorship is the graveyard of
political hopes proved untrue in the case of Charles N. Herreid. Not only was
he elected to the highest office of the state after serving in that office, but
his administration is generally conceded to have been one of the most prudent
and economical in the history of the state, thus far. It may be admitted that
the period was one of great prosperity and progress, which may offer a
suggestion to the condition of general contentment which prevailed during
Governor Herreid's administration.

Charles Nelson Herreid, however, was not only an excellent public servant, but
