had qualities as a human being, which achieved for him the general esteem of
those with whom he came in contact. He was a man of commanding appearance, of
medium height and of rather stocky build. He wore a mustache during his entire
manhood, which was closely cropped in later years. He was an able speaker,
being much sought after as a campaign orator. Contemporaries, commenting on his
public utterances, declare he was a master of diction and had a fine sense of
dramatic values, as employed by the public speaker. Especially during the
latter part of his career, he gave some time to writing, being the author of a
book entitled "Monumental Military Extravagances." Several pamphlets and
numerous newspaper articles came from his pen during this period. He was a
member of the Presbyterian church, a thirty-third degree Mason and held high
offices in the Knights of Pythias.

As already suggested, Governor Herreid made his home in Aberdeen following his
term as governor and was a prominent factor in many business and municipal
ventures. When war was declared he offered his services to the armed forces,
but was rejected because of his age. However, his energy found outlet as
director of the state food administration to which he donated his talent and
time wholeheartedly and with marked success.

Being himself a pioneer, he was intensely interested in things historical and
served as president of the State Historical Society. He devoted much time and
energy in preserving historical data and relics and had a collection of many
interesting items gathered during his earlier life. This collection has now
been loaned to the Northeast South Dakota Historical Society and is on display
in the museum of the State Teacher's College in Aberdeen.

Governor Herreid died in Aberdeen July 6, 1928. Mrs. Herreid still maintains
the family home in Aberdeen, but spends winters in the south or with her
daughter in Mason City, Iowa.

JAMES M. BROWN

James M. Brown, pioneer lawyer and judge, was born on a farm in Oneida County,
New York, on the 10th day of January, 1861. His parents were John and Hannah
Brown, nee Mitchell, both of whom were born and reared in the same county.

Judge Brown came of staunch old colonial stock, the genealogy in the paternal
line showing that the family was founded in 1500. His ancestors were driven out
of England during the persecution of those identified with the Quakers, and
they fled to Holland and thence to America at the opening of the sixteenth
century. Mr. Brown's grandfather was a soldier in the Revolutionary War, and
must have deviated from his ancestral faith, inasmuch as Quakers are opposed to
war. James Brown's father was one of the public-spirited farmers of his county,
and the fact that he held public office in his county for a number of years,
shows he wielded no little influence on his community life.

Judge Brown received his early educational training in the public schools of
his native county, and then entered Hamilton College, in the same county, in
which famous old institution he continued his studies until his health became
so impaired as to compel him to abandon his course and seek a change of
climate. Accordingly he went to the south, and at Galveston, Texas, in 1876, he
joined the engineering department of the United States government, with which
work he was identified for the ensuing six years, in various portions of the
south and west.

        James M. Brown

In 1883, he came to South Dakota and located in the then new town of La Grace,
located on the Missouri River, in Campbell County, where he lived for three
years. He then moved to Eureka where he lived continuously until the year 1910.
While in Eureka he was closely identified with Eureka's material, civic and
political development and progress.

In the meantime he had taken up the study of law and so thoroughly covered the
field of jurisprudence as to secure admission to the bar of Dakota Territory in
the year 1887. For many years afterward, he was connected with the legal
affairs of McPherson County, and the State of South Dakota, as a lawyer,
general practitioner, prosecutor and judge. He was state's attorney of
McPherson County for several years, and served as county judge of said county
for three terms. He served numerous terms as city attorney and city auditor of
the City of Eureka. In 1901, he was appointed by Governor Herreid as one of
three code commissioners to revise and codify the laws of the State of South
Dakota, the other two commissioners being Judge Bartlett Tripp and Judge Gideon
C. Moody.

On January 9, 1895, he was united in marriage to Miss Hattie A. Van Gorder, who
was born and reared in Prairie du Chien, Wisconsin. The Browns had no children.
In 1910, Mr. Brown moved to Aberdeen, South Dakota, where he remained in active
practice of law until his death in December, 1936. He was buried in Riverside
Cemetery, Aberdeen, South Dakota. His wife still resides in Aberdeen.

Judge Brown was a Knight Templar Mason and identified with the Order of the
Eastern Star, the Elks, Rotary International, the Knights of Pythias, and the
Modern Woodmen of America.

The following tribute was paid to Judge Brown by a neighbor and friend of many
years: "Highly esteemed among the legal profession in Aberdeen and in the State
of South Dakota; of a fine and lovable nature, quiet and yet jovial, such is
the man I knew as Judge James M. Brown."

LYMAN T. BOUCHER

Lyman T. Boucher was born in Washington County, Illinois, on the 27th day of
February, 1858. His father and grandfather were natives of the state of
Kentucky. The former served in the War of the Rebellion, being a Union soldier.
He died enroute to his home at the close of the war, from disease contracted
during the Wilderness Campaign.

Lyman T. Boucher passed his boyhood days in his native county, where he secured
his early educational training in the public schools, after which he entered
McKendree College, at Lebanon, Illinois. He graduated in June, 1880, with the
degree Bachelor of Laws, being duly admitted to bar of his native state. He
engaged in the practice of profession at Belleville, Illinois, until the spring
of 1883, when he decided to cast his lot with the pioneers of the future
commonwealth of South Dakota. He opened an office in Leola, and later in
Eureka, being one of the early settlers of the town, where he labored earnestly
and successfully in his profession, attaining prestige as an able and
discriminating attorney and counselor and having the respect and confidence of
the people of the community. He as at all times at the forefront in urging
forward all measures tending to advance the general welfare and social and
material progress of his county and state.

He was a member of the state constitutional convention of 1889 and served as
prosecuting attorney of McPherson county, prior to the admission of the state
to the Union. He served as state's attorney for several terms. He was a member
of the state board of regents of education from 1893 to 1896, and served
several years as district judge.

On the 26th of December, 1888, Mr. Boucher was united marriage to Miss Helen
Melvill of Galena, Illinois. They were the parents of four sons, namely:
Melvill, John M., Lyman T. Jr., and Hiram A.

The family moved to Minneapolis, Minnesota, after leaving Eureka. Both Judge
and Mrs. Boucher are dead.

ORVIN J. ROE

Among the few native-born Americans, who have had part in the development of
the Eureka community, was Orvin J. Roe, born in the heart of New England.

Orvin J. Roe, who came to Eureka as editor and publisher of the Eureka Post,
was born at Larabee's Point, Vermont, on the 13th of November, 1851. Authentic
data available indicates the first generation of the Roe family in America was
headed by Hugh Roe, who was married in Weymouth, Massachusetts, in 1655.

Roe was about nine years of age when his parents moved to Michigan. The
economic circumstances of the family were such that he was soon thrown upon his
own resources. He came as a pioneer to Leola, on the 28th of May, 1883, and
made his home there until October, 1896, when he removed to Eureka.

He was clerk of the courts of McPherson County from November 2, 1889, until
January 1, 1896. In October, 1896, he purchased the Eureka Post, which was
published in both English and German editions. Mr. Roe was married to Miss
Dilla M. Sears on the 21st of August, 1872. She died in 1893.

Mr. Roe remarried on the 27th of November, 1895, to Miss Pauline Ansmus of
Rockford, Illinois. One daughter, Ramona, was born to this union.

In the early part of nineteen hundred, Mr. Roe disposed of his business
interests here and moved to the state of Oregon, where he still resides. Mrs.
Roe died several years ago.

VALENTINE METTLER

Born in Kassel, South Russia, in 1862, and arriving in America in 1874, surely
qualified Valentine Mettler for the pioneer roll. He accompanied his parents to
the new country where they paused in the Yankton vicinity for about ten years.

He was married to Anna Marie Hoff in 1883, and the following year they came to
McPherson County, settling about 15 miles northeast of the present site of
Eureka. The nearest point from which they might obtain what little lumber they
needed for the roof of their sod shanty home was Frederick, and from that point
it was hauled by the traditional ox team.

An incident in connection with that trip for lumber which Mettler made,
accompanied by his friend and neighbor, George G. Neuharth, may be related for
it is an example of the terrors and thrills experienced by pioneer women in the
early days.

When the men were away for lumber, Mrs. Mettler and Mrs. Neuharth stayed
together in an improvised shelter, constructed of boards, tepee fashion. It was
open at both ends for the weather was warm. Mrs. Mettler had a small baby, and
before retiring one evening she had hung some of the traditional baby wardrobe
on a line to dry. Waking with a start during the night, she saw the white
garments waving in the wind. Her overwrought imagination pictured it as in the
hands of Indians signaling to another part of their band. She called her
companion and the two lay quietly, in an agony of fear, lest the next moment
the red skins would enter their shelter and kill them. But soon the light of
dawn came and happily proved their fears entirely unfounded. But, oh, what a
night for those two women on the wild prairie.

The Mettlers had little or no money when they started their farming operations,
relying on their industry and frugality to bring them through. Both industry
and frugality were needed, for they did not escape the disappointments of poor
crops and low prices for the products of their farm. A family of twelve
children were born to the Mettlers, nine of whom survive, namely: Fred V.,
Magdalena (Mrs. Henry Roemmich), McLaughlin, S. D.; Henry and Daniel, Eureka;
Bertha (Mrs. August Heupel), Forbes, N. D.; Jacob, Edward, and Fred of Eureka;
and Ida (Mrs. John Kirschenmann), also of the Eureka community.

Valentine Mettler grew to be a successful man in the conduct of his farming
interests and, as a result, was chosen county commissioner, in which position
he served from 1906 to 1910. He passed away in 1911, but Mrs. Mettler still
resides on the original homestead.

GEORGE J. HIEB

One of the Eureka community pioneers, who has seen this section grow from a
prairie wilderness to a region of fine farms and enterprising towns, is George
J. Hieb. In coming here with the great majority of Eureka folk, he is a native
of South Russia, having been born near Odessa on February 12, 1860. His parents
were Jacob and Clara Hieb. The family ancestors came from Wuertemburg, Germany,
in 1812, when Adam Hieb and his parents joined the tide of immigration to the
region around the Black Sea in Russia. Adam Hieb was the grandfather of the
subject of this sketch.

When political and social conditions in Russia made it an unpleasant place to
live for the freedom-loving Germans, they decided to seek new homes in America.
Among them were the Hiebs. On May 2, 1874, George Hieb, then a lad of 12,
accompanied his parents on the long journey to America. After an ocean journey
of two weeks and eleven days of travel by rail, the family arrived in Yankton,
Dakota Territory, on May 27 of that year.

A few weeks later the Hiebs settled in Hutchinson County where they made their
home for several years. Meanwhile, young George grew up, and on December 17,
1883, took unto himself a wife. She was Katharina Perman, born on December 4,
1863, the daughter of Christoph Perman, a resident of that community. To this
union was born a family of children that would have delighted President
Theodore Roosevelt, as there were no less than 16 boys and girls.

However, soon after their marriage the Hiebs decided to join the tide of
immigration to McPherson County where there was still land open to homestead,
pre-emption and tree claim entry. Accordingly, George and his young wife loaded
their movable property on a freight car on May 2, 1884, and left Menno,
traveling by rail to Frederick, via Aberdeen. Frederick was then the nearest or
most convenient railroad town to the far-flung stretches of McPherson County.
Here they loaded their wagon and started the slow trek toward their future
home.

It was an interesting train. They had two mares, two years old, a pair of oxen,
two cows, six chickens, a wagon, and a breaking plow. Most important, they had
$40.00 in cash. They drove westward as far as Spring Creek in Campbell County.
But the young couple didn't like the land so well there and retraced their
tracks back to McPherson County where they selected their claims in Section 11,
Township 127, Range 71.

The land had been surveyed only into townships, so each family picked their
locations by guess. There were, namely ten families who came with the Hiebs and
were a part of the exploratory trip. On May 19, 1884, the Hiebs pitched camp on
the site of their new home. Their first concern was of course to provide some
sort of shelter, but it must not require too much time. Accordingly, they built
a wall of sod and leaned a few scrap boards against it at an angle. This was
their first home in McPherson County.

But an even more important worry met them the day after they arrived on the
claim. Mrs. Hieb discovered they were out of bread and the water jug was empty.
They had no stove nor oven. Necessity, the mother of invention, stood by. Young
Hieb scooped a hollow in the earth in an adjacent bank and drove a pole from
the top, which, when removed, provided an opening for a chimney. Then came the
question of fuel. The young couple hitched up trusty oxen and drove over to a
valley, which to this day known as Hieb's hay lake. There they cut the long dry
grass with a bread knife, tied it into wisps of suitable size for the
fireplace. The balance was used for a bed in the improvised house. The oven
worked admirably and Hieb recalls the bread was extra good. The slough water
was, of course, not good for drinking purposes, so they called the cow over for
a cup of milk whenever they felt in of liquid refreshment.

It was not long, however, until a well was dug and a good supply of drinking
water obtained. Day by day they worked and provided one after another the
little comforts that make life more pleasant. All of it made them very happy.

The coming of the winter made a more substantial house imperative and this was
one of the principal occupations for some time. They built not only a house but
a barn of sod, both of which had roofs of rafters and boards, covered with sod
smeared over with mud in order to make them as waterproof as possible. In order
to complete the houses a trip to Frederick was necessary, since that was the
nearest source of supply for lumber. The horses were too young to drive, so the
trip was made with the ox team and required four days -- a decided contrast to
the rapid means of travel of the present time.

Making hay was quite as imperative as providing shelter. But cutting the hay
with the bread knife might have been all right to provide fuel for baking
bread, but the oxen, the young mares and the cow would need a good supply of
fodder for the winter -- and the Hiebs had no mower -- and the precious forty
dollars must be conserved.

George Hieb had a good neighbor, however, in the person of Valentine Mettler,
and best of all, Mettler had a mower and a rake and was willing to loan them.
So the hay was made.

All work and no play is not so good, the saying goes. The Hiebs therefore took
the opportunity to make a visit to the old home at Menno while their neighbor,
Mr. Stein, took care of the cows and chickens in return for the use of the oxen
in breaking some sod on his claim. The Hiebs together with Mr. and Mrs. George
Neuharth, made the trip which required four days. While in Menno, the Hiebs
helped their parents harvest the crop and stayed for the threshing, earning a
bit of money.

This done, both the Hiebs and the Neuharths gathered up seed wheat, feed and
other necessities and loaded all of it, including their teams, in a freight
car, shipping it to Frederick. Reaching that point they unloaded and proceeded
with the teams and wagons loaded to capacity to their claims. Part of the
supplies had to be left behind and another trip was necessary to haul them to
the new homes.

On the return trip to Frederick, the pioneers gathered buffalo bones along the
trail, loading their wagons with about a ton or so. These bones were in demand
and brought them about $8.00 per ton. In November came another long trip, this
one to Aberdeen where the new settlers filed their final papers on the claims
they had taken, at the U. S. Land Office at that place.

The first crop was seeded in the spring of 1885. The yield was small and the
prices of grain low. Mr. Hieb now recalls his wheat yielded only 10 bushels per
acre and brought from 35 to 40 cents per bushel. Flax sold for 90 cents to a
dollar a bushel and yielded 8 bushels per acre, and oats only 15 bushels per
acre. The latter grain was saved for seed and feed.

The crop was harvested with a combination mower and harvester purchased in
Ipswich at a cost of about $100. This trip to Ipswich remains vivid in Mr.
Hieb's memory as it took four and a half days, the traveling being mainly at
night in order to escape the heat of the day. Oxen were unable to stand
traveling in hot weather, Mr. Hieb says. While the oxen were resting he was
busy picking buffalo bones and had accumulated about a ton when he reached the
town. These he sold for $13.00.

The new town, Eureka, did not come into being until three years after the Hiebs
settled on their claims. Soon after the first trains arrived George Hieb,
together with Jacob Hoffman, drove to the new town to get a load of lumber
each. But they were disappointed as no lumber had as yet arrived. The town was
composed of a mere half dozen buildings or so, he recalls, all situated east of
the railroad tracks on what was known as the school section. The present site
of the town had not yet been surveyed, he says.

Mr. Hieb continued his farming operations until 1927, when he retired to a
comfortable home in Eureka. At the time of his retirement he had added to his
original land holdings, owning over fourteen quarter sections, passing the task
of cultivating the tract to younger shoulders after forty-three years of active
work.

Talking over old days with Jubilee book writers, he recalls that of the ten
families who came with himself and his wife to McPherson County, only six
persons survive, namely, Mr. and Mrs. George Neuharth, Henry Schnabel, Nick
Lechner and himself. Mrs. Hieb died in 1933. He still makes his home in Eureka,
but has spent the winter months in the milder climate of California with his
son, George, who resides at Lodi, in the Golden Gate state.

There, as snow covers the wide fields of his old farm, he perhaps dreams of the
old days, the cold, stormy winters of Dakota and the memorable blizzard of
January 12, 1888. He perhaps remembers drouths of other years and compares them
in retrospect with the most severe of all, those of 1936-36. In spite of all
hardships and privations, it may safely be guessed that his farm and the men
and women who passed through the pioneer period with him still hold an
important place in his affections.

A list of the names and residences of surviving members of the Hieb family
follows: John J. Hieb, implement business, Eureka, South Dakota; Henry G. Hieb,
farming, Eureka; Christina Werner, farming, Eureka; Adam Hieb, merchant,
Marion, South Dakota; Jacob Hieb, farming. McIntosh, South Dakota; George Hieb,
factory superintendent, Stockton Box Company, Lodi, California; Magdalena
Neuharth, farming, Eureka; Katharina Mehlhaff, farming, Eureka; Emma Mehlhaff,
housewife, Eureka; Willhelm Hieb, farming, Lodi, California; Emil Hieb, civil
engineer, United Air Lines, Cheyenne, Wyoming; Gustave Hieb, machine operator,
box factory, Lodi, California; Helen Holman, housewife, Denver, Colorado.

DANIEL OPP AND FAMILY

Daniel Opp was one of the first settlers in this territory, coming here in the
year 1884. His father, also named Daniel, at the age of three accompanied his
parents from their home in Germany to Hungary. Here the family lived for some
time, later migrating to South Russia, where they located at the colony of
Gluecksthal. Like practically all other immigrants to Russia from Germany at
that time, their occupation was farming.

In the latter part of the year 1884, Daniel Opp, with his wife, Katharina, and
six children, joined a party of twenty-seven families from the colony of
Gluecksthal, and emigrated to America. Their reason for leaving the rich and
fertile soil of southern Russia was to evade military service and acquire free
land in the United States. Upon arriving in Menno, South Dakota, the immigrants
scattered in all directions, some remaining in Menno, others going to their
relatives or friends at Scotland or Tyndall. The Opp family, with their friend
and neighbor from the old country, John Nies and family, were received into the
homes of Mr. Opp's cousin, Andrew Mettler, and his son Fred Mettler, at Menno,
where they remained for about two weeks.

They soon learned that there was no more free land available in that part of
the state. However, a new settlement had been started farther north in what is
now McPherson County, and Mr. Opp and Mr. Nies decided to locate here. They
each purchased a pair of oxen, a cow, a wagon, break plow and the necessary
provisions, and after loading this equipment into a freight car they took the
train to Frederick, South Dakota. This was at that time the nearest railroad
station to McPherson County territory.

While they were unloading their equipment at Frederick, they noticed several
other immigrant wagons nearby, and upon investigating they were overjoyed to
find that they were acquaintances from their old home in Russia. These people
had migrated to this country in the spring of 1884, and had settled about sixty
miles west of Frederick. From them the Opp family learned the exact location of
the new settlement for which they were bound.

The first night of their journey westward was spent on the prairie, under the
open sky. While it was late in October, the weather was still warm Early the
next morning the journey was resumed, and they covered a distance of about
twenty-five miles that day. Their progress was slow due to the fact that the
wagons were heavily loaded and there were no roads. There was no wild life in
evidence, and nothing to be seen along the way but stones and bleached buffalo
bones. The second night was passed in Koto, a small village located about seven
miles north of the present site of Leola. In the evening of the third day they
again made camp on the prairie, but the weather had turned colder and they
spent a very uncomfortable night. The water in a nearby pond had frozen so
thick that it was necessary to break the ice with stones in order to water the
live stock. There was no fuel for a fire, and the children had to sleep
directly on the frosty ground, their parents, in half reclining positions
nearby, watching over them.

At noon on the following day they came to the home of Valentine Bittner, where,
during the ensuing conversation with these people they learned that Mrs.
Bittner was Mr. Opp's cousin. They were asked to stay for dinner, which
invitation they gladly accepted as they were all still suffering from hunger
and exposure. After dinner they continued on their way, and after traveling
another nine miles they arrived at the farm of Valentine Mettler, which was
their final destination.

        Jacob D. Opp

In this locality, about thirteen miles northeast of Eureka, Daniel Opp filed
claim on three quarter sections of land, a pre-emption, homestead and timber
claim. Since winter was fast approaching, it was necessary to erect a dwelling
as soon as possible. The lumber, consisting of common boards, was hauled by ox
team from Frederick, and the building was temporarily put up on Valentine
Mettler's land. Due to the fact that the ground was frozen, the house could not
be covered with sod until the next spring. In this one room dwelling, fourteen
feet long and twelve feet wide, the entire family of eight passed the winter,
which was an especially severe one. The house was heated by an old cook stove,
which was given them by Andrew Mettler, of Menno, and the fuel consisted of hay
and rushes. Obviously, it was difficult to keep the house warm with this sort
of fuel.

The first winter seemed a very long one. There was no form of entertainment,
and visits between neighbors were rarely made, due to the poor roads and severe
weather.

In the spring of 1885, the Opp family moved their shanty to their own land and
proceeded to dig a well and erect barns. The barns were constructed of sod and
mud, and the only tools used were a spade, hammer, hatchet and saw.

It was during this spring that an unusual occurrence took place. The weather
had been ideal up to about the 10th of May, when a snowstorm suddenly came up
and raged for three days. This caused much hardship for newly immigrated
families, whose only recourse when caught in the blizzard was to place the
wagon box on the ground and crawl under it for shelter.

In the summer of 1885, Daniel Opp acquired his first pair of horses, bought on
credit from Andrew Mettler, of Menno. The necessary trips to Ipswich for food
supplies could now be made in much less time than by ox team, which was a great
improvement.

The crops during the first five years ranged from poor to very good, wheat
selling for around $1.00 per bushel. In 1891, however, the price dropped to
about 40 cents per bushel, and many immigrants, unable to pay their debts,
became discouraged and left the country.

There were no schools to speak of during the early years, and consequently no
opportunity for the young to receive an education. Church meetings were held in
private homes, and when there was an occasional visit by a minister the people
came from far and near, some by ox team and others on foot.

Daniel Opp passed away in June, 1916. His wife survives him, and now resides in
Eureka with her daughter, Mrs. Rose Meidinger. Mrs. Opp is one of the very few
of the older pioneers in this county, and is now nearly ninety years old.

Thirteen children were born to Daniel and Katharina Opp, four of whom died in
infancy. Those surviving are: Jacob D. Opp, who is married to Katharina
Neuharth. They live on a farm about thirteen miles northeast of Eureka, and
reared a family of ten children, who are all now married.

Daniel D. Opp married Katharina Hoff. There were six children born to them. Mr.
Opp has held the position of county assessor, and for eight years was treasurer
of McPherson County. He now resides in Leola, South Dakota.

Katharina Opp married John G. Neuharth. They brought up a family of thirteen
children. Her husband passed away in 1935, and Mrs. Neuharth now resides on her
farm twelve miles northeast of Eureka.

John D. Opp married Elizabeth Neuharth. They have eleven children and live on
their farm fourteen miles northeast of Eureka.

Christian D. Opp married to Christina Neuharth and residing on their farm
fifteen miles northeast of Eureka. They have a family of seven children.

Fred D. Opp married to Lydia Hoff. They have a family of five children and live
on their farm about fourteen miles northeast of Eureka.

Henry D. Opp married to Anna Sinkbeil. Has been engaged in business in Eureka
since 1912. Owned and operated the Eureka Drug Store until 1916, and then
entered the implement business with John J. Hieb, in which business he is still
active. Mr. Opp has held several prominent offices in the city, such as five
years alderman, eleven years city treasurer, and nine years member of the board
of education. They have three children, Harold, Norman, and Hugo.

Rosina Opp married to August Meidinger. Her husband was engaged in the
implement business at Lemmon, North Dakota, until his death in 1918. Mrs.
Meidinger, with her two daughters, now resides in Eureka.

Christina Opp married to Fred Wittmayer, residing in Portland, Oregon. They
have five children.
                                        --Contributed by Jacob D. Opp.

JOHANN KNAPP

Johann Knapp, a native of Kassel, South Russia, was born on May 12, 1860. With
his uncle, Konrad Schnabel, he came to this country at the age of 15, making
the trip with a large party of other emigrants.

They arrived in Yankton, South Dakota, on May 31, 1874, his uncle taking up
land 40 miles north of Yankton. Here Mr. Knapp remained for ten years, working
for his uncle. In 1882, he was married to Philbina Koerner, a daughter of Jacob
Koerner. The nuptials were performed by Reverend Bonekempfer, in the Reformed
church of Menno.

        Mr. and Mrs. Johann Knapp

In 1884, he and his wife joined a group of families who had decided to locate
farther north. He borrowed $100.00 from his uncle for traveling expenses, and
on May 6th of that year the party took the train from Menno, shipping their
live stock and farm equipment by freight. At Frederick, the end of the line,
the men started westward by horse in search of land. The first day they reached
Koto, a small village eight miles north of where Leola now stands. This village
was later abandoned, the inhabitants removing to Leola. From here they
continued, going as far as the Missouri River, where some of them located. The
others turned back again, Mr. Knapp finally filing on a homestead in Odessa
Township, fifteen miles northeast of Eureka.

Here he erected sod buildings and began breaking up the soil. To determine the
boundaries of the land it had to be measured off with rope, since it was not
yet surveyed. His live stock and farm equipment consisted of two horses, a pair
of oxen, two cows, some pigs, a plow, wagon and harrow. Part of this he bought
in Ipswich on credit. In this first summer of 1884, he planted potatoes and
sowed about seven acres of flaxseed. The crops failed to mature, however, due
to late planting and lack of rain. In order to eke out a living he gathered up
buffalo bones and sold them in Frederick for from ten to twelve dollars per ton.

The next year rain was plentiful, the crops as a result, turning out good. This
year he sowed flaxseed and wheat, the flax yielding 184 bushels and the wheat
200 bushels. Since threshing machines at that time were conspicuous by their
absence, the grain had to be trampled out by the horses' hoofs.

Snow and rainfall, according to Mr. Knapp, were more abundant in the early
years than now. The winters, especially in 1888, were more severe, blizzards at
times lasting three or four days. The snowdrifts sometimes practically covered
the buildings, hiding them so that people drove over them with their ox teams
without being conscious of it.

Buffalo, deer and antelope were also still in evidence. The young antelope were
sometimes caught and taken home to be domesticated.

Mr. Knapp recalls an amusing incident which took place in the winter of 1885.
His neighbor's wife was baking bread in the outdoor oven, which was built into
the ground. A pair of runaway oxen hitched to a stoneboat came along, broke
through the oven and trampled around on the bread. As a consequence, a new oven
as well as a fresh batch of bread had to be made.

Mr. and Mrs. Knapp brought up a family of nine children. Mrs. Knapp passed away
in 1901, and one of their daughters, Philbina, died in 1932 as a result of an
automobile accident. The other children are: Christina, married to Johann Heyd,
a farmer near Long Lake, S. Dak.; John, farming near Linton, N. Dak.; Karolina,
married to Jacob Koerner, near Eureka; Fred, farming near Eureka; Magdalena,
married to Johann Laemmle, a farmer near Long Lake, S. Dak.; Jacob, farming
near Eureka; Katharina, married to John G. Neuharth, a farmer near Eureka.

Mr. Knapp was remarried to Elizabeth Spitzer in 1912. He gave up farming in
1920, locating in Eureka where he and his wife now reside.

PHILIP MILLER

One of the earliest pioneers who settled in McPherson County was Philip Miller.
Mr. Miller was born in Kassel, South Russia, on the 23rd of October, 1858. In
1876, he emigrated to America and located at Menno, Dakota Territory. While at
Menno, he was employed on the crew that built the railroad from Yankton to
Menno. After two years here, he moved to Freeman, Dakota Territory, where he
took out a homestead claim on a quarter of land, and after five years of
occupancy proved up on the same.

In 1883, his interest was arrested by large tracts of land which were being
opened up for homestead entry in the northern country. He located on a claim of
land about sixteen miles northeast of Eureka, and for twenty-two years
afterward, he lived on this land continuously. During the early years of Mr.
Miller's stay on the homestead, it was necessary to go to Ipswich for lumber
and supplies. The trip was generally made by ox team, and buffalo bones were
taken along and sold as a means of raising money. Mr. Miller states that these
sold for $15.00 a ton at this time.

The life of the Miller family in early Dakota does not vary much from that of
other pioneers. They endured the same hardships, had the same joys, and
possessed the same courage which was manifest among all of the early settlers.
In 1903, an especially severe calamity came into the lives of the Millers. A
tornado struck their farmyard and completely demolished their farm buildings,
killed their stock, except a hen with a brood of chickens. Only the family,
which sought refuge in a storm cellar, was left unharmed. The family was much
discouraged after this catastrophe, but through the help of neighbors, and
friends in Eureka, they were able to rebuild their farm and continue their
farming careers.

Philip Miller married Magdalene Kenner while still in the old country. Eleven
children were born to this union, eight of whom are still living. The names of
the Miller children are: Kathryn Kranzler, who died in 1921; Jacob Miller, of
Firesteel, S. Dak.; Emilia Baite, Linton, N. Dak.; Philip, Aberdeen ; Magdalena
Job, Linton, N. Dak; Andrew, Wishek, N. Dak.; August, Linton, N. Dak.; Henry,
Lancaster, Pa., who died in 1933; John, St. Cloud, Minn.; Philipina Huber,
Wishek, N. Dak.

JOHANN SCHNABEL

Another of the earliest colonists in this territory was Johann Schnabel, who
located twelve miles east of Eureka. He was born in Kassel, South Russia, on
November 5, 1858. At the age of 22, he was married to Katharina Kraemer, the
ceremony being performed by the Reverend Mr. Becker, in the Lutheran church of
Kassel. In May, 1883, with his wife and three children, he joined a party of
emigrants to the United States. Other members of this group were Adam Haas,
Peter Hauck, Philipp Rueb, Friedrich Springer, Johann Meidinger and August
Boschee, all accompanied by families.

The Schnabel family arrived in Menno, S. Dak., near the end of May, 1883, where
they met Johann Hoff, who took them out to his farm to spend the night. After
visiting his cousin, Daniel, for two days, Schnabel and his family were taken
to the farm of his brothers, Henry and Jacob Schnabel, who lived in the Tripp
territory. Here they remained until fall.

During the haying and harvesting season, Mr. Schnabel found employment on the
farm of Johannes Gimble, where he worked for two months at $20.00 per month.
Later in the summer he was employed at threshing, receiving from 50 cents to 75
cents per day.

With the approach of winter, it was necessary to find a habitation for himself
and family until spring. Not far distant was the farm of Jacob Herr, Sr., which
was tenantless. After receiving permission from the owner, Mr. Schnabel and his
family took up their residence there, occupying the house for the duration of
the winter with the family of one David Becker. During part of this period he
and Mr. Becker found work at husking corn, receiving as payment two sacks of
corn each per day.

In the spring of 1884, Mr. Schnabel began to make preparations for the journey
northward. He had brought along about $300.00 in cash from Russia, and this
sum, in addition to what he had earned the previous summer, was sufficient to
buy live stock and necessary equipment. After purchasing two oxen, two cows,
three pigs, some chickens, a wagon, plow and a few tools, he loaded his
possessions on a freight car and, with his family, took the train to Ipswich.
Here they unloaded their live stock and equipment, hitched the oxen to the
wagon, and continued their journey across the prairie. They were on the road
for two days, stopping the first night at the farm of Fred Vilhauer, in the
Hosmer territory, and the following evening arriving at Henry Schnabel's farm.

Now began the search for suitable land. After driving over the surrounding
country with Henry Schnabel, who owned a team of horses, he selected a site
twelve miles east of the present location of Eureka. Here he took up a
preemption and timber claim, later also filing on a homestead.

The years 1886 to 1888, Mr. Schnabel recalls, were not very productive, the
crops as a rule being small. The year 1888, however, brought favorable weather
conditions, with sufficient rainfall. He harvested 330 bushels of wheat.

Until Eureka was founded, Ipswich was the nearest source for food supplies the
trip occasionally being made on foot. Money being scarce, the farmers were
sometimes compelled to borrow some to tide them over the lean years. Interest
rates, however, were very high, and Mr. Schnabel recalls one instance when he
borrowed $15.00 from a bank. When he repaid the loan six months later, the
interest amounted to $10.00 -- about 133 per cent per annum.

Eleven children were born to this union, six of whom died in infancy. Mrs.
Schnabel passed away in 1901.

The surviving children are: Konrad, a farmer, fourteen miles east of Eureka;
Gottfred, farming twelve miles east of Eureka; Adam, a farmer near Venturia, N.
Dak.; John, employed in Zeeland, N. Dak.; Rosina, married to William Berreth,
conducting a meat market in Zeeland, N. Dak. Mr. Schnabel retired from farming
in 1927, and is now living on his old homestead with his son, Gottfred.

HENRY SCHNABEL, SR.

Henry Schnabel, Sr., was born near Kassel, South Russia, on September 9, 1862.
As a boy he came to this country with his father, George Schnabel, and brothers,
Konrad, Ludwig, Philipp and Daniel, making the trip with a party of about 100 
other emigrants.

They arrived in Yankton in the spring of 1874. Here his father took up land 35
miles from the city. Henry Schnabel remained there for ten years when he
decided to try his fortune farther north. He was then 22 years of age.

Among others who left Yankton with him were: George Neuharth, George Hieb and
Johan Knapp, with their families. Loading their possessions on a freight car,
they traveled by rail from Menno to Frederick, which was then the end of the
line. Here they unloaded and the men started westward by team, in search of
suitable land. They looked over the country as far as the Missouri River, where
some of them stayed while Mr. Schnabel and a few others turned back again. They
finally found a satisfactory location in Odessa Township, 11 miles northeast of
Eureka, where they filed on their homesteads.

With lumber he hauled from Ipswich, Mr. Schnabel proceeded to build a roof,
which served as a temporary dwelling. This roof was set with the eaves directly
on the ground, and was later used in the construction of the sod buildings. He
had brought live stock and farm equipment consisting of a cow, wagon and a
breaking plow. He now purchased a pair of oxen, which took the last money he
had -- about $140.00.

This first summer he cooked his meals and baked bread in a homemade stove and
oven built into the ground out in the open. The cook stove was made out of mud,
while the construction of the oven was still more ingenious and today would
appear as quite a novelty. First, a hole about four feet square and five feet
deep was dug. About half way down another hole about two feet in diameter was
dug sideways into the ground, from which a small opening, serving as a chimney,
was made to the surface. In this sidelong hole the fire was made and the bread
baked on top of it. The results, it is claimed, were very satisfactory.

No crops were planted this first spring, due to the fact that the ground was
too dry. The land had not been surveyed and no one knew where his boundaries
were.

In the late summer of 1884, he erected a house and barn. On October 24th of
that year, he was married to Fredericka Stein, a daughter of his neighbor,
Johannes Stein. The next spring he commenced breaking up some of his land and
sowed about 25 acres of flaxseed, which yielded about 300 bushels and was sold
for $1.00 per bushel.

In the year 1911, a tornado passed over his farm and demolished the house and
barn. Several of the children were covered with debris from the house, but
miraculously were uninjured.

Mr. Schnabel eventually acquired 960 acres of land, all except 160 acres
located in McPherson County.

Five children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Schnabel, namely: Christina, located in
McIntosh, S. Dak.; Henry, living on the old homestead; John, died in 1919 at
the age of 23; Rosa, married Jacob Hieb, a farmer near McIntosh, S. Dak.;
Katharina, who died in infancy.

Mr. Schnabel retired in 1911 and located in Eureka, where he now resides.
After the death of his wife in 1933, he was remarried to Barbara Heberle.

JACOB HUMANN

In the year 1884, a covered wagon started north from Scotland, Dakota
Territory, for McPherson County. That may hardly be said to have been worthy of
mention, but this particular wagon was perhaps longer remembered by those who
saw it, than many others, for there was one ox and one horse hitched to it.
With this unusual motive power, Jacob Humann and his good wife, Christina, made
the long journey to their new home. One may well imagine they caught a bit of
the humor of the situation, but these two new settlers were in dead earnest.
They had come from Russia two years earlier where both had been born in
Friedensthal, the young husband on December 11, 1857, and the wife on June 2,
1858.

Upon arrival in Scotland in 1882, Jacob Humann, who was a blacksmith, worked
for two years at that trade. But he was not content with the prospects for the
future in that line of endeavor and so came to the conclusion it would be best
to join the tide of immigrants who were wending their way north in ever
increasing numbers. The fact that their resources were limited did not stop
them, for had not people who had less started out? Why not they?

Accordingly, with their poorly mated team, the young couple and their little
family started out. One may conjecture it was a slow trip, but they arrived in
McPherson County without serious mishap, settling on a claim in Section 20,
Township 128, Range 72. Once arrived, they took up eagerly the arduous duties
which the pioneers found awaiting them. It goes without saying they met and
overcame all the hardships and privation which were the common lot of the new
settlers. The Humanns never said much about them. Apparently, they took it all
as a matter of course.

After residing in South Dakota for a number of years, Mr. Humann, perhaps
mindful of the milder climate of South Russia, sought a milder climate in the
state of Washington. The entire family moved to Lind in that state, but found
it disappointing. Consequently, they returned to their McPherson County farm
and continued to operate it until 1908. At that time Mr. Humann moved to Eureka,
entering the general merchandise business with his sons, Andrew and Gottlieb.
The partnership occupied the building occupied at the present time by Humann
Brothers.

The Humanns made their home in Eureka continuously until December 14, 1931,
when Mr. Humann died. The mother lived two years longer, passing on July 8,
1933. Both are buried in the Joachim cemetery about twelve miles northeast of
Eureka.

The Humann family was composed of eleven children. Three died in infancy and
two, Conrad and Dorothy, in maturity. The surviving children are: Peter, Jacob,
Andrew, Gottlieb, Emil, and Mary (Mrs. John Iszler).

WILLIAM BRAMAIER

One of Eureka's first pioneers was William Bramaier. Mr. Bramaier was born in
Cincinnati, Ohio, on he 15th day of February, 1857. At the age of three, he
moved with his parents to Buffalo City, Minnesota, where he remained until
1870, when with the current tide of emigration, he proceeded westward and
located at Menno, South Dakota. He remained at Menno until 1884 when he headed
northward, and located in the now extinct town of St. Petersburg, which was
located on the school section directly adjacent to the present town site of the
city of Eureka. At this place and in conjunction with his brother-in-law,
Charles Pfeffer, he operated a machine and farm implement business.

In 1887, when the Milwaukee railroad was extended northward from Roscoe, and
the town of Eureka was started, he moved his place of business to the original
townsite. He is generally conceded to be one of the first business men in
Eureka. He acquired extensive real estate interests and in May of 1893, caused
certain lands lying south of the present state highway No. 10 platted and added
to the city of Eureka under the name of Bramaier's addition. He also was the
managing officer of the Bank of Eureka for a considerable period.

William Bramaier occupied several positions of public trust. In 1892, and
again in 1894, he was elected city treasurer. He was not able to complete his
second term, dying while in office. He was a member of the first state
legislature that convened in the new State of South Dakota.

In 1894, he died a comparatively young man. He never married, and after his
death, practically all of his estate was inherited by his sister, Mrs. Charles
Pfeffer. Rumor has it, that this inheritance furnished considerable of the
original capital, through which the Pfeffer family afterwards built up their
large fortune.

GEORGE G. NEUHARTH

"Hey! Yipee! Youuu, get along there! Haww! Haww!" might have been the shouts of
young George G. Neuharth, some fifty-five years ago, as he wearily trudged the
long dusty miles from Menno, South Dakota to the "Eureka Country" with a herd
of forty frisky, hard-to-manage, young cattle. Prairie fires, raging blizzards
which piled snow shanty high, stock raising, wine business, and promoting the
worth-while hospital project have been just a few of the many pioneering
experiences of this fine Eureka citizen.

George G. Neuharth, born November 20, 1861, at Kassel, South Russia,
accompanied his parents as a young lad to the United States in 1874. The family
first settled thirty-five miles northwest of Yankton, South Dakota, then the
farthest point west of settlement in that section.

Life was a hard battle during those first few years in this country. The
necessity of a cash income prompted the performing of varied tasks. The winter
of 1880, one of deep snow, was spent in cutting wood which was hauled as fuel
to Menno. During this winter period trains had stopped running and desperate
measures had to be taken to supply fuel to homes in order to keep from freezing.

On January 16, 1883, George G. Neuharth married Christina Goehring, a daughter
of Mr. and Mrs. Gottfried Goehring, at the Bright Church, Menno.

Experiencing difficulty in providing water for stock in that vicinity, young
George Neuharth decided to look for another location. Land was being opened for
homestead entry farther north, and as a result the young couple determined to
push on into this wild new country. The following year, Mr. and Mrs. Neuharth,
accompanied by a large party of settlers, left Menno for what is now the
northern part of the state. Included in this group were the following persons:
Mr. and Mrs. George J. Hieb, Jacob Burrer, Christ Burrer, Val. Mettler, John
Knapp, Fred V. Preszler, Nick Lechner, Henry Schnabel, Jacob Meidinger, John
Becker, Karl Fink, John Quaschnick, Dan Quaschnick, Martin Bickel, Andrew Hoff,
Fred Hoff, and Phil Thurmaier. The party traveled west from Frederick where Mr.
Neuharth loaded his team with lumber and provisions and entered McPherson
County on May 10, 1884. They journeyed as far west as the present site of
Herreid, South Dakota, and not being impressed with the land ahead, turned back
and finally settled in the territory about fourteen miles northeast of the
present site of Eureka. At that time the county was surveyed only into
townships, so to determine present day sites is now rather difficult.

The first week after their arrival was spent in a tent until a 12 x 16 shanty
had been erected. In the fall a more substantial dwelling, a sod house, became
their home. Eight years later, their economic circumstances permitted the
construction of a frame building which is still occupied and in good condition.
The shanty was used as a church for years.

Mr. Neuharth was resourceful and his activities were varied. He spent much of
his time assisting newcomers in locating their claims. Carpenter work was
plentiful, and he worked at it intermittently. One of his prize possessions is
a small work-bench which he made when he was seventeen years of age.

        Mr. and Mr. George C. Neuharth

Trading during the first three years was done at Frederick and Ipswich, South
Dakota. Buffalo bones were the stock-in-trade. They were picked off the
prairies, loaded on wagons, hauled to distant towns and bartered for
provisions. A load of buffalo bones varied from $8.00 to $12.00 in value.
Travel was by ox team, and exceedingly slow; several days were spent on the
trip.

A Prairie fire that ravaged the country-side made the fall of 1884 a very
gloomy one. Feed and fuel had been destroyed. After a torturous winter of weed
burning to provide warmth and fuel for cooking, the warmer days of spring were
hope-renewing. Mr. Neuharth had returned to the Menno country during the summer
of '84 and had harvested a 600 bushel flax crop which he sold for $400.00. This
sum was a Godsend during the dreary winter that followed.

His first flax crop sown here, mainly for seed, was picked by hand. The lack of
implements hindered progress and sometimes accidents and weather conditions
apparently stopped it temporarily.

During the spring of 1885, Mr. Neuharth walked back from the Menno territory
with forty head of young cattle which were later sold as oxen. His live stock
during that year consisted of two horses, two oxen, and one cow.

At this time 200 acres of grain were usually seeded each year. One or two young
men, as yet unsettled, were hired to assist with the work.

The winters of 1886 to 1889 were especially severe with heavy snowfall. Mr.
Neuharth took care of about seventy-five head of stock through the winter
months of these years. The snow was so deep that the house was entirely covered
and tunneling from house to barn was a common necessity. The snow crusts were
so hard that it was nothing unusual for the stock to walk over the tops of the
buildings. The men had to carry all the feed by hand and they often labored in
the barns all day. Lights were required in the daytime because of the snow
blockade.

Although the hardships were at times discouraging, every year brought some
progress. More land was acquired. Life became somewhat easier as closer markets
removed the necessity of making tedious journeys to market; increased
settlement brought about a more normal social life. When a church had been
established, Mr. Neuharth spent many years as a Sunday School teacher and as
an organist for church services.

A severe diphtheria epidemic during the winter of 1896 claimed many lives in
the community. At this distressing time the Neuharths lost two sons and a
daughter within a period of three days.

In 1904 Mr. Neuharth gave up farming, moved to Eureka, and acquired an
interest in the general merchandise business of Fred Preszler and John Pietz.
In 1905, because of his son George's health, Mr. Neuharth disposed of his
business interests and moved his family to Lodi, California. The son, George,
died there. Not satisfied with California surroundings, the Neuharths returned
to Eureka where Mr. Neuharth became engaged in the wholesale wine business,
dealing with former associates in California. Ten years in this occupation
resulted in the disposal of an even 100 carloads of wine. After prohibition was
declared the fruit business occupied his interest for three years, after which
he entered the hardware business with his son, John. They operated in
connection with the Eureka Bazaar.

After retiring from active business in 1923, Mr. Neuharth has devoted much time
to civic activities. He served for several years as first chairman of the
Eureka Community Hospital Association, and is at the present time still
interested as a director of the board. At one time he served as a bank
director, and was interested in the Dakota Utilities Company and the Dakota
land and loan Company. As city alderman for several terms, Mr. Neuharth
displayed fine citizenship.

The Neuharths happily celebrated their Golden Wedding Anniversary on January
16, 1933. Mrs. Neuharth has the distinction of being the only woman of the
eighty-four immigrants, with whom she arrived, to be living in Eureka today.

Their family of fourteen children consisted of nine sons and five daughters.
Seven of this number are living today. There are twenty-five grandchildren and
eleven great-grandchildren.

The living children of this fine union are: Christina, born January 12, 1885,
now Mrs. Christ D. Opp, Eureka; Katharina, born February 10, 1888, now Mrs.
Fred J. Hoff. Eureka; Margaret, born June 3, 1889, now Mrs. Christ J. Ehresman,
Roscoe; John, born May 22, 1898, Eureka; Gottfried, born April 24, 1900,
Aberdeen; Elisabeth, born January 17, 1902, now Mrs. Theo. Mehlhaff, Eureka;
Edwin, born April 8, 1905, Eureka.

KARL FINK

Another of the earliest pioneers of this territory was Karl Fink, who came here
in 1884. He was born in Kassel, South Russia, on February 4, 1868. At the age
of eleven, in the company with his mother, brother-in-law, Jacob Meidinger, and
two sisters, Barbara and Eva, he emigrated to the United States in the year
1879.

The Fink family arrived in Yankton, Dakota Territory, on June 5, 1879, where
they were greeted by a relative, Konrad Fink, and by Gottfred Werre, Sr. The
latter took them out to his farm about forty miles northwest of Yankton, where
they remained for about a month. During this period Mr. Fink's mother and his
brother-in-law, Jacob Meidinger, each filed on a homestead in that locality.
Logs were hauled from the Missouri River and a sod house and barn were
constructed, and the family established themselves in their new home.

Since they had come here practically penniless, it was necessary to earn money
for subsistence. During the harvest season, Jacob Meidinger and his wife,
Barbara, found work with the neighboring farmers, receiving $1.00 per day each.
The following spring young Karl, who was then twelve years old, was put to work
herding cattle, which occupied him for about five months and brought the sum of
$35.00 into the family purse.

After farming here for five years, it u as decided to locate in the northern
part of the state, as more land was available there. They proved on their
homesteads, Jacob Meidinger selling his for $800.00, while Mrs. Fink gave her
160 acres to her daughter, Eva, who remained there and later married Theodore
Buchholz.

In August of 1884, the family boarded the train from Scotland to Frederick,
shipping their possessions by freight. Since there were no roads west of
Frederick the journey had to be continued over the rough prairie by ox team.
Recent fires had raged over the land, and the outlook for miles was one of
bleak desolation. After two days of travel, they arrived at John Becker's farm,
where they remained while inspecting the surrounding country.

Up to this time, it was impossible to file on land due to the fact that it had
not been surveyed. This task was accomplished by October, however, and
accordingly Mrs. Fink and Jacob Meidinger each took up a pre-emption and timber
claim, located in Petersburg Township, 15 1/2 miles northeast of Eureka.
Temporary dwellings were erected, consisting of only a roof made of boards,
which was set with the eaves on the ground. This form of dwelling was quite
satisfactory during sunny weather, but during a heavy rainfall the unshingled
roof leaked badly and the occupants sometimes had to sleep in the mud, soaked
to the skin. After digging wells and making hay, these roofs were completed
into sod houses, which, needless to say, were much more comfortable.

In the year 1889, Karl, who was now 21 years of age, filed claim on a homestead
for himself. He erected a sod house and barn, and, with the help of his mother,
began farming operations. His live stock and equipment consisted of two oxen, a
pony, a colt and a plow. He also had the use of a grass mower and hay rake
belonging to his brother-in-law.

These early years were fraught with much hardship. Due to the many prairie
fires, farm property was often in danger of destruction. The snowfall during
the winter was usually very heavy, sometimes almost completely covering the
buildings. The oxen, walking over the roofs of the houses, occasionally broke
through, and the sudden appearance of an ox hoof descending from the kitchen
ceiling was not an uncommon occurrence.

Working with oxen was also, at times, a severe strain on the patience. While
plowing they would suddenly take it into their heads to run into a nearby pond,
dragging the plow along. Throwing stones and yelling at them was of no avail --
they insisted on demonstrating their traditional stubbornness by refusing to
come out until cooled off.

Mr. Fink also recalls an Indian scare in 1889. The rumor was circulated that
the Indians were rising up against the whites, and the farmers all dropped their
work and hurried to Eureka. It turned out, however, that the rumor was
purposely started by a few rascally individuals, who, after the farmers left
their homes, took advantage of them by stealing their property.

School and church buildings there were none during the early years. For church
services the people came together in some farm home, although they had no
minister, except occasionally for a baptism or marriage ceremony. School was
started after a few years at the farm of Christoph Mohr, the first teacher
being John Schulz.

In the year 1893, Karl Fink was married to Magdalena Schmierer, a daughter of
Jacob Schmierer, residing near Ashley. The marriage ceremony was performed by
the Reverend Ulrich Reue. Six children were born to this union, one son dying
in infancy and another, John, passing away in 1919, at the age of 28. Those
surviving are: Jacob, Christian and William, all farming 20 miles northeast of
Eureka, and Reinhold, living with his parents.

In spite of the low prices for farm products, Mr. Fink gradually acquired more
land as his financial circumstances permitted, at one time owning 1,080 acres.
A survey of his land purchases from time to time reveals the rising trend of
real estate values in this territory. In 1909 he bought 160 acres for $300.00;
about two years later, 160 acres for $1,450.00; in 1915 he purchased 120 acres
for $1,500.00; in 1920 he paid $3,200.00; and about a year later $4,000.00 for
a quarter section.

Mr. Fink, with his wife and son, Reinhold, is still engaged in farming on the
old homestead.

JOHN LIEDLE

John Liedle, one of the earliest pioneers in this territory, was born in
Gluckstal, South Russia, on December 14, 1864. He came to America in 1875 with
his parents and four brothers and sisters, Jacob, Christiana, Christina and
Friedericka. The family located near Yankton where his father filed on a
homestead. After the death of his father in 1885, the family came to this
territory and homesteaded on land nine miles east of the present site of Eureka.

About two years later John Liedle and his brother Jacob engaged in business in
the settlement of Petersburg, located where the experiment farm is now
situated. When the railroad was extended to Eureka they took up their residence
there and began operating a hotel. After a few years John Liedle found
employment in a grain warehouse, later building his own elevator. He was active
in the grain business until shortly before his death, a period of over forty
years.

In the year 1898 he was united in marriage to Elizabeth Buckenberger, the
ceremony being performed by Rev. Reinhardt. There were six children born to
this union, namely: Herbert, residing in Eureka; Eugene, employed as cashier of
the Eureka State Bank; Edna, died in infancy; Otto, employed by the Northern
Power and light Company of Mobridge, South Dakota; Adina and Wanda, both
residing in Eureka.

In 1894 John Liedle was elected sheriff of McPherson County, serving four years
in that capacity. He also served as alderman of his ward several times, and in
1898 was marshal of the city of Eureka for about a year. He visited his old
home in Russia in 1893, in company with Fred Hepperle and Peter Wittmayer.

His death occurred in June, 1936, as a result of a paralytic stroke. Mrs.
Liedle still resides in Eureka with two daughters, Adina and Wanda.

ADOLPH MEIDINGER

Adolph Meidinger, a native of Kassel, South Russia, was born on November 8,
1868. He was married to Barbara Mehlhaff in 1881, in the Lutheran church of
Kassel, Reverend Becker officiating. Three years later, he and his wife joined
a party of emigrants from his village, bound for the United States. Other
members of the group were: Johann Boschee, Fred G. Mehlhaff, George Adam
Preszler, Valentine Boschee, Jacob Ketterling, Henry Stoebner, Johann Springer,
Friedrich Rueb, Adam Preszler and Philip Martel, all with their families with
the exception of Fred G. Mehlhaff, who was unmarried. These families all
settled in this neighborhood.

They arrived in Menno, South Dakota, in 1884 and passed the winter there. The
following spring, on April 28, 1885, Mr. Meidinger and his wife, accompanied by
Jacob Bender, who had preceded them to Menno, took the train to Ipswich. From
there they continued by ox team, locating finally in what is now Kassel
Township, ten miles southeast of Eureka. Here they filed claim on a homestead
and commenced putting up sod buildings. One feature of the house was the oven
built into one of the walls. Constructed of stones and mud, this oven served to
heat the house as well as cook and bake. It was stoked from the outside, where
a small shed was built, the fuel consisting mainly of hay and manure. These
ovens remained warm all day, and required no further stoking after being heated
up in the morning.

Mr. Meidinger was fortunate in having $200.00 in cash when he came here. With
this money he purchased a wagon, a breaking plow, a harrow, two pairs of oxen
and some necessary tools. He also acquired two cows, a few chickens and a pig.
Hard work and thrift brought its reward, and in spite of the low prices of farm
products, he eventually owned 2,240 acres of land, 1,440 acres in McPherson
County and 800 in North Dakota.

The winters in the early years were much more severe, with more snow than now,
according to Mr. Meidinger. He recalled an amusing incident that happened
during the year of 1887. "That winter," he said, "we had an exceptionally heavy
snowfall, and many furious blizzards. One day I ran out of tobacco, and since
it was a nice day I started walking over to my neighbor to borrow some. As I
neared his farm I saw a black object rising up against the white background of
the snow. I quickened my pace, and upon coming up to the house saw it was his
wife climbing out of the chimney. She explained that the snowdrifts had
hopelessly blocked the door, and since her husband was not there to shovel a
path, that was the only way out of the house. She placed two chairs on top of
each other in the fireplace, and by that means climbed out. After clearing a
path to the door for her, I got my tobacco and returned home."

In those days there were no church or school buildings, Mr. Meidinger relates.
School and church meetings had to be held in private homes, people driving to
them on a stoneboat or hay rake hitched to an ox. Despite their many hardships,
however, the people were content, perhaps more so than they are today, with all
modern conveniences.

Thirteen children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Meidinger, all living except two --
Martha, who died in childhood; and August, a victim of influenza. Those living
are: Katharina, married to Daniel Dammel, a farmer near Medina, N. Dak.;
Adolph, a cream buyer at Lemmon, S. Dak.; Fred, a farmer living in North
Dakota; Henry, a farmer near Thunder Hawk, S.D.; Paulina, married to Karl K.
Kundert, a dairy farmer at Lodi, California; Albina, married to Henry Fauth, a
farmer east of Eureka; Otto, a farmer living east of Eureka; Albert, farming
east of Eureka; Emil, of Lodi, California; Ida, married to Peter Quaschnick, a
mechanic in Eureka; and Bertha, employed in a beauty parlor at Bemidji,
Minnesota.

Mr. Meidinger retired in 1920 and took up residence in Eureka. Since the death
of his wife in 1927, he has resided with his daughter and son-in-law, Mr. and
Mrs. Peter Quaschnick.

JACOB M. KREIN

The following is the substance of a communication received by the historical
committee in response to their newspaper solicitations for data on pioneer
Eureka:

"It is indeed a great pleasure for me to give you my congratulations on the
celebration of your fiftieth anniversary. I am grateful that my father, Michael
Krein, was an early pioneer of McPherson County, South Dakota. He came to this
county in 1885, with his wife and one child, myself, who at that time was only
nine months of age. My mother's name was Elisabetha. My father filed on a
homestead about twelve miles southeast of Eureka, where he remained until 1905,
at which time he moved to North Dakota. My father died in North Dakota, in
1918, and my mother in 1926. I have an uncle living in Hosmer, South Dakota, by
the name of Jacob Will. I helped Hebron celebrate its fiftieth anniversary in
1935, and if nothing unexpected happens, I expect to help Eureka celebrate its
Golden Jubilee in 1937. In thinking back over the past fifty years, I cannot
help but marvel at the wonderful advancements that the Eureka territory has
made, and I think that it would behoove all of us to give thanks to our God for
His blessings during these years. Present hard times cannot last, and I am sure
that the spirit which prompted our pioneer fathers to prevail over all
hardships, will carry us out of the present depression. With best
wishes for your 1937 Golden Jubilee, I remain,
                                "Jacob M. Krein and Family, Antelope, N. Dak."


GEORGE ADAM

Two of the most pleasant and congenial pioneer people now living in Eureka are
Mr. and Mrs. George Adam. Though they both have endured years of privation and
hardship, they have maintained a cheerful outlook on life. Today they are quite
youthful appearing, despite the fact Mr. Adam is in his sixties and Mrs. Adam
in the late fifties.

Like the greater number of pioneers in this community, the Adam family, too,
came from South Russia in 1886. George Adam, of German parentage, was born
March 16, 1876. He was nine years of age at the time of his long trip across
the ocean to America with his parents.

The elder Adams were glad to suffer any hardships they might encounter, for
they believed that the new country would provide more opportunity for their
children than could be found in Russia.

Getting off the train at Ipswich, the Adam family came overland to their
homestead, six miles southwest of Eureka, in a covered wagon drawn by a team of
oxen. They filed on a pre-emption claim, also on a tree claim, and later on a
homestead in Section 18, Township 126, Range 73, now Bauer Township.

During their years of homesteading the Adams endured summer drought and cold
winters, those of '86, '86, and '87 being the most severe. They met all
adversity courageously and successfully.

Jacob Adam farmed until 1910, when he turned the homestead over to his son,
George. The latter was married to Magdalena Schlacht, daughter of Mr. and Mrs.
Michael Schlacht.

In 1922 the elder Adams moved to Eureka, building an attractive home in the
southern part of the city.

Their homestead, comprising 600 acres, is now tenanted by their daughter and
her husband, Mr. and Mrs. Otto P. Hoff. The other daughter, Amanda, is a rural
school teacher near Eureka.

Mr. Adam has rightfully gained the respect of his fellow citizens, as is shown
by the fact that he is at present serving his second term as city alderman from
his ward, has held the office of trustee in the Zion Lutheran Church in Eureka,
and for two years served as County Commissioner in McPherson County.

HEINRICH STOEBNER

February 4, 1864, is the birth date of another of Eureka's pioneers, namely,
Heinrich Stoebner. He was born of German parentage at Kassel, Russia. There he
grew to young manhood and on November 3, 1876, was united in marriage with
Christina Spitzer, her birth date being March 8, 1856. This young couple
desired to leave Russia for the faraway land, America, and during the year of
1884 made the long journey, arriving at Menno, South Dakota. There they
remained over the winter with an uncle and aunt, and, in the spring of 1885,
came to the northern part of the state, arriving at Ipswich on a dark, rainy
day. There was no hotel or other lodging to be found, so the young couple was
obliged to take up residence in a barn for several days. Mr. Stoebner then
purchased an ox team and completed the trip to McPherson County.

        Mr. and Mrs. Heinrich Stoebner

Mr. Stoebner belonged to the type of persons, who, once having an object in
mind, works energetically and tirelessly to realize the goal of his ambitions.
The first home, built of sod, exemplifies that fact. The house was 14 feet by 30
feet and the barn 14 feet by 16 feet.

The necessity of breaking fifteen acres of prairie land to seed the first crop
came first. This land was then seeded into flax, which Mr. Stoebner says was a
good crop since the land was well broken and there was ample moisture falling
almost daily during the month of June. The harvesting of the flax was done with
a grass mower. Later the threshing was accomplished by the flail method learned
and used in Russia, and by driving the oxen over the straw, crushing the
kernels out of the heads.

In the years to come, Mr. Stoebner and his family were able to purchase more
and larger tracts of land. As the children grew older, Mr. Stoebner decided to
turn his farming interests over to his sons. His daughters, too, received their
share of property.

The daughters of Mr. and Mrs. Stoebner are: Mrs. Magdalena Wittmayer, Avon, S.
Dak.; Christina Sayler, deceased; Albina Schell, Underwood, N. Dak.; Lydia
Herman, Hillsview, S. Dak.; and Martha Heer, deceased.

Andrew Stoebner, the youngest son, lives in Eureka and is the active secretary
of the Northwest German Farmers Mutual Insurance Company. He has served on the
city council and is at present a member of the Eureka Independent School Board.
The other three boys, Henry, Adam and August are prominent farmers near Eureka.

Mr. and Mrs. Stoebner are still quite active despite the years of hardship they
have endured. They had the honor of celebrating their Golden Wedding
Anniversary on November 3, 1936.

MICHAEL PFEIFLE

Friedenstal, in Bessarabia, South Russia, was the birthplace of Michael
Pfeifle, one of the first settlers in this territory. He was born on April 1,
1864, and, at the age of twenty, came to America with his parents, his sister
Rosa, and five brothers, Andreas, Johann, Gottlieb, Konrad and Christian. A
brother and sister, Jacob and Dora, had preceded them by about six months,
while his sister Mary, and her husband, Franz Weisenburger, came here eight
years earlier.

The family arrived in Scotland, South Dakota, in November, 1884, and remained
over winter with an uncle, Peter Pfeifle, who had a farm near there. Andreas
Pfeifle, the father, was in better financial circumstances than most immigrants
of that period. He had brought with him the sum of $1,000.00, and during the
winter, purchased live stock and equipment including two horses, two oxen, four
cows, a pig and two wagons. Since there was no more land available in that
territory, the Pfeifles decided to find a home farther north.

Accordingly, in the spring of 1885, they loaded up their possessions and on May
4th started out for Ipswich. Michael, however, remained behind, as he was
employed on a farm near Scotland. In Ipswich, they loaded up lumber for
building purposes and proceeded on their way, arriving at their destination
after two weeks of travel. A location had already been chosen during the
previous March, when several members of the family had filed on land in Detmold
Township, about ten miles northeast of Eureka. The necessary farm buildings
were constructed and farming operations begun.

In the autumn of 1885, Michael left Scotland and joined his parents in their
new home. Shortly after his arrival, he was engaged by Philipp Kirschenman, a
neighboring farmer, for a period of nine months, at $20.00 per month. His
payment consisted of several cows, in addition to cash. At the expiration of
that period he returned to Scotland, where he worked at day labor for sometime,
after which, he was employed by a farmer named John Luxemburger. Among his
duties here was the breaking in of young steers, which were later sold as oxen.

In the fall of 1887, he decided to locate permanently on land which he had
previously taken up, and which was situated not far from his father's
homestead. Since the Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul Railroad company had
completed their road to Eureka during this year, he made the trip by rail.

He now constructed a dwelling, built of rock, and a sod barn. His property,
which by this time was considerable for that day, included two oxen, two
ponies, thirteen head of cattle, some pigs and chickens, a wagon, plow and
harrow. By the spring of 1888, he had broken up about thirty acres, which he
sowed in flaxseed, wheat and other grains. The crops that year were good, and
prices also were satisfactory, flax being $1.35 and wheat $1.00 per bushel.

The winter of 1888-1889 was an exceptionally severe one, Mr. Pfeifle recalls.
Unfortunately, that year his well ran dry, and his live stock had to be driven
daily to his neighbor's farm to be watered.

In October, 1888, he was married to Elizabeth Weisz, who was then employed on
the farm of Andreas Heer. The ceremony was performed by the Reverend Mr.
Zimmerman. There were eleven children born to this union, one daughter dying in
infancy. Those surviving are: Andreas, a farmer thirteen miles east of Eureka;
Gottlieb, farming thirteen miles east of Eureka; Adam, a farmer eleven miles
southwest of Leola; Emil, farming thirteen miles east of Eureka; Solomon,
living on the old homestead ten miles northeast of Eureka; Michael, employed in
Eureka; Arthur, farming on the old homestead; Julius, employed in a store in
Eureka; Edna, living on the old homestead; Elizabeth, married to John
Steinbach, a farmer sixteen miles southwest of Eureka.

Mr. Pfeifle gradually increased his land holdings to 1,360 acres, all located
in McPherson County. After his retirement in 1920, he bought a home in Eureka,
where he now resides with his wife and son, Julius.

ANDREAS KOERNER

Andreas Koerner, another early pioneer, came to Dakota Territory in 1880. He
was born in Kassel, South Russia, on June 15, 1857. At the age of 22, he was
married to Emilia Herrmann, and in 1880, he and his wife emigrated to the
United States.

Upon arriving in Yankton, South Dakota, Mr. Koerner took up a pre-emption near
Freeman, in Hutchinson County. Here he farmed for five years when he sold his
land for $1,050.00 and removed to McPherson County, shipping his property
consisting of five horses, two cows, two oxen, a wagon, plow and harrow, to
Ipswich by rail. In Odessa Township, fifteen miles northeast of Eureka, he
bought three quarters of land for which he paid the sum of $900.00. Since this
land was not yet improved, it was necessary to erect buildings, which were
constructed of sod, with log roofs.

Due to low prices for farm products, the farm income was small for the first
few years, but as he became more prosperous Mr. Koerner bought more land, at
one time owning 1,760 acres.

He is now retired, and, with his wife, resides in Eureka. There were eleven
children born to Mr. and Mrs. Koerner, namely: Karolina, married to Melchoir
Bonnett, a farmer near Leola, S. Dak.; Emilia, married to John Schanzenbach of
Selby, S. Dak; Jacob, farming near Eureka; Katharina, married to Jacob
Schumacher, a farmer near Long Lake, S. Dak.; Bertha, married to Henry B. Hoff,
engaged in the trucking business in Leola, S. Dak.; Adolph, August, John and
Henry, all farmers near Eureka; Rosina Gilbert of Minneapolis; Maggie, employed
in Minneapolis.

CHRISTIAN BOSSERT

Christian Bossert left Russia in 1884, in company with his sister, Magdalena,
his brother-in-law, Peter Schmidt, and Johann Schmidt Sr. His brother-in-law
advanced him $60.00 to pay for his passage, since his father was unable to give
him any money. They arrived in Menno, South Dakota, on December 28, 1884, where
they met Johann P. Schmidt, Friedrich Leicht and Friedrich Thurn, who had made
the trip from Russia somewhat earlier. Here they stayed over winter; Mr.
Bossert, then a young man of nineteen, working for his board and room until
spring.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christian Bossert

Moving still farther westward from his native Bessarabia, South Russia, where
he was born February 14, 1865, Christian entrained for Ipswich, South Dakota,
on April 29, 1885, which was then the end of the railroad, and from there
continued the journey by ox team. When the company, Peter Schmidt and Johann
Schmidt, Sr., and Bossert, came to Sutley the men folk left their families
there and proceeded northward to locate the land on which they had filed their
homesteads the previous winter. They were accompanied by a resident of Sutley,
who was acquainted with the land. The next day they returned, loaded up their
families and started out for their new homes, located in Campbell County about
fifteen miles northwest of where Eureka now stands.

The first night Mr. Bossert and a companion, Jacob Schmidt, then a boy of
twelve, slept under an inverted wagon-box. During the night a fierce blizzard
