came up, which by morning covered them with a foot of snow. The next day Johann
Schmidt, Sr., erected a temporary dwelling, which was nothing more than a roof
with the eaves set directly on the ground, each end closed up with boards,
except for a door. In this building, about 18 x 28, Mr. Bossert lived for two
weeks with the families of Johann Schmidt, Sr., Johann P. Schmidt, Friedrich
Leicht, Friedrich Thurn and Peter Schmidt -- a total of twenty-five individuals.

The spring of their arrival was an exceptionally dry one. There was no pasture,
and with the exception of a few wild ducks and geese, hardly any wild life to
be seen. Frequent rains, however, revived the fields, and made possible a crop
that fall. These rains were also their only source of water supply. The water
was scooped up from the ground and used for household purposes, until time was
found for digging wells.

The first spring and summer Mr. Bossert worked for relatives and neighbors,
helping them break up the land and sow their crops. He was also employed in
digging wells, at one time assisting a neighbor at this work for the meager sum
of 35 cents per day. These wells were dug by hand, and it was slow, tedious
work.

Anxious to earn enough money to pay his debt, on July 19th he set out on foot
for Ipswich. Accompanying him were Friedrich Thurn and Friedrich Leicht, who
were also seeking employment. From Ipswich they walked to Aberdeen, and finally
to Ordway, where work was found on the railroad. The wages were $1.10 per day,
board and room costing 60 cents per day. Later in the summer Mr. Bossert worked
with a threshing crew near Aberdeen, receiving $2.00 per day. When he returned
home that fall he had saved $93.00, which enabled him to pay his debt of $60.00
to Peter Schmidt.

The next year, 1886, the crops failed due to drouth, so it was again necessary
to look for work. It was in this year that the Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul
Railroad was extended to the new town of Eureka, and here he was employed in
helping to lay the bed for the new railroad. The following year, 1887, he
entered the bonds of matrimony with Magdalena Bieber, a daughter of Jacob
Bieber, who lived four miles north of Eureka. The ceremony was performed by
Rev. A. Hahn. He now proceeded to erect buildings on his land. For the roofs,
logs covered with sod were used, while the walls were constructed entirely of
sod. The logs had to be hauled from the vicinity of the Missouri River, and
cost $1.25 per load. These sod houses were warm and comfortable, and served
their purpose for a long time.

His farm equipment the first year consisted of a breaking plow, an old wagon
and a pair of oxen, bought with borrowed money. Later on he acquired a grass
mower and a binder, for which it was also necessary to borrow the money. In
this connection, Mr. Bossert wishes to express his gratitude to Charles Pfeffer
for his aid to him and many other pioneers. Mr. Pfeffer's ready willingness to
lend money and sell them machinery on easy terms helped many a needy farmer on
his feet.

These first years were very lean ones. Their food for the first year consisted
solely of bread and coffee, and on rare occasions, butter. They were happy and
contented, however, and, as time went on, gradually paid off their debts and
saved money to buy more land. Mr. Bossert at one time owned 1,120 acres, all
located in Campbell County.

Up to this time the people of his neighborhood had no place of worship, church
services being held in their homes. In the year 1887, they erected a church,
which, like all their other buildings, was constructed of sod and logs. They
organized as the Hoffnungsfeld Lutheran congregation, and embraced twenty-eight
families. Services were held every Sunday, conducted for the most part by
members of the congregation, with an occasional visit by a minister.

In the year 1897, Mr. Bossert was appointed County Commissioner for Campbell
County, to fill a vacancy caused by the resignation of one of the members. He
served in this capacity for about a year and a half. He was also a member of
the school board at various times.

Seven children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Bossert, three boys, who died in
infancy, and four girls. The girls are: Mary, born December 2q, 1889, married
to Jacob Buechler, a farmer living near Hosmer, S. Dak.; Magdalena, born
February 27, 1892, married to John Buechler, a farmer residing near Bowdle, S.
Dak.; Katharina, born December 28, 1897, married to Fred Scherle, a farmer
living on the old homestead fifteen miles northwest of Eureka; Elizabeth, born
December 31, 1898, married to Jacob Rueb, a farmer living near Mound City, S.
Dak.

Mr. Bossert retired from farming in 1916, and together with his wife removed to
Eureka, where they reside at the present time.

JOHN G. FEIOCK

John G. Feiock was born in Rohrbach, South Russia, March 1, 1863. He grew to
manhood in that territory, and, when of age was married to Margaret Fuhrman.

The young couple heard of the fine possibilities in America, through friends
who were already located in Dakota, so they decided to come here, also.

        Mr. and Mrs. John G. Feiock

Mr. Feiock came to McPherson County alone, leaving his wife with friends at
Scotland. This was in the winter of 1885. He looked the territory over and
decided to take up land. On this trip we was accompanied by Michael Klundt,
John Zumbaum, Christian Blumhardt and Philip Grosz. This party, including Mr.
Feiock, had first looked over the country near Kimball, South Dakota, but
finding nothing to their liking, ventured farther north.

The group returned to Scotland, and the following spring Feiock came back
accompanied by his family and his worldly possessions. They came as far as
Roscoe, by train, as the railroad had not been extended as far north as Eureka
at that time. At Roscoe they unloaded their precious cargo, which consisted of
a pair of oxen, a cow, and a goodly number of chickens, and started for their
final destination. All the cash possessed by the man and wife was ten cents;
but they had something finer than a desire for wealth, they were eagerly
looking forward to the time when they could set to work tilling the soil and
reaping a harvest. Such were the qualities possessed by the pioneers of Eureka
and surrounding territory. By the time they reached their destination the
chickens had produced enough eggs to enable them to purchase a milk pail and a
quart of oil. They slept under the wagon until a sod house could be built.

Mr. and Mrs. Feiock have resided in the city of Eureka for a number of years
and are in good health. They have eight children, namely: Katharine Trautman,
Eureka, Emma Hoffman, Eureka; Fred Feiock, Leola, S. Dak.; Johanna Nies,
Ashley, N. Dak.; Emilia Bertsch, Ashley, N. Dak.; Sophia Bertsch, Ashley, N.
Dak.; Edward Feiock, Ipswich, So. Dak.; and Johnny Feiock, Eureka.

JACOB HOFFMAN, SR.

Jacob Hoffman, Sr., was born in Bergdorf, South Russia, on September 8, 1852.
He was married to Katharina Goehring in 1873, the ceremony being performed by
Reverend Biernbach. In 1884 he and his wife, with about 20 other families
emigrated to the United States, arriving in Menno, South Dakota, on October 20,
of that year.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Hoffman, Sr.

Mr. Hoffman was then 32 years old, and had $900.00 in cash which was a large
sum at that time. They remained in Menno over the winter, during which time he
bought a wagon, breaking plow, harrow, a pair of oxen, two cows, five chickens
and two pigs. In the spring of 1885 they took the train to Ipswich, shipping
their live stock and equipment there by freight. From Ipswich they continued
northward by oxen, finally locating in what is now Long Lake Township, 20 miles
northeast of Eureka.

Here Mr. Hoffman built a sod house and barn and began to break up the land. The
first summer he sowed about 10 acres of flaxseed, yielding about 100 bushels.
The corn, however, which had grown very tall, with large, well filled ears, was
killed by frost on August 16th.

The winters in those years, according to Mr. Hoffman, were much more severe and
stormy than they are now. Drouth years were also experienced, notably in 1890
and 1911. Farm products were very low in price, wheat being 40 cents per
bushel, flaxseed 75 cents, butter 2 cents per pound and eggs 4c per dozen. In
spite of the low prices, however, he prospered and at one time possessed 3,360
acres of land, of which 2,720 acres were located in McPherson County and 640
acres in North Dakota.

Mr. and Mrs. Hoffman raised a family of eight children. One of their sons,
Benjamin, was elected Register of Deeds for McPherson County in 1930, serving
in that capacity for two years. He is at present employed in Leola, S. Dak. The
others are John B., Jacob, Julius, Edward, all four living on farms about 20
miles northeast of Eureka; Arthur, living on the old homestead northeast of
Eureka; Christina, married to Albert Werre, a farmer near New Leipzig, N. Dak.;
Rosina, married to Theodore Brenner, residing at Linton, N. Dak.

Mr. Hoffmann gave up farming in 1926, and removed to Eureka, where he and his
wife live at the present time.

MRS. CHRISTINA BENDER GROSS

The following is the personal story of a courageous pioneer woman who came to
Dakota Territory with her husband to find a home for themselves and their
children. The story, appearing in the first person, was related by Mrs.
Christina Bender Gross who was born in Kassel, Russia, in 1861.

"My husband and I came to America from Russia in 1884. We came directly to
Menno, South Dakota, arriving there on the 8th of October. There we remained
for the winter. Deciding to go to the northern part of the state, we purchased
live stock, chickens, and household supplies. On the 1st of April we took the
train to Ipswich, shipping our two oxen, two cows, a few chickens, 500 pounds of
flour, two hams, a small amount of sugar, and furniture, made by my husband.
The articles of furniture possessed were one bed, one trunk, and perhaps the
greatest necessity of all, a cradle.

"At Ipswich, we hitched our oxen to a newly purchased wagon, and after loading
our other property, started northward to McPherson County. We drove five miles
the first day and spent the night with an American farmer who treated us very
nicely. Our only difficulty remained in the fact that we did not speak the same
language. They spoke no German, and we knew no English. They lived in a
two-story house, and upon retiring, asked us to sleep in an upstairs room, but
we were too frightened, having never even heard of sleeping upstairs, much
less having the experience. Our kindly hosts, noting our consternation, allowed
us to sleep in the downstairs bedroom.

"The next morning we asked for the privilege of cooking our own breakfast,
having some eggs from our chickens with us. However, the lady insisted upon our
eating with them, and served us with pancakes and coffee. That was a real treat
for we had never tasted pancakes before. The following summer we ate more
pancakes than at any other time in our lives.

"The second day we went as far as Long Lake. At nightfall we stopped at the
home of Fred Bitner. Mr. Bitner was a German and Mrs. Bitner an English woman.
They were disinclined to be bothered with us, but finally gave us a pile of hay
to make a bed on the kitchen floor. We asked to make our own breakfasts in the
morning, but they would not allow us to use their stove, so we were forced to go
on our way with empty stomachs.

"The third day we went only ten miles. This took us to the home of Jacob
Meidinger. We stayed here until we found a homestead for ourselves which was
located ten miles southeast of Eureka, on the Northwest Quarter of Section 24,
Township 126, Range 72.

"At that time we had only two children, John, who was three years of age, and
Christina, who was eight months old.

"After going to our homestead, our first task was to build a house. This house
was 14 feet by 16 feet and was built in teepee style, requiring only half a
day's work. It was built with a few boards, covered with sod. There we lived
for six months. During those six months, we broke up about thirty acres of sod
and seeded flax. We had a good crop, reaping 125 bushels of flax. This we
hauled to Ipswich and received 70 cents per bushel. We thought ourselves very
fortunate, and built a new sod house. It was 42 feet by 16 feet in dimensions,
and had three rooms. This new house had windows in it, which were lacking in
the old one. We lived in this home for nine years. At that time we built a
house 26 feet by 28 feet in dimensions, having eight rooms. This house is still
on the homestead today, and is owned by my son, August.

"When we came to this country, we had $400 with which we had bought our farm
and household equipment and paid our pre-emption claim. After taking up the
homestead and timber quarter, we made good headway. We raised a large number of
cattle, planted big crops and were successful.

"We lived on our farm until 1914. My husband died in 1906, and I stayed on the
farm with my children for the next eight years. Then, on May 2, 1914, I
married Henry Gross, who lived seven miles southwest of Hillsview. There I
lived four years, and in 1917 we moved to Eureka where we now reside. I am
seventy-five years of age, and my husband is eighty-one. I have lived through
many pleasant, and many unpleasant experiences. The times have changed greatly
from pioneer days until the present, but I think these times are the most
difficult. Yet, we can thank God that we have a home in which to live and
enough to eat."

The twelve children of Mrs. Gross's by her first marriage are: Emil, Eureka;
August, Eureka; Mrs. Hertha Werre, Eureka; Albert, Eureka; John, Elgin, N.
Dak.; Mrs. Kathrina Dittus, Elgin, N. Dak.; Mrs. Rosa Windmiller, Bentley, N.
Dak.; Mrs. Lydia Mehlhaff, Hillsview, S. Dak.; Mrs. Emma Lapp, Eureka; Mrs.
Magdalena Lapp, Eureka; Julius, Eureka; Mrs. Christina Preszler (deceased).

JOHANN STROBEL

Glucksthal, South Russia, was the place where Johann Strobel first saw the
light of day on September 13, 1866. At the age of 19 he, in company with his
father and two brothers, Jacob and Fred, set out for America. His sister,
Fredericka, with her husband, Johann Ritter, had preceded them by about six
months.

The family arrived in Ipswich, South Dakota, in June, 1885. Financially they
were better off than most immigrants, possessing about $1,000 in cash. In
Ipswich they purchased the necessary equipment, consisting of two pairs oxen, a
wagon, plow, reaper, grass mower and hay rake. They continued northward to a
point four and one-half miles north of the present location of Eureka, where
they filed on their homesteads. With lumber brought from Ipswich they erected a
dwelling, while the barn was constructed of sod.

In addition to farming, Johannes, the father, who was a blacksmith, worked at
his trade with the help of his boys. A shop was set up on the farm and proved
to be a profitable venture. The blacksmith of those days did not have the
advantage of modern machinery and tools. His equipment often consisted of little
more than a forge, anvil and hammer. What was lacking in tools had to be made
up in brawn.

        Johann Strobel

In the year 1888, Johan Strobel, the son, was united in marriage to Katharina
Rempfer, daughter of George Rempfer, a resident of North Dakota. The ceremony
was performed by Reverend Henry Hetzler.

There were nine children born to this union, namely: Marie, married to Gott. G.
Gross, merchant of Morris, Minnesota; Katharina, who died in childhood; Dora,
married to Karl K. Schock of Eureka; John J., engaged in implement business in
Eureka; Elizabeth, married to Julius M. Wudel, employed in a clothing store in
Mitchell, S. Dak.; Emma, married to Reverend Theo. Strobel, pastor of the
Congregational Church of Laurel, Montana; Daniel G., a farmer three miles north
of Eureka; Albert E., manager of bakery in Eureka; Harvey H., college student.

Mr. Strobel continued farming for 21 years, eventually acquiring 1,440 acres of
land.

In 1906, the three brothers, Johan, Jacob and Fred, removed to Eureka and
entered the business world. They bought the implement business of Jacob Schall
and a grain elevator belonging to Emil Schamber. A stock of furniture was added
later, and the business was conducted as a partnership, known as Strobel
Brothers. This partnership was dissolved some years ago, Johann remaining in
the implement business, which, in connection with a grocery store, at present
is managed with the assistance of his son, John J. Strobel. The present year
thus brings Johan Strobel a total of over thirty years of continued business.

JOHN STOLLER

John Stoller, one of the leading representatives of the Russo-German families,
who settled early in McPherson County, was born near Odessa, Russia, on the
24th of June, 1862. He is a son of Dominick Stoller, who was born and reared in
that same locality. In 1872, the Stollers, in company with about forty other
families from the same district, came to America, proceeded west from New York
to Sandusky, Ohio, where they temporarily located, until such time as a
selection of a place of permanent abode could be determined upon. Dominick
Stoller and three other men of the company of sturdy and worthy immigrant party
set forth in search of a location, making an investigation tour through various
states. They finally decided to colonize in what is now the state of South
Dakota. The entire company moved forward to the new home on the frontier,
arriving in Yankton on the 22nd of April. Previous to coming to America, Mr.
Stoller had been engaged in farming and sheep growing, and he continued in the
same line of industry after coming to South Dakota.

John Stoller received his rudimentary education in the schools of his native
land and was a lad of about ten years at the time of the immigration to America.

In the spring of 1885, he came to McPherson County, taking up a quarter section
of land in the center of the county, and there remaining for two years, engaged
in farming and stock raising. He then removed to the village of Eureka, being
one of the first settlers in the town, and here for three years and seven
months thereafter he was employed on salary in a local mercantile
establishment. He then engaged in the hardware business in Eureka, forming at
the time a partnership with C. J. Hezel, manager of the Golden Rule mercantile
house in Aberdeen, and this association continued until 1893, when Frederick
Hepperle purchased Mr. Hezel's interest in the enterprise. The firm of Stoller
and Hepperle continued, its interest greatly expanding in importance through
wise management and fair dealing. In 1902, they opened a second store in
Eureka, the same being located one and one-half blocks south of their original
and still retained quarters. In this second establishment they also carried a
full line of merchandise, running the two stores in conjunction. Mr. Stoller
took active part in public life, serving as school commissioner and justice of
the peace, and in 1902 he was elected to represent his district in the state
senate.

On the 9th of January, 1883, Mr. Stoller was united in marriage to Miss
Elizabeth Kost, who was born in Odessa, Russia, and who accompanied her parents
on their immigration to America when a child, the family coming to South Dakota
in the autumn of 1874, thus being numbered among the early pioneers of the
present state.

Mr. and Mrs. Stoller had five children, namely: Amelia (deceased); Magdalena
Lortcher, Durand, Wisconsin; John F. Stoller, La Moure, N. Dak.; Rosetta
Stoller, Leola, S. Dak.; Hildegarde Meidinger, Leola, S. Dak.

The Stoller family moved to Leola, where Mr. Stoller and his son, John, engaged
in the automobile business. Both the father and mother are dead.

PETER MORLOCK

When twelve years of age, Peter Morlock accompanied his parents, Peter Morlock
and wife, Dorothea Eberhardt, to America from Neudorf, Odessa, South Russia,
where he was born August 25, 1872.

The family settled in the southern part of the state. In the spring of 1885,
the family removed to McPherson County, and took up a homestead twelve miles
east of Eureka. This farm is still in Mr. Morlock's possession, where he is
residing with a large family, at the present time. Upon arriving here, they
found shelter for three days with friends, the John Stein family. Shortly
thereafter they commenced erecting their own home, which was a sod shanty, the
dimensions being 18 x 30. They dug a hole into the ground which was used as a
bake-oven. When all was in readiness, the elder Mr. Morlock decided that, due
to low finances, he would drive to Frederick, South Dakota, to find employment.
He took his son, the subject of this story, and a daughter with him. They used
oxen to make the trip which required two days. They were unsuccessful in their
quest, so returned home. A week later they went to Aberdeen, seventy miles
distant, the trip requiring three days. When within one-half mile of Aberdeen,
they stopped and remained overnight. The following morning the elder Mr.
Morlock walked into Aberdeen, returning later with two other men also looking
for work. They found employment on the farm of Adam Bienz. The father received
a monthly salary of $35.00, the son and daughter each $18.00. At the expiration
of a month, they returned home finding everything in good condition.

        Mr. and Mrs. Peter Morlock

They then cut their flax, using oxen and a grass mower. When this was
completed, they hitched up a cow and raked it. Threshing it was simple. They
placed it on hard ground, hitched horses to a wagon, and drove over it several
times until the seed was crushed out of the hulls. When completed they lifted
the straw, and the seed was winnowed by letting the wind blow through it.

The family owned one cow when they arrived here, and soon purchased another.
These were trained to work like oxen. Hitched in front of the oxen they soon
learned what was expected of them. They also provided transportation when the
family attended church.

At one time the family was out of supplies, and due to the amount of snow, it
was impossible to reach Ipswich, where the trading was done. So the father
hitched up his oxen and started for a neighbor's place, that of Philip Martel,
who had received some flour from his friends living in the Mennonite district
near Menno. Martel's farm was only six miles away, but it took the father two
days to make the trip. This incident occurred shortly after the big blizzard of
1888.

Their farm equipment, upon arrival here, consisted of a pair of oxen, an old
wagon, a cow, and a breaking plow. This had been purchased from Gottlieb Hieb
at Menno, South Dakota, for $175.00, "on time."

At that time eggs brought only 3 cents per dozen, wheat 40 cents per bushel,
and flax 80 cents per bushel.

When Peter, Jr., was twenty-three years old, he was married to Elizabeth
Pfeifer, a daughter of Gottlieb Pfeifer, Sr. They were married on December 31,
1895, by the Reverend Henry Reinhardt. She died, and he married Anna Maria
Delzer, daughter of Andreas Delzer, of Johannesburg, South Russia.

The following children were born: John Morlock, farmer, Fredonia, N. Dak.;
Henry Morlock, farmer, Medina, N. Dak.; Mrs. Anna Bader, Wishek, N. Dak.; Mrs.
Emma Guntz, Medina; Edward Morlock, farmer, Eureka; Mrs. Ida Ehresman, Hosmer,
S. Dak.; and Edmund, Rosa, and Gottlieb Morlock, all at the parental home.

Peter Morlock's second wife died in 1920, and he married Mrs. Magdalena Heyd.

Mr. Morlock was a member of the school board in Odessa District for a number of
years.

HENRY AMAN, SR.

Among early settlers in McPherson County who relate interesting tales of
pioneer hardships, is Henry Aman, Sr. Mr. Aman, with his parents, came to
America at the age of 21, having been born in Russia, October 30, 1864. In
April, 1885, he arrived in McPherson County, filing on his pre-emption claim,
Section 22, Township 127, Range 72, which is now in Rosenthal Township.

        Henry Aman, Sr.

He was married to Freda Leicht, and after their marriage the young couple began
farming for themselves. At that time Mr. Aman owned two oxen, and in 1887
purchased his first team of horses. The nearest town was Ipswich. Both Aman and
his neighbors encountered many hardships, making the long, slow trips to
Ipswich for food and other necessities.

He remembers crops of 1886 did not bring good prices. For example, wheat was 38
cents per bushel, flax 60 cents, eggs 4 cents per dozen, and butter 2 cents per
pound. Mr. Aman says that though times were hard, people got along better than
today, in his opinion. However, through hard work and economical living, he and
his wife accumulated considerable property, including 1,280 acres of good farm
land.

Mr. Aman's wife died and some time later he married again. This wife also
passed away some years later.

Mr. Aman's three children are: Christ H. Aman, one of the enterprising farmers
of Rosenthal Township; a daughter who is the wife of Jacob G. Neuharth, also a
successful farmer of Rosenthal Township; another daughter, Mrs. John Obenauer
residing in Eureka.

Mr. Aman retired from his farm several years ago to take up residence in
Eureka. He has now passed his seventy-second birthday and enjoys good health.

CHRISTOPH MEHLHAFF

One of that great throng of German-Russian immigrants who came to McPherson
County in 1885 was Christoph Mehlhaff. He was born in Kassel, South Russia, on
the 24th of February, 1864, a son of Jacob and Elizabeth Mehlhaff, nee Koerner.
In 1874, he came to America with his parents and settled on a farm near Menno,
Dakota Territory.

Upon his arrival in McPherson County, he filed on a very select piece of land,
three miles south of the present site of the city of Eureka. A sod house was
soon constructed, and with a small amount of farming equipment, consisting of
two oxen, a team of horses, a wagon, a plow, a drag and a few cows, he began
his long and successful farming career.

During the summer and fall of 1886, he was one of the men who were employed to
help with the construction of the new grade of the Chicago, Milwaukee and St.
Paul railroad which was being constructed northward from Roscoe.

In that same year he was married to Caroline Stoebner, who died after eight
years of happy married life. Two children were born to this marriage;
Christoph, who is farming near Leola, South Dakota, and Henry, who is farming
near the village of Hillsview. A short time later, he was again married. This
time to Katherina Steinhauer, who bore him two children, both of whom are
living in Eureka. They are Theophil, who is engaged in the carpenter trade, and
Albert, who has retired from his farm. His second wife died a short time later,
and in 1900 he married Christina Mehlhaff, who is still living. Two children
were born to this union. They are: Elsa Mehlhaff, who is married to Bernhard
Rieger, an instructor in the Eureka Public Schools, and Calvin, who is living
on the old family homestead near Eureka.

Christoph Mehlhaff achieved the reputation of being one of the most progressive
and industrious farmers in the Eureka territory. His farm was modern in every
respect, and exemplary of the thrift and hard work which characterizes his
race. He was very prominent in activities of the German Reformed Church in
Eureka, of which he was a member at the time of his death.

In 1928 he moved to Eureka, where he died on the 21st of April, 1934, at the
age of seventy years, one month and twenty-seven days. His wife is still living
in a fine, modern and comfortable home in the southern sector of the city of
Eureka.

JOHAN JOB

Johan Job was born in South Russia on April 30, 1862. His parents were Martin
and Katharina Jutze Job, both of German birth. He married Carolina Boschee,
daughter of Jacob and Eva Katharina Neuharth Boschee. Their ancestors, too,
were Germans.

As a young man, Johan Job wanted to see the world. Many people in South Russia
were then speaking of America, "the land of opportunity," as a good country to
settle in. So, in 1885, Johan Job, his wife and two children joined a group of
immigrants and sailed for America.

After arriving in the United States, most of the newcomers traveled directly to
the southern part of South Dakota. Mr. Job purchased a yoke of oxen, a wagon and
a plow at Tyndall and Scotland, South Dakota, and, with several other pioneers,
loaded his live stock and machinery in a freight car and shipped it to Ipswich.
But, not wishing to subject his wife and small children to the first hardships
of building a home on the prairie, he made arrangements for her to remain at
Tyndall until he could build a sod house. He filed a pre-emption claim on
Section 1, Blaine Township 125, Range 70, in McPherson County.

Mr. Job wrote to his wife, after he had completed the building of his sod
shanty, and asked her to join him. Mrs. Job received the letter and started for
Ipswich with the two children. There was no rural mail service in those days,
so it often took weeks and months for mail to reach those out on the prairies.
Consequently, Mr. Job did not receive word of his wife's coming, and was not at
Ipswich to meet her. Naturally, Mrs. Job was greatly troubled because her
husband was not at the station. She became acquainted with another woman in the
same predicament. While the two women were trying to find a way to get to their
husbands, another old Eureka pioneer, Mr. George G. Neuharth, entered the
depot. He told them he knew of a new settlement in McPherson County, and
offered to take the ladies to this settlement, where their chances of locating
their husbands would be much improved. Gratefully, the women and children
climbed upon his wagon. They drove until nightfall, stopping somewhere in the
vicinity of Blaine Township, and camped until morning.

        Johan Job

When daylight came, Mr. Neuharth found, to his consternation, that the ponies
had broken loose during the night and escaped.  His only hope of finding them
was to trail them, so he started following the tracks in a northwestern
direction.  After walking for miles, he came to a small sod shanty beside which
a lone man sat, gazing toward the east.  Mr. Neuharth explained his trouble to
the stranger, and asked him to take a yoke of oxen and go after the stranded
women and children while he went on tracking the ponies.

This the man agreed to do. He was a newcomer by the name of Johann Job. Imagine
his joy when, upon reaching the women and children, he recognized one of the
women as his own wife!

Mr. Neuharth, after walking fifteen miles, found his ponies and returned to the
Job farm that evening for his wagon. The other lady found her home the next day.

About a month later, the Jobs were almost out of groceries. Like so many of our
other pioneers, they had very little money. Mr. Job hitched his oxen to the
wagon and started driving over the prairie, picking up buffalo bones which
could be hauled to Ipswich and sold. After working several hours, he turned
around and started home. He drove several miles in the direction in which he
thought his farm lay. Suddenly he realized that he had lost his way. He kept on
driving, however, until he knew that he was hopelessly lost. He stopped the
oxen and unhitched them. Tears came to his eyes as he imagined the terrible
things that might happen to his family if he should fail to find them. Then the
thought came to him that perhaps the oxen could find the way, so he again
hitched his faithful team to the wagon and let them travel. Mile after mile the
creatures plodded on, finally bringing Mr. Job to his home. Mrs. Job tearfully
but happily greeted her husband when he returned, for she had become very
anxious for his safety.

The township was sparsely settled at this time, but in 1886 and 1887 more
settlers arrived. A few years later Mr. Job filed on more land, this time on
Section 6, Township 125, Range 70. Altogether he acquired 640 acres of land.
Mr. and Mrs. Job retired in 1927, turning the management of the farm over to
their son, Samuel. They moved to Eureka, where they still live.

They have two daughters, Maria and Paulina; and two sons, Samuel, who is on the
farm, and Fred J., who works as road patrolman for the county.

JOHN DOERING

Worthy of mention as a pioneer of the Eureka territory, is the name of John
Doering. A son of Gottlieb Doering and his wife, Christina, nee Kohn, he was
born in Eigenheim, Bessarabia, South Russia, now a part of Roumania, on the 5th
of February, 1867. In 1879, at the age of twelve, he accompanied his parents to
America, and settled with them on a homestead a few miles south of Tripp, in
what is now South Dakota. In 1885, he proceeded northward, being tempted by the
large tracts of fertile land which were being opened for homestead occupation
by the United States government, and settled on a farm close to the town of
Ipswich. He farmed this land until 1890, and then after being married to
Margaretha Hirsch of Tripp, he located in the then comparatively new town of
Eureka.

In Eureka, he played an important part in the industrial and political
development of the town. His first business venture was as a partner with his
brothers, in operating a general merchandise store, on the present site of the
Humann Brothers store. He continued in this line until 1905, when he sold out
and entered the political world. During this time, and up to the time of his
removal to Tripp, South Dakota, in 1920, he held the position of Deputy Sheriff
of McPherson County, Commissioner representing the fifth district of the
county, and serving on the city council as alderman from his ward for a number
of years. While Commissioner, he was recognized as one of the ablest
representatives that this district ever had on the board.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Doering

From 1920 to 1932, he lived with his wife at Tripp, South Dakota. He then moved
to Dell Rapids, South Dakota, where for four years he lived with his son, Edwin.
He died on the 30th of December, 1935, at Dell Rapids, after a serious illness
of more than one year.

Surviving him are his wife and five children. The widow is still living at Dell
Rapids. The names of the children are as follows: Mrs. Emma Schaeffer, Tripp,
S. Dak.; Edwin D. Doering, Dell Rapids, S. Dak.; Mrs. Frieda Schleuder, New
Ulm, Minn.; Herbert Doering, New Ulm, Minn.; Mrs. Annie Dupper, Chicago, Ill.

FRIEDRICH RUEB

Friedrich Rueb, born in Kassel, Russia, in 1855, came to America at the age of
29, residing at Tyndall, Dakota Territory, for one year. In 1885, he came
northward to Ipswich. There he unloaded his belongings from a railroad car and
came by wagon to Arena Township in McPherson County.

The first Rueb home was a sod house 14 feet by 20 feet, with a 10 foot by 14
foot barn attached to the house. After the house was built, the Ruebs broke up
the land for the seeding of crops.

Mr. Rueb relates that when they first settled on their homestead, their live
stock consisted of two oxen and one cow. When the oxen were working, the cow
became lonely and started back toward Ipswich. It took him more than a day to
catch and bring her back.

Food was not too plentiful at that time, and the settlers did not make any more
trips to Ipswich than were necessary. Mr. Rueb said there were three families
living in their house one winter. The only meat they had during the entire
winter was one hog divided between the families.

When farm work did not keep them busy, Rueb went out on the prairie with their
oxen and wagon to pick up buffalo bones. The bones were later hauled to Ipswich
and sold to Charles Pfeffer for $12.00 per ton.

Speaking of crop conditions during early days, he recalls that drouth hurt the
crops in 1886. The crops of 1887 were a trifle better, and those of 1888 good.
In the latter year he threshed 700 bushels of wheat which he sold at $1.15 per
bushel, and 300 bushels of flax which brought $1.25 per bushel.

        Friedrich Rueb

After their children were grown, Mr. Rueb divided the land among them and moved
to Eureka where he now lives. There were twelve children in the family, seven
of whom are living. They are: Jacob F., Christoph, Philip, and Friedrich
Junior, who are farming in Arena Township; Christina Bechtold, Montana; Emma
Hoff, Eureka; Martha Mettler, Eureka. The latter two are the wives of Henry
Hoff and Reinhold Mettler, respectively.

CHRISTIAN DIEDE

The grandparents of Christian Diede originally migrated to South Russia from
the Province of Baden, Germany. His parents, in order to evade compulsory
military training, and to secure free land, emigrated to America in the year
1885. There were four children in the family: Philibina, Christian, Elizabeth
and Eva.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christian Diede

After stopping at Menno for a short time, the family went to Ipswich by rail.
From there the journey was continued by ox team. His father had sufficient
money to buy a pair of oxen, two cows and a wagon. They located in Township
128, Range 73, and filed on a pre-emption, homestead and timber claim. For a
temporary abode, a rough shanty was erected, and later a sod house was built.

During the early years, mostly flax was grown. At harvest time the crop was cut
and raked together with a grass mower and hay rake, and was slow work. It
required the services of five men, two manipulating the mower and rake, two
leading the oxen, and a fifth piling up the flax.

Mr. Diede says that they experienced occasional drouths in those years but none
were as severe as the present one. Crop damage due to rust and grasshoppers was
unknown. The winters were, as a rule, very severe, and the snowdrifts sometimes
completely covered the houses, shutting out all the daylight.

He recalls his first visit to Eureka, which, at that time, consisted of only a
few scattered houses. During the great wheat rush he worked in a flat-house for
Frank Schumacher.

In the year 1893, Christian Diede was united in marriage to Katharina Perman.
The ceremony was performed at the home of the bride's parents, by the Reverend
Mr. Braun, an Evangelical minister. There were four children born to this
couple, namely: Emma Kaseman, residing in McIntosh County, N. Dak.; Emil,
living on the old homestead; Katharina Kramer, of Linton, N. Dak.; Helena
Kautz, of Ashley, N. Dak.

Mr. Diede retired about seventeen years ago and now lives in Greenway, South
Dakota.

JOHN PRESZLER, SR.

Among the well-known pioneers of Eureka is John Preszler, Sr. He was born in
Kassel, South Russia, in 1865.

His father, George Adam Preszler, also a native of Kassel, removed from that
country in 1884, the chief reason being to evade military service for three
sons. His family consisted of three sons and a daughter, of which John ranked
third in age. The latter did not accompany his father to America, but arrived
here a year later. He spent several months in the southern part of the state
before joining his father in McPherson County. He lived with his parents until
he was of age. He then took up land in the Opp settlement, located eighteen
miles northeast of Eureka.

Several years later he sold his homestead and bought a farm eight miles east
and a half mile south of town. He resided there until 1921, when he retired and
removed to Eureka where he has since lived.

He married Magdalena Stein, a daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John Stein, natives of
Bergdorf, South Russia, who had arrived in the county in 1884. Ten children
were born to them, namely: Magdalena Wanner, Eureka, S. Dak.; Lydia
Scherbenski, Artas, S. Dak.; John and Daniel, Hillsview, S. Dak.; Jacob and
Adam J., Eureka; Martha Maier, Long Lake, S. Dak.; Paulina Kom and Rose
Albrecht, Aberdeen, S. Dak.; Julius W., Leola, S. Dak.

The mother died in March, 1927. Funeral services for her were held in the
Reformed church with the Reverend Henry Treick officiating. She was buried in
the city cemetery.

In November, 1936, Mr. Preszler moved into the country where he resides with
his son, Daniel, during the winter months.

MICHAEL KEIM

Michael Keim belongs to the group of pioneers who came to this section in 1885.
Both he and his wife, Rosina, born Schutz, were natives of Glucksthal, South
Russia, where Michael was born in 1854.

The young couple decided early in their married life to seek their fortune in
the new world and came from their Russian home, together with a small son, to
McPherson County. Upon arrival they had a cash capital of $300.00 and plenty of
determination to acquire a home and competency.

Their first purchase was a pair of oxen and a cow. With these and the usual
bare necessities for tilling the soil they went to work, immediately breaking
sod for a crop. Their story varies little from that of hundreds of others who
began with limited resources and battled through to success.

The original homestead was located fifteen miles east of Eureka and here the
Keims resided for thirteen years. Upon selling this land they bought another
farm a mile and a half north of Eureka where they lived until 1910 when they
retired and moved to Eureka. Mr. Keim died in 1921 and was buried in the City
cemetery.

The Keims endured many hardships, blizzards, Indian scares and other incidents
that were grave problems to the new settlers. Mr. Keim was not inclined to take
the danger of Indians to heart as much as many others. When his neighbors fled
to Eureka for safety he remained behind and took care of the property and
animals. The Indians, of course, never came.

After his retirement, Mr. Keim resided in Eureka and several years after the
death of his first wife he married Mrs. Maria Kopp, who passed away last year,
1936, surviving Mr. Keim twelve years, since he died in 1924.

Following is a list of names and addresses of children born to Mr. Keim and his
first wife: Fred M. Keim, Forrest Grove, Oregon; Chris B. Keim, Eureka, S.
Dak.; Rose Destner, Ashley, N. Dak.; Gottlieb M. Keim, Wishek, N. Dak.; Charles
Keim, Ashley, N. Dak.; Emil Keim, Bowdle, S. Dak.

Mr. Keim's two sons Chris and Emil, held public offices at various times in
McPherson County.

Chris Keim has been Deputy Sheriff and Sheriff of McPherson County for eleven
years. He is the present Sheriff.

Emil Keim officiated as Justice of the Peace in 1934.

JACOB BIEBER, JR.

Although only seventeen years of age when he came to the Eureka community,
Jacob Bieber, Jr., experienced much of the same hardship as his elders. He was
born November 9, 1867, in Glucksthal, South Russia. His parents were Jacob John
Bieber, who was likewise born in Glucksthal, December 24, 1835, and the mother,
Christina Ritter, in 1842. The parents died in Eureka, the father in 1916, and
the mother December 23, 1924. They are both buried in the Lutheran cemetery.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Bieber, Jr.

Jacob, Jr., accompanied his parents to McPherson County May 1, 1885. They came
by train to Ipswich, and then drove by oxen to their final destination, which
was six miles northeast of Eureka. The trip required three days. The father
took up a homestead, and after the necessary requirements had been completed,
young Bieber decided to seek his fortune. He was the oldest son of the family
which included the following: Mrs. Magdalene Bossart, Eureka, S. Dak.; Jacob
Bieber, Jr., Eureka, S. Dak.; John Bieber (deceased), Bowdle, S. Dak.; Rosina
Hausauer, Eureka, S. Dak.; Carolina Strobel, Eureka, S. Dak.; Henry Bieber,
Eureka, S. Dak.; Fred Bieber, Java, S. Dak.; Christian Bieber, McIntosh, S.
Dak.; Barbara and Maria Strobel, both dead.

In 1885, Jacob, Jr., went on foot to Ipswich to look for employment. He found a
job in the harvest fields at a salary of $1.00 a day and his meals. When
harvest was completed they returned home, a distance of forty-five miles, to
spend the winter. In 1886, he accepted another job that paid him $100.00 and
board for four months. The same year, in January, he and an acquaintance, Peter
Weber, went to Leola and Ipswich for supplies. It was very cold, the
thermometer registering between 40 and 46 degrees below zero. On the return
trip, Weber froze his feet, so Mr. Bieber left him at the home of Christ
Schumacher, three and one-half miles from home. Due to the amount of snow it
took him one whole day to reach home; and when he finally got there the house
was buried in snow.

In 1887, he accompanied his brother and several other young men to Groton,
located twenty-two miles east of Aberdeen, to look for employment. He found a
job and remained there for one and a half months.

After he returned home he decided to establish his own home. He was married to
Miss Julianna Krause, November 17, 1892, the Reverend Zimmerman officiating. One
son, Jacob J. Bieber, residing in Eureka, was born to them. Mrs. Bieber's
parents were Samuel Krause, born in Tarutina, South Russia, February 28, 1839,
and his wife, Beatta Hiller, also born in South Russia, February 12, 1841. The
Krause family came to Campbell County in 1885. Later they moved to Eureka where
the parents died, the father in 1917, and the mother in 1915. They are buried
in the Lutheran cemetery.

At the time the young Biebers started housekeeping the home was made of sod. An
oven, built by Mrs. Bieber, consisting of stones and clay and heated with
straw, provided sufficient heat for cooking and baking purposes.

In 1888, they experienced the great blizzard. It lasted for three days, the
first day being the most severe. It began at ten o'clock in the morning. It was
so dark that they were compelled to burn a light until the storm ceased. Mr.
and Mrs. Bieber, Jr., are residents of this city, where they removed to
directly from the farm, and have resided here since then.

MRS. JOHANNA HAUSAUER

Mrs. Johanna (Klein) Hausauer, was the daughter of Ludwig Klein and Katharina
Fauth Klein, and was born January 12, 1847, in Glucksthal, South Russia.

She lived with her parents until her marriage which occurred November 5, 1865.
Her husband, Karl Hausauer, was also a native of southern Russia. They became
the parents of eleven children.

After changing locations several times in the old country, they finally decided
to emigrate to a country full of new hopes and promises. They had heard much
about the possibilities in the "new land," in America.

In January of 1885, they commenced their journey. Arriving in Tyndall, Dakota
Territory, they decided to remain there until spring. Then came the trip to
McPherson County. Mr. Hausauer filed on a homestead five miles north of Eureka.
This property is still in the possession of the Hausauer family.

The hardships of the late eighties were experienced by this sturdy couple, who,
by diligent labor and determination overcame all obstacles, won the battle and
emerged with an enviable record of achievement. Twenty-four years of fortune
and misfortune awaited the Hausauers at every turn and at the end of that
period they laid down their burdens and moved to town.

A year later, September 11, 1910, Mr. Hausauer died at his home in Eureka. His
widow resided alone in her home, the house now occupied by her son Karl. She was
very interested in Mother Nature, and worked continually spring and summer in
her garden, which was known as one of the finest in town.

The outstanding feature of Mrs. Hausauer's life was her service to others. For
many years she worked as a midwife. Hundreds of children in this community came
into this world with Mrs. Hausauer's assistance. She was a rather reserved
woman, but had a most pleasing personality. She had a way of inspiring
confidence, which many times changed the atmosphere of the homes to which she
went on duties.

She was the mother of six children, namely: Karl Hausauer, Jr., Johanna Layer,
Katharina George, Rosina Bieber, Gustav Hausauer, and Lydia Bauer.

A number of years later, while Mrs. Hausauer was enroute to her home from a
shopping trip she suffered a stroke. She was ill for ten days and then died.

Services were held in the Lutheran church, Director George Sandrock
officiating. Interment took place in the Lutheran Cemetery.

FRIEDERICK KREIN

Another of Eureka's pioneer couples was Friederick Krein and his wife. Mr.
Krein was born December 12, 1855, in Neudorf, South Russia. His wife was also a
native of Russia, having resided in a village known as Wonitch. In the spring
of 1880, they were married at the home of the bride's parents.

In 1885, they left Russia and arrived in McPherson County May 18th.

They came by train as far as Ipswich, and the remainder of the trip was made
with oxen. Enroute to Eureka they spent the night in the open several miles
from town.

Their finances were very low, but they possessed an enviable courage to face
difficulty and hardship.

They commenced at once to break up the hard sod and plowed and sowed, happy in
the thought that it was their own, and eager to see the results.

The first year they sowed two bushels of flax and got 80 bushels in return.
This encouraged them immensely. Potatoes were planted with most gratifying
results.

Their farm stock consisted of one cow, one pair oxen, one calf, two small pigs
and a small number of chickens. Three years later they bought a team of horses.

The Krein homestead was located eight miles east and three miles south of
Eureka. The house and barn were under one roof, with a partition between.

In the late eighties there was more snow than we have now, so it was necessary
to do a great deal of shoveling. Mr. Krein happened to be on a mission of this
kind and shoveled snow for three weeks, receiving only $3.00, and besides
having to go all the way back to Ipswich to receive his salary. It was at this
time that the big blizzard of '88 came in full force. The Krein home had been
built along the side of a small hill in order to be protected from the wind, so
this naturally was an ideal place for the snow to settle down. The following
day Mrs. Krein, who was alone with her little family during the storm, tried to
look or get out but it was impossible to open the door. There was no other
alternative but to go up through the chimney, and by placing kettles on top of
the stove she managed to get out. A desolate sight greeted her. Everything was
buried in snow. For miles all she could see was chimneys sticking through the
snow.

        Mr. and Mrs. Friederick Krein

Fourteen children were born to them, six of them dying in infancy and two when
grown up. They were as follows: Maria Guthmiller, Leola, S. Dak.; Christina
Mehlhaff, Great Falls, Mont.; Edward and Theo., deceased; Paulina Martell and
Helen Preszler, Hosmer, S. Dak.; Emma Stoebner and Julius Krein, Eureka.

Nine years ago Mr. and Mrs. Krein removed to a cozy little home in the north
part of Eureka, where they expected to enjoy their declining years, but death
intervened and claimed Mr. Krein on February 2, 1936. He was buried in the
Calvin's Reformed Church cemetery.

PETER WITTMAYER

Peter Wittmayer and his wife Eva, born Heiser, immigrated from their native
Gluecksthal, South Russia, arriving in Yankton, Dakota Territory, in 1873. He
filed on a homestead near Tyndall soon after and resided there for twelve
years, dividing his time between the farm and work as a stone mason. There was
considerable work of that nature at that time since concrete was unknown. Stone
masonry was constructed with a mortar made from sand and lime.

It was on this farm that John B. Wittmayer, who was to play a prominent part in
the business and political life in the Eureka section, was born on August 20,
1878. With a growing family, the elder Wittmayer decided to join the tide of
immigration to the region opened for homestead, pre-emption and tree claim
entry, farther north in the Territory. As a result, the family moved to
McPherson County in 1885, where Peter filed on a pre-emption and tree claim on
Section 13, Township 127, Range 71, the present Odessa Township. A man named
Kappel filed on a homestead for him for a cash consideration, as was often done
in those days.

Here six sons and three daughters of the Wittmayers grew to manhood and
womanhood. They were rather better equipped than the majority of the new
settlers, but they faced and overcame the many hardships which were the common
lot of the pioneer settlers. The long haul to market of the first few years,
the low prices for farm products, drouth, the severe winters and the loneliness
of the bare prairies could be only partially offset by the better financial
status of the Wittmayers as compared with many of their neighbors.

        J. B. Wittmayer

Peter Wittmayer's experience in business in America as well as his good
judgment in community affairs brought his election to the county commission in
1889, a position held for a number of years with much credit to himself. The
year previous to his election to the commission he opened a harness shop in
Eureka, operating the business until 1900, when he returned to the farm.

Four children of the Wittmayers have died. They are: Katy, who passed away
December 24, 1903; Eva, November 9, 1922; Jacob, January 14, 1916; and Henry,
November 6, 1936. Surviving are John B., Peter J., Christ, Emil, Barbara, and
Fred. The father passed away on October 20, 1911, and the mother on May 22,
1917. The former is buried in the Reformed Church cemetery northeast of Eureka,
while the latter rests in the Eureka City cemetery.

John B. Wittmayer bought the old homestead in 1909, and now operates the 1,340
acres he owns, and in addition some leased land. His initial experience in
farming was, however, on a homestead seven miles east of Linton, North Dakota,
in 1900. He resided there until, as already noted, he returned to McPherson
County in 1909. He has long taken active part in public life and has held a
number of offices of public trust. He was first named auditor of McPherson
County in 1912, being re-elected for a second term. Later he served three terms
in the state legislature, namely, 1918, 1920, and 1922. At the 1937 session of
the legislature he served as sergeant-at-arms of the Senate.

Mr. Wittmayer was married in 1900 to Eva Nies. The union was blessed with eight
children, all of whom survive. They are: Albert, Theodore, Esther, Julius,
Hilda, Mildred, Richard, and Benjamin.

FREDERICK MILLER

Frederick Miller's introduction into active life came in the Russo-Turkish war.
He served five years with armies of the Russian czar, during that troubled
time. Five years after the war he was married to Barbara Reub, and settled down
in their native village, Kassel, in South Russia, where their German
forefathers had come many years before.

But they heard much about America, the great land to the west, and decided five
years after their marriage to seek their fortune in the new country. They were
uncertain where to find a home after they got here, but found their countrymen
around Menno, Dakota Territory, most friendly and helpful. They had little
money, but managed somehow to acquire a team of oxen and other bare
necessities, generally brought on trips to the north by homesteaders. Friends
near Menno also presented them with a small pig and five chickens, gifts that
made them feel very happy.

They loaded their property in a freight car and traveled by rail to Ipswich,
which was then the nearest railroad station to McPherson County. Mrs. Miller,
who is still alive, remembers it was a beautiful day in spring when they
arrived in McPherson County, May 4, 1885, to be exact. They found a homestead
about seven miles northwest of Eureka, unloaded their wagon and began the long,
hard battle with the wilderness.

They felt it was wilderness, for they saw no wild birds, not even jackrabbits,
only the wide trackless prairie and the vaulting blue sky. It was a fine day,
but they felt they must provide some kind of shelter, for it was uncertain what
the next day would bring. They put up the wagon box to serve as one wall and
built another wall of sod, covering the top with an old bed frame -- and their
first home in America was complete. It wasn't much of a home, but it sheltered
them and their three children, the oldest of whom was five years of age and the
youngest a year and a half. Their sleeping accommodations were limited to one
small cot. There was room only for the mother and children. Father probably
found his rest on a few sacks of flour, which must share a part of the shelter,
for flour was precious. In fact, flour, a few potatoes and about a half gallon
of drippings was their entire store of food. Their water supply came from the
lake, which they recall was about the color of tea.

Their early provision of shelter was fortunate. On the day of their arrival on
the claim the weather was lovely, but the next night it snowed and the snow
found its way even to the cot. It was cold too. The kettle in which they had
left a few potatoes and flour was frozen and there was no fuel. A few buffalo
chips and dry rushes from the lake had served as fuel -- and now they were wet
with snow. It was a distressing situation, but somehow the hardy couple and
their children battled through.

In order to build a more substantial home for the winter, Mr. Miller felt he
must have a bit of lumber. It was necessary to drive all the way to Ipswich, 65
miles, in order to get it. The trip required a week and the lumber cost $14.00.
The young farmer, however, had only $10.00 in cash. He managed to borrow the
balance and brought the lumber, which served for the roof of the sod house and
for several necessary articles of furniture, which he fashioned with the simple
tools at hand.

His first crop was flax. Seeding it was a problem. He had a breaking plow and
could break the sod, but it was necessary to wait for a rain to wash the seed
into the soil, since drills were unknown. The rain came fortunately enough, the
early part of June and the seed grew.

Grass sprang up and the oxen and cow prospered. The little pig was also
prosperous. In fact he grew sleek and fat and still apparently ate but little.
It was a puzzle to them for some time. Finally they found out he had become
chummy with the cow -- in fact so chummy that he got his meals from her at all
hours. One may suspect the pig's meals were supervised after that discovery.

When the sod house was completed and the furniture in place, Miller, years
afterward said he felt he was the luckiest person in the world, living in his
own home, even if that home was not so elegant and the land on which it stood
wasn't paid for, as yet. There were, of course, drawbacks. Ipswich was far away
and to make the trip there with the ox team was a slow proceeding. Miller was a
good walker and several times made the trip on foot, rather than at the slow
pace the oxen traveled.

But best of all the flax grew nicely. It rained almost every night during the
entire month of June, it seemed. This was fine for the flax crop, but somewhat
inconvenient for the Millers in their makeshift shelter, open at both ends.
They would put the wagon box in front of the opening on the windward side to
keep the rain from driving in on the strong wind which accompanied the rain.
But sometimes there was more than one shower a night and when the wind changed
it was necessary for the head of the family to go out and shift the wagon box
to the other side.

But the flax was soon ripe. Then arose the question of how to harvest it. Well,
a machine was out of question. So the scythe, which had earlier cut hay, was
brought into play to cut the precious grain. A hand rake gathered it up, the
feet of the oxen crushed it out of the pods and it was winnowed in a sieve,
which the resourceful young farmer made from small twigs of willows and other
trees.

The sieve did a fine job and many people admired the seed after it was cleaned
by the slow, laborious process.

That fall they saw the first wild life, when ducks and other waterfowl
appeared. Various sources of income made life a bit easier. They bought a
stove, a hay fork and several other articles they needed. Flax and a load of
buffalo bones brought them cash. But misfortune of overwhelming proportions
lurked around the corner.

In the fall when the grass was less palatable for the oxen they strayed to the
spot where the flax had been threshed and winnowed. The chaff had somehow
become poisoned and both the oxen died as a result of eating it. It was a most
distressing situation. The oxen were dead, and they had no money to replace
them. But somehow during the winter they were able to finance the purchase of
another pair. While in search of oxen Mr. Miller and one of his neighbors had a
most thrilling adventure. They tramped through the deep snow, and as night
fell, found themselves apparently far away from any human habitation. They sat
down on a stone to rest and think matters over. To spend the night out in the
snow seemed dismal and terrifying. Just then, out of the snow at some distance
a man emerged, much as a gopher creeps from his hole.

Long after, Mr. Miller said this incident was one of the most pleasant he could
remember from the early days. They found a place to sleep, and a warm supper in
the cabin of the settler, even if it was buried in the deep snow.

Many other interesting incidents in the life of the Millers might be related if
space, permitted. They battled on bravely, undaunted by drouth, Indian scares
and manifold other hardships which were the common lot of pioneers.

As time went on they prospered and in 1915, Mr. Miller bought his first car --
a great contrast to the ox team, which required a week's time to make a trip to
Ipswich. Frederick Miller, in later years, reviewing his experiences, told his
friends that in spite of hardships he endured, there was no complaint to make.
He had always had enough to eat, and a home since coming to this great new land.

He died on the old homestead at the age of 79. Mrs. Miller is now 77 and still
resides on the farm she played so important a part in developing.

The following children were born to the Miller family: Fred A. Miller, Heaton,
N. Dak.; John B. Miller, Streeter, N. Dak.; George Miller, Eureka; David
Miller, Eureka; Daniel Miller, Eureka; Christina Brenneise, Eureka; Maria
Kiesz, Eureka; Magdalena Keim, Acme, Alberta, Canada; Katharina Unterscher,
Wadena, Minnesota; Elizabeth Dais
(deceased).

FRED SCHUMACHER

South Russia, the wild prairie of McPherson County in Dakota Territory, and the
far-flung distances of Texas, figure in the life story of Fred Schumacher.  He
was a native of Glucksthal, South Russia, born there on November 6, 1858. He
was married to Barbara Goehring in 1882, and two years later with his wife and
one child emigrated to America.

        Mr. and Mrs. Fred Schumacher

They arrived in Menno, South Dakota, in October, 1884, where they stayed at the
home of Johann Neuharth until the following spring. Mr. Schumacher's financial
resources consisted of $350.00, with which he purchased two oxen, a cow, wagon
and plow. Shipping his live stock and equipment by freight the family took the
train to Ipswich, from where they continued their journey by ox team. After
several days of travel they reached the farm of Christian Burrer, where they
remained about a day. During the previous winter Mr. Schumacher had filed on a
pre-emption and timber claim, located eighteen miles northeast of Eureka, on
which he erected a temporary dwelling, and proceeded to break up the sod. All
work was done with oxen until the year 1887, when he purchased two horses for
which he paid the sum of $475.00.

He continued farming here until 1911, when he bought land in Texas and located
there. He returned to South Dakota eleven years later and took up his residence
in Eureka, where he still resides.

There were eleven children born to Mr. and Mrs. Schumacher, six of whom are
still living. They are: Barbara, married to John Wolff, of Eureka; Lydia,
married to Jacob Gohl, a farmer in the state of Washington; Emma, married to
Gustav Schlitzkus, residing in Texas; Bertha, married to Fred Job of Eureka;
Jacob, residing in Eureka; Edward, engaged in business in Eureka.

GOTTLIEB JOACHIM

Gottlieb Joachim, now 82 years of age, tells an interesting story of his life
as an immigrant to the United States from the German colony in Russia. He was a
native of the Odessa section, starting for America in company with two brothers
from that city. They traveled by way of Bremen, Germany, where they boarded a
steamer in 1876, arriving in Baltimore after an 18-day sea voyage, fraught with
hardships of various kinds.

Arriving in Yankton, Dakota Territory, they hoped for better things, but it
was, on the contrary, the beginning of their real hardships, Mr. Joachim
recalls. They were unable to speak the English language and without funds.
Finally, he secured a job working for board and room. In the spring of 1877, he
found employment on a flat boat on the Missouri River, receiving from $30.00 to
$70.00 per month in wages.

This job smacked a bit of adventure, as the craft proceeded up the great river
and young Joachim saw the historic battlefield where Custer and a detachment of
the seventh cavalry had been massacred by the Sioux only two years or so
before. There was still a regiment of soldiers on guard there, Mr. Joachim
writes to the editors of the Jubilee book. It was to bring the garrison
supplies that he made the journey.

The voyage of that flat boat was not without its tragedies. From April 1st to
October 1st, no less than eighteen young men (presumably attached to the boats
on the Missouri) lost their lives either by drowning, or at the hands of
hostile Indians. Everywhere the boat landed it was met by unfriendly Indians,
so life was full of thrills, though perhaps not of the most pleasant kind.

Four years after coming to America, Mr. Joachim made a trip back to Russia for
a visit. He was uncomfortable there, however, as he felt he was being watched
by Russian officials. So he married and went back to America with his wife,
settling at Scotland. Here he worked at the carpenter trade until 1885.

This occupation seemed to offer him no assurance of the competence which he was
ambitious to acquire, so he joined the immigrants to McPherson County in 1886.
The first years on the homestead and tree claim brought more hardships than any
he had known before. Without funds to buy proper equipment or lumber for
buildings, it was a distressing situation. The first Joachim home in McPherson
county was merely a hole in the ground over which the wagon box was inverted.
This served as shelter until a sod shanty could be built. To add to their
troubles the Joachims got no crop at all, and he went to Jamestown, where he
earned enough money to live through the winter.

After that, crops were better, but on one occasion a short crop forced him to
borrow $20.00 for a short period. He was asked to mortgage six head of cattle
and four horses as security. He used the money from March 18 until November 1,
and paid $40.00 principal and interest. One may feel, at this time, that he was
justified in telling his creditor that he would never again borrow money. Mr.
Joachim pays high tribute to the wives of the pioneers. He tells how most of
them not only did the house work, cooking meals, rearing children and sewing
all the clothing required by all members of the family, but somehow found it
possible to assist the husband with the work in the fields as well. Not that
the man of the family did not work. "Many times," says Mr. Joachim, "I dug rocks
by moonlight so that I might have clean land to break the next morning." Often,
also it fell upon the head of the family to make long journeys on foot to bring
small quantities of food supplies. A walk of seventy miles wasn't uncommon. Ten
or twenty miles was a mere pleasure jaunt.

Mr. Joachim has resided in Venturia, North Dakota, for the last twenty years,
making his home with his children. Mrs. Joachim died since they took up their
residence there and is buried near that city.

FRED W. SCHAMBER

Fred W. Schamber played a prominent part in the business life of the new town,
Eureka, being a member of the firm, Martin Schamber & Sons, early hardware and
farm implement dealers. He was born in Kriem, South Russia, December 13, 1860,
the son of Martin Schamber and his wife, Maria Mueller Schamber. The Schamber
family immigrated from Alsace Lorraine to Russia as a part of the great tide
that swept in from German provinces at the beginning of the 19th century.

Martin Schamber, the father, had a good education and had served as a
schoolmaster, teaching both the German and Russian languages in his native
land. He was, also, a linguist of no mean gifts, speaking in addition to German
and Russian, French and Turkish, a circumstance which put him in position to
master the English language rapidly. As a result, he was naturally looked up to
by those less fortunate in educational equipment and conceivably inspired his
family in acquiring more than average educational background.

Arriving with his parents and the rest of the family at Yankton in 1874, on
August 13 to be exact, Phillip Schamber accompanied the group to the farm they
acquired about twenty-five miles north of Yankton. Here, he labored at farm
work for several years. The Schambers remember those years as trying ones, with
crop failure, grasshoppers and other trials. However, in 1885, they had
accumulated a bit of reserve capital and started a mercantile business in
Scotland. Later a farm implement business was established in Tripp, which
continued until the family removed to Eureka in 1885.

In Eureka he was, at least nominally, the manager of the firm Martin Schamber &
Sons, other members of the firm being his father and brothers, Julius, Emil and
Philip. The firm prospered, having a fine reputation for honesty and fair
dealing.

Fred Schamber, in addition to his business activity took active interest in
public affairs and was honored by the community with several offices of high
public trust. He was elected to the state legislature in 1892 from McPherson
County, and in 1898 he was again honored, being named a member of the state
senate from this county. He served with distinction in both bodies of the
legislature, earning the respect of his contemporaries.

In 1899, he was named mayor of Eureka, giving the city an efficient
administration during his two-year term of office. A lasting and appreciated
memory of this period are the trees which the mayor had hauled from the Missouri
River with his own team and wagon. Jack Parrott, who was city marshal,
performed the work and the trees were planted in the City Park, w here they
have added much to the beauty of the city and the enjoyment of the citizens and
visitors.

Mr. Schamber was married to Matilda Hetzler, a native of Missouri and the
daughter of the Reverend and Mrs. Henry Hetzler of Scotland, Dakota Territory,
December 13, 1885. Two children were born to the Schambers; Edwin, now residing
in Olivia, Minnesota, and Arthur, of Sioux Falls, South Dakota.

GOTTLIEB QUASCHNICK

Gottlieb Quaschnick was born in Josephdorf, South Russia, on November 13, 1864.
His father, Daniel, while still a boy, migrated there from Wurtenburg, Germany,
and made it his home until 1883. That year the family, composed of parents,
five children and an uncle, John Quaschnick, emigrated to America, arriving in
Scotland, South Dakota, in July 1883.

Gottlieb Quaschnick worked on a farm near there for eighteen months and then
came to Campbell County, where he filed on a pre-emption, homestead and timber
claim. At that time there were no towns in Campbell County, which made it
necessary to drive to Bowdle for equipment and supplies. He purchased a pair of
oxen, plow and wagon on credit terms, and after building a sod house started
breaking up the sod.

Due to lack of equipment in those days, some of the work had to be done in a
more or less crude manner. An oven was made by digging a hole in the side of
the creek bed, from which a small opening, serving as a chimney, was made to
the surface. Stones were then heated in the large hole, after which the ashes
were taken out and the dough placed on the hot stones. The results, it is
claimed, were quite satisfactory.

In the year 1887, Gottlieb Quaschnick was married to Elizabeth Grenz. Six
children were born to this union, namely: Lydia Jasmer, of Leola; Elizabeth,
died at the age of seventeen; Louisa Ell, deceased; Emma Tilden, of Columbus,
Ohio; Rosa, employed in Phillip, S. Dak.; Herman, died at the age of ten.

Mr. Quaschnick farmed for seven years, after which he took up his residence in
Eureka. Here he worked at common labor for twelve years, and was also employed
as a clerk in the Eureka Bazaar. In 1906, he entered business in Leola, from
which he retired about twelve years later. He now resides in Eureka.

MR. AND MRS. GEORGE C. KNICKERBOCKER

To the pioneers of McPherson County and especially to the pioneers of Eureka,
the name "Knickerbocker Hotel" is synonymous with true western hospitality, and
a place where the payment of a silver dollar meant "value received." The owners
of this institution were George C. Knickerbocker and Sarah Ellen Knickerbocker.
George C. Knickerbocker was born in Aurora, Illinois, on the 11th of October,
1850. His father was William Knickerbocker, who removed from the state of New
York to Aurora, Illinois about the year 1848. The Knickerbocker ancestry can be
traced back to 1625, when John Van Berghan Knickerbocker, of Brabaut was a
captain in the Navy of the Netherlands. Young Knickerbocker secured such
educational advantages as were afforded in the common schools of his native
county, and also learned the mason's trade. As a young man he was successfully
engaged in the contracting and building business in Illinois and adjoining
states.

On the 16th day of February, 1871, he was united in marriage with Sarah Ellen
Knickerbocker, formerly Jones, who was born at Parkridge, Illinois, on the 7th
of February, 1849. After marriage, the lives and experiences of this young
couple, up to the time of Mr. Knickerbocker's death, read practically as one.

When Mr. Knickerbocker was twenty-seven years of age, he and his wife located
in the city of Minneapolis, Minnesota, where he became engaged in the furniture
business, and operated one of the biggest establishments of its kind in the
city at that time. In 1882, this place of business was destroyed by fire at
great loss to Colonel Knickerbocker, which was the name by which he was
affectionately known, later in life. In 1885, the Knickerbockers proceeded
westward and located in McPherson County, being among the first settlers in the
western part of that county. In 1887, they made their permanent home in the
then new town of Eureka and Mrs. Knickerbocker has generally been conceded to
be the first white woman resident of that town.

The Knickerbockers took "time by the forelock" and before the town had even
been platted, they erected the first hotel building in Eureka. Work wag begun
on Sunday and by Monday a hotel of more or less haphazard construction had been
completed on the right of way of the Milwaukee Railroad Company. The railroad
officials, perhaps admiring the enterprise and courage of this young couple,
made no serious protest. The Knickerbockers rebuilt their hotel several times,
their last structure being located on the present site of the Standard Oil
service station in the heart of Eureka. This last building was widely known for
its beautifully decorated and spacious dining room.

George C. Knickerbocker was very active in civic affairs. He served on the
Board of County Commissioners of McPherson County, for five years. He also was
a member of the first board of aldermen for the city of Eureka, and held
various other offices of local trust. On the 23rd of February, 1901, he was
appointed colonel on the staff of Governor Charles N. Herreid.

In 1913, Mr. Knickerbocker was appointed postmaster for the city of Eureka by
President Wilson, after which he disposed of his hotel interests. He held this
position until the time of his death, the 25th day of January, 1920.

After the death of her husband, Mrs. Knickerbocker moved to Aberdeen, South
Dakota, where she lived in the Wilson Apartments until 1931. She then moved
into the Good Samaritan Hospital, where she lived until the time of her death
on the 31st of December, 1933.

The Knickerbockers had two children, both of whom preceded their parents in
death. Gertrude B. Knickerbocker was married to John E. Reagan and died at
Eureka in 1898, leaving three daughters surviving. Harry M. Knickerbocker, a
son, was an accomplished violinist and musical instructor. He was capable of
playing almost any kind of instrument, but the violin was his specialty. He
died in the prime of his life, a son and daughter surviving. At the time of the
death of Mrs. George C. Knickerbocker she left surviving her five grand
children and fifteen great grand children.

        Knickerbocker Hotel

Mr. and Mrs. Knickerbocker are buried side by side in the city cemetery at
Eureka. The influence which both of them had on the development of the pioneer
city of Eureka is such that the Knickerbocker name will long be remembered by
the people of the city.

HENRY BIEBER

Among pioneers of McPherson County, who have made the best of meager
educational opportunities in their new homeland, is Henry Bieber. His entire
formal schooling here, according to his own statement, was only sixteen months.
During that time, however, he learned the English language surprisingly well
and was accounted a good speller. Lack of opportunity to use that knowledge, he
admits, has caused his spelling, to use his own words, "to go to the dogs."

However, he has natural talent as a story teller, a fact which the reader may
judge for himself in a story from his pen, appearing elsewhere in this volume.

Henry Bieber was born on December 20, 1877, in the village of Glucksthal, South
Russia. He came to America with his parents in 1884, landing in New York on the
29th of September. From there the family went to Tyndall, Dakota Territory, and
spent the winter.

On May 1, 1886, the Biebers arrived in McPherson County, settling about four
and one-half miles northeast of Eureka.

Mr. Bieber relates that a big flock of wild geese stayed here that summer. His
uncle, John Ritter, caught seven or eight young geese and raised them. A big
prairie fire that fall destroyed practically all of their crop except some of
the wild grass which they used for hay.

In 1886, the first traveling Lutheran minister came to the Bieber home. Later,
in 1888, the Reverend August Zimmerman arrived there. The first residing
minister located in Eureka about a year later.

In 1888, at the age of ten, he helped his father with the ox team, hauling
wheat. They received as high as $1.08 per bushel for some of the wheat that
year.

He married Katharina Strobel of McIntosh County, North Dakota, on November 24,
1898. The young couple moved to Campbell County the next year, where Mr. Bieber
still resides. His wife passed away several years ago.

Ten children were born to them, nine of whom still live. They are: Gideon,
farmer in Campbell County; Henry and Fred, farmers, Trail city, S. Dak.:
Alfred, manufacturer of feed, Sioux City, Iowa; Herman and Daniel, who live on
the home farm; Martha, teacher in the city schools of McLaughlin, S. Dak.;
Jacob, teacher in Campbell County; and Gottlieb, attending Normal College at
Aberdeen, S. Dak. Mr. Bieber was one of the founders and directors of the
Eureka Community Hospital.

FRED G. MEHLHAFF

"Der Schwartz Mehlhaff," was the name attributed to Frederick G. Mehlhaff
during his younger days. This man, a son of George Mehlhaff, was born in
Kassel, South Russia, on the 16th day of February, 1864. At the age of twenty
years and alone he migrated to this country and settled at Menno, South Dakota.
