His advent into Menno was celebrated by him spending his only dollar for some
Bull Durham smoking tobacco. However, young Mehlhaff did not lack industry, so
he immediately got a job by offering his services to a man by the name of
Mettler at a price of $125.00 for the year.

        Mr. and Mrs. Fred G. Mehlhaff

The year being completed and with quite a substantial sum of money in his
pocket, Mr. Mehlhaff proceeded northward to file on lands which were then being
opened for homestead entry by the United States government. On November 11,
1885, he arrived at Ipswich, South Dakota, which at that time was the terminus
of the Milwaukee railroad. Mr. Mehlhaff was the cautious type and made several
exploratory trips into the new country before finally filing upon a tree claim
and homestead, on what is now known as the North Half of Section 20, Township
126, Range 71. The title to this land is still in his name today.

Fred Mehlhaff's financial means were very limited during this period. However,
he did manage to purchase a team of oxen at a cost of $120.00, a wagon at
$60.00, and a breaker plow at $20.00, and begin the business of farming. He
also constructed a sod house and sod barn for his own needs with the help of
his neighbors. The first years of Mr. Mehlhaff's farming life were typical
"lean years"; but at the same time through concentrated effort, he was able to
get enough produce from his farm to provide seed for the next year, and furnish
him with flour.

Even matrimony had its difficulties in those days, but in spite of many
obstacles young Fred was finally married to Margaretha Bochee, also a former
inhabitant of his native Kassel, who had migrated to this country with her
widowed mother, two sisters, and one brother. The marriage license was
purchased at Leola after a drive of more than thirty miles by team and buggy,
and then the proud bridegroom together with his wife and her brother, proceeded
to Eureka to be married by Reverend Hetzler, then the only German minister in
the community. However, upon their arrival they were told that the minister was
out on his own claim, a considerable distance from town. They then drove out to
where he was supposed to be, but were unable to find him there. Nothing
remained to do but to journey back to Eureka, where they were married by
Justice of the Peace White, who had his offices on the present site of M. M.
Smith's Confectionery store.

The business activities of Fred G. Mehlhaff were varied. During the years
1897-1898-1899, he was in partnership with Jacob Mehlhaff and Fred Mehlhaff, in
buying grain at Hillsview, South Dakota. In June, 1900, he moved to Eureka and
operated a furniture store near the present site of Emil Maisch's cream
station. After two years in this business, he sold his equipment and stock to
Henry Straub. He then entered the general merchandise business as a partner of
Stoller & Hepperle, which at that time had its place of business on the present
site of the Phillips 66 service station. This partnership existed only for a
short time, before Mr. Mehlhaff sold his interest to the other partners, and
accepted a job as a clerk in the Eureka Bazaar, where he remained for three and
one-half years. He then formed another partnership and engaged in the general
merchandise business with Jacob Hoffman. After six months, young Fred purchased
the interest of Hoffman in the business and for a time conducted the same as
sole owner. He sold this business to John Bender in October, 1908, and again
accepted a job with the Eureka Bazaar, where he served as a clerk for another
one and one-half years. He then went into the employ of F. W. Homeyer, the
owner of the Eureka Roller Mills, and stayed in his employ for four years, as
bookkeeper, grain buyer and flour salesman.

While employed at the mill he was induced by his banker to buy a business
building, known as the Hatz store and later occupied by E. Dierenfeldt as a
meat market. Mr. Mehlhaff leased the building to others, who were unsuccessful
and he was finally forced to take over the fixtures in lieu of rent.

It was therefore largely by chance and with the idea in mind of making the best
out of what seemed a bad investment, that Mr. Mehlhaff became engaged in the
meat market business. In April of 1914, he took over the present Mehlhaff's
Market from Charlie Hirsch, and he and his son Fred have operated the same
continuously down to the present day. In 1927, a new building was constructed,
and in 1931, the store became affiliated with the Red and White Stores and the
name changed from F. G. Mehlhaff Market to Mehlhaff's Market. Since 1931, his
son, Fred K. Mehlhaff, has been in sole charge of the business.

The Mehlhaff family has been very active in civic affairs. Fred G. Mehlhaff
served a number of terms as alderman from his ward, and his son, F. K.
Mehlhaff, has been a member and officer of the Board of Education for more than
ten years.

Fred G. Mehlhaff's advice to the coming generation is as follows: "Have faith
in this country. We had hard times before, and we will have hard times in the
future. Mother Nature, when she responds in this country, responds bountifully,
and I personally have never been sorry that I have made Eureka the place of my
residence."

To the marriage of Mr. and Mrs. F. G. Mehlhaff were born nine children, four of
whom died in childhood. There are living at the present time nineteen grand
children of this couple.

The names, addresses and occupations of the Mehlhaff children are as follows:
Pauline Sinkbeil, Zeeland, N. Dak., a wife of Adolph Sinkbeil who is manager
of the Thompson Lumber Yards at that place. The Sinkbeils have eleven children,
Walter, Verna, Gertrude, Carl, Raymond, Edgar, Leslie, Calma, Eileen, Bernice
and Gladys; one child died in infancy. Katharina Jahraus, Eureka, S. Dak., a
wife of Robert Jahraus, who is employed by the Division of Taxation of the
State of South Dakota. They have one child, Curtis Robert. F. K. Mehlhaff,
Eureka, S. Dak., manager of Mehlhaff's Market, married Lillian Gerdes and has
five children, Shirley, Dean, Willis, Margaret and June. Emma Schatz, Eureka,
S. Dak., a wife of J. H. Schatz, who is owner and operator of "The
Luncheonette" at Eureka. The Schatzs have one child, Vivienne. Gertrude Kortan,
Huron, S. Dak., a wife of Steve Kortan, a civil engineer of that place.

ADAM MERKLE

The name of Adam Merkle must be included in the roll of pioneers who bore the
brunt of privation and hardship of the earlier history of McPherson County.
Born in Odessa, Russia, in 1854, he immigrated to America and came to Eureka
from Nebraska in 1885, thus qualifying as "one of the first."

He was active in the period when Ipswich was the nearest town. That city was
distant from the Merkle farm by four or five days, measured by ox team travel.
There were times, however, when for some reason even ox team transportation was
unavailable or else undesirable. At any rate, Merkle told in after years of
pushing a supply of flour from Roscoe to his farm on a wheelbarrow. On another
occasion during the winter he carried a 50-pound sack of flour on his back from
the same town to his home. Jubilee book writers can only pause to remark that
those were days when there were "Giants on the Earth."

Several times he joined caravans who traveled at breakneck speed to Eureka to
seek protection from the Indians, who were reported on the war path. The scares
proved unfounded, but had the reports been true, discretion would surely have
been the better part of valor. It is difficult for the present generation to
appreciate fully the situation which confronted these settlers with regard to
the Indians.

Adam Merkle's pioneering ran the gamut of the common lot of the settlers on
this frontier prairie wilderness but had a full measure of the courage and
tenacity which won the battle.

He was married to Elisabetha Kurle in 1889, and six children were born to the
union. All except one reside on farms in the vicinity of Eureka and all have
apparently inherited a bit of their sire's qualities of character, for they
met with a generous measure of success. There were five boys and one girl,
named as follows: William, John, Christ, Fred, Edward and Lydia. Mr. and Mrs.
Merkel both died in 1933, only a few months apart.

Christ, the third in order of the Merkle family, has been in public life for a
long period of years, serving as state's attorney for two terms. He has been
county judge for the past six years and is the present incumbent of that office.
He is a veteran of the World War, a graduate of the Northern Normal and
Industrial School and of the law school of the State University.

GEORGE ZELLER

Fifty-two years ago, on the 1st of October, George Zeller left his native
Bessarabia with his parents and the rest of the family to come to this country.
On October 30, 1884, the family arrived in Menno, South Dakota. The first
winter was spent with George Zeller's uncle, Jacob Zeller. George, his brother
Jacob, and his sister Elizabeth "worked out" for their board and room.

The next spring his father purchased a team of horses, a span of oxen, two
wagons, a plow, two cows, a dog, a few necessities for house and kitchen, and
the family joined an immigrant train to Ipswich, which was then the end of the
railroad. The wagons were loaded there, and the pioneers set out to homestead
claims. The Zellers traveled west into Campbell County, after spending one
night in Roscoe. The second night was spent with two English settlers named
Henry and William Goodwin. The next day the family homesteaded six claims,
about nine miles west of Eureka.

A few days were needed to build a rough hut. The mother, when she first saw her
new home, wept. They had no neighbors, no schools, no churches. Her husband
assured her they would soon have neighbors, and that churches and schools would
follow. He was right. Soon they had neighbors on every side. Many of them were
so poor, however, that their first houses were holes in the ground with wagon
boxes inverted over them.

Until 1887, the closest town was Ipswich, fifty-five miles away. In that year
the railroad came to Eureka.

During the Zellers' first year on the new homestead, they built a better house,
and the boys broke up the prairie for seeding. Potatoes, flax, and oats were
planted that year. Oxen were used to thrash the flax. The flax was piled up to
a height of about three feet, and the oxen walked around on it until the flax
seeds separated from the straw.

These pioneers knew when Sunday came. They had no churches and no ministers, so
the father read to the family from his prayer book. Later, of course, ministers
traveled from house to house, until the Hoffnungsthal Church was established
eight miles west of Eureka.

George Zeller married Rosina Grenz, the daughter of a pioneer neighbor, in
1890. The young couple started out for themselves with practically nothing.
Hard work and clean living repaid them. At one time they owned nine quarter
sections of land.

In 1908 George Zeller moved to Java, South Dakota. Until 1917 he owned a
machine and implement shop there. He returned to the farm during the war. In
1925 he sold all his live stock and machinery and retired. He bought a house in
Eureka and settled here with his wife and youngest children.

Twelve children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Zeller, eleven of whom are still
living. They are: Gottlieb Zeller, Java, S. Dak.; Magdalena Wolff, Cardston,
Alberta, Canada; Elisabetha Schnaible, Java, S. Dak.; Rosina Spiry and Rosina
Bender, Java, S. Dak.; George Zeller, Dayton, Ohio; Johannes Zeller, Java, S.
Dak.; Bertha Bieber, Trail City, S. Dak.; Agnes Linder, Cardston, Alberta,
Canada; Martha Marquardt, Britton, S. Dak.; Kathryn Hamann, Eureka, S. Dak.
Emma Keim died at the age of thirty-four.

Mr. Zeller is now sixty-nine years old; his wife is sixty-seven.

MRS. HENRY BITZ

Mrs. Henry Bitz, whose maiden name was Rosina Huether, was born in Petersburg,
South Russia, September 23, 1858.

On October 15, 1877, she was married to Henry Bitz, born November 14, 1852, and
raised in Hoffnungstahl, South Russia. The Reverend Becker conducted the
ceremony.

Following an impulse to see the "so-much-talked-of-America" by relatives and
friends, they arrived in Eureka in 1885.

The railroad was being built into Eureka at that time, and Mrs. Bitz provided
meals and lodging for some of the crew. She has many interesting stories to
tell of those early days, her memory being exceptionally clear despite the fact
that she is almost eighty years old. Her husband and she were prominently
identified with the building of the local Evangelical Church.

They filed on three claims, a pre-emption, a timber claim and a homestead.
These claims were located about three miles from the present site of the town
of Eureka. Through unfair dealings, the Bitzs lost the two former.

In the early years of 1900, they moved to Canada, later returning to North
Dakota. Her husband died of cancer of the lung in 1911 at Sheridan County,
North Dakota. In 1926, she married George Bitz. He, too, died, and then she
married Peter Orth of Herreid, South Dakota. Her third husband died in 1933.

Mrs. Bitz has a comfortable little apartment with the Gottlieb Broeckel family
in Herreid, South Dakota.

Mr. and Mrs. Bitz were the parents of eight children, namely: Heinrich Bitz,
Martin, Manitoba, Canada; Andrew Bitz, Winnipeg, Canada; Daniel Bitz, Canada;
Jacob Bitz, Canada; Elizabeth Schnell (deceased), Canada; Wilhelm Bitz, farmer,
North Dakota; John Bitz, Milwaukee, Wisconsin;
Katie Bogda, Venturia, N. Dak.

JACOB WOLFF

Jacob Wolff was born in Marienberg, South Russia, on March 8, 1876. As a boy of
nine, he left Russia in 1885, with his parents and four brothers, Johannes,
Christian, George and Gottlieb. Among other emigrants who came with them and
settled in this territory were his uncle, Friedrich Wolff, Karl Fischer, Johann
Laemmle, David Laemmle and Friedrich Merkel.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Wolff

In April, 1885, they arrived in Menno, South Dakota, where they bought oxen, a
cow and a wagon. After a two weeks stay in Menno, they loaded their live stock
and equipment on a freight car and boarded the train for Ipswich. The party
included the Wolff family, Friedrich Wolff and Karl Fischer. At Ipswich, the
end of the railroad, their equipment was unloaded and they continued the
journey by ox team.

A night was spent on the prairie, and the following day they came to the farm
of Johannes Stein, about ten miles northeast of the present location of Eureka.
Here they stayed for two weeks, while the men drove around the country with
Ludwig Hoff, who owned a horse, looking for land on which to file their
homesteads. They finally found a satisfactory location eight miles north of
Eureka, in Spring Creek township.

The next step was to drive to Ipswich for a load of lumber and a breaking plow.
This trip was also made by oxen, and took three days. After these purchases,
Johannes Wolff, the father, had just 50 cents in cash left. On May 17, a
temporary dwelling was erected and the work of breaking up the land begun. The
first year they sowed a bushel of flaxseed and planted seven acres of corn. The
corn froze due to a frost in August, while the flax yielded twenty bushels for
which they received $1.25 per bushel. The threshing was done by laying the flax
on the ground and driving the oxen over it, later letting the wind blow out the
chaff.

After the crops were put in, they proceeded to construct a sod house and barn.
In order to get logs for the roofs, it was necessary to drive to the Missouri
River. They made this trip three times that summer, each trip requiring three
days time.

The rainfall was heavy in 1885, raising the creek so that it overflowed the
land. Nevertheless, in spite of favorable weather conditions, they could not
raise a large crop that year, due to the lack of equipment and seed. Their food
the first summer consisted of bread and butter and coffee. In order to make a
little extra money they picked up old buffalo bones and hauled them to Ipswich,
where they received from $6.00 to $8.00 per ton for them. Food supplies, also,
had to be brought from Ipswich for two years, until Eureka was founded.

In 1895, Mr. Wolff's father died, his mother passing away two years later. In
that year, 1897, he was married to Christina Gohl. The ceremony was performed
by Rev. H. Reinhardt of Eureka. The couple remained on the old homestead for
one year, when they took up land a mile farther north. Here Mr. Wolff erected
buildings, using lumber for the house and rock for the barn, and started
farming on his own account. He prospered as the years went by and gradually
increased his possessions and land holdings until eventually he owned 2,160
acres, all located in McPherson County. His farm today boasts a fine, new house
with modern improvements.

Mr. and Mrs. Wolff brought up a family of fourteen children, all living except
a boy, Herbert, who died in 1926 at the age of twenty. Living are Jacob, a
farmer at Yakima, Washington; Christina, married to George Rohrbach of Long
Lake, S. Dak.; Ida, married to Gust. Beck, farmer near Eureka; Theodore, a
farmer near Long Lake, S. Dak.; Ferdinand, a farmer near Eureka; Mary, married
to Carl Nies, a farmer near Eureka; Magdalena, married to Gottlieb Hoffman of
Leola, S. Dak.; Emma, married to Otto Opp, a farmer near Eureka; Otto, a farmer
living on the old homestead nine miles north of Eureka; Martha, a school
teacher living with her parents in Eureka; Frieda, married to Theodore Martel,
a school teacher at Long Lake, S. Dak.; Alma, employed in a department store at
Eureka; Esther, attending school and living with her parents.

In 1934, Mr. Wolff retired, taking up residence in Eureka, where with his wife
and three daughters, he is living at present.

JACOB STROBEL

When he was fourteen years of age, Jacob Strobel came with his parents to this
country, from South Russia, where he was born on January 3, 1871.

The Strobel family arrived in Ipswich, South Dakota, then the end of the
railroad line, on the first of June, 1886. They had no money but owned two
colts, two oxen, three cows, a hand plow and a harrow valued at approximately
$200.00.

A homestead was filed upon in Section 10 of what is now Glucksthal Township.
Like the homes of other pioneers, the buildings erected on the Strobel farm
were made of stone and sod.

In 1893, Jacob Strobel was married to Barbara Bieber. The marriage ceremony was
performed by the Reverend H. Reinhart of the Lutheran Church of Eureka. Eight
children were born to this union. They are: Dorothea, born May 5, 1895, now
married to Jacob Balliet, Trail City, S. Dak., farmer; Martha, born February
15, 1901, now married to Jacob M. Hoff, Eureka, S. Dak., farmer; Mathilda, born
September 25, 1906, married to Earl Thomas, Pueblo, Colorado, vaudeville
entertainer; Bertha, born January 14, 1908, married to Jacob Teske, Java, S.
Dak., section foreman; Arthur, born June 8, 1909, Eureka, S. Dak., farmer;
Walter, born May 25, 1911, Eureka, S. Dak., farmer; Emma, born February 26,
1914, married to Arthur Hausauer, farmer; Elmer, born June 25, 1919, at home.

        Jacob Strobel

Mr. Strobel, recalling conditions at the time he came to this country 51 years
ago, notes a great difference between then and now.

At that time, Ipswich, forty-five miles distant, was the nearest railroad
station and was the only town and marketing place for this territory. Mr.
Strobel says that it took him three days to make the round trip to Ipswich with
oxen, whereas today the same trip can be made in approximately two hours. There
were no roads, no section lines, fences, or telephone lines to travel by so the
travelers merely cut across the prairie in the direction of the town. Trail
markings to be followed were made on large rocks lying on the prairie.

Prices were somewhat lower for farm commodities in the early days. Strobel
recalls wheat that sold at 45 cents per bushel, barley at 25 cents per bushel,
butter at 5 cents per pound, and eggs at 4 to 5 cents per dozen.

He believes that he was happier in those days, for while there were occasional
poor crops due to drouth, for better agricultural conditions existed then than
now. The worst drouths of early days that he is able to recall, occurred in
1890 and 1898. However, those were incomparable to those of 1932, 1933, 1934,
1935, and 1936. As for grasshopper plagues, Mr. Strobel remembered none prior
to those of the last three and four years.

Mr. Strobel is at present living on his farm. For a number of years he resided
in Eureka and served two terms as city alderman. He has also served on the
school board in Glucksthal Township.

CHRISTIAN KURLE, SR.

In far-away Bergdorf, Russia, there lived a family of German descent. Many of
their friends had been leaving Russia to travel the great distance over land
and sea, establishing new homes in America. Reports were that these courageous
people had acquired large tracts of fertile farm land upon which homesteads
were founded.

Thus it was, Christian Kurle and Christina Fischer Kurle, with their family,
deciding to try their fortune along with other pioneers, arrived at Yankton,
Dakota Territory, in April, 1885. There Mr. Kurle purchased the pioneer
settler's traditional equipment, two oxen, two cows, a wagon, a plow, and a
harrow. Loading their property on a northbound train, in company with other
immigrants, the Kurles set their destination as McPherson County. However, the
Milwaukee Railroad extended only as far as Ipswich, so upon their arrival
there, they unloaded their belongings and completed the trip to McPherson
County by wagon, coming here on April 29, 1885.

Mr. Kurle had filed on a pre-emption claim in Section 10, Township 127, Range
72, now Rosenthal Township, while in Ipswich. The Kurle family immediately set
to work plowing sod for the erection of a sod shanty. The early spring nights
were cold and windy, necessitating a shelter without delay.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christian Kurle, Sr.

On May 7th, eight days after their arrival, a slight rain began to fall,
rapidly turning into snow from which a twenty-four hour blizzard resulted. In a
few days, the snow melted and nice weather followed. During May, the first sod
for a crop was broken and flax was planted. That year brought a good yield, due
to the plentiful moisture and favorable temperature. Sloughs and lakes were
full of water throughout the summer as there was almost a nightly rainfall
during the months of June and July.

Christian Kurle, the second, who was born in Bergdorf, Russia, on February 12,
1866, was now a young man nineteen years of age. He was the eldest of the
children in the family. Having faithfully assisted his parents in the erection
of their new home, there arose the necessity of his finding work elsewhere to
obtain money.

Early one morning he started for Frederick, a small town sixty miles to the
east, on foot. He remained in Frederick a week, seeking employment in vain.
Footsore and weary, he turned homeward to remain for several weeks. Later in
the summer he was again sent forth to seek employment and this time met with
success. He stayed at Frederick until harvesting and threshing was completed,
again traveling the sixty miles homeward on foot. The money which he had earned
bought groceries for the family during the winter.

The next spring, the young man helped his father seed the crop, and upon its
completion, again set out upon his slow journey to Frederick. Again he failed
to obtain employment immediately, but did not give up hope, and finally
succeeded in finding a job which lasted until fall. Upon his return home, news
of the extension of the railroad line westward from Ipswich reached the
residents of the northern part of the county. He trudged his weary way to
Roscoe, hoping to find work. But no new men were being hired. Again he returned
home via "shanks mare," spending the winter months with his parents.

There was great rejoicing and excitement in McPherson County when word came the
next spring that the railroad would be extended northward. Heretofore, Ipswich
had been the nearest town, and that was fifty-five miles distant. The coming of
the railroad meant employment for more local people; also, that a new town
would be started.

At the time the rails were laid on the grade in the spring of 1887, the town of
Eureka began its growth. New settlers were coming daily to establish
homesteads, filing on as large tracts as were available, fearing the land would
soon be taken. Practically all of these people left their homes in Russia to
come here, as did the Kurle family.

In the spring of 1889, young Kurle filed on a claim of land for himself. This
land is, to the present day, owned by Mr. Kurle and his family. The land was in
Section 14, Township 127, Range 72, now Rosenthal Township. Later in the same
year Mr. Kurle was united in marriage with Karolina Merkel, born in 1870.

Mrs. Kurle had come to America in 1885 with her parents, Philip Merkel and
Elizabetha Hinger Merkel. The Merkel family, too, had stopped at Yankton, where
they remained two years. At that time, the future Mrs. Kurle was but fifteen
years of age. Being in need of money, she hired out to work for $6.00 per
month. The next year she received $7.50 per month, but was required to toil
hard.

In the year of 1887, the Merkel family journeyed to McPherson County, coming
directly to the new town of Eureka. There were but three or four buildings
completed at the time. To Mrs. Kurle, the chorus of blows of half a hundred
carpenters' hammers was not unlike the rat-a-tat-tat of a machine-gun.

Mrs. Kurle obtained employment as assistant and waitress in a restaurant owned
by Jim Johnson, located on the lot where the Farmers and Merchants Bank
building now stands. The restaurant building, being hurriedly erected, was not
altogether substantial. In fact, one day during a strong wind, Mrs. Kurle and
Mrs. Johnson with the latter's two children, were forced to abandon the
restaurant and seek protection in the general merchandise store operated by
Hezel, Hepperle and Sauter. When the wind velocity had decreased somewhat, they
returned to the restaurant to resume their tasks.

A short time later Mrs. Charlie Pfeffer called upon Miss Merkel, advising her
of a place to work in a private home in Ipswich. Mrs. Kurle accepted the
position and remained there more than a year. Her employer's name was Sam
Marshall. It was in the fall of 1889, that her marriage to Mr. Kurle took
place.

Eight children were born to this union. They are: Christian, Edna, Lena, Rosa,
Edgar, Ruben, Frieda, and Martha (deceased). These children were all born and
raised on the homestead their father filed for in 1889.

Today finds Mr. and Mrs. Kurle happy and contented. They have encountered grief
and hardship, as have other pioneers, hut with perseverance and courage,
coupled with plain hard work, they established a comfortable home for
themselves and their children. At the time they turned their farming interests
over to their children, they owned 1,440 acres of farm land, and were free of
debt.

Throughout their lives Mr. and Mrs. Kurle have taken an active part in church
activities. In early days they walked miles to attend services, later going by
team and buggy, and now by car. They express their faith in God, and do not
believe any distance too great, or any method of travel too difficult, to hear
His Word spoken.

ADAM PRESZLER

The careers of Adam Preszler and his wife, Magdalena Spitzer Preszler, came to
a close within five months of one another. The old pioneer passed away in
March, 1933, and his faithful helpmate preceded on the last long trail in
October, 1932. Both were natives of the village of Kassel in South Russia, the
husband being born February, 1862, and the wife in 1864.

A year or so after they were married, the Reverend Mr. Kern performing the
ceremony, they embarked on the long journey to the new world with Menno, Dakota
Territory, as their destination. Arriving there in the fall of 1884, they
remained over winter, journeying to McPherson County the next spring. They
homesteaded on Section 80, Township 127, Range 72, and there was their home for
a long period of years, retiring to Eureka to spend their old age.

Their story, during earlier years, was similar to that of the hundreds of other
immigrant farmers and they met their difficulties with the same fortitude--and
acquired security for themselves.

On their arrival in America, they had only one child, the other children being
born here. Surviving the aged pioneers are: Magdalena Hoff, Wishek, N. Dak.;
Katie Hauck, Hosmer, S. Dak.; Christina Stoebner, Eureka; Barbara Bender,
Leola, S. Dak.; Adam A. and Fred, Eureka; Daniel, Medina, N. Dak.; and Paulina
Martell, Eureka.

KARL HAUSAUER, JR.

The fact that women and children contributed an important part to the settling
of this community cannot be disregarded. Most of them diligently aided their
husbands and fathers by frequently doing difficult manual labor. Karl Hausauer,
however, gives much credit to them and he tells how he as a child helped his
parents, and how his wife and children helped him.

        Mr. and Mrs. Karl Hausauer, Jr.

Mr. Hausauer, who was born of German parents on November 11, 1870, was brought
to America when 14 years of age. After weeks of travel with other immigrants
coming here, the group reached McPherson County on April 28, 1885, settling on
their homestead five miles north of the present site of Eureka.

The sixty mile journey north of Ipswich was made by team and wagon. When
reaching their land only miles of open prairie, void of dwellings, could be
seen in any direction. The Hausauer family was confronted with the problem of
finding shelter for the night. Immediately, every member able to work,
industriously helped dig a hole in the ground measuring approximately 7 feet by
12 feet. Having no lumber for a roof, they used the two ends of a wooden
bedstead for covering the dug-out. In such courageous manner, using their own
initiative, they provided acceptable shelter until a better home could be built.

The day after their arrival, Karl Hausauer, Senior, departed with the team and
wagon for Ipswich to purchase lumber and supplies. He was gone four days. How
terrifying it must have been for the brave mother and children to be alone four
whole days in a strange country, far from friends and neighbors! Who knew at
what moment a hungry wolf might find their rude dwelling or a band of
unfriendly Indians happen their way!

Undaunted by such possible upsetting thoughts, Mrs. Hausauer and her sturdy
youngsters worked every minute of the four days until the return of their
father bringing lumber for the new house. When he came, they immediately
erected a frame dwelling in which to live.

On May 7th, snow began falling heavily. In a few hours, on the wings of a
strong wind, a typical prairie blizzard was raging. All pioneers in the
community suffered ill effects from the storm to some degree, and many very
severely, because of insufficient food and shelter. For fuel during the storm,
the Hausauers used hay which they had cut the day before in a slough.

When twenty-two years of age, Karl Junior, wishing to possess land of his own,
filed on a homestead and a year later was married to Rosina Bieber. They moved
on to their homestead and Mr. Hausauer built their first house of sod. The
property with which they began farming was an amount similar to that of his
father when settling here. They had a plow, a wagon and a harrow, and in live
stock possessed two cows and two oxen. Besides that they had a sum of $26.00 in
cash.

While Mr. Hausauer plowed and seeded, the young wife hitched the oxen to the
harrow, walking beside the team on the field all day long.

Mrs. Hausauer verifies her husband's word in saying that they were happy in
those days. They could look ahead and see their hopes gradually realized. Each
year they accomplished more in the way of gaining a comfortable home. Their
neighbors were laboring under like conditions and each helped the other
whenever he could.

When the Hausauers began farming, they owned 160 acres of land which amount
increased to 1,560 acres in 1925, all free of debt. At the latter date, Mr. and
Mrs. Hausauer retired from the farm, moving to Eureka.

The five children of Mr. and Mrs. Karl Hausauer, Junior, are: Henry, Edward,
Albert, Paul, and Katharina who is married to Christian H. Aman, a farmer in
Rosenthal Township. All are progressive farmers of this community, with the
exception of Albert, who is minister in the Congregational Church at Plevna,
Montana.

MR. AND MRS. KARL SCHUTZ, SR.

Rosina Dollinger was born September 26, 1860, in Gluecksthal, South Russia. Her
life was spent at the parental home until 1881, when she married Jacob Ladner,
also a resident of Gluecksthal. The ceremony was performed by the Rev. Mr. Kerm
on February 10, 1881.

They remained in Russia for three years, and in 1884 embarked for the "new
land," as America was known to them.

It was in the fall of the year when the Ladners and two small children arrived
in Scotland, Dakota Territory. They spent the winter there. Early in the spring
they came to McPherson County, having been delayed several months by low
finances. Finally they borrowed enough money from Henry Ladner, a brother, to
finance the trip.

When they got to McPherson County it was a forlorn looking sight that greeted
them. Eureka had not been established, and homesteads were miles apart; but Mr.
Ladner filed on a homestead fourteen miles northeast of Eureka. After the house
was partially built he departed for Frederick to seek employment. The
responsibility of completing the house was thrown on the courageous wife's
shoulders. She walked two miles to get water to mix the clay used for building
the house, which she completed entirely by herself.

Next she built a stove which was used for cooking and baking. She also planted
the crop that was to be their sole means of support for the coming winter.
Having no one to tend to her little ones, she took them along to the field
where she worked.

Provisions then must he purchased at Ipswich. It was a two-day trip for the ox
team, their only means of transportation.

Her husband died in 1897, as a direct result of impaired health suffered by the
Russo-Turk war in which he had taken part. He was buried near the farm home in
the Opp settlement northeast of Eureka.

Mrs. Ladner married Karl Schutz, Sr., a widower, in 1899, the Rev. H. Reinhardt
performing the ceremony. The couple then moved to the husband's farm about nine
miles southeast of Eureka, which he had purchased from Casper Seitz for the sum
of $300.00.

Mr. Schutz, also a native of Gluecksthal, was born May 14, 1857, and came to
America in 1899. He resided in Dawson, North Dakota, prior to coming here. His
first wife was Christina Schnaidt, also a native of Gluecksthal, South Russia.
They were united in marriage in 1879 by the Rev. Birnbach, and became the
parents of eight children.

Mr. Schutz was considered well-to-do in comparison to most newcomers; he had
$2,400.00 in cash upon arrival here. But the cash resources were conserved with
extreme care. His family of ten occupied one small room, which was so small
that at night when they retired they just spread straw on the floor and lay
down on it, one after the other. The last one into the house slept by the door
so a lock was not necessary.

The Schutz-Ladner families continued their residence together on the farm until
1910, when they moved into Eureka, where they still reside. Their union was
blessed with one son, Julius, who travels extensively.

Mrs. Schutz' children by first marriage are: Katherina Hoffman, Leola, S. Dak.;
Rosina Feix, Kulm, N. Dak.; Jacob Ladner, Eureka; Christina Schutz (deceased),
Eureka; John Ladner, Tulsa, Oklahoma; Edward Ladner, car salesman, Albuquerque,
New Mexico.

It might be added that Mrs. Schutz' son, John Ladner, despite meager early
education, has made an enviable record as a lawyer. He has the distinction of
being the youngest judge in the state of Oklahoma. He resides in Tulsa At the
present time.

Mr. Schutz' children include: Karl Schutz (deceased), Eureka; John, a farmer;
Jacob, a clerk, and Fred, a farmer, all of Eureka; Christ, a farmer,
McLaughlin, S. Dak.; George, a farmer in Montana; Gottlieb, Isabel, S. Dak.;
and Rose Ackerman, Isabel, S. Dak.

JACOB F. JAKOBER

Jacob F. Jakober, born in Gluecksthal, South Russia, on May 20, 1877, when a
lad of eight accompanied his parents to America.

The family came direct to McPherson county and settled on a homestead located
twelve miles northeast of Eureka.

They built a sod house and barn, and with farm equipment consisting of a pair
of oxen, a cow and a hand play, the value of all being $185.00, started to
build their future, with high hopes and anticipation. At the time flax sold for
75 cents per bushel. Other products, such as butter, were sold for 3 and 4
cents per pound; eggs were 4 cents a dozen. In 1886, no crops were realized due
to a drouth.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob F. Jakober

Mr. Jakober remembers vividly the blizzard of '88. The wind came up very
strongly just before sunrise, which was about eight o'clock, on January 12, and
before long, the storm was in full force. There was a foot and a half snowfall,
and it was severely cold. Many people froze to death and hundreds of head of
cattle perished during this blizzard.

On October 11, 1900, Mr. Jakober, Jr., was married to Katherine Viehlhauer of
Hutchinson County. The Rev. Mr. Haag, Lutheran pastor, performed the
ceremony.

Eleven children blessed this union. They are: Bertha Voegele, McPherson County,
Arthur, Reinhold, Jacob, Julius, Christ, Edward, Fred, Samuel, Milbert and
Katherine, all at home.

His wife died and in 1926 he was married a second time. His second wife was
Johanna Mutshall, of Trail City, a widow having eight children.

His mother died in April, 1916, and the father died in September, 1917. Both
are buried near the home farm.

Mr. Jakober is in good health and enjoying the results of his efforts at good
farming.

FRED PRESZLER

Embarking to the United States when he was ten years old, Fred Preszler took up
his abode with his parents at Menno, Dakota Territory, in 1874. Mr. Preszler
was born in Kassel, South Russia, on March 26, 1864.

He remained with his parents in the southern part of the territory until he was
twenty-one years of age, when he became possessed with the desire to establish
a home of his own. Rumors were afloat in this section that there was land to be
had for the asking in the northern section of the territory. It was on May 5,
1885, when Fred Preszler homesteaded on three quarters of land one mile south
of the present site of Eureka, where he resided for twenty-one years. His
possessions were not many when he arrived in Eureka; a plow, a drag, two horses
and two cows. The season being far advanced when he arrived on his newly
acquired farm, only a few acres of flax could be sown for that year's crop.
During the first summer Mr. Preszler


spent in this territory he inherited $100.00 with which he was able to build a
small house and barn, and had money enough left to take him back to Menno to
visit his parents. Upon returning, he found employment on the railroad and
remained at this work through the summer of 1886.

"I cannot say too much about the little store in Eureka at that time," said Mr.
Preszler. "The store was very small and was operated by Mr. Werre. He kept us
well supplied with many of the essentials, such as sugar, salt and coffee."

In 1905, Mr. Preszler decided to go into business and at this time purchased
the general merchandise store from Daniel Rietz. He remained in this business
until 1912, when he retired from active service in favor of his ever growing
farm interests. He owned a fine farm and was particularly interested in raising
thoroughbred live stock.

Having lost his life's companion in the death of his wife, he was confronted
with the problem of raising a family of small children. On December 20, 1910,
Mr. Preszler remarried, taking for his second wife Mrs. Fred Hoff, nee Margaret
Guthmiller, who lived ten miles east of Eureka.

Mr. Preszler remained on the farm until 1917, and it was during this time that
he reached the height of his land possessions, owning fifteen quarters. He was
always ready and willing to assist in furthering community projects, including
the erection of his church, German Reform, the Lutheran college, and at one
time was a stockholder in the German newspaper, The Rundschau.

Again he received the call to enter into business and purchased an interest in
a hardware store at Wishek, North Dakota. Mr. Preszler remained in this
business until 1923, when he disposed of his interest and retired from active
life. He now resides at Wishek, North Dakota.

The following is a list of the children: Fred, Jr., Lehr, N. Dak.; Margaret,
Mrs. John J. Pietz, Eureka, S. Dak.; Henry, Oakes, N. Dak.; Pauline, Mrs. Mike
Stutz, Isabel, S. Dak.; Barbara, Mrs. Streile, Hazelton, N. Dak.; Elizabeth,
Mrs. Christ Herr, Wishek, N. Dak.; Martin, who was accidentally killed while
hunting in 1911; Helen, Mrs. Wm. Hein, Ashley, N. Dak.; Gottfried, who died in
childhood; Edward, Wishek, N. Dak.; Martha, Mrs. Walter Lebis, Detroit, Mich.

JACOB MEHLHAFF, SR.

One of the early arrivals in the Eureka vicinity in 1885, was Jacob Mehlhaff.
He had come with his parents from South Russia in 1874, the family settling
first in the Menno community, not so far from Yankton. Jacob Mehlhaff had been
born in the village of Kassel in 1861, but grew to manhood in the southern part
of Dakota Territory, and was married there to Christine Koerner in 1885.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Mehlhaff, Sr.

The same year the young people came to McPherson County, settling on a
homestead in the Eureka community. Here Mehlhaff and his wife started with
little more than other new settlers. But their thrift and good management
brought them more than ordinary success. Before he ended his active career as a
farmer in 1927, he had acquired 3,520 acres of land, which he divided up into
eight farms. Later he built two residences in Eureka.

With the children of the family settled in life the elder Mehlhaffs moved to
Eureka, where they planned to spend their old age in comfort and ease. And they
took much comfort in their home in this little city, enjoying visits with their
children as well as a number of grand children.

Twelve children were born to the Mehlhaffs, ten sons and two daughters, all of
them surviving. They are: Jacob, Edward, Emil, Julius, Wilhelm, Gotthilf, Otto,
Herbert and Arthur, all residing in the Eureka community; Johann at Hosmer;
Mrs. Albina Dietrich, of Hosmer; and Mrs. Ida Schaible at Wishek, N. Dak.

A few years after retiring, Mr. Mehlhaff was afflicted with rheumatism, which
marred his enjoyment of life for some time and his health gradually failed. His
death came on April 8, 1934, and the remains were interred in the Eureka City
cemetery.

Mr. Mehlhaff was a charter member of the Reformed Church and remained active
for its welfare during his entire lifetime, serving as deacon and elder for a
long period.

Mrs. Mehlhaff still resides in Eureka and enjoys good health and the esteem of
many friends.

FRIEDERICH SCHOTT

When Friederich Schott came to America, according to his own admission, the
first purchase he made was not an ox team but a German-English dictionary,
which he believed would help acquire a working knowledge of the English
language. He had finished the public school in his native village,
Hoffnungsthal, Bessarabia, Russia, where he was born September 23, 1863. Later
he attended a grammar school for two years, thus acquiring a bit more education
than was the fortune of many of his contemporaries.

His father, Peter Schott, was a farmer and blacksmith in Hoffnungsthal and
young Friederich learned the trade and also the art of farming. The blacksmith
in those days fashioned about all the farm implements used. All labor was done
by hand and it required an immense amount of toil to sow and reap harvests.

When he was twenty-two years old he courted and married Magdalena Ricker, the
ceremony being performed by the Reverend Mr. Peterson at Kloestitz. A baby was
born to them, a daughter whom they named Katherine, and who was to be the only
one of their eleven children to be born in Russia.

As the head of a family, young Friederich Schott was dissatisfied with his
prospects in Russia. The military service, the small farms and the glowing
pictures painted by pamphlets sent from America no doubt added to this unrest.
At length, the decision to seek their fortune in the new land was reached. Not
only young Friederich and his wife, but the father, mother and family set out
on the journey which was made by way of Bremen, Germany, landing in New York on
June 2, 1886.

        Mr. and Mrs. Friederich Schott

The ocean voyage was a pleasure, but they were happy on their arrival at
Lesterville, Dakota Territory, to find friends and acquaintances who welcomed
them to America. In Lesterville, the Schotts outfitted with the traditional
homesteader's equipment and shipped their effects by rail to Ipswich.

From Ipswich they set out in a northwesterly direction, finally arriving in
McPherson County on July llth, where they found distant relatives and friends.

They found a homestead and began their farming careers much as did hundreds of
others, breaking a few acres of sod and sowing their seed by hand. Friederich
Schott sowed his first crop in the spring of 1887, and was fortunate in reaping
a good crop. It brought him about $700.00 as the prices were better than they
were to be for a long period of years to follow.

One may imagine, if imagining be permitted by writers of the story of an
immigrant pioneer, that the Schotts were tremendously happy over so fine a
result from the small patch of ground they were able to plant with nothing but
their ox team for power. From then on their farming experience was probably
little different from that of the majority of other new settlers. As better
equipment was acquired, the labor was less arduous and the results more
satisfactory, even though the prices of farm products were extremely low during
many of the earlier years.

An experience from the early years that stands out in the story of Mr. Schott
is a trip he and a neighbor made to Ipswich in 1886. The snow was deep, a
blizzard was raging, and the temperature way below zero. But they were anxious
to get their mail and a bit of news from the outside world as well as bring
back such small supplies as they might carry. The trip required three days
going and four days to return. The two pedestrians set out to follow the grade
of the Milwaukee railroad which had been completed but bridges and rails had
not as yet been put in place. The grade was a good guide but wherever a gap in
it was left for a bridge it was level full of snow, and on several occasions
they worked themselves out of the deep snow with difficulty.

Arriving in Ipswich they found the town full of settlers, who like themselves
were there to get mail and news from the outside world. The town was
snow-bound, however. One train and two snowplows were marking time, waiting for
the storm to subside. Schott and his friend remained overnight and were happy
the next morning to see that the sky had cleared and a fine
day in prospect.

They started on their homeward trip gaily, but were soon dismayed to note that
another blizzard was bearing down on them from the northwest. They trudged on,
however, stopping with some settler overnight, but so fierce was the storm that
it took them four days to reach their homes. It was a grand effort, but
speaking of it in after years, Mr. Schott says that at times they were so weary
they kept going only by exercising their last
ounce of strength.

During their residence in McPherson County ten children were born to the
Schotts, two of whom died in infancy.

The surviving family are: Katherine Hausauer, born in Russia; Anna Maria
Fischer, Greenway, S. Dak.; Paulina Hieb, Eureka; Fred P., Idaho Falls, Idaho;
Magdalena 0st, Fredonia, N. Dak.; Regina Schuetzle, Plevna, Mont.; John,
Rudolph and Edward, Eureka.

Mr. and Mrs. Schott had the distinction and pleasure of celebrating their
golden wedding anniversary in the presence of their children and friends on
January 10, 1935. The pioneers are still residing on their farm in retirement,
having built a home near one of their sons.

JACOB L. KLEIN

Jacob Klein's pioneer story varies a bit from that of the majority of early
settlers in the Eureka community. In common with other people in this part of
the country, he is a native of South Russia, being born in Gluecksthal,
December 12, 1860. He, too, came to America, and Tyndall, Dakota Territory, was
his first destination. He had married a young woman, Christina Wittmayer, by
name, in Bergdorf of his native country, but before he brought his family out
to the wilds of McPherson County he made a scouting trip to see what it was
like.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob L. Klein

He took the train to Westport, and then walked westward to McPherson County,
finally filing on a claim six miles north of the present site of Eureka.
Returning to Tyndall, he remained there until fall, when he came back to
Ipswich and took up the task of establishing a home on the wild prairie. He
built a house, a barn, and a granary of sod and the battle for crops was on.
That story has often been told in these pages and need not be repeated here.
From a start with little or nothing, he provided a good home for himself and
his family and afterwards moved to Eureka. As a citizen of the municipality, he
took active part in public affairs, serving on the city council for a period of
six years.

After moving to Eureka in 1905, he engaged in the livery business, being in
partnership with John Stock for four years. Buying Stock's share, he operated
the business alone until selling his holdings to Henry Eckman, Sr. & Sons in
1912. Three years later, he engaged in the automobile sales business with
Obenauer Brothers, a partnership which continued a year and a half.

Mrs. Christina Klein died in 1912, and a year later Mr. Klein married a second
time, taking as his wife, Mrs. John Dupper, born Johanna Moser.

Twelve children were born to the Kleins, five of whom survive. They are: Mrs.
Henry Bader, Wishek, N. Dak.; Mrs. Wm. Rohrbach and Theodore, residing on farms
near Eureka; Mrs. August Lutz, Selby, S. Dak.; and Jacob, manager of a store in
Sioux City, Iowa.

Thus, in brief outline, reads the story of the Kleins. In between the lines,
the reader will perceive all the elements that might make the story of these
pioneer settlers an arresting tale of adventure and fine achievement. It is
forever a part of the warp and woof of the great fabric of history, future
generations will know as the history of McPherson County.

MICHAEL WENZEL

When Michael Wenzel and his wife, Magdalena Gerhard Wenzel came to McPherson
County in 1886, they were considered well equipped to start farming. They had
come from their home in South Russia to Scotland, Dakota Territory, a few weeks
earlier and while there purchased two horses, two yoke of oxen, several cows, a
wagon, a plow and a mower. In addition they supplied themselves with such food
stuffs as might be carried for a distance, and necessary household goods.

In order to save time they loaded an immigrant car at Scotland, proceeding to
Ipswich by rail. Unloading there, the trek to their destination in McPherson
County was made by wagon. After looking around a bit they filed on a
pre-emption and a tree claim. Then came the long hard pull for the
establishment of home and providing the necessities of life for their growing
family.

The Wenzels had their share of toil and hardship in common with their
neighbors, but their courage and industry triumphed and they achieved a modest
fortune and were thus assured of a comparatively carefree old age. Mrs. Wenzel
died in 1904, and two years later the family moved to Eureka, which was then
the family home until 1923, when Mr. Wenzel passed on. The Wenzels had a family
of seven children, Magdalena, Christian, Anna, Michael, Jr., Helena (deceased),
William and Lydia.

JOHANN SCHERBENSKE

Johann Scherbenske was born in Hoffnungsthal, Bessarabia, South Russia, on
April 15, 1859. When he was twenty years old he married Magdalena Naasz. The
Rev. J. Peters of Kloestitz, performed the ceremony.

In 1886, Johann Scherbenske and his family came to Campbell County, locating
about ten miles northwest of Eureka, near Spring Creek. They experienced many
hardships, due chiefly to the lack of funds. At that time flour had to be
hauled from Ipswich by oxen. Because he had no funds to pay for good flour, Mr.
Scherbenske was once forced to buy flour that had grown stale and bitter. The
bitter flour was sold to him "on time" by Chas. Pfeffer, later a picturesque
figure in the business life of Eureka.

In 1916 he retired from the farm because of his wife's health and moved to
Artas, South Dakota. Four years later his wife died. Of the thirteen children
born to them five are still living. They are: Johann J. Scherbenske, Arena, N.
Dak.; Wilhelm Scherbenske, Roscoe, S. Dak.; Margaretha Grenz, Artas, S. Dak.;
George Scherbenske, Eureka, S. Dak.; and Elias Scherbenske, Artas, S. Dak. Mr.
Scherbenske married Mrs. Margareta Lutz, a widow, in October, 1921. They moved
to Eureka, where they now live. For the past two years his health has been very
poor.

SOLOMON ISAAK

Solomon Isaak, one of the leading business men of Eureka, being one of the
well-known firm of Isaak & Keim Brothers, was born near Odessa, Russia, in the
year 1865, and was a son of Gottlieb Isaak. He was born about one hundred miles
west of the city mentioned, his father having taken up his abode there upon his
immigration from Germany. In 1878, Gottlieb Isaak came with his family to
America and located in what is now South Dakota. He took up government land ten
miles east of the present town of Parkston, Hutchinson County, becoming one of
the first settlers in the county and resided there for a period of eight years.

While the family resided in the Parkston vicinity, Mr. Isaak tells with huge
enjoyment of how he was "boss syrup maker" of the neighborhood. The settlers
planted sugar cane and had a mill for extracting the sweet juices, which were
afterward boiled down into syrup or sorghum molasses. The boiling down process
required expert attention and was an art which Solomon Isaak acquired and
practiced with a good deal of pride.

As his children were growing up and wanted land of their own and he felt better
opportunities might be afforded them farther north in the territory, Gottlieb
Isaak then removed with his family to Mercer County, North Dakota, where he
engaged in farming and stock growing during the next seven years, then moving
to Eureka.

Solomon Isaak secured his elementary education in his native land and
supplemented this by attending night school, the only opportunity afforded
after the family came to the territory of Dakota. However, he broadened his
education through the associations and experiences of a signally active and
successful business career.

In 1888, Solomon Isaak initiated his independent career by engaging in farming
in Mercer County, North Dakota, where he remained seven years. His place was
fifty miles from the railroad and he preferred not to be thus isolated from
civilization for a longer period, and he accordingly disposed of his live
stock, rented his farm and then came to South Dakota once more and took up his
abode in Eureka. For four years he engaged in c1erking in mercantile business.

He next began buying and shipping live stock on his own account, continuing
operations two years, meeting with gratifying success. He also began buying
grain during this period. In 1900, he entered into partnership with John and
Jacob Keim, under the firm name of Isaak & Keim Brothers. At the outset they
purchased a grain elevator in Eureka, but later acquired two others, located at
strategic points. In 1902, the firm also purchased the feed store of William
Robb, in Eureka.

Mr. Isaak exchanged his land in North Dakota for land in Franklin County,
Washington, where he now owns a half section. He was progressive in his methods
and was held in high regard as a citizen and business man.

On the 23rd of December, 1889, Mr. Isaak was married to Miss Maria Mueller, who
was likewise born in Russia and who came to America when a child.

        Mr. and Mrs. Solomon Isaak

She was the daughter of Friederich and Christina Mueller who resided in
Leipzig, in what is now Roumania, but then a part of Russia. Her experiences
after coming to America might indeed make a thrilling story. The immigrant girl
got employment in the family of a physician attached to the U. S. Indian
service and naturally came in daily contact with the Indians, even learning a
bit of the language. She thus came face to face with the people who were little
but an unpleasant legend to most of the white settlers.

The Isaaks were the parents of fourteen children. Surviving ones are: Dr.
Richard Henry Isaak, Optometrist, Eureka; Lydia Pallviny, Portland, Oregon;
Edwin S. Isaak, manager of elevator, Hillsview, S. Dak.; Mathilda Mertz,
Leonard, N. Dak.; Dr. Otto Isaak, Dentist, Tyndall, S. Dak.; Ann Nelson,
Eureka; Theodore Isaak, Eureka; Hulda Travaille, Mitchell, S. Dak.; Erna
Wenzel, Esther Isaak, and Elaine Isaak of Eureka.

In 1920, Mr. Isaak retired from the elevator business, his son Walter J.,
assuming the responsibility of same.

Nearly all of the Isaak family were gifted with musical ability, a circumstance
which provided much to the happiness and enjoyment of the family circle. For a
time a family orchestra was maintained, appearing at public functions of
various kinds, and playing concerts around the family hearth almost daily.

Tragedy entered the happy family circle when failing health made it imperative
for Walter, one of the younger sons, to seek the milder climate of Florida in
1936. Together with his wife, they journeyed to that state and had no sooner
arrived than a most distressing accident brought death to Mrs. Isaak by
drowning, on January 21, 1986. The grief-stricken husband brought the body back
for burial and was himself taken violently ill soon after. Two months later he
died, the best medical aid proving futile.

The father has been in failing health since 1981, when an attack of arthritis
halted his usual activities. Since 1934, he has been bed-ridden, but tenderly
cared for by his wife, who is constantly with him. He smiles and maintains a
cheerful disposition, chatting pleasantly with old friends who drop in to visit
him.

He feels he has lived a full life and a happy one despite the hardship
attendant upon pioneer days and the tribulations that destiny has decreed.

CHRISTIAN HEISER

Included among the early arrival of pioneers in the present vicinity of the
city of Eureka, South Dakota, was Christian Heiser. Mr. Heiser was born in
Hoffnungstahl, South Russia, on the 6th of March, 1876. His parents were
Leonhart Heiser and Rosina Heiser, nee Ehrmann. In 1886, at the age of nine, he
emigrated to this country with his parents and three other children. Two of
these children, namely, Barbara Dollinger and Michael Heiser, have already
passed away, and the other, Johanna Riegert, is now living in Artas, South
Dakota. The family's first destination was Hutchinson County, Dakota Territory,
where they spent one year working for various people.

In the spring of 1886, being attracted by large and spacious lands that were
being opened for homestead entry in the north, they set out for the town of
Ipswich. Here they purchased farm equipment, a yoke of oxen, some lumber and
other incidentals, and set out to locate on various claims. They finally found
a suitable location in Campbell County about nine miles southwest of the
present site of the city of Eureka, and immediately filed on three claims, one
a homestead, the other a tree claim, and the third a pre-emption. During that
first year, ten acres of ground were broken up and seeded to flax. The yield
for that year, however, was very scarce, and the family was only able to
subsist on the $1.50 weekly wage of the eldest daughter, Barbara, who found
work in Ipswich. The second year more land was broken up and a better yield was
gotten. It was during these two years that the flax crop was threshed by having
the oxen walk over the harvested crop and then tossing the same into the air
and having the wind separate the seed from the straw.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christian Heiser

Concerning the early winters, Christian Heiser states that they were very
severe. Often times the snow was so deep that the sight of the farm dwellings
was obscured from human vision.

When Christian Heiser was fourteen years of age, his father died. However, he
continued living with his mother on the old family homestead, helping her
along. His mother died when he was about thirty years of age.

Various positions of public trust have been held by Mr. Heiser. For six years
he was a director of the Northwest German Farmers Mutual Insurance Company of
Eureka, South Dakota. During his entire lifetime, he was an active official of
the Hoffnungstahl Lutheran congregation, whose church is eight miles west of
Eureka, South Dakota.

As a young man, Christian Heiser was married to Katharina Schick, a daughter of
Christian and Margaretha Schick, nee Walz. Mrs. Heiser was born in Neudorf,
South Russia, and came to this country with her parents when she was but nine
years old. This union was blessed with eleven children, two of whom died in
infancy. The names and addresses of the Heiser children are as follows: Amelia
Scherle, Artas, S. Dak.; Bertha Fischer, Artas, S. Dak.; Emma Bieber, Trail
City, S. Dak.; Anna Pfeifer, now temporarily residing in Lodi, California; and
Ernestine, Katherina, Gustave, Leonhardt, and Ada, all of whom are now living
on the old homestead with their parents.

During his lifetime, Christian Heiser was able to accumulate 1,440 acres of
land, and build a set of fine and modern buildings on his farm. They live there
today on the same land that was homesteaded by Mr. Heiser's parents, more than
fifty years ago, happy and contented.

WILLIAM BOETTCHER

Another of McPherson County's pioneers residing in Eureka today is William
Boettcher, who was born of German parentage in South Russia, July 30, 1878. His
parents' names were Carl L. Boettcher and Fredericka Burke Boettcher.

Like many of their friends and neighbors in Russia, the Boettchers thought of
America as a land in which they could make a better living and provide more
opportunities for their children. They had but little money, so after borrowing
a sufficient amount for the long journey on land and sea, the family arrived at
Ipswich, South Dakota, in 1886. This small town was situated at the end of the
railroad line, so in order to get farther north into Campbell County, the
family was seated atop a load of lumber and set out for the location of their
homestead.

The first home was built of sod. As the Boettchers possessed no horses or oxen,
the kindly neighbors assisted them by plowing the sod and each member of the
family old enough to work carried the blocks of sod to the house site.

When the house had been completed, the next thing to do was pay back the money
borrowed to come to America. Therefore, each and every member of the family was
obliged to work hard to aid the elder Boettchers in paying their debts and make
a living.

Carl Boettcher was a substantial farmer, active in community affairs, and was
affiliated with the Lutheran church. He passed away in July, 1904, and his wife
in July, 1908. The children were: William, Friedericka, John (deceased),
Katharina (deceased), Ludwig, Johann, Christina, and Wilhelmina (deceased).

        William Boettcher

William Boettcher, growing up on that South Dakota homestead, knowing many
hardships and privations, was a studious type of person and a diligent scholar
in local schools. He later attended the South Dakota State College at Brookings
for one year, and a business college in Minneapolis.

In 1904, Mr. Boettcher became assistant cashier of a bank at Perth, North
Dakota; from 1908 to 1915, he was teller in the German American State Bank at
Lincoln, Nebraska. For the six succeeding years he was associated with the
Farmers and American State Bank of Golden Valley, North Dakota. In 1921, he
came to Eureka as vice president of the Guarantee State Bank and from 1926 to
1930 was president of that bank. He had then been in the banking business
twenty-six years.

During the World War, Mr. Boettcher was an energetic worker on the local
committees carrying on war work. He also served on the building committee at
the time the Zion Lutheran Church in Eureka was erected. The community always
found him ready to do his share, whether working for the community or the
nation.

On June 20, 1906, William Boettcher and Miss Marie E. Schulze, of Lincoln,
Nebraska, were united in marriage. Mrs. Boettcher, one of ten children, too,
was of German parentage. Her father, a Nebraska farmer, was Ernst A. Schulze
and her mother was Elizabeth Kaiser Schulze.

To this union were born four children. They are: Orville W., born August 20,
1911, now a Lutheran Minister; Ress H., born June 10, 1913, teaching school;
Rhea L., born December 20, 1917; a high school graduate; Bernice M., born March
13, 1922, a high school student.

This home and entire community was saddened on December 18, 1928, by the tragic
death of Mrs. Boettcher, the result of a kerosene stove explosion.

Since 1930, Mr. Boettcher has been writing insurance, and has also held
positions with the government under the ERA and WPA.

CHRISTIAN SCHICK

Christian Schick was a boy of seven when he emigrated to the United States with
his family from Zibrico in Russia, where he was born in 1879. The family
arrived in Ipswich, Dakota Territory, in June of 1886, and from there continued
northward by ox cart. His father filed on land south of Eureka, and after
making final proof located in Campbell County, with a colony of Zibrico people.

In the year 1902, he was married to Katharina Neuharth, a daughter of Friedrich
and Dorothea Neuharth. This union was terminated by the death of Mrs. Schick
late in the same year, and the following year Mr. Schick was married to
Karolina Albrecht, a daughter of Heinrich Albrecht. Twelve children were born
to this couple.

With the exception of a few years spent in Texas, Mr. Schick has remained a
resident of this community.

CHRISTOPH J. HEZEL

One of the first men to engage in business in Eureka was Christoph J. Hezel,
who opened a store here in 1887, soon after the railroad had entered the town.
Mr. Hezel was born in Odessa, Russia, on December 25, 1864. His parents were
Jacob and Regina (Keller) Hezel, also natives of that section of the great
empire.

Christoph was the third of a family of nine children. The family was in
comparatively good financial circumstances and he had the advantage of an
excellent education in Russian and German schools of his native city. When he
was twenty-one the family immigrated to America, arriving at Menno in 1885.
However, the Hezels remained only a year in that locality, moving to Campbell
County in 1886, where they located on Spring Creek.

The following year Mr. Hezel moved to Eureka where he opened a general
merchandise store. In 1888 he took in two partners, Jacob Sauter and Fred
Hepperle. The former remained in the partnership only a year and the latter
until 1892. The firm thereafter continued as C. J. Hezel & Co., the father,
Jacob Hezel, being the silent partner. It was a successful business, operating
under the name until 1897 when it was incorporated as "The Eureka Bazaar," a
concern which has since remained a leading business enterprise of Eureka.

        Christoph J. Hezel

In 1901, he severed his connections with the business life of Eureka, moving to
Aberdeen where he founded a business known as The Golden Rule Store. It was
located in the Ward hotel building and was purchased from T. F. Marshal. The
enterprise met with gratifying success under his management and was accounted,
in its day, one of the outstanding establishments of that city.

Disposing of his interests in the Golden Rule Store, Mr. Hezel founded the
Sherman Dry Goods Company with quarters in the Sherman hotel building. This
enterprise, also, prospered and continued with marked success until fire
destroyed the building in 1926. During his residence in Aberdeen Mr. Hezel was
active in other business enterprises as well as in public life. He was one of
the founders of the Dakota National Bank, serving on its board of directors.

While a resident of Eureka, he served on the city council two terms and was
also a member of the city council in Aberdeen several terms, serving as
chairman of that body for one term.

Mr. Hezel was married to Caroline Schamber, a daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Martin
Schamber, Sr., of Scotland, South Dakota, on September 11, 1892. Four children
were born to this union, namely, Mrs. Othilia Daulton of Aberdeen; Hugo, Elsie,
and Edgar, now residing in Los Angeles.

In 1927, Mr. Hezel suffered a paralytic stroke and retired from active
business, removing shortly thereafter to the milder climate in California. He
died in Los Angeles on January 20, 1934, and is buried in that city.

MRS. ELIZABETH ULRICH REUE

Mrs. Elizabeth Reue, a Eureka pioneer now living in Leola, South Dakota, has
many interesting facts to relate concerning pioneer days. She was born at
Lengerich, Westphalen, Germany, on January 13, 1854, and came to the United
States when she was thirty years old. On November 5, 1884, she married Rev.
Ulrich Reue, at Indianapolis, Indiana, with the Rev. Stern officiating. In
1887, her husband received a call from the German Reformed Church to come to
Dakota to do missionary work. He accepted the call and they came to Eureka in
October, 1887.

At that time Eureka was being built, and there were very few permanent houses.
The Reues ate their first lunch in a barn, which had to serve the purpose of a
home until one could be built.

Shortly after their arrival here, they moved ten miles north and spent their
first Dakota winter in one end of Henry Jahraus' two room sod shanty. The
Jahrauses lived in the other end.

Sometimes the nights seemed very long, because the oil in the lamps would give
out. There were few churches and schools at that time. Small churches were
organized and people would travel forty and sixty miles to attend services. The
Rev. Reue had no team, so the farmers came with bob-sleds, or just small
stoneboats to which a spring seat had been fastened, on which the pastor could
sit and cover up warmly with robes. He often traveled thirty or forty miles in
sub-zero weather, a week at a time, going from one charge to another to deliver
sermons.

In 1888 Rev. and Mrs. Reue filed on a pre-emption claim, on which they were
obliged to live for six months. They broke ten acres of land, dug a well, and
built a house, sixteen by eighteen feet.

In June, 1888, while Mrs. Reue was alone with her two sons, Calvin and Ted, a
cloudburst came. Mrs. Reue sat nervously, holding her sons, expecting to be
swept away any minute. A thunderstorm accompanied the rain. By the next morning
Spring Creek and all its branches had overflown, forming a vast body of water
resembling a sea. Rev. Reue, returning from mission work on the other side of
the creek with another minister, was helped across the stream by Fred Moos, a
neighbor of the Reue family, who hitched up a team of oxen and brought them
safely across. As an aftermath of the cloudburst, a mosquito plague arose. Day
and night the pests bothered man and beast. A flea plague followed and there
was no rest while these lasted.

In the fall of 1888, the Reues moved to their homestead and built a frame
house, eighteen by twenty feet in The small house was moved from the
pre-emption claim, and fastened to the new building, which resulted in what was
considered a very large house in those days.

Mr. and Mrs. Reue lived on their homestead for twelve years, and during all
that time had only two good crops.

In 1900 the Reue family moved to Leola, South Dakota, where Mr. Reue presided
as Clerk of Courts for twenty-two years. He was an important factor in the
building of the Leola Reformed Church.

Ruth, a daughter, served as School Superintendent of McPherson County from 1922
to 1926. The other children are T. B. Reue, Leola; Idelette Reue Lundquist, a
nurse, Leola; Julius Reue, merchant, Deadwood. Reverend Reue died in 1926. Ruth
Reue is now an instructress in San Diego, California.

JOHN ARNOLD SMITH

John Arnold Smith, well known and popular citizen of Eureka, was born in Le
Sueur County, Minnesota, August 22, 1862.

He arrived in McPherson County in September in 1886, when twenty-four years of
age. Two years later, April 10, 1898, he was married to Susan Hammersmith. The
Reverend N. J. Dahlmann performed the marriage ceremony. Eight children were
born: Anne (deceased); Joseph, Kansas City, Missouri; Mathew, Minneapolis,
Minn.; John, Minneapolis, Minn.; Virginia, Sioux Falls, S. Dak.; Nicholas,
Minneapolis, Minn.; Cleo, Minneapolis, Minn.; Carmen, Minneapolis, Minn.

Mr. Smith carne here as construction foreman when the Chicago, Milwaukee and
St. Paul railroad built into Eureka. He adopted this village as his future home
and lived here a greater part of his life.

He was at one time Deputy Sheriff of McPherson County and also served as Chief
of the Eureka Volunteer Fire Department. In the early part of 1900, he
conducted a saloon.

When Mr. Smith arrived in the county his financial circumstances were very
meager, but diligent toil and thrift overcame the obstacles which most of the
pioneers encountered.

From Eureka the family moved to Hillsview, South Dakota, residing there several
years, where Mr. Smith passed away in 1920; later the family moved to
Minneapolis.

Mrs. Smith and most of her family are still residents of that city.

ADAM OBENAUER

Adam Obenauer's parents, Friedrich and Johanna Singer Obenauer, both of German
descent, were natives of Hoffnungsthal, Bessarabia, Roumania, then a part of
Russia. Here he was born and resided until 1887, when he came to America.
Immediately upon arrival in McPherson County, he filed on a preemption claim.
He lived on the claim for the required twelve-month period, and then sold it to
pay the debt he had incurred to finance his trip to this country. A short time
later he filed on Section 35, Township 138, Range 72, in Detmold Township,
where he has since resided.

In 1890 he married Wilhelmina Weixel. A son and a daughter, John A. and Lydia,
were born. His wife died in March, 1898.

The following winter he married again. Thirteen children were born to this
union. They are: Emil, Maria, Bertha, George, Paulina, Emma, David, Henry,
Otto, Freida, Esther, Richard, and Theodore.

Because the farm income was not sufficient to support his family, Mr. Obenauer
worked in the grain elevators or coal yard every fall. He was very economical,
and did not use liquor. With the money he saved he bought more farm land, live
stock, and machinery. At one time he owned 1,800 acres of land.

Mr. Obenauer says his wife deserves much credit in making the farm home. During
the fall months he worked in town, she took care of the children and managed
the farm. In his opinion, wives and mothers of the pioneers are entitled to
equal honors with their husbands, for building up this country. The pioneer
women not only worked in the fields with the men, but they prepared the food,
made clothes, raised the children, and kept house for the family as well.

Mr. Obenauer is now seventy years old. His health is poor, but Mrs. Obenauer,
in spite of the hard farm work she has done, is well and active

        John A. Smith Saloon

JOHANN J. HARR

Among the most picturesque and colorful characters of Eureka pioneers was
Johann Jacob Harr. During the latter period of his life he was known both in
the community and outside as Judge Harr. Possessed of a good education from his
native land it was only natural that he should assume certain leadership in
many enterprises in the community. Although an ardent democrat, during his
entire career in this country, he never sought major political office. Active
in local political circles, his sole service in public office was as police
judge, which office gave him the title, "Judge."

        Mr. and Mrs. John J. Harr

After his retirement from active work he remained, by virtue of his vivid
personality, one of the characters of the community that lend color and charm.
His remarkable memory for men and events made him an oracle, whose opinion was
sought on all manner of subjects. Judge Harr had an extensive acquaintance not
only among his own countrymen, but was widely known throughout this section of
South Dakota.

A brief story of his life, published in the Northwest Blade and written by its
editor, sums up the train of events which will conceivably grow into legend.
The story follows:

Johann Jacob Harr was born in Neufreudental, Russia, in the year 1858. In his
youth he attended schools in Russia and upon reaching manhood, was employed as
a teacher of Russian and German schools in Russia, later he was traveling agent
for a Bible publishing company in that country. He was united in marriage in
October, 1876, to Anna Marie Kunz. They located for a year at Yankton, South
Dakota, then came to this vicinity and resided here since that time. As a young
man Mr. Harr was employed in various general stores in Eureka, including the
store of Martin Schamber, Sr., where he worked for many years until the time
that he homesteaded on a claim south of town, later selling this property and
buying another farm one-half mile west. They retired from the farm in 1909. Mr.
Harr also traveled for the Dakota Freie Presse for a number of years. Mr. and
Mrs. Harr were the parents of eleven children, three of whom died in infancy.
His wife preceded him in death a few years. The surviving children are: John F.
Harr, Stockton, California; Katherine Pietz, Eureka; Elizabeth Grosz, Tolstoy;
Theo. Harr, Miller, and Martha Temple, Marion, S. Dak. Two brothers also
survive: Daniel Harr, Eureka; Gottlieb Harr, Siberia, Russia; two sisters: Mrs.
Karolina Schmierer, Streeter, N. Dak.; Katharina Schweigert, Bismarck; eighteen
grand children: Irma and Herbert Stickel; Max, Leo, Walter, Linda and Anna
Harr; Ella Pietz Fergus; Verona, Hugo, Robert, Vera and Quentin Grosz; Elmer,
Raymond, Ann May and Ella Harr and Bernice Harr.

Mr. Harr was an ardent church worker and was the last of the charter members of
the Eureka Lutheran church and was reader of the sermons for years before a
regular minister was secured. He donated the plot of ground for the Lutheran
cemetery and was an untiring worker in any worthy cause. Mr. Harr was one of
the organizers of the Northwest German Farmers Mutual Insurance Company and
served in the capacity of secretary of the organization for fourteen years. He
was also justice of the peace for many years. He was an enthusiastic follower
of the democratic party during his entire life in this country. Mr. Harr was a
devoted husband and father.

GOTTFRIED G. WERRE

Gottfried G. Werre, who was born in Gluecksthal, South Russia, May 10, 1867, is
one of the men who was connected with the earlier business enterprises of
