Eureka. He had come to America with his parents in 1875, with Yankton as his
destination. The family remained there until 1886, when the elder Werre came to
McPherson County, establishing a store in the village of St. Petersburg, then
located near the present site of the Fair Grounds.

When Eureka was platted the following year the store was moved to a location on
Main Street now occupied by the Hamm Brewing Company buildings, at which place
the business was conducted for several years.

Gottfried Werre came to Eureka on October 10, 1888, bringing with him two
carloads of stock and machinery. He worked as a clerk for some time in his
father's store. The business was discontinued shortly after the father's death.

Mr. Werre was married to Friedericka Bertsch in 1891, the ceremony being
performed by the Reverend Ulrich Reue. After the Werre store was closed, he was
employed in various places in the city, later taking up work with the Quinton
Falls Nursery Company of Owatonna, a position he held for a long period of
years. In later years, he has represented the Rawleigh Company of Freeport,
Illinois, a connection he still maintains.

He has been active in public life, serving as alderman from the second ward. He
was also city marshal for one year in the administration of Mayor Fred
Hepperle.

        Mr. and Mrs. Gottfried G. Werre

Mr. Werre relates interesting stories of the service rendered by early day
stores. While Eureka was still the end of the railroad, many farmers were so
far distant from the city it required a journey of from two to four days for
them to reach market with their produce and to obtain supplies. As a result,
they remained in town overnight and often completed their trading late in the
evening. They would then put their bed rolls on the floor of the store,
catching a little sleep before resuming their journey at dawn the following
morning. It was an unconventional thing, but lodging their customers was a
rather common practice at the time.

The Werres became the parents of eight children: Lena, Hertha (Mrs. John C.
Mutschler), both deceased, the former passing away in July, 1936; Edwin,
manager of Thompson Yards, Oakes, N. Dak.; Theophil, Mt. Ayre, Ind.; Rheinie,
Eureka; Herbert, chiropractor, Fargo, N. Dak.; Eugene, teacher, Eureka; and
Mrs. Elsie Spitzer, Ashley, N. Dak.

The Werres reside in Eureka, their home being on west Main Street.

JAMES CLAYTON

In the year 1887, James Clayton left his father's farm in Illinois and came to
McPherson County. He located in Leola, and found employment with his brother,
A. G. Clayton, who had established a blacksmith shop in Leola.

In about 1892, James Clayton opened a blacksmith shop in Eureka, which he
operated until 1907. His shop was located on the present site of the Karl
Koerner harness shop. In the year 1907, Mr. Clayton entered politics and was
elected auditor of McPherson County, which office he held for four years. He
took up his residence in Leola again, and later was elected register of deeds,
in which capacity he served four years.

At present, he is conducting an abstract office, known as the Leola Abstract
Company.

ELIAL W. HINSDALE

One of the most interesting and unique characters of early Eureka history was
Elial W. Hinsdale. "He was an original, and I think the mold must have broken,"
comments the Hon. J. W. Parmley, life-long friend and early day companion of
Hinsdale. He settled at Roscoe in 1883, having come west with the great, but
then waning, tide of emigrants in search of free land. He filed on "a claim" a
mile southwest of Roscoe where he made his home until 1886, when the railway
extended its line northwest from Roscoe and established the towns of Hosmer,
Hillsview and Eureka, at which latter town he located permanently and became
one of its best known citizens.

Elial Wait Hinsdale, son of David Schuyler Hinsdale and Ann Julia Bostwick, his
wife, was born at Lisbon, New York, on July 3, 1858. His parents moved to the
village of Oxbow, New York, when Elial was about three years of age. It was
there he grew to manhood, attending the school in that village and helping his
father with the work about their farm. He died at Eureka on July 28, 1936, at
the age of 78 years and 15 days.

The first Hinsdale to come to America was Deacon Robert Hinsdale, who arrived
at Dedham, Massachusetts, from his home in England in 1637. This ancestor of
Elial Hinsdale was slain with his three sons at Bloody Brook, near Deerfield,
Massachusetts, in 1675.

        E. W. "Shorty" Hinsdale Pool Hall

Given to adventure and the great outdoors, the west beckoned him and in 1883 he
selected South Dakota as his permanent home. Railway building was in its
hey-day and he worked with a surveying crew of the Milwaukee while building its
many extensions west of Aberdeen. In the winter, he spent much time trapping on
Spring Creek and along the Missouri River, but Eureka was his permanent home,
where he built a house and occupied it until all alone and without
announcement, he went on his last great adventure. He never married. One of the
very few native Americans to reside in Eureka from its earliest history until
the end of the career, he was well esteemed and entrusted with two public
offices.

He was appointed City Surveyor in 1911, and in 1915 Police Justice. His proper
name was Elial but he was generally called "Lias" or "Shorty" as more familiar
and endearing terms.

He was a happy-go-lucky boon companion of all of his many acquaintances, and
would give his last dollar to a friend or a down-and-outer in distress. He was
always anxious to do something towards the development of the country.
Intensely interested in the conservation of water for the prairies, he often
visited the dam at Mina, S. Dak., as it was being built and made valuable
suggestions as to the planting of trees and beautifying the country adjacent to
the lake, which was to result from the dam impounding the water of the two
branches of Snake Creek.

He had a good education and was always industrious. He would tolerate a man of
mediocre ability but had no use for a lazy man, or one who shirked a duty.

It was often said that "Lins" was the black sheep of a prominent New York
family and heir to a considerable fortune. Early day residents remember that
for a time he received remittances from the estate of a very substantial nature
every month. It is also said there was much litigation in connection with the
estate and then for reasons no one ever knew, "Shorty's" remittances ceased. He
never spoke of the matter either in regret nor disappointment. In fact, he
never mentioned the matter to anyone.

The history of Eureka -- its adjacent territory, and of this section of the
northwest is not complete without recounting the life and many kindnesses of
one of earth's noble men -- Elial W. Hinsdale -- in much more extended form
than space will permit in Eureka's Golden Jubilee Historical souvenir.

        "Heaven is not reached by a single bound;
        We build the ladder by which we rise;
        From the lowly earth to the vaulted skies
        And mount the summit round by round."

PETER SCHENKENBERGER

Few details of the career of Peter Schenkenberger are available to Jubilee book
writers. The following meager facts were supplied by his daughter, Mrs. Bertha
Gould of Mobridge, South Dakota.

Mrs. Gould states her parents were married at Long Lake on February 15, 1887,
moving to Eureka soon after. Here he was identified with the business life of
the new town for many years, serving as alderman from the third ward in 1905.
During his residence here it is recalled that during the fire which destroyed
the Eureka flour mill, Mr. Schenkenberger at grave risk to his own life entered
the burning structure to rescue another man who would probably have died in the
fire had his rescuer been unsuccessful.

The Schenkenbergers were the parents of a family of nine children: Edward,
Leonore, Conrad, John Bertha, Emil, Edwin, Ruth and Marian. Two of the sons,
John and Emil, died after reaching maturity.

        Mr. and Mrs. Peter Schenkenberger

Mr. Schenkenberger died suddenly from a heart attack in 1928, while away on a
business trip. His wife died equally unexpectedly while returning to her home
from a Sunday afternoon visit with neighbors. Her death came in 1936.

Both Mr. and Mrs. Schenkenberger are buried in the Eureka City cemetery.

MRS. JOHN (CHRISTINE) BAUER, SR.

The life history of Mrs. Christine Bauer varies little from that of other
pioneers of the eighties. Her hardships were the same as those of other
pioneers in the Eureka territory; her good times and rejoicing, likewise were
much the same. She was born on Christmas Day in 1858, at Neufriedensthal,
Russia, and was married to John Bauer, Sr., in their native Odessa. They
arrived in McPherson County in 1887, and settled on a farm one and one-half
miles south of Eureka. All their worldly possessions at that time consisted of
a little household furniture, a yoke of oxen, a wagon, a plow, purchased at a
cost of $18.00, and a harrow for $7.00, only part of which was paid for in
cash. Their first farm structures were a sod house and barn.

Crop conditions in general were poor, Mrs. Bauer says. If it did happen that
crops were good, hail usually came to harvest them. However, the Bauers did not
rely on their small grain crop very much as the means of earning a livelihood.
They always had a herd of from 40 to 50 milk cows, from which they sold milk
and cream. Every day a ten-gallon can of cream was taken to Eureka and sold.
The price varied from 8 cents to 21 cents, the latter being the highest price
they received.

Mrs. Bauer recalls the Indian scare of the early nineties vividly. She states
that one night a huge throng of settlers from the Sutley territory in Campbell
County came to her home and said that the Indians were coming. This group
stayed at the Bauer home that evening and were made comfortable by placing
straw on the floor in lieu of mattresses. The next day the group proceeded to
Eureka and learned there was nothing to fear; the Indian rumor
was unfounded.

In 1912, the Bauer family moved to Java, South Dakota, where they have retained
a permanent residence since that time.

The names, addresses and occupations of the Bauer children are as follows:
Margaret Hipfner, the wife of a farmer, Elgin, N. Dak.; Kathryn Bohle, LacLeod,
Canada; Fred E. Bauer, merchant, Java, S. Dak.; John E. Bauer, Jr., merchant,
Java, S. Dak.; Evelyn Bauer, clerk, Java, S. Dak.; Ottilia Schlepp, Java, S.
Dak.; Ida Spiry, Java, S. Dak.; Martha Hansen, Aberdeen, S. Dak.; Hertha Bauer,
Aberdeen, S. Dak.; Hulda Bauer, was killed in an automobile accident at
Aberdeen, S. Dak., on the 31st of July, 1926.

FRIEDRICH REINER

One of the first claims taken in the Eureka vicinity was settled by the parents
of Friedrich Reiner when he was a lad seventeen years of age. The Reiners, born
of German ancestry, came to America in 1877, from Kassel, Russia, where
Friedrich was born on August 17, 1869.

For nine years the Reiner family farmed near Yankton, coming northward to
McPherson County in the spring of 1886. They came as far as Ipswich by rail and
from there on to Eureka in a wagon.

They located in what is now Jackson Township, filing on a claim in Section 11,
Township 125, Range 73. After building a sod shanty to house the family, sod
was broken for the seeding of flax. The flax seeded, there was but little work
to be done on the farm prior to harvest.

The prairie was sparsely settled, and it was practically impossible to find
employment anywhere in the community. However, in the late summer of 1886 the
surveyors for the Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul Railroad came to this
territory surveying for the future railroad. When the grade construction was
begun, Mr. Reiner was employed steadily until the roadbed was completed to
Hillsview.

Friedrich Reiner was married to Katharina Gross in 1892. They started farming
for themselves that year. Like other pioneers, they encountered difficult
times, but, by keeping up their undaunted courage, succeeded in building a
permanent home for their family.

Mrs. Reiner tells how hospitable the pioneers were. Those living in more roomy
sod houses were never lonesome, for weary travelers always made a point of
stopping to rest at the larger houses. Doors were never locked in those days.
Friends and strangers alike, had standing invitations to make themselves at
home.

        Mr. and Mrs. Friedrich Reiner

Five children were born to the Reiners. They are: Eva, Rosina, Julius, Edwin
and Herbert. They are members of the Congregational church, and all take an
active part in community affairs.

When Mr. and Mrs. Friedrich Reiner left their farm in 1931, they possessed
1,120 acres of land. Upon their retirement, they moved to Eureka, where they
have since resided.

PHILIP WEIXEL

One hot summer day in July, 1887, a young man of about thirty, was trudging
slowly along the dusty road to Aberdeen, South Dakota. He had been walking
since early morning, and was tired and hungry. The sun was nearing the horizon
as he came into the village of Westport, about eight miles north of Aberdeen,
and although it was not his destination, he decided to rest there for the
night. In the rear of one of the stores, he found an empty crockery barrel with
a little straw in it, into which he crept, supperless, and slept until morning.
At dawn he left this rude shelter and sat down on the high, wooden sidewalk in
front of the store. As he sat there pondering his situation, the merchant came
up and began to question him. He explained that he was looking for work and was
hungry and penniless. The merchant took him into his store, gave him a box of
soda crackers and a piece of sausage, and the young man, his hunger appeased,
went on his way with a grateful heart.

This was only one of the many hardships that Philip Weixel, one of the oldest
pioneers in this territory, had to endure. A native of Friedenstal, South
Russia, he was born on November 25, 1858. At the age of twenty-four, he was
married to Maria Iszler, in the Lutheran church of Old Arzis, with Reverend
Lesch officiating. Two years later he and his wife left their native land for
the New World with the following families: Gottlieb Joachim, Sr., Jacob
Pfeifle, Jacob Humann and Johann Lindemann.

The party arrived in Scotland, South Dakota, on March 27, 1884. Taking stock of
his financial resources, Mr. Weixel found that he owed Gottlieb Joachim, Sr.,
the sum of $34.00. This worried him somewhat, but he was consoled by the
thought that in this land of opportunity, he would soon find employment which
would enable him to repay the debt.

The first night was spent in Henry Sieler's store, and the next day the Weixels
were taken to the farm of their uncle, George Hirsch. Shortly afterward, they
were both hired by William Walz, a farmer, for a period of seven months at
$15.00 per month, earning $105.00. His wife was then employed by another
farmer, receiving $35.00 and a cow in payment, which enabled them to pay their
debt of $34.00 to Gottlieb Joachim, Sr.

Mr. Weixel now took up a preemption on eighty acres of land in Bon Homme
County, ten miles west of Scotland. He erected a sod house and barn, and in the
spring of 1885, broke up some of the land, putting in a crop of flax and corn.
He had bought a pair of young steers, for which he paid $100.00 and had broken
them during the previous winter He acquired his breaking plow by hauling rock
to town and trading them for the plow.

In the fall of 1885, he proved up his preemption and immediately mortgaged it
for $250.00, which sum of money made him feel quite wealthy. He still needed a
wagon, which he bought for $20.00. His live stock and equipment now consisted
of two cows, a pair of oxen, a wagon and a breaking plow.

In the early spring of 1886, he, together with Henry and Jacob Klein, decided
to locate farther north. Shipping their possessions by freight, they took the
train for Ipswich.

They unloaded their live stock and equipment during the night and early the
next morning set out in a northwesterly direction, arriving at the farm of
Jacob Pfeifle on April 12, 1886. Since the pasture had recently been burned by
a prairie fire, there was no available feed for their live stock. However,
Henry Ladner, one of the neighbors, still had plenty of hay, and they bought
all they needed from him.

Mr. Weixel now filed on a homestead and timber claim in Detmold Township, nine
miles northeast of Eureka. His first job was to dig a well, striking water at a
depth of eight feet. This well is still in use. A sod barn and dwelling were
then erected. The family lived in this house for thirteen years, after which it
was converted into a granary. The first year he broke up twenty-eight acres of
land, sowing sixteen acres to flax and the balance in corn, watermelons,
potatoes, etc., but the crop failed to mature because of a drouth. What little
hay could be made that summer had to be mowed with a scythe, and was hardly
sufficient to feed his seven head of live stock.

In the late summer of this year (1886), Mr. Weixel, with his neighbor, Michael
Pfeifle, set out eastward on foot, in search of employment. They were gone from
home for three weeks, suffering many privations, and finding employment for
only two and one-half days, each earning the sum of $3.25. During these two and
one-half days they worked with a threshing crew, and after a hard day's work
naturally looked forward to a good supper in the evening. Great was their
disappointment, however, when they received only a cup of tea and a handful of
soda crackers. Needless to say, this meal did not go very far with a couple of
husky young men, especially after enduring hunger for several weeks.

Winter was again approaching, and it was necessary to lay in a supply of flour
and buy seed for the next spring. Being unable to earn any money, Mr. Weixel
decided to borrow some. Accordingly, he drove to Leola with his neighbor,
Andreas Hoff, who owned a team of horses, and borrowed $65.00 from the bank in
Leola. He gave a mortgage on his pair of oxen, two cows, a heifer, two sheep
and all equipment, and signed a promissory note bearing interest at the rate of
four per cent payable every three months -- slightly more than sixteen per cent
per year.

The crop of 1887, producing a limited yield, it was again necessary to seek
employment elsewhere. It was on this trip that the incident related at the
beginning of this story took place. Near Aberdeen he was employed by a farmer
for three months, earning $85.00. He returned home in November, by way of
Roscoe, continuing to Eureka on the newly constructed Chicago, Milwaukee and
St. Paul railroad.

In 1908, in partnership with his son-in-law, Fred J. Orthwein, he began
operating a creamery in Long Lake, at the same time managing his farm. Four
years later, the creamery building was removed to Eureka, and the business
conducted here for another year.

The residents of this district some years ago elected Mr. Weixel County
Commissioner for McPherson County, and he served in that capacity for seven
years. His son, Jacob J., was alderman from his ward for three terms and was
elected mayor of the city of Eureka in 1933.

There were five children born to Mr. and Mrs. Weixel, namely: Mary, married to
George Wolff, a farmer near Wishek, N. Dak.; Jacob J., engaged in the grain
business in Eureka; Fredericka, married to Fred J. Orthwein, who died in 1929,
Mrs. Orthwein now residing in Wiley, Washington; Lydia, married to John B. Hoff
of Billings, Montana; Emilia, married to Jacob M. Goehring, a merchant of
Eureka.

Mr. Weixel, with his family, took up a residence in Eureka in 1910, where he
and his wife are now living.

ANDREAS KALLENBERGER

The biography of Andreas Kallenberger reads like that of the hero in one of
Horatio Alger's famous novels for boys. Born on the 26th of January, 1865, in
the village of Franzfeld, near Odessa, South Russia, at the age of twenty
years, and in company with a comrade by the name of Gottfried Schechterle, he
departed for America. The money for financing the trip was borrowed from his
comrade, and together they arrived at Scotland, South Dakota, on the 16th of
October, 1885. Here the comrades separated, Schnecterle having been paid the
amount of his loan with money borrowed from a brother-in-law, Andrew Wagner,
proceeded into Nebraska, while Kallenberger remained in Hutchinson County,
hoping to find work.

His first employer was John Pietz from whom he received $8.00 a month for farm
work on the approximate site of the present town of Tripp. This was not very
much so young Kallenberger looked elsewhere for work, and finally was employed
by a certain Lawrence Turner, near Menno, South Dakota, at a salary of $13.00
per month.

In June, of 1887, at the suggestion of his brother-in-law, Andrew Wagner, they
proceeded northward by team to view lands that were being opened for homestead
entry. With all his worldly possessions in a newly purchased trunk, and with no
money in his pocket, he and his brother-in-law arrived at the farm home of Fred
Weisz, after seven days of travel.

Being pleased with the condition of the land, Wagner located on a homestead,
three miles east of the present city of Eureka. Both men were desirous of
having their father and father-in-law, Philip Kallenberger, come over to the
new country, so they made a loan of $300.00 on this land and sent it to him to
finance the trip across the ocean. Wagner then returned to Scotland, while
young Kallenberger remained on the claim. A shanty of timbers and willows was
soon constructed, in which, with a mattress of hay, and an oven made of stone
and clay, he tried to make himself comfortable. Being without funds, he earned
his board and room by helping Fred Weisz with his hay-making and harvesting
during this time

In October, 1886, young Andreas secured work with the contractor who was
constructing the grade for the Milwaukee railroad northward from Roscoe. For
his labors he received $1.50 per day, 50 cents of which was taken for board.
After thirty-six days of work, the job was completed on the 19th of November,
1886. To collect their pay, it was necessary for the men to go to Roscoe.
Therefore, a band of men of Irish, Norwegian and German nationalities, and in
the dead of winter with several wagon loads of scrapers and tools that had been
used in building the grade, started for Roscoe. It was very cold, and by night
the men became aware of the fact that they had lost the trail. They shouted at
the tops of their voices but no one heard them. Finally one of them suggested
that they pray. Then, really and truly, an awe inspiring spectacle took place
on the prairies of Dakota. These men, strong and hardened pioneers, men of
various creeds and nationalities, knelt in silent prayer to their God. As if in
answer to their prayers, a farmer heard their shouts, and approached with a
lantern fastened on a stick which he held high over-head. These men followed the
light and were guided to this man who took them into his home and gave them
shelter and food. Some of the men froze their ears, cheeks, hands and feet
slightly, but in the morning all of them were able to proceed on to Roscoe. At
Roscoe, they received their money and started on the return trip home, which
lasted two days, the first night of which was spent at the Christ Aman home
close to Hillsview.

The winter of 1886-1887 was one of heavy snow. Travel was difficult. However,
young Andreas made four trips to Roscoe and one to Ipswich that winter, on
foot, for the purpose of bringing back 50 pounds of flour each time. During
that winter, hay and grass were used as fuel. The years following, up to the
spring of 1889, were spent in working for various people. In the spring of
1888, young Andreas worked for Jacob Pietz, doing spring work for 40 cents a
day.

In July, he worked for Fred Aman, making hay for 60 cents a day. In the latter
part of July of that year, he and Philip Weixel went to Leola to seek
employment. Being unsuccessful there, they proceeded on to Aberdeen with
another man named Jacob Reiner, where they finally found employment on the farm
of one Jacob Colbert, who lived about seven miles southeast of Aberdeen. Mr.
Kallenberger's wages at this place were $25.00 per month during the summer and
$10.00 per month during the winter. He stayed here for about two years, being
well liked by his employer. Most of the money that he earned, he sent to his
father.

In 1889, he returned to Eureka, where on the 24th of March, 1889, he was
married to Friedericka Hoff, daughter of Jacob Hoff and his wife, Frieda. The
Hoffs were natives of Kassel, in South Russia, a village where so many other
pioneer settlers of the Eureka community first saw light of day. The young
couple immediately filed on a homestead of their own, and Andreas Kallenberger
proceeded to supply himself with the necessary farming equipment. He purchased
an ox team for $100.00; an old wagon for $15.00; a breaking plow for $18.00;
and a cow for $20.00, and started on an enviable career as a farmer. His first
crop yielded only 60 bushels, about one bushel to the acre. This made it
necessary for him to seek employment elsewhere than on his own farm.
Consequently, with a team of horses he departed for Ashley, North Dakota, where
he found work on a threshing rig for $2.00 a day. He worked about a month,
earning $60.00, with which he returned to his homestead, and waited for the
coming spring.

Concerning farm prices at that time, Mr. Kallenberger states that eggs sold for
5 cents and 6 cents a dozen and butter sold for 5 cents a pound. Wheat during
the years 1890 and 1891 sold from between 40 cents and 50 cents a bushel. He
states that 1892 brought an exceptionally fine yield; the grain running from 20
to 25 bushels an acre, and bringing from 60 cents to 70 cents a bushel.

In 1895, he undertook the raising of cattle. His first herd consisted of eight
cows which he bought with $200.00 borrowed from Charley Pfeffer. They sold the
butterfat and were soon able to save a little money. Already possessing a
homestead and a tree claim in 1896 he borrowed $600.00 from Mrs. Charley
Pfeffer with which he bought his first land at a price of $600.00 a quarter. In
1897, he had accumulated enough money to pay his debt to Charley Pfeffer, but
the latter advised him to keep the money and buy more cattle, which he did. He
purchased 16 more cows at $15.00 a head and now had a herd of 23 good cattle.
In 1899, he sold 18 head for $30.00 a piece. With this money, and other money
that he had saved, he paid what he owed on his cattle and land and was out of
debt.

In 1896, Kallenberger built a small frame house and barn. In 1901, he bought
another quarter of land for $800.00; in 1905, two quarters for which he agreed
to pay 3,000 bushels of wheat in five years; in 1911, he bought two quarters
for $7,000.00; and in 1916, he bought another quarter for $2,800.00. In 1920,
he also purchased two quarters near Isabel, South Dakota, for $5,200.00. This
shows the upward trend of land values at that time. He built a house and barn
on the Isabel land in 1920, at a cost of $2,000.00, which he presented to his
son, John. In 1923, he built a house, barn and dug a well at a cost of
$2,300.00 which he gave to his son Edwin. In 1925, he gave his own farm to his
son Ruben, and built a neat bungalow for himself at a cost of $3,300.00
directly south of the old homestead. This he later gave to his son Ernst.

Mr. and Mrs. Kallenberger state that they have enjoyed good and bad times
together. However, they say they feel that better times will soon be here
again. Mr. Kallenberger attributes his success to planning ahead. He told this
writer that he always kept enough seed to assure a supply for the following
year, if the present crop failed. This advice he passes on to the farmers of
today. This old couple is now living in a comfortable home, three miles east of
Eureka.

The Kallenbergers have seven children, all of whom are living today. Their
names and addresses are as follows: Ida Hoff, farm wife, Eureka, S. Dak.; John
Kallenberger, farmer, Isabel, S. Dak.; Magdalena Stock, farm wife, Eureka, S.
Dak.; Edwin Kallenberger, farmer, Eureka, S. Dak.; Ruben Kallenberger, farmer,
Eureka, S. Dak.; Claudia Preszler, farm wife, Medina, N. Dak.; and Ernest
Kallenberger, farmer, Lodi, California.

THE GUHIN FAMILY

While James W. Guhin must be accounted one of the real pioneers of Eureka, his
death at the age of 43, has left more vivid memories of his family in the city
than of himself. Mr. Guhin came into Eureka on the first train entering the new
town to open the station for the Milwaukee railroad. Soon after becoming
established in the new town, he was married to Margaret Doyle, a native of
Prairie Du Chien, Wisconsin, at Ipswich, on October 4, 1887, to be exact. He
remained with the railroad company until 1896 when he engaged in the grain,
wood and coal business for himself. He had barely gotten well under way in the
new venture when he died while on a visit to his native city, Maple Grove,
Wisconsin, where he had been born in June, 1863.

Surviving him was his wife and eight children, the oldest of whom was William
G. Guhin, then a lad of seventeen. The brave struggle of the widowed mother and
the fatherless children to win a livelihood somehow enlisted the interest and
sympathy of the community. William took over the management of the business and
the family continued to reside in Eureka until 1910. Speaking of their memories
of Eureka, William Guhin, now residing in Aberdeen, said to a Jubilee Book
writer: "The Guhin family are happy to take advantage of an opportunity to
express its deep and grateful appreciation of the kindness of the good people
of the entire Eureka community for their assistance at a time when they were
sorely tried. The pleasant relations that existed during our entire residence
there and the interest they have taken in us since leaving there are one of the
finer experiences that have come to us."

        Mrs. Margaret Guhin

Upon leaving Eureka the Guhins made their home in Aberdeen and have since
resided there, except that two members of the family have removed to other
parts of the country, namely, Clem J., who resides in North Platte, Nebraska,
and Mrs. Helen Bowler of Los Angeles.

Residing in Aberdeen, in addition to William, are Frank E., who is a member of
the city commission; James, George, Donald and Mary. The mother, Mrs. Margaret
Doyle Guhin, died in Aberdeen on September 23, 1917, in the fiftieth year of
her life.

JOHN E. REAGAN

John E. Reagan, pioneer merchant of Eureka, was born at Ripon, Wisconsin, on
the 4th of August, 1857. As a young man of thirty, he proceeded westward.
Having knowledge of the fact that large and select tracts of land were open for
homestead in McPherson County, he filed on the Southwest Quarter of Section 35,
in Township 127, Range 73.

        Reagan and Hooper Exchange Bank

However, the life of young Reagan was not to be that of a farmer. The new town
of Eureka just a few rods from his homestead, was growing as if by magic.
Realizing the advantages of getting an early start, Mr. Reagan and F. H. Hooper
entered the general merchandise business in the new town in 1887 under the name
of Reagan & Hooper. The business was conducted in conjunction with a deputyship
which he held under Frank Stockton, the first postmaster of Eureka. In April of
1888, Stockton resigned, and John Reagan received the appointment as chief
postmaster.

Mr. Reagan was quite active in the civic affairs of the new town. In 1892, he
was one of the first aldermen elected to serve on the city board, after its
incorporation in 1892. He also served as mayor of the city from the year 1896
to 1898, and also represented McPherson County in the legislature for a certain
length of time.

Mr. Reagan's original homestead being adjacent to the original town-site of
Eureka, he saw the advantage of having certain portions of it platted and added
to the city. Consequently in 1889, Reagan's Addition was made a part of the
city. Since that time, various other portions of the old homestead have been
surveyed, platted and added to the city under the names of various additions.
The present city lake and park are located on the old Reagan homestead.

In June of 1888, he was married to Miss Kittie G. Pickett. She died shortly
after their marriage, leaving one son, E. P. Reagan, surviving. This son is now
a salesman living in Minneapolis, Minnesota.

Not being content to live the life of a bachelor, John Reagan married again,
this time to Gertrude K. Knickerbocker, a daughter of Mr. and Mrs. George C.
Knickerbocker, pioneer hotel owners in Eureka. His second wife died in 1898,
and after that Mr. Reagan never re-married. This union was blessed with three
daughters, who are still living today. They are: Mrs. Georgia Keim of Fargo, N.
Dakota; Mrs. Grace Swanson of Aberdeen, S. Dak.; and Mrs. Genevieve Anderson,
also of Aberdeen, S. Dak. John E. Reagan is still well and active today,
spending most of his time with his daughters in Aberdeen.

MARK M. SMITH

Mark M. Smith was still an infant when he first came to McPherson County. He
was born in Manville, North Dakota, on March 13, 1884. His parents, Daniel and
Elizabeth Smith, originally came from Ireland, from where they migrated to
Canada.

At the time they left Canada, the family consisted of parents and seven
children, five boys and two girls. One son, Patrick, remained in Canada with
relatives, and did not join his parents until some years later.

The family homesteaded on land about twelve miles southwest of Eureka. This
locality later became known as the "Black Shack" settlement, due to the dark
color of the sod houses erected there. When the Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul
Railroad Company extended their line to Eureka, Daniel Smith, the father, was
employed in the building of the road. He continued to work for the railroad
company until his death, which occurred in 1888. He was the first man to be
buried in Eureka, and since there were no undertakers in Eureka at that time, a
coffin had to be shipped in.

Shortly before his death, Daniel Smith and his family removed to Eureka, where
they lived in the "section house" erected by the railroad company. After the
death of her husband, Mrs. Smith continued to live there for a time, conducting
a boarding house in order to make a living for her family.

In 1890, Mrs. Smith erected a hotel building known as the "Commercial Hotel,"
on the site of the present "Cottage Rest," and, the business prospering, an
addition was put up later. This building was eventually acquired by John Smith,
who moved part of it across the street and remodeled the remaining part into a
dwelling. These buildings still stand today, one occupied by the "Cottage Rest"
and the other by the Goehring & Beck store.

When Mark was still a young boy, his mother retired from the hotel business,
and it became necessary for him and his brothers to find employment. At the age
of fourteen, and while still attending school, he started to work at the Koch &
Penski drug store. After leaving school, he found a full time job at the
restaurant of C. L. Parkhurst, who also bought cream, butter and eggs. Mr.
Parkhurst sold the first cream separators in this locality, and was the first
to buy cream separated by machinery.

After this job, came employment with the Red Wing Butter and Egg Company, and
when the McPherson County Creamery was built, Mark worked there as a cream
tester. This creamery building was located in the southern part of Eureka, and
part of it, now used for dwelling purposes, still stands.

In the year 1909, Mr. Smith opened his own cream station, and continued in this
business for about 25 years.

About two years after the death of his mother, which occurred in 1908, he was
married to Louise Weber. There was one child born to this union -- Gerald, who
has taken up civil engineering as a career.

Mr. Smith has been active in local politics. In 1932, he was elected alderman
of his ward, and at present is serving his second term in that capacity.

In the year 1928, Mr. Smith opened an ice cream and confectionery parlor, in
which business he is now engaged.

WILLIAM ENZE

William Enze was one of the youngsters to come to the Eureka community in its
earliest days and to share in the colorful and picturesque experiences that
came to many of the active men in that period. He was a native of Alt
Freudenthal, South Russia, having been born there March 20, 1872, according to
the Russian calendar. Translated into the calendar in use in this as well as
most European countries, it would be about eight days or so earlier. At any
rate, Enze says he has celebrated March 12 as his birthdate.

He came to this country with his parents, Christoph and Philabena Becker Enze,
in 1883, with Scotland, Dakota Territory, as their destination. In the summer
of 1887, the family decided they could better their fortunes farther north and
started the trek toward Campbell County. William Enze was then about fifteen
years old.

As they approached the present site of Eureka, Enze remembers the railroad
graders were at work about three miles south of the site of the new town. There
were lots of horses and mules, he recalls. A sort of dug-out in the side of a
hill, where the animals were sheltered in rough weather, has left traces that
may still be seen, he declares.

There was no town on the site of Eureka -- only three or four small shacks that
looked like claim shanties. Among the men owning the "stores" he says, were
Jacob Liedle, Billy Bramaier and "Little Serr."

The Enzes did not remain at Eureka but pushed on farther northwest, locating on
a homestead in Campbell County near Spring Creek. But young William did not
take kindly to farming. He had already worked at the brick layer and stone
mason trade and took this up. One of the first places of employment was the
Indian school then built on the west side of the Missouri River not far from
their homestead as the crow flies.

Coming to Eureka, he followed his trade largely, although employed in other
lines at intervals. He recalls having been employed by Walker and Israel in the
construction of the Eureka City Hall. He remembers, too, he built a chimney for
Casper Hatz when the latter erected his store.

Being young and energetic, he was among those who sought more activity than was
connected with the earning of a livelihood. He was a member of the ball team,
the band and orchestra, and above all, the Fire Department.

He described to a Jubilee Book writer, an incident in the organization of the
Fire Department and its first call to fight a fire. The irrepressible Charley
Pfeffer was chief. It was a winter night with deep snow when the chief had
perfected the organization and there was a membership of twenty-five Chief
Charley Pfeffer was so well pleased that he invited the group to be his guests
at a near-by rendezvous where brewage and vintages were sold. No sooner had the
group reached the climax in the celebration than the fire bell called them to
duty.

They rushed out and could see the flames shooting high in the sky of the dark
winter night near the present site of the Lutheran church. Away they went
dragging their hose carts through the deep snow, headed by Chief Charley, in
the direction of the conflagration. Arriving, they found a huge crockery barrel
and a small mountain of wooden boxes on a vacant lot, burning furiously. They
extinguished the fire with a fine display of efficiency in the art of fire
fighting. It was not until the next day that they finally learned that a group
of women, aided and abetted by a couple of draymen, arranged the conflagration.
The joke played on them was taken in good part, even though there was no joke
in dragging heavy hose carts through deep snow to a fire, which would have
burned itself out harmlessly.

"Bill" Enze's recollections of similar situations and amusing incidents would
fill several pages of this book. Space will permit only the mention of a few
facts of his life story. He was married to Carolina Webber in 1897, the Rev.
Mr. Bauer of the Reformed church performing the ceremony. They made their home
in Eureka until 1927 when they moved to Aberdeen and have resided there since
that time.

JOHN KALLENBERGER

As a lad of thirteen, and in response to the solicitations of an older brother,
Andreas, who had preceded him to America, John Kallenberger, accompanied his
parents, Mr. and Mrs. Philip Kallenberger, to this country. The family arrived
at Scotland, Dakota Territory, in 1886, and after remaining there for two
months, the father headed northward for McPherson County, leaving young John
and the rest of the family in the southern part of the territory. It was here
that Mr. Kallenberger found his first job. His employer was a man by the name
of Carl Hein and he was paid $25.00 for herding cattle for a term of one year.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Kallenberger

In 1887, he accompanied his brother, Jacob Kallenberger, who is now residing at
Marlene, Washington, and his sister, Christine, who is now the wife of David
Kurle of Eureka, to a new home in McPherson County. The railroad had just
recently been completed, and the trip was made by train. He remained with his
parents for one year, and then sought work elsewhere. He found work with John
Handel, a farmer who resided four miles west of Eureka, with whom he stayed for
one year, earning $50.00. He then returned home, and in 1891 was offered a job
with Jacob Colbert, who resided about eight miles southeast of Aberdeen. Young
John stayed in his employ for about fourteen months, and was paid at a rate of
$125.00 a year. He then again returned home and worked with his father until
the time of his marriage. This occurred on the 9th of December, 1899, when he
was united in marriage with Miss Magdalena Goehring. The wedding ceremony was
performed by the Reverend Mr. Fisher of the Evangelical Church.

In 1914, he moved to Eureka where he entered the field of private business. His
first business associates were Andrew Ottenbacher and Karl G. Kafter, a
partnership known under the name of the Golden Rule, Inc. This firm conducted a
general merchandise business for about three years, in the building which now
houses the Quality Hardware Store; after which John Kallenberger purchased the
interests of the other partners and conducted the business as sole owner.
Reinhart Harr later purchased an interest in the store and they conducted the
business jointly for another year before moving their store into the Eureka
Bazaar and limiting their business to the sale of groceries. The old building
was rented out for garage purposes.

After five years, these partners sold their interests to Schanzenbach and
Humann. Mr. Kallenberger then entered into the hardware business with George G.
Neuharth and son, John G. G. Neuharth, who later became his son-in-law. After
three years, he sold his interest to Gottfried Neuharth, a brother of John G.
G. Neuharth, and once more established himself in the general merchandise
business. This time he purchased the interest of L. E. Balliet in the Balliet &
Goehring store, and the firm name was changed to Goehring & Kallenberger. After
fifteen months of successful operation, his interests were sold to Rueben Beck.
His last business enterprise was the erection of a filling station on State
Highway No. 10. After operating this for eighteen months, he sold out to the
Sterling Oil Company. In 1930, he accepted a position in the Quality Hardware
Company and still holds this position.

John Kallenberger has held several offices of trust in local government. He
served on the school board of Stickel Township for a number of years, while
living on the farm. In 1915, he was elected to represent the first ward on the
city council of the City of Eureka.

Mr. and Mrs. Kallenberger are the parents of seven children, one of whom died.
The living children, their names, occupations, and addresses are as follows:
Magdalena Neuharth, house-wife, Eureka, S. Dak.; Hildegarde Lend, wife of a
minister, Tacoma, Washington; John T. Kallenberger, Bowdle, S. Dak.; Ida
Kallenberger, saleslady, Eureka, S. Dak.; Herbert Kallenberger, rural school
teacher, Eureka, S. Dak.; and Lillian Kallenberger, rural school teacher,
Eureka, S. Dak.

GEORGE BECK

The poor crops of the past few years have nothing on those George Beck
experienced during his early pioneering days back in 1885. After seeding ten
acres of flax by hand during his first year in the Eureka vicinity, the crop
was of such poor quality that even the few hungry chickens and a pig on the
farm refused to eat what the sun and the elements had destroyed.

George Beck, son of George Beck, Sr., and Katharina Wall, was born July 28,
1858, in Hoffnungstahl, South Russia. On February 10, 1883, young George
married Elizabeth Steinbach in Russia and arrived in America the following year
in October.

The Becks remained in the Yankton territory for one winter before venturing
northwest to Campbell County during the spring of 1885 where they took a
preemption and a tree claim; later on they homesteaded on another claim.

During the first year of settlement they purchased two oxen in partnership with
Gustav Trautman in Eureka. Their only farm equipment consisted of a plow and a
drag; they seeded by hand and built a sod house -- and the crop result of that
year's labor was even spurned by the chickens and a pig!

Mrs. Beck died on May 9, 1896. The children born to this marriage were:
Christian Beck, who now lives 15 miles southwest of Eureka in Campbell County;
George Beck, now at Schuler, Sask., Canada; Gottlieb Beck, living 10 miles west
of Eureka; Christina, wife of John C. Schick, living eight miles southwest of
Eureka. Two other children died in infancy.

        Mr. and Mrs. George Beck

Mr. Beck re-married on July 9, 1896. The bride, Charlotte Steinbach, was the
sister of Mr. Beck's first wife. Nine children were born from this marriage,
two of whom died -- one in infancy and the other at the age of eighteen. The
following children from this second marriage are: Anna Maria, wife of Gustave
Metzger, Campbell County; Elizabeth, wife of David Fischer, Campbell County;
Friedrich Beck, Campbell County; Emma, wife of Adolph Ehrman, Eureka; Gustav
Beck, Eureka; Ruben Beck, Helena, Montana; Matilda, wife of Eduard Schumacher,
Eureka.

JOHN PIETZ, SR.

Prominently connected with the political and civic development of the city of
Eureka is the life of John Pietz, Sr. To him goes the honor of having devoted
more years to public service than any other person in the history of the city.
At various times, he occupied the office of county commissioner from the fifth
district, served as county treasurer, represented McPherson County in the state
legislature at Pierre, and served on the city council of the city of Eureka,
both as an alderman and as a mayor. Mr. Pietz was born on the 13th of April,
1885, in Kassel, South Russia. In 1875, he was united in marriage with Barbara
Straile, also a resident of his native Kassel.

One year after his marriage, he and his wife, in the spirit of adventure, and
with the hope of advancing their own interests, departed for America. Their
destination upon their arrival in this country was Hutchinson County, where the
young couple remained for ten years, acquiring several tracts of
land.

Ten years later, again answering the call of the pioneer, they headed northward
into McPherson County, in search of more land. Finding some very fine looking
land about eighteen miles east of the present city of Eureka, the Pietzs filed
on the same for homestead purposes. Shortly after their arrival into the new
country, the Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul Railway was extended northward,
and the then new city of Eureka was growing as if by magic. Realizing this, and
feeling that the life of a farmer was too tame for him, John Pietz moved his
family to Eureka. Better educational facilities for his children was another
reason that prompted Mr. Pietz to
give up farming.

John Pietz's business debut in Eureka was made as employee, and later as a
partner of the firm of Brandt & Mehlhaff, who were operating a general
mercantile store. Later he purchased the mercantile business of August Schmidt,
and operated the same under his own name on the present site of the Red Owl
Store. Still later, he purchased the Eureka Roller Mill from Charles Pfeffer
and owned and operated the same until 1914, when he sold it to F. W. Homeyer.
After purchasing the mill, he sold his store to Fred
Preszler.

In 1916, his wife died leaving him a widower. The following year he married
Maria Kundert, who passed away in November, 1936. At the present time, he is
living at the Good Samaritan Home in Eureka.

        Mr. John Peitz, Sr.

Thirteen children were born to Mr. Pietz as a result of his first marriage,
five of whom are now dead. There were no children in the second marriage. The
names and addresses of his children are: Regina Schock, Portal, Canada;
Gottlieb Pietz, Portland, Oregon; Henry Pietz, Eureka, S. Dak.; Emil Pietz,
Medicine Hat, Alberta, Canada; Fred Pietz, Linton, N. Dak.; Christina Kundert,
Lodi, California; John J. Pietz, Eureka, S. Dak.; Barbara Hoff, Wishek, N. Dak.

CHARLES PFEFFER

The name Charles Pfeffer is one that must always remain an important one in the
annals of the Eureka community. Coming to the new town as townsite agent for
the Milwaukee railroad, he was an important factor in the development to the
community from 1887 until his removal to the Pacific coast shortly after the
new century came in. He was implement dealer, banker, miller and mayor of the
city during his residence here.

Previous to coming to Eureka in 1887 he was engaged in the general merchandise
and implement business in Ipswich. Immediately after the Milwaukee railroad had
established service in Eureka he opened a farm implement business on the east
side of the railroad track, combining the sale of implements with his duties as
townsite agent. His restless energy sought further outlet and he established
the Bank of Eureka in which he placed Wm. Bramaier as manager. In 1888 he
established the Eureka Roller Mills, the enterprise which probably was closest
to his heart.

Probably through his banking interests, he early became a land-owner, adding to
his holdings as the years went by until he is said at one time to have owned
about 200 quarter sections of land. These real estate holdings were, however,
of more or less temporary nature, as at the time he left for the Pacific coast
most of them had been sold. Of the remainder he donated several quarter
sections to schools of the county and the city schools of Eureka were also
remembered.

While he was perhaps a bit too imperious in manner and often too abrupt and
blunt in speech to become generally beloved, he was none-the-less generally
popular and looked up to as a leader in the commercial and political life of
the city. In fact he was known, especially in adjacent communities as "Der
Russlander Vater," a term which was not always accepted by his friends and
neighbors as being entirely deserved. However, his contribution to the early
development of the community has been generously recognized and a street in
Eureka is named in his honor.

        Bank of Eureka

Charles Pfeffer inherited a considerable sum of money upon the death of a
relative, but his energy and business acumen increased it. He accumulated a
fortune, which was once estimated at a million dollars, a figure probably
considerably exaggerated, but reliable sources indicate that he was a rich man
when he moved to the Pacific coast, much of his wealth being in bonds and
stocks of recognized worth. While he profited in the transactions, it is
admitted that the large majority of people to whom he loaned money profited as
well. He is credited with making few loans that did not return a mutual profit,
which is the real test of a financier.

        1888 -- Eureka Roller Mill -- Pfeffer and Bramaier

In the early years of the present century Charley Pfeffer disposed of his
business interests in Eureka, and he had already sold the greater part of his
farm lands. Just why he decided to move to the west coast has never been
learned. He told friends that he planned to retire from active business, but it
is known he acquired extensive business interests in his new home.

As Eureka prepares to celebrate its golden jubilee anniversary its older
citizens recall with a certain wistfulness the stocky figure of Charley
Pfeffer. They are reminded of his massive head, which required a hat of
dimensions not usually stocked. Likewise they tell of him wearing size 20
shirts, which suggests, with due allowance for such exaggeration as the passing
of time brings, that he was of substantial proportions. They remember him best
not at a desk but along the streets and around the flour mill talking with
farmers and other visitors to the city.

They remember him as mayor of the city for the term beginning in 1892 and again
in 1898, promoting various improvements he considered necessary or desirable.
They recall how he thrived on opposition and his smug satisfaction when his
ideas triumphed. Charley Pfeffer, to all who knew him, is still a vital,
forceful and dynamic memory though he has been absent a quarter of a century
and went on the long trail in 1923.

Details of his early life, as to date of birth, arrival in America and his
activity previous to coming to Ipswich are lacking. It is known that he was
born in Germany as was his wife, and that he found a great deal of pleasure in a
trip to his native land after he had acquired a competence. On the same journey
he traveled in Russia and visited relatives and friends of many of his Eureka
customers and acquaintances in and near Odessa.

The Pfeffers had no children, but adopted a daughter, Hazel, who is now Mrs.
William P. Mattai and resides in Tacoma, Washington. Since moving from Eureka
they lived in Tacoma and both died there. Charley Pfeffer died in 1923, in an
automobile accident suffered when his car overturned. Mrs. Pfeffer, who was
riding with him was seriously injured, never fully recovering and passing away
the following year, 1924.

W. S. SIEGELE

W. S. Siegele, born in Germany, on January 6, 1867, lived in the southern part
of the state prior to his coming to McPherson County. He came to America as a
lad of nine years of age.

He married Friedericka Holzwarth on November 20, 1890, the ceremony being
performed by the Reverend Mr. Zimmerman, pastor of the Lutheran Church. The
wedding took place at Jacob Heiser's home in Campbell County. Their honeymoon
trip was surely novel, if not so pleasant. That night a rumor spread that the
Indians, with Sitting Bull as their leader, were coming to wreak havoc among
the whites. By four o'clock in the morning the entire countryside was rushing
for Eureka. Mr. Siegele and his bride were among those who sought safety in town

        Mr. and Mrs. W. S. Siegele

He relates several incidents of that Indian scare. One man from near Spring
Creek slipped his coat and belt on over his night clothes and rushed to town.
The next day he was obliged to walk around in that attire until he went back
home. Some of the people threw their meat and valuables into the wells so the
Indians wouldn't find them. One farmer fleeing frantically, hit a stone pile
with his wagon and broke the axle. The whole family cowered on the stone pile,
expecting the Indians to descend upon them at any minute. Another farmer was in
such a hurry when he harnessed his team of mules, that, when he got them
hitched to the wagon, the harness was so tangled he could not drive the team.

Mr. Siegele followed various occupations, such as farming, butchering, painting,
general merchandise, grain buying, and at one time was in the saloon business.

The Siegeles now reside at Herreid, South Dakota, where Mr. Siegele and his
son, Arthur, operate an off-sale liquor store.

Mr. and Mrs. Siegele have two daughters and three sons: Lydia Robbins, Herreid,
S. Dak.; Bertha Boessler, Livermore, Iowa; Arthur Siegele, Herreid, S. Dak.;
Ervin Siegele, insurance agent, Aberdeen, S. Dak.; and Herbert Siegele,
principal of schools, Blunt, S. Dak.

KONRAD TRAUTMANN

Konrad Trautmann, with his wife, Katharina, and three children emigrated from
Russia in 1886. They arrived at Freeman, in Dakota Territory, in October of
that year, and remained over the winter with Mrs. Trautmann's uncle, Christian
Delzer.

They purchased two oxen, a cow, breaking plow and wagon, which exhausted their
financial resources. In March of the following spring, they loaded their live
stock and equipment into a freight car and took the train for Roscoe, then the
end of the railroad line. Here the oxen were hitched to the wagon and the
journey northward begun. They were accompanied by Christian Oster, with his wife
and three children -- the Oster family driving their own ox team.

On the first day of their trip, which was to be a very arduous one, they
covered a distance of seventeen miles, and passed the night at the farm of
Christian Aman. The weather was stormy and snowbanks as high as a railroad car
were still in evidence. The journey was resumed the next morning and progressed
nicely until Mr. Trautmann's wagon became stuck in a snowbank and the oxen were
unable to budge it. Christian Oster then decided to drive on, hoping to reach a
farm and summon help. While the Trautmann family sat waiting in their wagon, it
gradually grew dark, when suddenly they saw a light in the distance. Picking up
her youngest child, Mrs. Trautmann started walking toward it. After much
difficulty she came to the Mehlhaff farm and found Christian Oster had already
arrived there. Mr. Mehlhaff then took his team of oxen, pulled out the stalled
wagon and brought the rest of the Trautmann family to his home.

After spending the night at the Mehlhaff farm, they continued their trip on the
following morning, only to get stuck in another snowbank after driving about a
mile. Friedrich Aman, a farmer nearby, seeing their difficulty, came up to them
and offered assistance. They finally decided it was useless to continue with
the wagon, and after leaving some of their live stock and equipment at Mr.
Aman's farm, they borrowed his stoneboat and again started out. After driving a
short distance they came to a creek where the oxen had to be unhitched and
driven across. Mr. Trautmann and his wife then put on the yoke and pulled the
stoneboat, with the children on it, across the frozen creek.

On the following day they arrived at the home of Mrs. Trautmann's brother,
Stephan Delzer, where the family remained while Mr. Trautmann returned the
stoneboat to its owner and brought back his wagon and other property. They
finally reached their destination, a location fifteen miles north of Eureka.
Here a sod house was erected and the work of clearing the land of stones begun.
Until the house with its built-in oven was completed, they cooked their food on
a stove constructed of two large stones, under which the fire was built. They
had no furniture -- the floor of the house serving as a table and chairs.
During the first spring, Mr. Trautmann broke up a few acres of land and sowed
wheat. He eventually had three hundred acres under cultivation.

The couple now reside on the farm of their son, Henry Trautmann.

GOTTFRIED QUAST

This is the story of an emigrant orphan boy who won a home by industry and
thrift in a new, strange land. Gottfried Quast was born in Blotzka, South
Russia (now a part of Roumania) on June 6, 1866. Both of his parents being
dead, at the age of ten he left Russia with his uncle, Gottlieb Quast. They
arrived in Yankton, Dakota Territory, in May, 1876, his uncle homesteading on
land in Hutchinson County. Young Gottfried lived with him for five years, after
which period he found employment with neighboring farmers until he was
twenty-one years of age.

In the spring of 1888 he decided to locate in Eureka, arriving here in March of
that year. He soon found employment, first with the firm of Doering & Mix,
who were engaged in the implement business, and later with Liedle Brothers
(John and Jacob), who were managing a hotel at that time. Shortly after his
arrival he also filed on a pre-emption two miles East of Eureka.

In the year 1889, he was united in marriage with Friedericka Liedle, the
ceremony being performed by Reverend August Zimmerman. About a year after his
marriage he entered the transfer business with his brother-in-law, Jacob Serr.
They also conducted the Standard Oil Company agency, selling kerosene, gasoline
and oils. These products had to be hauled to the neighboring towns of Bowdle,
Bangor, Roscoe and other places by teams, and during the winter months,
especially after a snowstorm, these trips sometimes required a week's time. In
1900, Quast and Serr purchased equipment for moving buildings, and when they
dissolved their partnership a few years later Quast took over this equipment
while Serr continued in the transfer business.

In the year 1904, Mr. Quast erected frame buildings on his land west of Eureka
and began farming operations. He gradually increased his land holdings to 960
acres, and in 1916 retired and removed to Eureka, where he now resides with his
wife and two children, Emma and Calvin.

        Mr. and Mrs. Gottfried Quast

There were eight children born to Mr. and Mrs. Quast, of whom five passed
away--three in infancy, a son, Raymond in childhood, and Arthur, at the age of
forty. Those surviving are: Emma, residing in Eureka; Adeline, married to
Edward Ladner, employed in Albuquerque, New Mexico; and Calvin, residing in
Eureka.

CASPER HATZ

One of the picturesque business men of Eureka's early history was Casper Hatz,
who came here from Big Stone, Dakota Territory, where he had conducted a
business. This venture was a failure, according to his own statement, by reason
of extending credit to customers.

        Casper Hatz Store

Starting in Eureka with debts of $500.00, Hatz acquired a competence. His first
stock, as pioneers remember it, was composed mostly of tinware. The first
clerks were Amelia Harold and Bertha Kludt. Later, Jacob Suess and Jake
Kallenberger were in his employ.

The Eureka store was a cash store, no books being kept on items not paid in
cash. Reliable persons could, however, be accommodated. Hatz would call them
back to his office and give them the cash necessary to complete the purchase. A
memorandum note was then made and the transaction, so far as the store was
concerned, was cash.

Hatz is said to have accumulated $80,000 by the time he sold his interests in
Eureka. He erected a building in Aberdeen, still known as the Hatz block. Here,
he maintained apartments and a room or two for his guests, residing there until
his death. He was never married, being survived only by two brothers.

FRANK JONES

The J. J. Jones family, consisting of the father, mother and seven children, of
whom Frank Jones was one, left southern Wisconsin in 1887, in company with a
wagon train of other emigrants. After many weeks of travel they arrived at
Lacygne, Kansas, a small town about sixty miles south of Kansas City, Missouri,
where they spent the winter of 1887, and the early summer of 1888, when, being
tempted by large tracts of land which were being opened for homesteaders, they
headed northward into Dakota Territory. They made the trip in two covered
wagons, crossing the Missouri River by ferry boat at Niobrara, Nebraska, and
following the river northward. Concerning the trip, Mr. Jones states that they
encountered few settlers. Once in a while a small herd of cattle would be seen.
Especially noticeable to him was the bearded "needle grass" which they found on
the prairies. They had never seen such grass before and wondered if they might
not be the quills of some wild animal.

They traveled about fifteen or twenty miles a day, and arrived at Roscoe, South
Dakota, on the 1st of September, 1888. Here some supplies were purchased, and
the next day they proceeded on to the farm of an uncle, who was located between
the towns of Bowdle and Hosmer. Here they spent the winter of 1888, in a small
tar-papered shack which had been purchased from a homesteader who had gone back
east for the winter. The following spring, Mr. Jones, the father, filed on a
preemption claim and purchased a small frame building, 16 x 16, from Tom Macher,
and moved the same over a cellar dug in the ground. This served as a home for
the Jones family for some years to come.

Water was the big problem for the Jones family during their first few years in
this country. The water for household purposes had to be hauled by team and
wagon, while the stock had to be driven a distance of three miles. A well was
dug by a man named Frank Whitney, but after drilling for more than 160 feet, as
Frank Jones would say, "It was as dry at the bottom as at the top." Later on,
sufficient water for household purposes and to supply a team of horses was
obtained from a small well close to the home. Hay was always made in large
quantities. It was used to feed the stock, to sell to other people, and used
for fuel. Twisted hay was the chief fuel burned in the stoves of the early
pioneers.

By keeping young stock, the Jones's were soon able to accumulate a small herd
of dairy cows. These, along with the oxen, were kept together with ropes,
because fences were unknown in those days. The younger stock was generally
given into the care of parties who gathered up stock and took them to places
where water and hay was sufficient for the summer months. The general charge
was $1.00 a head for this pasturing.

Mr. Jones states that wild game was still plentiful in the early nineties.
Deer, antelope, wolves and foxes were often seen on the prairies.

One of the great fears of the pioneer was the danger of prairie fires. Barrels
of water and old sacks were always kept handy to combat fires. The pioneers
would always join with their neighbors in fighting these fires, which usually
came in the fall of the year, after the frost had killed the grass, or in the
spring when the grass had not yet turned green. He recalls a fire which started
west of Eureka, on the 2nd of April, 1889, and whipped by a very strong wind,
burned the entire country-side up to the town of Leola, burning many buildings
and destroying much other property in its course.

In 1895, the Jones family moved to Campbell County where they located on the
"Spring-vale ranch," which was a few miles southeast of the now extinct town of
La Grace, and was owned by the Fenelon Brothers. They generally kept a herd of
about 300 head of cattle at this place, and many horses. Black-leg was the
scourge of the rancher of that day. As soon as signs of it became noticeable,
the cattle would be rounded up in corrals, and the young ones would be
"rowelled." This consisted of causing a sore on the shoulder, either by means
of pushing a needle threaded with binder-twine soaked in turpentine, or dulop
tying them, and then cause the sore by walking in this manner. This would stop
the disease while the sore remained open. When it was healed up, the same
process would again be repeated. The neighbors would generally gather together
when this job was done, and would have plenty of "snakebite medicine" on
hand. A drink of this medicine would do much to aid the men in forgetting the
strain of the job that they were performing.

Mr. Jones recalls the winter of 1896 as having been an especially cold and
severe one. The thermometer registered as much as 57 below zero at Fort Yates,
North Dakota. He recalls a neighbor boy, by the name of Wm. Kirkland, being
frozen to death when he went to the barn to feed a pair of mules. He became
lost in a blizzard and wandered away two miles, where he was found the next
day, frozen to death. It was during that same winter that the Jones family,
republicans in politics, went to Bowdle, South Dakota, to attend a political
rally. While there, a McKinley ox was barbecued. The trip down was made by
horse and buggy, but the trip back was made by sleigh, and because of the vast
amount of snow that had fallen in the meantime, it took more than three days.

Frank Jones vividly recalls the murder of the Spicer family in January, 1898.
They were homesteading on some land just across the river, and a few miles away
from an Indian reservation. One day five Indians came to the place and shot Mr.
Spicer in the back with a shotgun while he was out doing the chores. They then
picked up an axe from the wood-pile and killed Mrs. Spicer with it. They also
killed Mr. Spicer's mother, and clubbed to death two twin babies that were
about one and one-half years old. Two of the Indians were arrested and confined
in the penitentiary at Bismarck. The other three were arrested and while in
jail awaiting trial, were taken to the edge of town by a mob of infuriated
citizens and lynched.

Eureka, a distance of forty miles away, was the trading town of the Joneses in
those early days. The trip would be made about three or four times a year, and
live stock would usually be taken along and sold on the market on these trips.
Among the activities of Frank Jones during his early days, was the job of a
freighter. Wheat, merchandise, supplies, and heavy articles were the chief
things which they hauled. He also worked for a time with the crew that
constructed a telephone line from Eureka to Mound City. The construction boss
was E. E. Hodgeson, of St. Paul. Fred W. Boettcher was in charge of the pay
roll. When the Spanish-American war broke out, many of the young men from the
crew enlisted in the army. Frank Jones and George Douglas did not relish the
idea so they returned to the old ranch and began making hay and "wrangling
dogies."

It was about this time too, that he filed on a homestead close to La Grace.
After five years of occupancy, he got title to this property. During this
period Mr. Jones and George Douglas secured various contracts for putting up
hay. He estimates that they put up about 700 tons of hay during that time, most
of which was sold to F. M. Lautzenheiser of Pollock, South Dakota, Andy Marsh
of Vanderbilt, and Carl Smith, also of Vanderbilt. For this work they received
$1.50 per ton.

In 1904, Mr. Jones moved to McPherson County, and in the spring of that year
planted about 500 acres of grain. A good crop was raised that year, and was
just being harvested, when in a few minutes a hail storm came up and destroyed
the entire crop, none of which was covered by insurance. Mr. Jones states that
this storm covered much of southern McPherson County and destroyed one of the
best standing crops that he has ever seen.

From that time on down to the present time, Frank Jones has been engaged in the
farming and dairying business. He has also been engaged in the trading of horses
to some extent.

PHILLIP KEISZ, SR.

Among the many immigrants from Gluecksthal, in South Russia, to settle in the
Eureka community was Phillip Kiesz, Sr., and his wife, Wilhelmina Schell, the
daughter of Christoph and Katharina Schell. Mr. Kiesz was born on June 20,
1854. The couple were married in 1877, and eleven years later, 1888, they
embarked on the journey to America together with seven children. One child died
on the journey.

They came directly to Eureka, as the Milwaukee railroad had been established
the year before, arriving on April 27, 1888. They took up land about two miles
north of Eureka, where their first home was the traditional sod shanty of
pioneer days. When they arrived here their finances were extremely low. What
little money they had was expended for necessary farming equipment.

They recalled later, when their industry had brought them a generous measure of
prosperity, that the sod hut was their home for many years and that their food
at one period consisted mainly of corn meal mush. At that time they owned no
table and meals were served on a blanket on the floor with a single tin cup
accommodating the whole family when there was tea or coffee with the meal.

But their industry and frugality were rewarded. In later years they enjoyed a
fine home. Four children were born to them after their arrival in America,
making a total of eleven in the family. Mr. and Mrs. Kiesz retired to a
comfortable home in Eureka in 1912, where they spent several years happily
together. Mrs. Kiesz died in 1923, and the husband survived until last year,
1936. They are both buried in the cemetery near their former home north of
Eureka.

Five of the children survive: Jacob Kiesz, who is prominently identified with
the business life of Eureka; Phillip P., Lodi, California; John P., Eureka;
Christian, a farmer residing north of Eureka; and Wilhelmina, the wife of John
J. Keim, of Eureka.

FRED HEPPERLE

One of Eureka's pioneer citizens who achieved success in the political world
was Fred Hepperle. Mr. Hepperle was born on the second day of February, 1863,
in Guldendorf, Odessa, South Russia, a son of John Hepperle and his wife,
Elisabeth, nee Keller. His parents were part of a large group of German
inhabitants who migrated to Russia soon after the turn of the nineteenth
century. He pursued his education in the public schools of his native land, and
in 1885, at 22, he left that country for the new world.

Landing in New York, he immediately proceeded westward, and after spending one
year in the then new state of Nebraska, he located on a farm in Campbell
County. In 1888, he moved to Eureka and became interested in the Eureka Bazaar,
first as an employee, and later as a part owner. It was there that he became
acquainted with the mercantile business, with which he remained familiar
throughout the course of his life. In 1893, he sold his interests in the Eureka
Bazaar and purchased the interest of C. J. Hezel in a hardware establishment
conducted under the name of Hezel & Stoller. The new firm assumed the name of
Stoller & Hepperle and continued in the hardware business until 1896, when they
expanded and added a stock of general merchandise. This firm continued in
business for a considerable length of time, until Fred Hepperle purchased the
interests of Mr. Stoller and continued the business under his own name as sole
owner. Many years later he sold his interests to Schanzenbach & Humann, and the
business is now under the name of the Humann Merc. Co.

Fred Hepperle was very active in civic affairs and in public life. At various
times he held the office of Justice of Peace in Eureka and served on the School
Board. He was mayor of the City of Eureka for two terms, namely, from 1902 to
1906. He served as county treasurer of McPherson County from the year 1894 to
1898 inclusive. In 1907, he served McPherson and Campbell Counties as their
senator in the state legislature. In 1913, he was appointed by Governor Byrne
to fill a vacancy in the office of Commissioner of School and Public Lands. In
1915, as the republican candidate, he was re-elected by a substantial majority.

Mr. Hepperle was regarded as one of the leaders of the republican party in the
state during his later life, and his keen and intelligent foresight into
affairs, whether the same were economic or political, made him a valuable asset
to Eureka, to McPherson County, and South Dakota.

On the 6th of August, 1893, Fred Hepperle was united in marriage with Mathilda
Weber, a native of Austria, who came to this country with her parents when only
a child. This union was blessed with four children, three of whom are living
today. They are: Bruno E. Hepperle living at Aberdeen, S. Dak.; Herbert O.
Heperle, a prominent attorney, living in Oakland, California; Werner H.
Hepperle living at Aberdeen, S. Dak. One son, Carl J. Hepperle, preceded his
father in death, while a senior attending the law school at the University of
South Dakota.

On the 29th of April, 1924, Mr. Hepperle died at his home in Eureka, a victim
of a long and lingering cancerous illness. His wife, Mathilda Hepperle,
followed him to the grave in October, 1928, following a serious operation in
Rochester, Minnesota. Both of them, together with their son, Carl, are buried
in the Lutheran cemetery at Eureka.

MRS. BERTHA C. RIETZ

Possessing a fine personality and making friends wherever she went -- such is
the memory Eureka pioneers cherish of Mrs. Bertha C. Rietz. She was born in
Germany on the 4th of August, 1867. At the age of twenty-one, and in company
with her husband, George C. Rietz, she came to McPherson County and settled on
a homestead twelve miles south of Eureka.

The financial condition of the Rietzs, compared to that of other pioneers of
this territory, must be admitted, was good. At the time of their arrival, they
possessed about $1,000.00 in cash. This enabled them to immediately build a
fine frame dwelling house and sod barn on the homestead premises, as well as
purchase all necessary farming equipment. Their first farming equipment
consisted of a yoke of oxen, a wagon, a plow, a mower and a rake, as well as
few cattle. These purchases, in addition to the cost of constructing the
original farm buildings, however, left very little in the family treasury.

Mrs. Rietz recalls that farm products were low in price. Eggs sold at 5 cents
a dozen, butter could not be sold at all until 1895, when all farm dairy
butter, regardless of grade, was sent in bulk lots to Minneapolis to be
renovated. Weather conditions up to the time of her departure from South
Dakota, in 1919, had not changed materially, according to her statement.

While on the farm her activities were similar to those of all pioneer women.
She served on the school board of her district as clerk for a number of years,
a rare honor bestowed upon pioneer women of this section.

Prior to her departure from South Dakota, she made her home in Eureka for a few
years, where she made a host of friends. She has visited in this city several
times since leaving here and has always been a welcome guest in the homes of
her old friends. She rejoices in re-living her pioneer experiences.

After leaving Eureka, she established a residence in Wisconsin for a number of
years before proceeding to Holly, Michigan, where she now lives.

Mrs. Rietz was the mother of three children, one of whom died as a young child.
A son, Edwin Rietz, is the proprietor of a store in southern Minnesota. A
daughter, Alma, lives with her family in Michigan.

WILHELM JOACHIM, SR.

An aged blind man living in Eureka today narrates an interesting account of his
life while founding a home for his family on Dakota prairie. During middle-age,
he suffered a painful misfortune costing his eyesight.

In the year of 1815, the population of Germany had increased to the extent of
making farm land scarce, and it was impossible for anyone to have a very large
farm. Consequently, many German people emigrated to Russia or neighboring
countries seeking new homes. It was at that time that the ancestors of Wilhelm
Joachim, Senior, left Germany, settling in Besserbuam, Roumania, then a part of
Russia.

Wilhelm Joachim was born in Roumania on the date of January 9, 1862; his
parents were George Adam Joachim and Katharina Schaeffer Joachim.

He came to America and directly to Eureka in 1888, having $300 in cash. After
being united in marriage with Fredericka Iszler, he filed on a 160-acre
preemption claim, and bought a team of ponies, farm machinery, seed, food, and
household equipment. With the ponies and plow, he cut sod for the house and
tirelessly traveling the miles to the Missouri River, there cutting trees for
the rafters in the house.

The first crop in 1890, was a failure due to drouth. The second year, seed was
