scarce and the ponies were too weak to do the heavy work. With the aid of his
friends, Mr. Joachim managed to tide over another year. Better fortune favored
the Joachims during the ensuing years, and from the original 169 acres of land
filed upon, the amount of land holdings increased to 2,560 acres. He also had a
considerable amount of money loaned out on interest.

As an example of how poverty stricken he once was, Mr. Joachim tells of an
incident during his first year of residence in McPherson County. He wished to
send a letter to relatives in Roumania. A 5-cent stamp was required to send it.
He did not have so large a sum, so borrowed a nickel from a friend, repaying
the money when he had earned it.

Wishing to retire after long years of hard work, Mr. Joachim turned his farming
interests over to his children and moved to Eureka. Often he drove out to the
farm to aid in the doing of different tasks. In May, 1922, he went out to help
dig rocks. Some were exceptionally large so dynamite was used to blast them.
Unfortunately a premature explosion injured Mr. Joachim's eye, necessitating an
operation. Infection later set in, resulting in the sight in both eyes.

Nine children were born to the Joachims, seven of whom are living. They are:
Wilhelm Junior, Greenway, S. Dak.; Jacob, Greenway, S. Dak.; Daniel, Dickenson,
N. Dak.; Magdalena (Mrs. William Merkel), Greenway, S. Dak.; Bertha (Mrs. John
Bertsch), Greenway, S. Dak.; Dorothy (Mrs. William Bader, Jr.), Wishek, N.
Dak.; Adam, Superintendent of City Schools, Waubay, S. Dak.

Mr. Joachim, despite his inability to see, is today cheerful person. He is
thankful for all blessings the Lord has bestowed, and does not complain of his
misfortunes.

EUGENE LIBIS

Eugene Libis crossed the sea in 1883 for the New World, locating in Canada. He
was born in Alsace Lorraine, on July 16, 1857. There he was married to Louisa
Kayser. After three years in Canada, however, he and his family returned to
Alsace Lorraine, living there until 1889, when he decided to emigrate to the
United States. He arrived with his family in Eureka in that year, being then a
young man 32 years of age.

The first thing to be done, of course, was to file claim on a homestead. He
chose land eleven miles southwest of Eureka, and proceeded to put up sod
buildings. In order to buy farm machinery he had to borrow money, as he was
completely out of funds. This equipment consisted of the traditional walking
plow, binder, harrow, grass mower and three oxen. His farming operations proved
very successful. Later on he bought more land, farming the larger acreage with
the help of his boys. He was especially fortunate in raising large crops of
wheat, and, due to this fact was known in the neighborhood as "the wheat king."

In those days wheat and flax brought 50 cents and 80 cents per bushel,
respectively. Eggs were 8 cents per dozen and butter 10 cents per pound. Dry
years were experienced in 1892 and 1893, but grasshopper plagues were unknown.

Among other hardships, it was necessary to walk the eleven miles to Eureka for
food supplies. One year, as it happened, he wintered a horse belonging to John
Liedle of Eureka. Occasionally, he hitched the animal to the hog trough and
drove to town in this improvised sled. This mode of travel, while not very
stylish, obviously was a decided improvement over walking.

Four children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Libis, namely: Leo, born November 1,
1883, now living on the old farm eleven miles southwest of Eureka; Gustav, born
September 5, 1885, farming southwest of Eureka; Katy, born January 28, 1890,
married to John Holzwarth, a farmer near Streeter, N. Dak.; Rosa, born in 1892,
married to Adolph Heckel, a farmer living southwest of Eureka.

Mr. Libis has been an ardent traveler, having crossed the Atlantic no less than
thirteen times. At present he is living at the Good Samaritan Home at Eureka.

JACOB RAU

Jacob Rau, a real pioneer of this territory, is one of the oldest residents of
this community. He has attained the advanced age of 86, having been born in
Bergdorf, South Russia, on January 29, 1851. At the age of twenty-one, he was
united in marriage to Eva Klipfel, the ceremony taking place in the Lutheran
church of Marienberg.

He was a prosperous farmer in the old country, but in 1889 decided to join a
party of about sixty families who were emigrating to America. With his wife and
five children he arrived in Eureka in the spring of that year.

While looking for suitable land, he and his family stayed at the home of Jacob
Adam. He finally found a satisfactory location seven miles southwest of Eureka,
where he proceeded to build a sod house and barn. After purchasing two pairs of
oxen, a plow, wagon, and harrow, he began breaking up the soil. In this he was
aided by his sons, the oldest of whom was fifteen. By working hard and steadily
they turned over about 125 acres during the first spring. This field was sowed
in flaxseed and yielded a good crop. The second year he sowed only wheat and
again the crop was satisfactory, with the additional advantage of a good price
-- about $1.00 per bushel.

Of twelve children born to this couple, six, all girls, died in childhood.
Those living are: Elizabeth, married to Philipp Himerich, a farmer near
Streeter, N. Dak.; Jacob, John and Fred, all three farming near Streeter;
George, farming on the old homestead southwest of Eureka; Gottlieb, living five
miles southwest of Eureka. As he prospered, Mr. Rau bought more land, at one
time owning 1,760 acres. After his retirement in 1913, he bought a home in
Eureka, where he and his wife have since resided.

ADAM J. HEZEL

One of the younger men to begin his career in America was Adam J. Hezel, born
in Odessa, Russia, April 3, 1876. He came to Eureka in 1888 in order to attend
school. After school and Saturdays he was employed in the general merchandise
business conducted by Hezel & Hepperle. He also attended school in Aberdeen,
Winona and St. Paul, gathering knowledge which was to be useful to him in later
life.

        Mr. and Mrs. Adam Hezel

After varied experiences, including a venture in a business of his own in
Fessenden, North Dakota, he returned to Eureka and became a stockholder in the
Eureka Bazaar when that enterprise was incorporated in 1897. Three years later
the Soo line was extended to Pollock and the town of Artas sprang up. The
Bazaar then established a branch in that city. Adam Hezel was made one of the
active members of this enterprise and was also named postmaster.

Returning to Eureka in 1902 he again assumed a position in the Bazaar,
remaining in that position for some time. It was during this period that Mr.
Hezel married Hulda E. Hinz, daughter of Louis and Wilhelmina Hinz of Markesau,
Wisconsin.

In association with his father, Jacob Hezel, and brother, C. J. Hezel, of
Aberdeen, he organized and operated the First State Bank of Zeeland, North
Dakota, moving there to assume active management of the institution in 1904.
Remaining there for seven years the family next moved to Morristown, South
Dakota, engaging in the general merchandise business, only to return to the
banking business, this time in the German State Bank at Zeeland. Mr. Hezel
continued in this position until the time of his death, which occurred in
Eureka January 19, 1935.

The Hezels had three children, two daughters and a son. One daughter, Grace,
died in infancy, the other is Mrs. Max A. Wishek of Ashley. The son, Walter R.,
resides in Zeeland.

PETER JUNKER

In the year 1804, Friedrich Junker, a great-grandfather of Peter Junker, left
his home in Osschwinzer, Austria Hungary, and migrated to South Russia, where
he helped in the founding of the village of Petersthal. There were three sons
in his family, Friedrich, Wilhelm and Peter. The two oldest children remained
in Austria Hungary, while Peter, the youngest, accompanied his parents to
Russia.

Peter Junker, the grandfather, was married to Anna Maria Christman, of
Freudenthal, and brought up four children, Peter, Johann, Jacob and Rosina. One
of the sons, Johann, was born April 7, 1830, and was married to Magdalena
Schopp. To this union there were born seven children, Johann, George, Peter
(the subject of this story), Juliana, Katharina, Rosina and Johanna.

Peter Junker was born in Petersthal, South Russia, on January 23, 1865. In the
year 1888, he was united in marriage to Magdalena Hirning, a daughter of Jacob
Hirning. About a year later, he and his brother George, with their wives, set
out for America, joining a party of forty families from Neuburg, South Russia.

They took passage on the Augusta Victoria, one of the largest ships on the
ocean at that time. The voyage was to be made in six days, but a heavy storm
was encountered at sea, and they landed in New York a day late. After going
through the customary inspection, the immigrants bound for South Dakota, were
put on a train. Several days later, the Junker families arrived in Eureka,
where they were met by Philip Kallenberger, an uncle of Mrs. Junker. After
visiting here for several days, Peter Junker and his wife took up their abode
with Andreas Kallenberger, where they remained until the following spring.
During the winter months, Mr. Junker inspected the country in search of
suitable land, and finally located about eighteen miles southwest of Eureka.
Here he filed on a preemption and erected a sod dwelling. On his arrival here,
he had $330.00 in cash with which he purchased two oxen, a cow, wagon, plow and
harrow. His first flax crop yielded 160 bushels, and was sold for 65 cents per
bushel, which enabled him to buy another cow. Complete crop failures, due to
drouth, were experienced in 1911 and 1926, while in 1905, a severe hailstorm
wiped out everything. While much hardship had to be endured the early years,
better crops and advanced prices brought on higher standards of living in later
years.

Mr. Junker recalls his experience in connection with the "Indian Scare" of
1890. "One night in December," he relates, "my family and I were in bed,
sleeping peacefully, when we were awakened by a loud knock at the window. Upon
investigation, a man's voice excitedly demanded admittance. I complied, and he
told us that the Indians had already crossed the Missouri River and were headed
this way, killing and scalping everyone in their path. This put me in a trying
situation, as my wife was still confined in bed with a baby only two weeks old.
There was only one thing to be done, however, so I hitched up my team of horses
and drove over to get my neighbor, Karl Bechtold and his family. We then
returned to my farm, loaded my wife and child on the wagon and hurriedly drove
to Eureka. After taking my wife out to her uncle, Philip Kallenberger, I
returned to Eureka and at once went to the Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul
Railroad Company depot, and requested that they provide a train to take the
women and children out of the danger zone. A few days later, however, we
learned that Chief Sitting Bull had been killed and that order had again been
restored."

        Mr. and Mrs. Peter Junker

Mr. Junker's wife passed away in 1897, leaving him with four small children,
the oldest six years and the youngest eighteen months old. He was later
remarried to Mrs. Eva Redmann, nee Schaeffer, who had two daughters. Four
children were born to this union, thus making a total of ten children brought
up by Mr. Junker. They are all living with the exception of one son, Henry, who
at the age of twenty-four, died in action in the World War. He was mortally
wounded on October 7, 1918, and passed away the same day. Those surviving are:
Magdalena, married to Wm. Roemmich of Isabel, S. Dak.; Jacob, employed in a
bakery in Milwaukee, Wisconsin; John, residing near Eureka; Arthur, employed in
a garage in Eureka; Bertha, married to Henry Delker, a farmer southwest of
Eureka; Frieda, married to Reinhold Kary, of Eureka; Alfred, serving in the
United States Army and stationed at San Francisco, California; Anna Redmann,
married to Jacob Hirsch, a farmer northwest of Miles City, Montana; Martha
Redmann, married to Andrew Bauer, Napoleon, N. D.

Altogether, three of Mr. Junker's sons have served in the United States Army,
namely, Henry, John and Alfred. John was in France during the World War and
after the armistice, served for a year in the army of occupation. Alfred joined
the Army seven years ago, during which time he served for two years in China.
He is at present stationed in San Francisco, California.

Peter Junker has always taken an active part in the Evangelical church, and has
also held various public offices and positions of trust. He served as County
Commissioner for Campbell County from 1912 to 1917, and for twelve years was
deputy assessor. In addition he has held all the offices in his country school
district. In 1894, he became agent and adjuster for the Evangelical Mutual
Insurance Company of Milbank, South Dakota, and from 1920 to 1926 was a
director of this company. At the present time he is Justice of Peace for Eureka.

Mr. Junker retired in 1928, and took up residence in Eureka His wife died in
1934, and since then his son, Arthur, and family have been living with him.

GEORGE OSTER

In spite of misfortune which has befallen him often during his lifetime, George
Oster has kept up his courage and has cheerfully tried to make the best of
existing conditions. George Oster was born on January 11, 1866, at
Hoffnungsthal, Bessarabia, Roumania, and came to America twenty years later
with his parents, Philip and Anna Maria Oster.

The first three years in this country were spent in the southern part of the
state, around Freeman and Menno. The Osters came of McPherson County and filed
on a homestead. Like other pioneers, their first home was built of sod and mud.
Their farm equipment consisted of two yoke of oxen, a plow, a harrow, a mower,
and a wagon.

George Oster was married to Fredericka Deg of Freeman, South Dakota, in 1888.
Five children were born to this union.

In 1897 the grain was good, but as Mr. Oster was beginning to harvest, a hail
storm destroyed the entire crop. Mr. Oster said bad luck left him rather down
and almost out, but he obtained work elsewhere and earned enough money to
support his family over the winter.

Three years later misfortune again singled out the Oster family. This time
their house caught fire. All household goods and some grain were burned, and
one child was badly injured in the blaze.

A year later the family moved to North Dakota where their luck was somewhat
better for three years. They acquired land and with good crops paid off all
debts. But again in 1905, their home was burdened with sorrow when the wife and
mother died.

In 1906 Mr. Oster married Elizabetha Kaesman. Five children were born to this
union.

While living in North Dakota, Mr. Oster met with a car accident, and suffered
injuries which incapacitated him for heavy work the rest of his life. The
accident cost a large sum of money, necessitating the mortgaging of the home.
After the depression came, there was little hope of paying off the mortgage, so
the Osters gave up farming.

Since 1932, Mr. and Mrs. Oster have established their home in Eureka. Most of
their children, however, have remained in North Dakota and Montana, where they
are occupied as laborers and farmers.

JOHN OTTMAR

John Ottmar, pioneer resident of Spring Creek Township, was born in Neuburg,
South Russia, on the 21st day of December, 1857. He was the second oldest son
of Wilhelm and Katharina Ottmar, whose name before marriage was Hummel.

When John Ottmar was twenty-one years of age, he was compelled to serve in the
army of the Czar. After five years of service, he received his honorable
discharge in the fall of 1883.

In 1889, he and his family, which at that time consisted of four sons and one
daughter, entered McPherson County with the current tide of immigration from
southern Russia. A farm was purchased in Spring Creek Township. This farm
produced bountifully throughout the years under the capable management of Mr.
Ottmar. It still remains in the hands of the Ottmar family.

The Indian scare of the early nineties was also experienced by John Ottmar.
During his lifetime he often related how he was awakened one night by a
neighbor who told him that the Indians were invading that section of the
country. Most people when they heard this rumor hid their food-stuff and
valuables. Mr. Ottmar does not recall whether he did likewise, stating that he
was too frightened to remember. However, he does state he rushed into the barn,
hitched up his fastest oxen, loaded his family into the wagon, and drove away
to Eureka.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Ottmar

On the 15th of February, 1884, while still in the old country, he was united in
marriage with Miss Elizabeth Gotz. She died on the 30th of May, 1894, leaving
him with five small children. It was a Pathetic situation, and Mr. Ottmar
realizing the impossibility of continuing on alone, married again. In November
of that same year he was united in marriage with Miss Katharina Broeckel, a
daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Gott. Broeckel, who was born in the old country.

John Ottmar was a writer of no little ability, and stores of his experiences,
and the experiences of his neighbors oft-times appeared for publication in the
newspapers. His health until just shortly before his death was exceptionally
fine. However, immediately preceding his death, he was subjected to several
serious illnesses, and died at his farm home on the 4th day of February, 1933.
Funeral services were held in the Zion Lutheran Church in Eureka. A large group
of friends and relatives gathered to pay their last respects to this old
pioneer. He was buried in the cemetery of the Friedens congregation located
northwest of Eureka.

His wife, Katharina Ottmar, moved to Eureka in October, 1936, and now resides
here.

The names and addresses of the children of John Ottmar by his first wife are:
John Ottmar, Wishek, N. Dak.; Mathilda Ackerman, Artas, S. Dak.; William
Ottmar, Wishek, N. Dak.; Henry Ottmar, manager of the Eureka Equity Exchange,
Eureka, S. Dak.; Christian Ottmar, Hartling, Washington.

Ten children were born by his second wife, three of whom died in infancy. Their
names and addresses are as follows: Katharina Stuckle, Greenway, S. Dak.; Karl
K. Ottmar, Greenway, S. Dak.; Emma Heer, Eureka, S. Dak.; Christina Wolf, Long
Lake, S. Dak.; Eva Ritter, Eureka, S. Dak.; Arthur Ottmar, Greenway, S. Dak.;
Albert Ottmar, Greenway, S. Dak.

GEORG GROSZ

George Grosz came to McPherson County in 1889, arriving in Eureka on the newly
constructed Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul railroad on April 27th of that
year. He was born in Gluckstal, South Russia, on May 5, 1854. At the age of 24
he was married to Elizabeth Rieger, the marriage ceremony being performed by
Reverend Biernbach. With his family, parents and three brothers, Christian,
Henry, and Martin, he left his home in Russia in the spring of 1889. Among
others who made the trip with them were John Stock and Philipp Flemmer, both
well known in this community.

After their arrival, Mr. Grosz and his family stayed for two weeks with the
Joseph Werre family. During this time he inspected the land in the Eureka
territory, eventually taking up a preemption and later a homestead and timber
claim in Jackson Township, nine miles south of Eureka.

A sod house and barn were erected and live stock and equipment purchased. This
included two oxen, a cow, pig, breaking plow, wagon, grass mower, hay rake and
harrow, costing in all, about $200.00. These purchases exhausted his financial
resources. In order to prove up his preemption he needed another $200.00, which
he borrowed.

The first spring he broke up about 15 acres and sowed flaxseed. The crop failed
to mature properly, however, and the yield was very small. Since he owned no
reaper, the flax had to be picked by hand, and threshed by pounding it out with
a flail.

The year 1890 produced a better crop, but grain prices during those years were
quite low, wheat bringing 62 cents and flaxseed about 65 cents per bushel.

There were 11 children born to this couple, two of them died in 1898 -- a boy,
Jacob, at the age of 14, and a girl, Christina, in infancy. Those surviving
are: Johann, employed in Mankato, Minnesota; Christian, a merchant in Bentley,
N. Dak.; Gottlieb, a merchant in Morris, Minnesota; Henry, a dairy farmer near
Superior, Wisconsin; Martin, a farmer 10 miles south of Eureka; Philip, a farmer
living on the old homestead south of Eureka; Fred, engaged in the banking and
insurance business in Bowdle, S. Dak.; Rosina, living with her father;
Magdalena, also living with her father.

As the years passed, Mr. Grosz gradually bought more land, at one time owning
1,520 acres. His wife passed away in 1913, and four years later he retired and
removed to Eureka, where he now resides.

MARTIN GROSZ, SR.

Another of the many Eureka pioneers born in Glucksthal, South Russia, was
Martin Grosz, whose birth date was March 13, 1858. On January 9, 1880, he was
married to Christina Bieber, a daughter of Jacob Bieber, Sr. The ceremony was
performed in the Lutheran church of Glucksthal, by the Reverend Mr. Biernbach.
There were no children born to this union.

With a party of other emigrants he and his wife left Russia in 1889, arriving
in Eureka by train on April 29th of that year.

        Mr. and Mrs. Martin Grosz, Sr.

He filed claim on a homestead in Jackson Township, ten miles south of Eureka.
His cash resources at the time were $150.00. A sod house was erected 14 feet
wide, 8 feet high, and 22 feet long. The barn, 14 x 18, was build adjoining the
house. He acquired equipment consisting of a plow, harrow, wagon and two pairs
of oxen, valued all together at about $360.00. He had no drill, the seed being
scattered over the field by hand. When the flax was ripe it was picked by hand
and threshed with a horse power threshing

While they occasionally had drouths in those years, states Mr. Grosz, none were
so severe or devastating as the present one. Grasshopper plagues also were
unknown. Mr. Grosz retired in 1907 and located in Eureka, where he and his wife
have resided since.

MARTIN STICKEL

Martin Stickel was born in Treplitz, South Russia, on April 18, 1859. He was
married to Friedericka Helm in 1878, in the Lutheran church. At the age of
twenty, he and his wife left Russia, coming to this country with two uncles,
Christian Zacher and Johann Hehn, and several other families.

They arrived in Yankton, South Dakota, in June, 1879. Mr. Stickel had about
$190.00 cash, and proceeded to purchase live stock and equipment consisting of
two oxen, a cow, wagon, plow and harrow. He homesteaded on land twenty-five
miles north of Yankton, in Hutchinson County, and pursued farming there for ten
years.

He sold his land in 1889, and came to McPherson County, arriving in Eureka in
March of that year. His live stock and machinery, which filled two cars, were
shipped by freight. He now owned five horses, three cows, two oxen, a plow,
wagon, harrow, grass mower and hay rake, but had no money. He bought land four
miles southeast of Eureka on terms, and erected sod buildings on it. The first
year he sowed flaxseed and wheat and planted some corn, harvesting a fair crop.

After twenty-one years of farming, during which he acquired 800 acres of land,
he gave it up and located in Eureka where he began buying horses, cattle and
hogs for the market. He was actively engaged in this pursuit until about 1931
when he retired, his son Reinhold continuing in the business.

There were twelve children born to Mr. and Mrs. Stickel. Of these, two boys,
Friedrich and Emil, died in childhood, and three, John, Emanuel and Henry, in
manhood. His wife passed away in 1924.

The surviving children are: Edward, residing in Eureka; Andrew, employed in
Eureka; Gotthilf, a farmer near Leola, S. Dak.; Julius, a farmer near Lemmon,
S. Dak.; Lydia, married to Robert Greiser, engaged in the newspaper business in
Wishek, N. Dak.; Ida, married to R. A. Dahms, employed in Eureka; Reinhold,
live stock buyer of Eureka.

JOHANN JUNDT

Johann Jundt, born in Glucksthal, South Russia, on April 22, 1861, was married
to Katharina Schumacher in 1889. The ceremony took place in the Lutheran
church, the Rev. Mr. Schrenk officiating. In the fall of the same year he left
his home in Russia with his wife and mother-in-law, Christina Schumacher, for
the land of promise, America. In the party were Joseph Rott, Christian Keszler,
and Adam Jung.

They arrived in Eureka by rail in October, 1889. Mr. Jundt had $400.00 in cash.
He homesteaded land in Long Lake Township, 17 miles northeast of Eureka, and
purchased equipment including a breaking plow, wagon, two pairs of oxen, and a
cow and calf. Sod buildings were then constructed and some of the land broken
up. The first year he sowed about 25 acres of flaxseed. The crop was fair and
brought about $1.00 per bushel.

The winters during the early years, states Mr. Jundt, were much colder, with
more snow than now. He recalls an experience he had one winter, which easily
might have cost him his life. He had been visiting his neighbor, George Huft,
who lived about a half mile away. While on his way home a severe snow storm
came up, completely obscuring the landscape. Luckily, there was a fence nearby,
which he followed and returned home safely.

The year 1911 was a tragic one for the Jundt family. There were frequent
thunderstorms that summer, although the rainfall was slight. One day, during
the haying season, their eldest son, Johann, with his two brothers, Jacob and
Friedrich, drove out to the field with the hayrack for a load of hay. While
they were loading, a thunderstorm came up. Discontinuing their work, they
hurriedly started for home, Johann driving the team and Friedrich, the
youngest, asleep at the rear of the hayrack. As they reached the haystack, a
sharp bolt of lightning struck the wagon, instantly killing Johann and
Friedrich, while Jacob, who was sitting in the middle of the load, was only
stunned.

        Mr. and Mrs. Johann Jundt

Of the seven children born to Mr. and Mrs. Jundt, those surviving are: Mary,
married to August Vilhauer, a farmer living on the old homestead northeast of
Eureka; Katharina, married to John Schumacher, farming east of Eureka; Lydia,
married to Emil Pfeifle, a farmer northeast of Eureka; Jacob, residing at
Eureka; Martha, living with her parents at Eureka.

Mr. Jundt retired in 1920 and removed to Eureka, where he and his wife reside
at present.

JOHN KEIM

Another native of Glucksthal, South Russia, to pioneer in the Eureka community
was John Keim. He was born there on September 20, 1863. He was married to
Christina Schanzenbach in the year 1883, and six years later emigrated to the
United States. Others known in this community who made the trip at that time
were Johan Kemmet, Gottlieb Walth, Jacob Walth, and Fred Walth.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Keim

With his wife and two children, Frieda and Rosa, Mr. Keim arrived in Eureka in
October of 1889. They visited the Daniel Schanzenbach family for a short time,
after which they took up their abode at the home of Philip Schaeffer where they
remained until the spring of 1890.

Mr. Keim then took up a preemption of 120 acres, located three miles north of
Eureka. Later he traded this land for two oxen and filed on a homestead in the
same locality. He erected a stone house and barn, and after purchasing a plow
was ready to begin farming operations.

As the years passed by he gradually accumulated more land and property, and in
the year 1905 removed to Eureka where he entered the implement business with
his brother Jacob, and Solomon Isaak. They also operated a string of elevators,
several of which were located in Eureka.

A few years after his retirement from business activities in 1908, he visited
his old home in Russia with his brother Jacob, and John Schanzenbach. He now
resides in Eureka, where his wife passed away in the year 1935.

There were four children born to Mr. and Mrs. Keim, namely: Frieda, married to
John P. Kiesz of Eureka; Rosa, married to W. F. Broich of Aberdeen; Gustav,
employed in Fargo, N. Dak.; Theodore, employed in Brookings, S. Dak.

DAVID ISAAK

David Isaak was born in Kulm, South Russia, on April 17, 1860, where he was
christened and later confirmed in the Lutheran faith.

He made the trip to America in the year 1877 with his parents, grandparents,
brothers and sisters. Their first destination was Hutchinson County, Dakota
Territory, where they located and lived for nine years. Here David Isaak was
married to Hannah Hetzler, and three years after his marriage, together with
his wife, he left Hutchinson County and traveled northward. Crossing the site
where Eureka now stands, he continued five miles north to the home of his
wife's parents, Reverend and Mrs. Henry Hetzler. Here they remained for the
winter and spring, and in the summer of 1887, continued their journey to Mercer
County, North Dakota, where they located on a farm near Stanton.

In the fall of 1889, Mr. Isaak decided to locate in the new town of Eureka,
where he entered the hardware business shortly after his arrival. He bought the
lot on which the David Isaac home now stands and built a two-room house, which
still remains as a part of the present home.

There were five children born to Mr. and Mrs. Isaak, two -- Annie and Richard,
dying in infancy. Those surviving are: Josephine, residing in Eureka; Albert,
engaged in business in Cedar Falls, Iowa; Edward, engaged in business in Eureka.

        Mr. and Mrs. David Isaak

David Isaak was one of the founders of the Zion Lutheran Church of Eureka, and
during his life was active in serving his church. He passed away on December
21, 1917, as a result of heart failure. Mrs. Isaak still resides in Eureka.

DANIEL METTLER

The biography of Daniel Mettler varies very little from that of many other
early pioneers who settled in the Eureka territory. His ancestry is traced back
to Frederick Mettler, a great-grandfather, who emigrated from Wurtenburg,
Germany, to the colony of Kassel, in South Russia, to escape military service
in his native country. At that time the Czarina of Russia had made the promise
that immigrants who helped to build up the country would be exempt from all
military service. His grandfather, Henry Jacob Mettler, followed in the
footsteps of his father, who died when young Henry was but seven years of age,
in tilling the soil of his native Kassel. His father, Andrew Mettler, emigrated
to America when young Daniel was only 16 years of age. Accompanying Andrew
Mettler to America was his wife and family consisting of 10 children, four
girls and six boys, one of whom was young Daniel. The cause of their migration
to America was the same as prompted their forefathers to leave Germany almost a
century before, namely, to escape compulsory military service, and to take up
free land.

The Mettler family arrived in Yankton, South Dakota, on the 31st of May, 1874,
and soon afterwards filed on three claims of land in Hutchinson County,
approximately 33 miles north of Yankton. A sod house and farm buildings were
soon constructed, and with the aid of a yoke of oxen, the land was soon made
tillable.

Young Daniel worked with his parents until he was 22 years of age, when he
married Margaret Huether. The Reverend Orth of Yankton, officiated at the
wedding. The newlyweds immediately filed on a homestead on 160 acres, and also
on a 120-acre preemption claim. However, on the 2nd of April, 1889, a prairie
fire ravaged large tracts of land in Hutchinson County, and completely destroyed
the Daniel Mettler farm home, all buildings, 55 hogs, all his machinery, and
about 1,000 bushels of grain, none of which was covered by insurance.

Sad and disappointed, Daniel Mettler decided to look elsewhere for a home.
Consequently, with the balance of his money, young Daniel purchased 18 head of
oxen and started out for northern South Dakota. Upon his arrival in Eureka, he
sold the oxen for $130.00 per pair, only a part of which was paid in cash. The
balance is still owing to him. He then purchased some lumber and hired some
carpenters and built a house on the corner where Market Street intersects the
present State Highway No. 10. Title to this property is still in the name of
the Mettler family. On July 4, 1889, the family arrived in Eureka and took
possession of the new home.

Mr. Mettler's first years in Eureka were spent as a stock buyer. For a time he
also bought grain in a flat house. In 1894, he purchased a one-third interest
in a general merchandise store, operated by Andrew Brandt.  Mr. Mettler was
never active in conducting this business. Soon afterwards the business failed
and Mr. Mettler not only lost his interest in the business, but was also forced
to pay $1,100.00 toward debts owing to wholesale houses. However, he was not
discouraged but continued in his own business of buying grain for many years
afterwards. In 1893, he purchased a half section of land. Later he purchased
another 480 acres of land about three miles southwest of Eureka.  Mr. Mettler
still has title to all this land except 320 acres, which he sold. At various
times he owned lots and houses in Eureka, and even caused a certain portion of
his lands be platted and added to the city under the name of Mettler's
Addition. He at one time owned the present Oster and Weixel elevators, as well
as owning an elevator in Hillsview. In conjunction with all these businesses,
he also bought live stock and wool, and sold farm implements.

In 1897, his wife died, leaving seven children. For a year he attempted to
carry on, but realizing his helplessness, he re-married. His second wife was a
widow, Mrs. Caroline Sinkbeil of Parkston, South Dakota, who at the time of her
marriage to Daniel was the mother of three children. To this second union was
born one daughter, Frieda Koelle, who is living in Eureka today.

The names of the children resulting from his previous marriage are: Margaret
Klein, Eureka, died in 1906; Emelia Heer, Denhoff, N. Dak.; Carolina Kafer
Schiermeister, Hazelton, N. Dak.; Christine Klifel, Lodi, California; Eva
Klifel, Modesto, California; Lena Bartel, Lodi, California; Reinhold Mettler,
Eureka, S. Dak.

The names of the step-children are: Adolph Sinkbeil of Zeeland, N. Dak., and
Mrs. H. D. Opp of Eureka, S. Dak.

Mr. Mettler retired from active business in 1920, after a long and successful
business career. He is in good health at the present time, except for
occasional sieges of eczema, and is living with his youngest daughter, Mrs. J.
G. (Frieda) Koelle. His wife died in 1936. He is 78 years old at this time.

The present economic depression and bank failures, as a result of which he lost
approximately $18,000.00, deprived him of many comforts in his declining years.
However, he is still happy and contented.

WILHELM SCHUMACHER

To Wilhelm Schumacher goes the honor of being the oldest living person whose
biography is contained in this book. Mr. Schumacher is 92 years of age, and is
generally conceded to be the oldest living person in McPherson County. He was
born in the province of Glucksthal, South Russia, on the 20th of May, 1845.

On the 28th of January, 1869, at the age of 24 years, he was united in marriage
with Rosina Schnabel, also a native daughter of the same village. The marriage
ceremony was performed by Reverend Kerm in the Lutheran church in the province
of Gluckstahl.

Having heard rumors and stories of America and the "land of promise," and more
in particular, being desirous of having spiritual and religious freedom, and of
finding a better home for themselves and their children, Wilhelm Schumacher,
wife, and family, consisting of the following children: John, Ludwig, Rosina,
Christina, Katharina, Carolina, Christiana, Jacob, Christian and Peter, in
company with other pioneer families, set sail for America. Included in this
group were the following families: Christian Grosz, George Grosz, Heinrich
Grosz, Martin Grosz, George Dais, Jacob Niesz, Jacob Treftz, Johann Hausauer,
David Hottmann, Johann Jacober, Gottlieb Krenzler, Franz Federer, Philipp
Dollinger, Jacob Nehlich, Phillip Schaeffer, Peter Schnabel, John Haagstud,
Christian Gugel, Gottlieb Klein, Jacob Niesz, Jr.; and the following single
persons, namely: John Stock, Philipp Flemmer, Katharina Gohl, Christian
Nehlich, Jacob Gohl, Rosina Hottman and Katharina Dollinger.

        Mr. Wilhelm Schumacher

On the 27th day of April, 1889, all of them arrived in Eureka on the newly
constructed Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul railroad. Immigrants in those days
were not accustomed to staying in hotels, and such housing facilities were
scarce, so they were generally met at the train by friends and relatives who
had preceded them to this country, and took up a temporary abode with them. The
Schumachers, upon their arrival, were met by Mrs. Katharina Liedle, the mother
of the late John Liedle. She immediate]y invited them to partake of her
hospitality and the first meal that the Schumacher family ate in the city of
Eureka, and in McPherson County, was prepared by the widowed woman, Katharina
Liedle.

Immediately after the noon-day meal, the Schumacher family was transported by
means of horses and wagon to the farm home of Fred Schumacher, a nephew of
Wilhelm Schumacher. They remained at his farm for approximately two weeks,
during which time, the spring crop was planted. The Schumachers aided their
relative in planting his crop, and in return received a portion of the yield
for their services.

The spring crop having been planted and there still remaining a great deal of
land in the immediate vicinity open for homestead entry, Wilhelm Schumacher and
his nephew made several exploratory trips before finally locating on three
claims, a preemption, a tree claim and a homestead, in what is now Township
127, Range 70. This land was of an inferior grade, but because of their large
friendship in the immediate vicinity, they decided to locate here. The large
rock piles located on these premises today remain as silent symbols of the work
and toil necessary to make this soil tillable.

The financial status of the Schumachers upon their arrival in this country,
like that of practically all other pioneer families, was poor. To finish the
trip to McPherson County, it was necessary for Wilhelm Schumacher to borrow
the sum of $100.00 from his traveling companion, Johann Hausauer. Their first
farming equipment, consisting of a team of oxen and a second hand wagon, were
purchased from Mr. Schumacher's nephew, on credit. To pay these debts, and as a
means of raising additional funds with which to buy more farming equipment, all
of the Schumacher children, except the six youngest, were given into the employ
of various farmers.

The first home of the Schumachers on their homestead consisted of a sod house
with a ship-lap finishing on the inside. A feature of this structure was a
temporary stove of sod and rocks, built into the ground, which was used during
their first summer to cook their food. Toward the fall of that same year, a
permanent sod house and sod barn adjacent to the house, were built. The new
structure also contained a built-in stove, and the chief fuel for this stove
consisted of straw and chaff left after threshing. The present wooden frame
dwelling house located on the Schumacher homestead, was built in 1901, when
times were getting better and the family was becoming more prosperous. However,
upon the completion of the structure, it was not entirely paid for and Mr.
Schumacher was forced to sell two of his best horses to pay the debt against
the house. Today, Wilhelm Schumacher and his daughter Friedericka, are still
living in this house.

Practically all of the early pioneers were upright and God-fearing people, and
the Schumacher family was no exception. Wilhelm Schumacher told the writer of
this biography how the early pioneer families would assemble in private homes
and conduct church services. The ways and means of getting to these gatherings
were oft-times odd to say the least. At times, an ox or horse would be hitched
to a stoneboat, and the family hauled to church in this manner. Other times, an
ox or team of horses would be hitched to a hay rake, and the family conveyed to
church while sitting on the teeth of the rake.

Havoc and destruction hit the Schumacher household on the 9th of June, 1911. On
that day a terrific tornado hit the farm home of Wilhelm Schumacher. In less
than two minutes, the farm buildings, with the exception of the dwelling house
and the summer kitchen, were almost completely demolished. One large wooden
barn was completely destroyed, and the roofs taken off of two others. The
windmill and two threshing separators were also destroyed. The summer kitchen
was moved off its foundation more than two feet; one son, Christ, who with his
wife and baby, were in the summer kitchen were uninjured. The rest of the
family rushed for the cellar, but the tornado was upon them and past before the
trap door could be opened. The large frame barn was soon rebuilt with the help
of the neighbors. However, after this experience, Mr. and Mrs. Schumacher, and
their two youngest children, August and Friedericka, moved to Ashley, leaving
the old homestead in the care of their son, Christ. They remained there for
approximately three years before the older folks again returned to their farm.

The Schumachers never sought public office. Wilhelm Schumacher served on the
boards of education of different rural precincts for a number of years, and
also served as a deacon in his church for some time. Two of his sons achieved
considerable prominence in the political field in the county. Ludwig Schumacher
represented district number four as commissioner during the years 1920 and 1929
inclusive. It was during this time that McPherson County's spacious and
ultra-modern $125,000.00 court house was constructed, free and clear of all
bonded indebtedness. August Schumacher was elected mayor of the city of Eureka
in April of 1936, and is occupying that office during the celebration of its
Fiftieth Anniversary.

A great grief struck Wilhelm Schumacher in the year 1930, when his wife and
comrade through all his trying pioneer days departed from this life at the ripe
age of 79 years. The rest of the family, with the exception of Peter, who died
in infancy, and Ludwig Schumacher who died recently, are still living, none of
whom have departed very far from the original habitat.

The 19th of May, 1935, was the occasion of the celebration of Wilhelm
Schumacher's 90th birthday. In honor of this event, a family gathering was held
at the old homestead, and a group of more than 200 people, consisting of
children, grand-children and great-grand-children of Mr. Schumacher, were
present. Only 27 members of the family tree were not able to be present. A
picture of this group was taken and has in it people representing four
generations of the Schumacher family.

Wilhelm Schumacher never amassed a great amount of wealth, despite all his toil
and hard labor. However, through clean and conservative living, he was able to
purchase more land from time to time, and was in a position to assist each one
of his 11 children in getting a start in their chosen field. The names of the
children and their addresses are as follows:

John Schumacher, married to Friedericka Maier, lives on a farm 10 miles east of
Ashley, N. Dak. Ludwig Schumacher married to Eva Heyd, lived on a farm 18 miles
northeast of Eureka. He passed away February 18, 1937. Mrs. Ludwig Thurn,
formerly Rosina Schumacher, lives at Ashley, N. Dak., where her husband is
engaged in the hardware business; Christina Hoffman, formerly Christina
Schumacher, is living at Leola, S. Dak. Her husband died about three years ago.
Mrs. Christian Schaible, formerly Katharina Schumacher, is living on a farm 22
miles northeast of Eureka. Mrs. Philipp Weisz, formerly Carolina Schumacher, is
living on a farm 18 miles east of Eureka. Mrs. Karl Heinrich, formerly
Christiana Schumacher, lives on a farm about 18 miles east of Ashley, N. Dak.
Jacob Schumacher, married to Katharina Koerner and lives on a farm 21 miles
northeast of Eureka. Christian Schumacher married Christina Kloosz, and lives
on a farm 18 miles northeast of Eureka. Friedericka Schumacher, who lives with
her father on the old homestead premises. August Schumacher, married Rose
Roesch, and is living in Eureka.

CHRISTIAN GOEHRING

An elderly gentleman leaned back in his chair and began to tell of his coming
to America from Russia. As the story progressed Mr. Goehring first related
incidents of the division of Dakota Territory into two states, North and South
Dakota. The state capitols were located at Yankton and Bismarck; South Dakota's
capitol later was changed to Pierre.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christian Goehring

South Dakota was admitted to the Union in 1889. Mr. Goehring witnessed the
rejoicing of the people over the fact that South Dakota was now one of the
great United States, and could look forward to a real part in national life.

Mr. Goehring came to America with his parents, Christoph Goehring and
Fredericka Gugel Goehring, both of German ancestry They located at Eureka,
Christian Goehring filing on a preemption claim and building a sod shanty.

In 1891, he was married to Magdalena Leicht. After his marriage he went into
partnership with his father-in-law. By farming in that way, the young married
couple escaped many of the hardships and privations met by other pioneers.

In 1906, Mr. and Mrs. Goehring moved to Eureka, renting their farm land. Mrs.
Goehring passed away in 1921.

Mr. Goehring again married in 1922, this time to Mrs. Karolina Schnuerle. They
are the owners of 600 acres of good farm land, free of debt, as is also their
home in town in which they now reside.

They are members of the Zion Lutheran Church in Eureka in which church Mr.
Goehring has been a trustee for a number of years.

In speaking of climatic conditions throughout the last 40 or 50 years, Mr.
Goehring said the winters had gradually been becoming milder, until the winter
of 1935-1936, when the mercury set a new record. Temperatures the latter year
dropped to 45 degrees below zero and thereabouts, thus comparable to a few
winters faced by early pioneers. Crops have also shown similar variations, as
have their prices. He stated he had sold wheat at 35 cents per bushel and again
at $2.50 per bushel, showing that although South Dakota residents have seen
hard times, the pendulum has again swung to the other direction bringing good
times occasionally.

JOHANN NIES

Looking back into the '80's when McPherson County prairie sod was first being
broken for farm land, many a picture is verbally drawn depicting the friendly
spirit of the first settlers.

Johann Nies relating the story of his personal experience, indicates that the
class of people first locating here were neighborly and helpful to anyone
needing aid. Regardless of the fact that they possessed the bare necessities of
life and lacked luxuries, these pioneers willingly shared both food and
shelter, however meager, with those less fortunate.

Johann Nies, who later settled with his family in the northern part of the
county, was born in Gluecksthal, Russia, in 1871. He came to America with his
parents, arriving here on April 27, 1889.

When his parents, Jacob Nies and Christina Guthjahr Nies, landed in America,
they possessed the total sum of $200.00. Already a number of their former
friends and neighbors in Russia had established homesteads in the Dakotas.
These people, ever glad to see acquaintances come to help settle the community,
offered lodging to the Nies family until they had their sod buildings completed.

The Nies family had, to begin their farming operations, two horses, two oxen,
three cows, a wagon, a plow, and a harrow. Johann Nies stayed home to help his
father, and after his father's death, continued managing the work on the home
farm. He believes that despite hard times and low prices for farm produce, the
pioneers still had a better opportunity for making money than do farmers today.

Mr. Nies was married to Christina Kurle, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Christian
Kurle, in the year 1893. By conscientiously pursuing their farm tasks, the
young couple gradually acquired more and more farm land. At one time they
possessed 1,760 acres which was a considerable increase over the 160 acres of
the first homestead.

Crop conditions were not always favorable. Mr. Nies recalls that in 1893, their
entire crop was hailed out. Drouth, also, did a great deal of damage to the
crops in the following years: 1894, 1898, 1900, 1901, 1902, 1911, 1933, 1934.
However, the drouths of those years were not to be compared to the devastating
drouth of 1936.

Being a conscientious and loving father, Mr. Nies gave some of his land to his
children that they might, in turn, have a fair chance to provide for their
families.

The children born to Mr. and Mrs. Nies are: Christina, Maria, Katharina, John,
Henry J., Margaretha, Karl, David, Martha, Jacob, Leo, Albert, Ruben and
Walter. Most of them live on farms in this community. However, Carl holds a
position as bookkeeper for the Nash-Finch Company in Minnesota, and Albert is
attending school in Grand Island, Nebraska.

MICHAEL HOFF, SR.

While industry and thrift brought success to many another pioneer, the example
of Michael Hoff brings forcibly to the attention, the possibility of
achievement as a result of these virtues. Coming here as a young man of 24,
penniless and with no equipment except two pairs of oxen, he eventually
acquired 3,200 acres of land in addition to much other property.

        Mr. and Mrs. Michael Hoff, Sr.

Mr. Hoff was born in Kassel, South Russia, on August 31, 1865. At the age of
14, he left his homeland with his parents, two brothers, Jacob and Johan, and
three sisters, Magdalena, Christina, and Katharina. His brother, Fred, had
preceded them to this country.

The family arrived in Yankton, Dakota Territory, in June, 1879. They were
greeted by an uncle, Johan Hoff, who took them to his farm near Menno, where
they remained until a suitable location was found. Here Michael lived with his
parents until 1889.

Attracted by the fact that there was more land available in the Eureka
territory, he left home and arrived here in March of that year. He immediately
took up a preemption and timber claim, and later filed on a homestead. After
erecting temporary buildings, be bought a plow and harrow on credit terms and
proceeded to break up the soil. In addition to clearing the land of stones, he
broke up 90 acres during the first spring -- an extraordinary accomplishment
for one man working with only a break plow and four oxen.

On June 6, 1889, Mr. Hoff was married to Katharina Gohl, Rev. Ulrich Reue
performing the ceremony. There were 10 children born to this union, namely:
Lydia, married to Fred D. Opp, a farmer 18 miles northeast of Eureka;
Katharina, married to Jacob Roesler, farming near Akaska, S. Dak.; Rosina,
married to Gottlieb Glesman, living south of Ashley, N. Dak.; Mary, married to
Christ J. Wolff, a farmer southeast of Eureka; Michael, Jacob, Henry and Emil,
all four farming 11 miles southeast of Eureka; Bertha, married to Christian
Geist of Eureka; and Martha, married to Edwin Voegele, a farmer near Trail
City, S. Dak.

Lack of money and credit, states Mr. Hoff, was the cause of most of early day
hardships. Many immigrants had very little or no money when they came here, and
during years when the crops failed they considered themselves very fortunate if
they were given sufficient credit to buy a few hundred pounds of flour. Even
low priced articles were sometimes hard to get without cash, as in the case of
one man who had to give a promissory note for 25 cents in payment of a mower
guard.

Mr. Hoff retired in 1921, and removed to Eureka. After the death of his wife in
1930, he was remarried to Mrs. Charlotte Beck.

GOTTLIEB D. BERTSCH

The Bertsch family, representing one of the most prominent and influential
clans in McPherson County, are located chiefly in Detmold and Petersburg
Townships. For the most part their ancestry can be traced back to three
brothers, namely, Jacob, Andrew, and our subject, Gottlieb D. Bertsch. These
three, their sisters, Elizabeth, Katharina, Rosina and Sophia, in company with
their parents, arrived in Eureka in 1889, having emigrated from their old home
in Johannesthal, South Russia. At Eureka, they were met by a certain Andreas
Brandt who was operating a general merchandise store at that time, and they
stayed with him until an uncle of the subject could be notified of their
arrival. This uncle, Gottlieb Bertsch, came soon afterwards and took them to
his home, where they stayed several days. They then filed on a homestead about
18 miles northeast of Eureka, and erected a temporary dwelling house. They were
forced to sleep on the ground for the first two weeks after their arrival,
their bedding still being in the course of transportation from the old country.
More permanent sod houses and other buildings were soon constructed, wells were
dug, farming equipment purchased, the soil cleared of rocks and soil prepared
for cultivation purposes. Their first crop was planted in 1890, and produced a
very meager yield. None of this was sold, but it was saved to be used as seed
for the next spring's planting.

The winter of 1892 was very severe and brought with it a heavy snowfall. This
made spring planting conditions ideal, and in early July a fine crop was
standing in the fields. However, Mr. Bertsch was being pressed for money, so he
had to cut and thresh a part of the crop before it had fully ripened. Great was
his disappointment, however, when upon inspecting some of it in his granary
later, he noticed that it was rotting away.

Gottlieb D. Bertsch was married twice. His first wife's maiden name was
Margaretha Lutz, and she died in September of 1931. Names and addresses of the
children of the first marriage are: Lydia D. Bertsch, Ashley, N. Dak. Her
husband was Adam A. Bertsch who died some years ago. August G. Bertsch, a
farmer at Ashley, N. Dak. Martha G. Humann, is the wife of Andrew Humann, a
merchant of Eureka, S. Dak. Adam G. Bertsch, farmer, lives on the old homestead
near Ashley, N. Dak.

In June of 1936, Gottlieb D. Bertsch re-married, this time to Effa Heill. The
old couple live in Venturia, North Dakota, at the present time.

Commenting on his success as a farmer, Mr. Bertsch attributes it to hard work
and thrift. He eventually acquired much land and was in a position to start
each one of his sons on a successful farming career.

FRED HILGEMANN

Fred Hilgemann, one of the best known and respected citizens of McPherson
County, was born in Lengerich, in the province of Westfalen, Germany, on
September 22, 1867. At the age of 21 he emigrated to America, arriving here in
the early part of the summer of 1888.

In the spring of 1889, his fiancee, Lina Kasenkamp, came to America, and the
couple were married in November of that year. The marriage ceremony was
performed by Mr. Hilgemann's uncle, the Rev. Ulrich Reue. The young couple
lived at the home of their uncle for two years following their marriage. Mr.
Hilgemann then filed claim on a homestead located 10 miles north of Eureka. He
owned a pair of oxen, a plow and a spade, and with this equipment erected a
house and barn. For the house, the sod was first plowed up and then cut into
pieces with the spade. These chunks were placed on top of each other and
plastered on the outside with mud, after which the roof, constructed of logs,
was placed in position on the walls. In building the barn, stones and mud were
used.

These buildings were sometimes buried under the snow during the winter, Mr.
Hilgeman recalls. On one occasion, after a three-days' blizzard, he had to
shovel a tunnel through the snow in order to get out of the house. While he was
working, he heard a noise above him and suddenly the snowdrift broke through
and a man came plunging down. The visitor turned out to be his uncle, the Rev.
Mr. Reue, who had come over to see how the Hilgeman family had fared in the
blizzard.

From 1904 to 1908, Fred Hilgemann served as Superintendent of Schools of
McPherson County. He was elected treasurer of this county in 1914, but was
taken ill and passed away after serving only a few months. In 1923, his widow
was re-married to Michael Schaffer of Alpena, South Dakota.

Two of Mr. Hilgemann's sons also held public office. Gustav was elected Clerk
of Courts of McPherson County in 1926, but died in February, 1927, a few months
after taking office. Calvin is at present Auditor of this county, having been
elected to that office in 1934.

Ten children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Hilgemann, two of whom, Fred and
Gustave, passed away in early manhood. Those surviving are: Emma, married to
Carl D. Sailer, a farmer near Burt, N. Dak.; Rudolph, a farmer living north of
Eureka; Esther, married to Arthur Werre, a farmer near Venturia, N. Dak.;
Theodore, of Alpena, S. Dak.; Ruth, married to Theodore Schaffer, a farmer near
Alpena, S. Dak.; Lena, married to Archie Yegge, a farmer near Virgil, S. Dak.;
Henry, living with his mother and stepfather in Alpena, S. Dak.

ADAM SCHICK

One of the brave company who bore the brunt of pioneer day hardships was Adam
Schick, born in Hoffnungsthal, South Russia, July 15, 1857.

        Mr. and Mrs. Adam Schick

When of age he was compelled to enter military service and served four years in
the army of the Czar

At the age of 22, in 1878, he was married to Magdalena Weller; one child was
born to this union. His wife died the following year, as did the child. In
1882, he married his wife's sister, Dorothea Weller, who was also a native of
Hoffnungsthal. Both marriage ceremonies were performed by the Reverend Mr.
Gustave Becker. His second wife was born February 8, 1861. Ten children blessed
this union, two of whom are dead. The surviving children are: Dorothea Heiser,
Mound City; Johannes Schick, Christina Libis, Rosa Libis, Katharina Bauer,
Lydia Kuebler and William Schick of Eureka, and Mrs. Emelia Bitz of Bowdle.

The Schick family arrived in McPherson County in 1889. They took up a homestead
11 miles southwest of Eureka. The elder Schicks removed to Eureka in 1916, and
the younger people continued farm operations.

Mr. Schick's retirement was prompted by failing health. After retiring, his
health was much improved. He died very suddenly, however, several years later.

Mrs. Schick still resides in her little home east of the Power & Light Company
building.

JACOB M. SERR

Jacob M. Serr came to America as a boy of eight with his father, Michael Serr,
and his mother, born Katharina Engel. The father was a native of Worms, and the
mother of Danzig. Jacob was born in the village of Nadenfeld on December 25,
1864.

On their arrival in America in 1873, they settled in the vicinity of Scotland,
Dakota Territory. They were more fortunate than many immigrants, bringing with
them about $4,000.00 in cash. As a consequence they acquired land in addition
to the homestead and timber claim they took up near Scotland. They prospered in
the new country, but felt cramped when they were unable to provide necessary
range for the considerable herd of cattle which developed. The elder Serr then
sold his holdings and retired to a home in Scotland while Jacob came to Eureka,
bringing with him three carloads of horses and cattle, which he disposed of to
advantage to farmers in the Eureka section. He arrived in Eureka on April 3,
1889.

Shortly after his arrival in Eureka, on July 14, 1889, to be exact, he was
married to Christina Liedle, whom he had known while residing near Scotland.
They removed to a home of their own and started to do their share in the
building of the new town. Except for two years or so, when the Serrs went to
the Yankton section, where they farmed, they have resided in Eureka and Mr.
Serr has been a part of its business life.

For 26 years he operated a dray line and was local agent for the Hamm Brewing
Company of St. Paul. He was also associated with the Standard Oil Company,
hauling their products in wagon tanks to points as far distant as Bangor,
one-time county seat of Walworth County.

He has now retired from active business and he and his wife are situated in a
comfortable home in the south part of Eureka.

        Mr. and Mrs. Jacob M. Serr

The Serrs have seven children, all of whom survive and reside in widely
separated parts of the country. Otto is a druggist in Winnipeg, Manitoba;
Edwin, a rural mail carrier in Eureka; Mrs. Alma Bender also resides in her
native town. Mrs. Ella Oberlander resides in Ipswich; Calvin is a telephone
lineman at Britton, South Dakota; William, a creamery employee at Yakima,
Washington; and Esther a bookkeeper in Eureka.

JOHN STOCK

John Stock, who was one of the prominent citizens of Eureka, was born in
Gluecksthal, South Russia, on January 27, 1863. He was the son of Johann and
Magdalena Stock, and was baptized and confirmed by the Reverend Kerm, a
Lutheran minister. He migrated to America in 1889, arriving in Eureka by train
in April of that year. He found employment at first on the farm of Johann Knapp
and later with Friedrich Hoff.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Stock

In 1890, he was married to Margaret Becker, and in that year commenced farming
on his homestead and timber claim located about six miles east of Eureka. He was
engaged in this pursuit until 1905, when he located in Eureka and operated a
livery barn in partnership with Jacob L. Klein. Three years later, he built his
own livery barn, which he eventually sold to Adam Preszler.

He became secretary of the Northwest German Farmers Mutual Insurance Company in
1911, which position he held for sixteen years. He was also active in politics
and has held various public offices, serving as deputy sheriff and later
sheriff of McPherson county for two terms. He was a member of the board of
education of his district and was deputy county assessor for nine years. In
Eureka, he served for some years as alderman of his ward, and was a director of
the Guaranty State Bank and the Farmers Investment Company.

Ten children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Stock, four dying in infancy. Those
still living are: Carl J., residing on the old homestead east of Eureka; Rosa,
married to Jacob Stabler, a farmer 10 miles southwest of Eureka; Jacob,
formerly sheriff of Campbell County and now residing in Mound City, S. Dak.;
William, employed by the Northern Power and Light Company of Lemmon, S. Dak.;
Mary, residing in Isabel, S. Dak.; Bertha, married to Walter Froh, engaged in
the newspaper business in Ashley, N. Dak.

Following a prolonged illness, Mr. Stock passed away in May, 1936. His widow
still resides in Eureka.

MICHAEL HENNE

Michael Henne, Eureka resident in pioneer times, came to America as a small boy
with his parents. The family first came to Marion Junction, South Dakota, on
May 14, 1887. The next year the father rented a farm one mile and a half west
of Freeman, South Dakota. "Mike," as he was immediately called, started out for
himself by hiring out to a farmer who lived nine miles north of Marion
Junction. On the 9th of November, 1889, the parents moved to Long Lake, South
Dakota, where the father, Michael Henne, Sr., took up a government homestead.

On the 15th of November, 1890, "Mike" Henne was engaged as a clerk in the store
and implement business of Jacob Hieb of Marion Junction, remaining there until
the 15th of November, 1892, at which time he left for Eureka, South Dakota,
arriving there on the 18th of that month. He had intended making a short visit
to his parents at Long Lake, but liked Eureka so well that when he was offered
a position with Philip Martel, he accepted and remained in Eureka for 10 years.
Mr. Henne worked for Fred and Julius Schamber who then operated a hardware and
implement business, and later for the Stoller & Hepperle general merchandise
concern. When he first arrived in Eureka, the city hall was half completed. The
city had 18 saloons and 14 general merchandise stores, and many other business
places, as the city was then the biggest primary wheat and grain market in
North America. There were 36 elevators and flat houses engaged in buying and
shipping grain, employing 164 men. In the year 1892, the wheat price was from
72 to 75 cents a bushel. The Eureka territory then extended to Linton, Napoleon
and Ashley, many settlers traveling over 100 miles from this direction to trade
there.

In 1896, Mr. Henne joined the Eureka Fire Department and still possesses a
picture of that band of fire fighters. In the year 1898, the city already
having installed a water system, purchased the first fire equipment, all of
which had to be moved by hand power.

Mr. Henne was married at Scotland, South Dakota, to Fredericka Serr, on January
16, 1898, and brought his bride to Eureka.

In 1902, he left Eureka, in company with Pete Schott, Anton Baron, the
Feinsteins, Haftners, and Sam Klaudt for the new town of Zeeland, on the
Milwaukee railroad.

In Zeeland, Mr. Henne engaged in the mercantile business which he followed
until about two years ago. He has always been a public-minded citizen, serving
on the first school board of Zeeland, and having served many terms on the city
and school board of this community. In 1923, he was elected and served a term
as Representative to the State Legislature. He also acted as postmaster of
Zeeland for many years. At the general election in November, 1936, the people
of McIntosh County elected him sheriff, which office he assumed on January 6,
1937, and now holds.

DANIEL BITZ

A huge ship arrived in New York harbor, and among other passengers coming down
the gangplank, was a young married couple carrying several bundles, in fact,
all they possessed in the way of material things. They found their way to a
railway station and announced their destination as Eureka, South Dakota. The
year was 1889, and travel by train was not as swift as in present times, but in
a few days they arrived in Eureka.

Thus another pioneer came to Eureka in the person of Daniel Bitz. He was born
in Old Freidenthal, Russia, of German parents, on February 10, 1862. He was
married to Fredericka Waltz while still living in Russia. When Mr. and Mrs.
Bitz came to McPherson County, they had no money so stayed with a brother for
four years. Buying a team of oxen, Mr. Bitz later established a homestead on
Section 25, Township 126, Range 73, now Bauer Township. Through years of hard
work and economical living, Mr. Bitz acquired 1,000 acres of excellent farm
land.

In 1916, the Bitz family moved to Eureka. Here Mr. Bitz and his son Henry,
purchased a general merchandise store from F. Honoroff. They operated the store
for several years, but due to the ill health of Henry, the store was sold.

The children now living are: John, Katharina, Elizabeth, Henry, Ernest, and
Christian.

Though Mrs. Bitz is rather frail, Mr. Bitz still enjoys quite good health. They
are often seen taking little trips to neighboring towns, Mr. Bitz driving his
own car. They are members and active leaders in the Baptist church of Eureka.

THEODORE J. P. GIEDT

Theodore J. P. Giedt undoubtedly was one of the foremost and influential
residents of McPherson County. A son of John P. and Elizabeth Giedt, he was
born at Danzig, South Russia on the 9th of November, 1873. His ancestors lived
at, or near Danzig, Prussia, a century or more earlier, and with a number of
others emigrated and settled in southern Russia. In May, 1886, his parents came
to America, settling in McIntosh County, now in North Dakota.

Young Theodore had already received a good education in the schools in Russia,
and now his studies were continued in Danzig, North Dakota. Here he was first
imbued with the ambition to become a leader of his people. His first grade
school teacher, a Miss Robinson, seeing latent possibilities in the boy,
encouraged him to seek a career, and subsequent events in his life have proven
the wisdom of her advice.

In March, 1889, Theodore J. P. Giedt came to South Dakota, and in Eureka found
employment as an apprentice in the printing office of Flinn and Lutgen, who
were then publishing the Eureka Independent. In April of the same year, they
added a German weekly, "Die Eureka Post." The following winter Mr. Lutgen
acquired the ownership of the whole plant and Mr. Giedt was made its manager,
acting in large measure as editor of both papers and also doing much of the
type setting and mechanical work about the office. In the fall of 1892, in
connection with M. O. Ronayne, he purchased the two papers and plant from M.
Lutgen and in the summer of 1894, added another paper, the McPherson County
Herald, which was later moved to Leola with Mr. Ronayne in charge. The
Independent suspended and an English edition of the Eureka Post was thereafter
issued in its stead. Mr. Giedt was in charge of the Eureka papers, but when
elected to public office he disposed of his newspaper interests and removed to
Leola, where he remained until the spring of 1903.

        Mr. Theodore J. P. Geidt

During the time he was engaged in the newspaper business he had pressed forward
with his studies, completing a course in the Winona (Minn.) Business College
with the class of 1891. In 1903, he became a law student in the University of
South Dakota, and in April, 1904, he was admitted to practice in the courts of
the state. He then began his long career in the legal profession, which he
followed up to the time of his death.

On the 25th of January, 1895, while on a visit to his old home in Russia, Mr.
Giedt was united in marriage to Augusta E. Pritzkau. The ceremony took place in
Dolinskoje, Russia. There were six children born to them, Lily, Oswald,
Cecelia, Florence, L. Joanne, and Walvin R., the latter three being the only
surviving ones. Following the death of his wife in 1907, he was re-married to
Emma N. Radak. One child, Virginia, who died in childhood, was born to him by
his second marriage. His second wife passed away in 1918.

The esteem in which Theodore J. P. Giedt was held by his fellow citizens is
attested to by the numerous public offices and other prominent positions he has
held. He was clerk of the courts in 1897 and 1898, and the following year was
elected county auditor, which position he filled until 1903. In 1907, he was
elected states attorney, serving in that capacity until 1910. That year
legislative honors were conferred upon him in his election as representative in
the general assembly for a term of two years. He was re-elected in 1912. He was
again elected states attorney in 1914 and in 1925, and became a member of the
State Senate in 1927, to which he was re-elected without opposition in 1930. At
the time of his death he held the position of county judge. As city attorney,
city auditor, and mayor of Eureka, he served a number of terms, and for two
terms was a member of the Eureka Board of Education and acted as its president.

He was also associated with various business interests, among which were the
McPherson County Abstract Company of Leola, the Leola Mercantile Company, German
Land and Loan Company of Eureka, and banks in Eureka, Bowdle, and Hosmer. He
was a member of the First Baptist Church of Aberdeen, South Dakota, and was
identified with several fraternal organizations, in which he held prominent
positions. His military record covers connection with Company A, of the South
Dakota National Guard, in which he enlisted in 1901, eventually becoming judge
advocate general.

A republican of the progressive type, Mr. Giedt played a prominent role in the
political activities of his community and state. He was one of the earnest
working members of the legislature during his connection therewith, and was
considered one of the leaders of the house. He has been identified with
practically every public enterprise of this city and county and has left the
impress of his individuality upon the movements which have been put forth to
promote development and progress.

His death occurred on October 17, 1935, at the home of his daughter, Florence,
and her husband, W. W. Rabey, at Long Lake, South Dakota. His memory has
recently been honored by the South Dakota Legislature of 1937, by the adoption
of a resolution introduced by Paul O. Kretschmar, senator for McPherson County.
The resolution reads: "Whereas, it has pleased God to take from this world a
former member of this body, Theodore J. P. Giedt, of Eureka, McPherson County,
South Dakota, and, Whereas, Mr. Giedt, who has served as a member of both the
Senate and the House of Representatives, during which time he was a highly
respected member and citizen who enjoyed the esteem and confidence of his
fellow men and Whereas, in the death of Mr. Giedt, this state has lost an
outstanding man, an effective and esteemed legislator, a professional leader,
and an upright citizen, now therefore, Be It Resolved, That this house express
its condolence to the bereaved and to the citizens of McPherson County, South
Dakota. Further, Be It Resolved, that a copy of this resolution be spread upon
the Senate Journal and copies forwarded to immediate members of the family of
the deceased."

MATTHEIS GOEHRING

Stories full of thrill and adventure related by Mattheis Goehring, might make
young modern people wish they had lived in the times when Indians were savage
and caused not a little consternation on the prairies.

Mr. Goehring was employed in a livery stable owned by Andrew Wallman during the
year 1890. At that time the Messiah Craze, a religious uprising between Indians
in South Dakota, was taking place. While the war was chiefly between Indian
tribes, many white people living near troubled areas were massacred.

The Sioux tribe, living on the Standing Rock Indian Reservation, was restless,
apparently beginning to harbor a rebellious spirit peculiar to their race at
that time. In the fall of 1890, a message came to Eureka settlers to prepare
for an unfriendly visit from the enraged Indians. Reports indicated that white
people between the Missouri River and Eureka were being scalped and murdered.
Excitement ran high in this territory.

Telephones did not exist here on the prairie at that time, so in order that
families living on homesteads might be warned of the coming attack, men
volunteered to ride on the fastest horses from homestead to homestead carrying
the terrifying message. The riders would arrive at some homes in the daytime
and farther ones at night.

The homestead people upon hearing the terrifying report would hastily prepare
and come to Eureka. The women and children grabbed what few clothes they had,
tied them in bed sheets, and packed the food on hand into the wagon. Meanwhile
the men were turning loose the live stock and hitching the teams to wagons. It
was necessary to turn the live stock loose and take all possessions possible,
for if the Indians did come they were certain to burn and destroy everything
left behind. Then the trip was made to Eureka, the horses being urged onward at
full speed that they might arrive ahead of the Indians.

Fright, horror, and often tragedy was the result of these Indian scares,
although they proved mere scares. Occasionally, an incident provoking laughter
presented itself, pitifully humorous though it may be. Mr. Goehring tells of
one amusing incident resulting from the make-shift garb of some settlers
because of their haste to reach town and safety. A man came into the livery
stable, Mr. Goehring relates, scared out of his wits, so to speak. The man wore
a fur coat, securely fastened, which he did not remove during an entire day and
night in town, though the weather was pleasant. Some curious townfolk
questioned him as to the reason for not removing his fur coat. He replied that
when the news came the Indians were only a few miles distant, so in his haste,
left for town attired in a nightgown, a fur coat, and shoes.

Mattheis Goehring is a native of South Russia, having been born in Marinburg,
April 17, 1868. His father was Christoph Goehring and his mother was Fredericka
Gugel Goehring. His parents came to America in 1889, but he could not come for
another year.

Mr. Goehring was married to Mrs. Katharina Leicht, another pioneer, who came to
America in 1884. Mrs. Goehring was born June 15, 1855. When they were married
Mrs. Goehring owned quite considerable property, so the couple escaped many of
the hardships suffered by other early settlers.

This couple had three children: Katharina Roesler; John M., who farms the old
homestead; and Jacob M., who is in the mercantile business in Eureka.

When the United States entered the World war, their son, Jacob M., was drafted.
While in the army, he served in the 305th Infantry of the 77th Division and saw
active service overseas. He was at one time badly wounded from gunshot and
shrapnel. Upon his return from the war service, he was married to Emelia
Weixel, daughter of Philip Weixel. Both Mr. and Mrs. Goehring take an active
interest in the American Legion and the Ladies Auxiliary. Mr. Goehring is now
vice commander of District 14 of the American Legion. He is also treasurer of
the Zion Lutheran Church of Eureka, and has served as treasurer of the Eureka
Independent Fire Department for six years, as well as serving as chief for some
time.

Mr. and Mrs. Mattheis Goehring at present reside in their own home in the city
of Eureka. Both are looking forward to the celebration of Eureka's Golden
Jubilee.

CHRISTIAN G. WOLFF

The citizens of Eureka will recall an old man coming to town a few years back
with a team of horses hitched to a buggy, sitting very proud and erect on the
seat of the buggy. This man was Christian G. Wolff, better known as "Bruelle
Wolff." Mr. Wolff was born in Bergdorf, South Russia on the 9th of February,
1861. In October, 1889, he, his wife, formerly Katherina Gutjahr, and family,
consisting of two children, Henry and John, arrived in McPherson County. At
that time they possessed about $300.00 in money. Mr. Wolff immediately
purchased some farming equipment and then filed on 320 acres of land in Moscow
Township about 17 miles southeast of Eureka. His only neighbor during that time
was Jacob Fuchs, who had been living in the same township for more than five
years.

        Mr. and Mrs. Christian G. Wolff

During that first winter Christian G. Wolff continued his profession from the
old country, and taught a country school, receiving therefor the sum of $35.00
for the entire year. In addition to that he had to walk four miles every
morning to get to the school house.

In the spring of 1890, he purchased some additional farming equipment
consisting of three untrained oxen, two cows, an old wagon, a plow and drag, a
pick and crowbar, and planted his first crop. The oxen were very unruly and Mr.
Wolff encountered much difficulty in training them. One ox laid down and all
attempts to make him get up were of no avail. Finally Mrs. Wolff tied an apron
around his mouth as tight as she possibly could, and the dumb ox immediately
got up.

In 1891, Mr. Wolff was very instrumental in establishing a Lutheran church in
his neighborhood. He gave the church its present name of "Salems." The first
pastor of the church was Reverend Roeschaur of Leola. Christian Wolff for years
was instrumental in the life of the church, both as a Sunday school teacher,
and as the person who read the services when the pastor was absent.

Conditions for the Wolffs during their first years in this county were like
those of other pioneers. They had good crops and poor crops. In 1895, an
exceptional streak of bad luck hit Mr. Wolff. He had just recently bought some
