new horses, and had them in his sod barn, when three of them were struck by
lightning and immediately killed. This just about drove him into bankruptcy,
but with the help of a kind friend who loaned him the money with which to buy
more horses, he was able to resume his farming operations. At about the same
time he lost 14 head of cattle because of a blackleg plague that hit the
country. This again caused him great hardship. In addition to that he was
stricken with an optical ailment and for a time was totally blind. Finally with
the help of an expert doctor and at great expense to himself, he was able to
restore his eyesight through the use of glasses. From this time on he was known
as "Bruelle Wolff." Speaking of those times, Mr. Wolff at one time remarked to
a friend that the restoration of his eyesight was the greatest joy that he ever
experienced. With a wife and six small children to support without the use of
his eyes, one can readily appreciate his joy when he could again see and
provide for his family.

In 1898, Christian G. Wolff was honored by being elected as a director of the
present Northwest German Farmers Mutual Insurance Company which is operating in
Eureka.

The Wolffs were the parents of eleven children, two of whom died in infancy.
Mr. Wolff was able to provide each of them with a farm when they were ready to
set out on their own. The names of the children are as follows: John, Fred,
Jonathan, Gottlieb, all of Eureka; Henry, Lemmon, S. Dak.; Christ, Hillsview,
S. Dak.; Jakob, Lemmon S. Dak.; Katharina Wageman, Hosmer, S. Dak.; Magdalena
Rau, died in 1926.

JOHN THURN

Among the immigrants who came to America on the crest of the tide which swept
in from South Russia was John Thurn, who landed in New York May 18, 1889.
Eureka was the first objective of his journey and on arriving here he stayed
with his brother, Fred Thurn, who had a homestead six miles northwest of the
city.

Later he accompanied his brother-in-law to the latter's homestead, located
about five miles northwest of the present site of Wishek, North Dakota. Here
the two built a one-room sod shanty, hauling timbers for the roof from the
Missouri River. Their water supply gave them much trouble, the only supply
being a nearby lake.

In the winter of 1890, that section experienced an exceptionally severe winter,
snow falling to a depth that made tunneling necessary in order to gain access
to the buildings.

In the early nineties he secured employment with a contractor whose work took
him over much of North Dakota. But this line of endeavor did not seem to him to
offer much of a future so he sought employment on a farm, working for Peter
Schott for two years. During this period he became ambitious to own a farm of
his own and as a result located on a homestead south of Eureka.

Wages in those days were small and the young Thurn had been unable to
accumulate much capital. His farming operations, as a result, were necessarily
hampered by lack of equipment. Mr. Thurn recalls his first crop was sown
entirely by hand and threshed by driving the horses over it.

But industry, good judgment and frugality brought their reward and in time Mr.
Thurn acquired a fine home, together with many of the comforts afforded by an
advancing civilization. John Thurn still resides on his original homestead,
making his home with his son.

JACOB KEIM

Jacob Keim was born in Gluecksthal, Russia, on July 24, 1859. His parents were
Katharina Schaeffer Keim and Jacob Keim, Sr.

When he was 21 years old, Jacob Keim was married to Katharina Schanzenbach,
daughter of Johan and Barbara Goetz Schanzenbach. They remained in Russia until
1899 when, accompanied by 25 other couples, they left for America. In the old
country farms were small and there was but little chance for advancement. Word
had come of the country across the ocean where land was free and fortune and
adventure awaited one. So this group journeyed to America to locate on this
free land.

Typical of people who had never traveled or spoken any language save their own,
the entire timid company agreed to remain together upon reaching American
shores. They planned to travel together to one community and live together in
one settlement, thereby keeping their new homes as much like those in Russia as
possible. Before they reached McPherson County, however, their plans changed.
Some had relatives at Hosmer and Roscoe, near whom they wished to settle. So
the original agreement was broken, and only a few families continued onward to
Eureka, the end of the railroad.

The Keim family made their temporary home with an uncle and aunt, Mr. and Mrs.
Phillip Keim, whose homestead was located two miles north of Eureka.
Disappointed because they were unable to obtain any free land, the Keims were
homesick for Russia, but lacking the return fare, they were forced to make a
final attempt to purchase a suitable claim. Fortunately, one Gustaf Helb wanted
to sell his tree claim at that time, so Jacob Keim purchased it. The
improvements on the claim consisted of a sod house and a barn. The purchase
price was $225.00, but Mr. Keim could not make the payment in cash. He paid
$100 in cash and a mortgage was placed on the live stock -- two steers and 15
chickens -- for the balance.  He then made a trip to Leola, the county seat,
and filed a preemption claim on the land.

That winter Jacob Keim worked in town at odd jobs. He unloaded whole carloads
of coal for 75 cents. Meals consisted of but little more than Arbuckle coffee,
which could be bought at the rate of 11 pounds for $1.00, and bread. The
chickens froze, so the family had neither eggs nor meat.

When spring came, the Keims were despondent, for farm implements and seed must
be purchased. But they saw the homes of Johan Strobel, Karl Hausauer, and Jacob
Bieber, all living north of the Keims, and resolved that if these families
could live here, so could they. They then gave up all thought of leaving, and
settled down to make a permanent home.

Two young unbroken oxen were purchased, but they were too weak to pull a plow.
John Keim, Sr., also had two oxen that could not pull a plow, so the two
brothers hitched their four oxen together, and thus succeeded in breaking up
the land. Rainfall was scarce that summer; a meager crop of wheat and flax was
reaped. The wheat was threshed as it had always been threshed in Russia, and
the flax threshing was left to the women.

The men found work at the rate of $1.00 a day on a threshing machine, and then
found work at $25.00 per month on the farms of Christ Zelmer and Rich Larson.
They walked 27 miles to visit their families and to collect the money due them
for the threshing work, only to find that the men operating the threshing
machine had left the country without paying the workers.

Mr. Zelmer could not pay Jacob Keim in money, so Mr. Keim had to take a cow,
which had the habit of jumping pasture fences, and eight sacks of corn in
payment for his work on Zelmer's farm.  That winter he sold one of his
mortgaged steers to buy flour, but the family lacked other foodstuffs.

A few weeks later Jacob Keim and Daniel Schanzenbach agreed to husk corn for a
bachelor farmer of Swedish nationality, for a fourth-bushel share. In four days
they earned a wagon-box full of corn. The bachelor, however, was not a good
cook, so the men had to eat potatoes and turnips that the cook forgot to wash
before cooking, and bread of very poor quality.

Soon things took a turn for the better. These people were poor, but with
courage and determination they cared for their crops and in time reaped good
harvests. For several years, however, Mr. Keim had to do outside work to
support his family.

At one time he and several other men built a sod barn for a farmer living in
the Missouri River bottom. Returning from the river, they passed the farm home
of Jacob Ackerman. The family had all their possessions piled into two wagons,
ready to leave the country. They informed Mr. Keim and the men with him that
Indians were coming. He told the Ackermans that he had just come from the
river, and had seen no Indians. The Ackermans were reassured. but the travelers
were worried and hurried on to Eureka to their families. They passed the
Feinstein farm where 40 families had gathered to fight the Indians. Eureka,
too, had received the disturbing news. Charles Pfeffer had ordered a special
train held for the women and children in the event that the Indians should make
good their threat. The whole thing was only a scare, as the Indians never
crossed the river.

Several years later, conditions improved and Mr. Keim, John Keim, Sr., and
Solomon Isaak formed a partnership. They bought and sold grain, flour, and
machinery. In 1910, Jacob Keim sold his interest in this partnership to his
son, John J. Keim. John P. Kiesz and Fred Klein bought the interests of John
Keim, Sr., and Solomon Isaak, and the three new partners continued in business.

When Jacob Keim retired from active life he owned 1,760 acres of land and an
elevator in Akaska, as well as other minor business interests.

The Keims today have a comfortable home on the north side of Eureka, where they
reside. They have five children, namely: Katharina (deceased), Maria, Lena,
Martha, and John J. Keim. John J. Keim and Jacob Kiesz for some time owned the
firm known as "Keim & Kies," and were the Ford dealers. John J. Keim now
operates an independent repair shop.


In 1910, Jacob and John Keim, and John Schanzenbach of Selby, made a trip to
Russia to visit relatives and friends. They returned the next year and have
remained here since.

HENRY GROSS, SR.

Henry Gross, Senior, was born of German parents in Gluecksthal, Russia,
December 5, 1855. He came to Eureka, South Dakota, in 1889, filing on a
homestead in Jackson township.

Mr. Gross experienced the hardships typical of pioneer days. His first home on
the prairie, was erected by work of his own hand. The only materials available
were sod and stones. The breaking of the sod for his first crops was done by
faithful oxen. For the most part, fairly plentiful harvests were reaped,
enabling Mr. Gross to gradually pay all debts and acquire 960 acres of land.

Today Mr. Gross is of the opinion that future farmers will never be able to
earn money for their labors as easily as was done in pioneer days. Farm produce
prices then were not high, but with ample production and a steady demand a
regular income was received.

The first marriage of Mr. Gross was to Christina Herman. They had nine
children, namely: Jacob, born in 1881; Rose, July, 1884; Henry, September,
1886; Christina, August, 1888; Eva, July, 1890; Christian, March, 1892; Martin,
June, 1896; Edward, May, 1898; Martha, September, 1900.

Some time after the death of his first wife, Mr. Gross again married, this time
to Mrs. Bender, also a Eureka pioneer.

Although rather elderly, Mr. and Mrs. Gross are enjoying good health and are
always happy to recall old times.

PHILIPP FLEMMER

Arriving in Eureka on October 1, 1889, was Philipp Flemmer, just recently of
age, having been born October 1, 1868. He left his native Gluecksthal, in South
Russia, to join the hundreds of his countrymen who had earlier gone to the
great new land to the west, where they hoped for better things than they might
expect in the land of their birth.

        Mr. and Mrs. Philipp Flemmer

On December 26, 1898, he was married to Katharina Schrum of Ashley, North
Dakota, where the ceremony was performed. Mrs. Flemmer was a native of
Beresina, Bessarabia, where she was born May 28, 1880. She had emigrated with
her parents, arriving in America in May, 1888.

Flemmer's first job in Eureka was with the Serr & Quast dray line. The young
couple, however, homesteaded about eight miles north of Java, spending three
years on the claim. Later they moved to Eureka where they have since resided
continuously. He was employed by the Eureka Bazaar for a long period, but has
now retired from active work.

The Flemmers are the parents of six children, all of whom, with the exception
of Chris, who operates a service station in Eureka, live at distant points from
their native heath.

Emma Harr resides in Stockton, California; Adeline Stroup at Cottage Grove,
Oregon; Mary Hepper at Mobridge, S. Dak.; Anna Graeves at Redfield, S. Dak.; and
Gertrude Kary at Bowdle, S. Dak.

Mr. and Mrs. Flemmer reside in a comfortable home in Eureka.

HEINRICH NIES

An elderly gentleman, though not loquacious, today relates a story not unlike
that of other early-day settlers in this territory. The story may vary a bit
from the biographies of other pioneers, but it reveals that he experienced
similar joys, sorrows, successes, and disappointments.

Heinrich Nies was born in April, 1875, of parents of German descent. His
father's name was Jacob Nies and that of his mother was Christina Guthjahr. At
the time of young Heinrich's birth, the family lived in Russia, but hoped to
leave for America some day in the future.

In the year 1889, when Heinrich was a boy of 14, the parents with their little
family came to America, directly to McPherson County. Here they filed a
preemption claim and later a homestead was founded.

A number of years later, Heinrich Nies was married to Sophia Bertsch, who was
born May 12, 1878. Seven children blessed this union. Namely, they are: Anna,
Emma, Carl H., Mathilda, Esther, Benjamin, and Christina. Mr. Nies with his
family has acquired 960 acres of fertile farm land, and today have a well
equipped farm. Mr. Nies is a prominent resident in the community and is an
active worker in the church of his faith.

LUDWIG ORTHWEIN

Among the old-timers living in Eureka who can give interesting accounts of days
when this part of South Dakota was settled, Ludwig Orthwein must be remembered.

He was born in Rumania on April 3, 1889. While there he was married to Rose
Loda, and together the young couple came to America in May, 1889. They came to
Eureka by train and traveled to Long Lake, Dakota Territory; there they filed
on a homestead.

The new home was 28 miles from Eureka and all farm produce was hauled this
distance. The Orthwein family had no money, and in order to begin their farming
operations, borrowed a small sum to build their home. By industry and economy,
they were able to repay their loan, and gradually gain a surplus amount of cash
with which to purchase goods to operate a country store. They eventually became
owners of 480 acres of farm land.

In the year 1912, they started a creamery at Long Lake. A few years later they
quit this business and moved to Eureka. Here they operated a general
merchandise store until 1927, when they retired.

        Mr. and Mrs. Ludwig Orthwein

Mr. and Mrs. Orthwein had one son, Fred. The latter died several years ago. Up
to the time of his death he was in partnership with his father in the store.

The Orthweins have never tired of watching the changes take place in the
country, and are always glad to see new improvements being made. They still
reside in Eureka.

FRIEDERICK OBENAUER

Friederick Obenauer, a native of Hoffnungsthal, Bessarabia, South Russia, was
born April 17, 1860. He was married to Miss Carolina Sackman, also a native of
Russia. They were the parents of the following children: Adam F. Obenauer,
dealer in farm implements, Eureka; John F., dealer in real estate, Eureka, S.
Dak.; Fred, farmer near Eureka; Magdalena, Eureka; and Bertha O. Ketterling,
Mercer, N. Dak.

        Mr. and Mrs. Friederick Obenauer

Mr. and Mrs. Obenauer arrived in McPherson County by train April 17, 1889,
possessing about $90.00. They 1ocated on a homestead 11 miles northeast of
Eureka, remaining there for one year. They moved into Campbell County in 1890,
where they spent the winter. However, due to inability to meet the financial
demand on a down payment, they lost the new land, and on June 21, 1891, moved to
Walworth County, where they again took up a homestead.

Here Mr. Obenauer was determined to make up for lost time and busied himself
with his farm equipment, which consisted of two oxen and a cow. Later three
horses were used for farm work. He also owned four oxen. One day two of the
oxen died. The next day in the forenoon he hitched up three horses and a cow to
do the work. In the afternoon he replaced the cow with an ox and his wife had
to lead the ox so the necessary work could be done, such as cutting grass. At
one time he owned four oxen and these he used mainly for plowing. There was a
small lake on the Obenauer farm, and whenever Mr. Obenauer approached it, the
oxen would make a bee line for the middle of it. The first time this occurred,
he had a most difficult time bringing them back to shore. He sent the dog in
after them and finally they came out. But Mr. Obenauer found a cure for them.
He attached a fifty-foot rope to their harness and pulled them out with the aid
of the dog which was sent into the water after them. At another time Mr.
Obenauer was sitting on a grass-mower, and when the oxen again approached the
lake, they plunged right in; but he stayed right on the mower until he finally
got them out. When the time for cutting grass came he sat on the mower and his
wife led the oxen. Later he cut hay with the aid of the horses, and his wife
raked it, using the oxen.

In 1896, he bought eighteen cows. The milk and cream produced by these cows
provided nicely for their provisions.

One son, John F., achieved considerable success in the political world. He
represented the 5th district of McPherson County as commissioner from
1922-1930, and served as chairman of the board when the new court house was
built. He is generally conceded to be the only chairman serving on the board
who was elected from the Eureka district.

In 1919, Mr. Obenauer and his family moved to Eureka, where they are at present
residing.

JACOB OTENBACHER, SR.

Among the Germans in South Russia, who did not relish the prospects of several
years in the army for his family of boys was Jacob Ottenbacher in
Hoffnungsthal. He had been born there January 26, 1845, and had married a young
woman named Katharina Bauer, born in 1843. By 1889, they had a family of six
children, four of them boys.

Economic conditions were not so good in Russia, but somehow Ottenbacher managed
to provide transportation for himself and his family to America. Unfortunately
there wasn't enough money to bring the entire family to their destination in
the Eureka vicinity. Accordingly the family remained in New York while the
father and Andrew proceeded to Eureka to arrange for the trip.

They were successful in their quest and so the family came on. But it was too
late in the year to begin farming, so arrangements were made for them to spend
the winter with John Schick. The next spring, the family started bravely out to
homestead in Campbell County, locating on Section 30, Township 126, Range 74.

The farming equipment was the traditional one, oxen, a cow, a few chickens and
a breaking plow, was all they could afford. But Jacob Ottenbacher had been a
blacksmith in his native country and that trade stood him in good stead in the
new land. Not only could he do his own blacksmith work, but helped his
neighbors, a service they were undoubtedly glad to get, since it was a long,
long way to town in all parts of Campbell County at that time.

An interesting relic of Ottenbacher's blacksmithing days is the hammer he made
with his own hands, using it in his shop in Russia and later on the Campbell
county farm. The old hammer is a prized possession of the family.

There were seven children in the Ottenbacher family, five of whom still
survive, namely: Jacob, Johannes, and John, residing near Eureka; Andrew,
Zeeland, N. Dak.; and Christian, near Herreid, S. Dak. Dorothea died as a child
of six, and Christina (Mrs. Jacob Schnaible) died in 1933.

Both the elder Ottenbachers are now dead, the father passing on October 18,
1919, and the mother in 1921.

HENRY STRAUB AND FAMILY

Henry Straub, a pioneer merchant of Eureka, holds a
record of forty-three continuous years in the furniture and funeral directing
business. He came to Eureka in the year 1890 and has had the opportunity to
watch and aid in it. progress.

He was born in Neu-Lustdorf, Russia, on the 27th of November, 1869, the son of
Johann Straub and Barbara Beck Straub. The grand-parents were Johannes Straub
and Louisa Grossman Straub, and Friedrich Beck and Margaret Ott Beck. The
grand-parents were born in Wuertenburg, Germany. In 1804 they migrated to South
Russia and established a permanent residence there as peasants.

When Henry Straub was 10 years of age, his father died. One year later his
mother died, leaving him to continue the journey of life without the guidance
of parents. Having been left an orphan he made his home with an uncle on a
farm. When 18 years old employment was obtained in the Maerckling Co., a
furniture manufacturing concern of Odessa, South Russia. With this firm the
cabinet maker's trade was learned, which was to serve him as a foundation for
his life's work. He remained with this firm until he decided to journey to
America in quest of the freedom that prompted so many to migrate during those
years.

Arriving in New York on the 27th of June, 1890, he worked at various odd jobs
for two months. After earning sufficient money to enable him to continue his
trip westward, he left the big city. The lure of free land and the big
opportunities offered a young man were a thrilling adventure.

        Mr. and Mrs. Henry Straub

Henry Straub arrived in Eureka on the 10th of August, 1890. Two dollars was the
total wealth he possessed upon his arrival. George Lill operated the restaurant
where he ate his first meal in Eureka. This place of business was located where
the present city jail now stands.

For the remainder of the year he made his home with a sister, Mrs. Henry
Huether, in the Sutley territory. Farm labor was done at a wage of 75 cents per
day. The following year employment was accepted with Ferdinand Gatzke at a
salary of $100.00 and five acres of flax for the entire year's labor. The work
consisted of clearing the land of rock, breaking new land, seeding and
threshing.

In 1892 employment was accepted with the firm Frey & Lyher of Eureka. They
operated a furniture factory and carpenter shop. This factory was located in
the building which is today known as the Emanuel's church building. The work in
this factory was the building of furniture and its specialty was the making of
church altars. Many pieces of furniture and church altars made in this factory
are in use today. Henry Straub aided in the erection of Eureka's first large
frame school house, later used as a creamery. Among other buildings built with
his aid is Isaak's Variety Store and Governor Herreid's house.

In 1894, Henry Straub purchased the Charles Lyher interest in the firm of Frey
& Lyher, thus changing the name to Frey & Straub. This marks the beginning of
the record of Henry Straub, which in this year of 1937 is 43 continuous years
in business.

On December 20, 1896, Henry Straub and Albina Brandt were united in marriage.
This ceremony was performed by Reverend Henry Reinhardt in the Zion Lutheran
Church of Eureka. Charles Mix and Sophia Glaser were united in marriage at the
same time, making it a double wedding ceremony.

Albina Straub nee Brandt, was born near Menno in Hutchinson County, South
Dakota, on the 23rd of February, 1880. She was the second eldest daughter of
Johann Brandt and Carolina Straile Brandt. In 1888, Mrs. Straub witnessed one
of the most severe blizzards South Dakota has ever recorded. In this blizzard
many lives were lost due to the fact that it came early in the fall when people
were not expecting such a terrific storm. Much live stock was lost in the deep
snow which covered up barns and homes. It required the tunneling of paths from
the house to the barn and to the well to provide for food and water. In 1889
the Brandt family moved to Eureka where Mr. Brandt entered the general
merchandise business with Emanuel Reiner. In 1891 Mr. Brandt built the store
which today is known as the Red Owl Store. Here a general merchandise business
was conducted. To the Brandt family goes the distinction of being one of the
largest families in the Dakotas. By a first marriage 10 children were born, and
by a second marriage 14 children were born. Of the 24 Brandt children, 21 are
now living.

The small home occupied by Mr. and Mrs. Henry Straub after their marriage was
located on the lots south of the present J. M. Serr residence. They made their
home here until the year 1904, when the present home located west of the
Evangelical Church was purchased. This was built by Christoph Mix.

In 1898, Frey & Straub sold their furniture factory building to a group of
lodge organizations. A new location was then purchased on the 8th of June,
1898, from Julius Schamber and Christoph Hezel. Here the furniture business was
continued. This building was known as the Mathews Store, and was located beside
the Koch & Gerdes Drug Store, on Main Street.

In 1900, Henry Straub took over the entire interest of the furniture store,
having purchased the share of Henry Frey. From this date on the manufacturing
end of the business was leased to Jacob Thurn and Adolph Bischof. Mr. Straub
devoted his entire time to the furniture and undertaking business. Continuing
the expansion of his business in 1907, Mr. Straub purchased the Koch & Gerdes
Drug Store building from John and Ed. Maag. He converted this building into
space for furniture display. In 1912, the two small stores used to this date
were wrecked to facilitate the erection of a new building. The new store
erected has a 36 foot frontage with a length of 80 feet, a full basement was
made to accommodate the surplus stock. The main floor was used for display with
a workshop in the rear portion. A balcony was built inside the entire store to
accommodate more items to be displayed. In 1932, a portion of the basement was
transformed into a modern funeral parlor.

To Mr. and Mrs. Straub were born six boys: Otto, Oscar, Theodore, Arthur,
Albert, and Werner. Also two girls, Emma and Erna. Otto, the eldest son, died
during infancy. In the year 1910, the most tragic event in the lives of the
Straubs occurred when four children were taken by death. Oscar, Erna, Arthur
and Albert died in a short period of two months during a scarlet fever epidemic.

Emma, the oldest daughter, is the wife of Reverend J. F. Graepp, pastor of the
Lutheran Church at Casselton, North Dakota. The family has three girls, Leona
14, Erna 12, and Naomi 9 years old.

Theodore Friedrich, the oldest son, is a member of his father's business. He
graduated from the Minnesota University School of Embalming in 1925. He has
served three terms as county coroner of McPherson County and is the present
office holder. He was chosen the General Chairman of the Golden Jubilee
Organization, sponsoring the celebration of Eureka's 50th Anniversary. He was
married to Margaret Axness of Veblen, South Dakota, on June 28, 1931. To this
union were born two girls, Charlotte Synneva age 5, and Kathryn Albina age 3.

Werner Martin, youngest of the family, is also a member of his father's firm.
He graduated from the University of Minnesota School of Embalming in 1931. He
is a member of the Eureka Volunteer Fire Department. He was married to Gladyce
Torvik of Sisseton, South Dakota, in 1934. They have one child, Barbara Ann age
3.

In the year 1918, the flu epidemic of six months duration took many lives in
Eureka and community. The deaths were so frequent that it was impossible to
supply caskets. The manufacturers were unable to take care of the demand, thus
making it impossible to ship the necessary caskets into Eureka. During this
shortage Mr. Straub returned to his profession of years ago as a cabinet maker
and made the caskets to supply the need. Assisting him with his work was his
wife and eldest son, Theodore. With the untiring efforts of this group about 60
caskets were made.

Henry Straub has been an active worker and builder, always having the interest
of Eureka at heart. He served eight years on the City Council. He was a member
of the Eureka Board of Education for seven years. Since 1892, Henry Straub has
been a member of the Zion Lutheran Church and held several offices in this
church. He was chairman of the building committee when the present Zion
Lutheran Church was erected in 1926. He was also a member of the building
committee when the Eureka Community Hospital was built in 1928. He is at
present a member of the Hospital Board. Many other appointive offices were held.

With civic pride and untiring enthusiasm he has aided the splendid progress
which Eureka has made in the past 50 years.
                                                        --Theo. Straub, 1937.

        Henry Straub Family

PHILIP SCHAMBER

Philip Schamber, who is one of the honored pioneers of McPherson County has
made his home in Eureka since the very first years of the town's beginning. He
has watched it grow and advance from very primitive early days to more highly
improved, modern and present ones, and if some of those years have been hard --
and some very discouraging -- he has faced them with a steadfast courage. He
has never wavered in his loyalty to this community and its people.

        Mr. and Mrs. Philip Schamber

Philip Schamber was born in Friedenthal, in the Crimea, South Russia, on the
1st of June, 1870, and is the sixth of a family of 16 children, eight of whom
were born in the old country and 8 in America. His parents were Martin Schamber
and Friedericka Mueller Schamber, who were likewise born and reared in that
locality, and married in 1860, the family having been established in South
Russia early in 1800, when one of "the two Catholic Schamber brothers" in
Alsace-Lorraine, turned Protestant, and migrated to Russia. Probably the
closest authentic date of this migration is 1803. Alsace-Lorraine was then a
part of France under the Peace of Westphalia.

According to the best information the family name at one time was Chambre,
which would merely be the French manner of pronunciation and spelling of the
more recent German form Schamber. There were two brothers both of whom were
army officers, the one brother remained and the other went into Russia. If one
is curious to know why one brother now left his native land to venture into a
so distant and unknown land, one need only be reminded of the great social,
political, and religious unrest that the whole of France, and all of Europe,
for that matter, was experiencing, which to a great extent, was all an
aftermath of the Reformation. France and all Europe, was fermenting with new
hopes and ideals, which amounted to a profound religious movement. France had
lived through its Revolution and its terrible Reign of Terror; its new Republic
was overthrown in 1799. It may be surmised that this Schamber brother, who was
opposed to the Napoleonic policies, perhaps sensed the catastrophe that was
still to strike his native land.

It may have been the inducements which Czar Alexander I offered colonists
continuing the policies of his brilliant, if profligate, ancestor Catharine II,
and it may have been the fact that his countryman, Duc de Richelieu, French
refugee, was governor of Odessa that helped the young army officer decide to
cast his lot in the new country.

It was Ludwig Schamber, the grandfather of Philip, who was the first ancestor
of the family whose name has come down to the present time. Martin, Philip's
father, born in Russia November 23, 1837, was head of the family when the trek
to the new world was undertaken in 1874. Landing in New York City the family
traveled to Yankton, Dakota Territory. They located on a farm 25 miles
northwest of Yankton, and three miles southeast of where Menno is now located.
They remained on this farm until 1883, trying to adjust themselves to the new
conditions and hardships. The work of improvement of the farm was continued,
some crops were raised the first few years, and more live stock was
accumulated. In the years 1877 and 1878, the grasshoppers devoured everything;
they came just before harvest, in swarms that obscured the sun. They kept on
swarming until the entire neighborhood was "cleaned out." The feed for the live
stock had to be hauled from long distances.

Everyone in the Schamber family was taught to do certain work and since there
were no fences in those days, Philip's work as a young boy, was to herd the
cattle and sheep. At night, they were kept in corrals. Martin Schamber, being a
scholar, taught his children the fundamental subjects thoroughly, during the
long winter months, and he stressed Bible History and German.

Philip Schamber attended his first public school in Scotland, Dakota Territory,
in the winter of 1881. The family then moved to a farm in the Jim River Valley,
between Scotland and Menno. In the fall of 1884, he attended the Wartburg
Seminary at Waverly, Iowa, where he was confirmed by Rev. G. Grossman,
principal at the Seminary, and President of the Lutheran Iowa Synod. He later
attended the Scotland Academy, a commercial school, when the family moved to
Scotland, South Dakota.

During the next few years he assisted from time to time in the implement
business his father had established. It was customary in those years, to trade
in live stock on machinery sales. It was thus that a band of horses had
accumulated during the summer's run of business in 1889, and it became quite a
problem how to dispose of them. The older brother, Julius, devised the plan of
taking them to a newer settlement, where farmers needed more horses, the
Bohemian settlement in the vicinity of Crow Lake, South Dakota. The necessary
arrangements were made, and a covered wagon was "rigged up"; Julius was the
trader, and Philip got his instructions as to the drawing of chattel mortgages
and notes, from his father; and in the beginning of August, 1889, the trip was
made. In about two weeks all horses were sold, including the wagon and harness,
except one horse and saddle; with this Julius came to Eureka, South Dakota, on
horseback, while Philip returned to Tripp and Scotland, South Dakota, wherein
the same businesses were conducted.

After receiving reports from Julius, at Eureka, South Dakota, the father
decided to dispose of the business in Tripp, South Dakota, and move it to
Eureka; accordingly, Philip Schamber arrived here with two carloads of hardware
and machinery, on September 3, 1889. The firm was known as "Martin Schamber and
Sons," and consisted of the father and his boys, Fred W. Schamber, Julius
Schamber, Emil Schamber, and Philip Schamber. Business was good, but during the
winter months it slowed up. Since country schools could not secure the
necessary teachers, Philip Schamber taught school in the Long Lake country, in
the Hoffman-Schumacher district, in 1890, teaching both English and German
classes. Shortly after, he sold out his interest in the implement firm and went
into the grain business for some years.

On May 25, 1896, Philip Schamber was married to Elizabeth Hezel, daughter of
Jacob and Regina Hezel, at Eureka, South Dakota, where they have since resided.
Their three children are: Lorchen T. Schamber, married to Dr. R. H. Isaak of
Eureka; Lillian A. Schamber, married to Dr. P. W. Treick of Eureka; Mrs. Treick
died on October 26, 1935; Ella Elizabeth Schamber, married to DeWalt Kieffer of
Sioux Falls, South Dakota. There have been five grandchildren, but those
surviving are: Richard Schamber Isaak, Kent Schamber Treick, Philip Warren
Treick.

Martin Schamber died in March, 1908, at the age of 70 years; his wife,
Friedericka Schamber, having preceded him in the year 1893, at the age of 53
years.

In 1901, Philip Schamber was appointed postmaster in Eureka. He served under
Presidents McKinley, Theodore Roosevelt, and Taft, or until 1913. In 1914 he
was elected president of the Farmers and Merchants State Bank of Eureka, South
Dakota, in which capacity he served faithfully to the fall of 1930.

He was a member of the Board of Education for 15 years, being president the
last five years; a member of the City Council for the first ward, president of
the Eureka Commercial Club for several terms, and president of the
McPherson-Campbell County Fair Association for about 10 years. He was always a
booster for the good of the city of Eureka, as well as the whole community.

At the present time he is holding the office as "Livestock Loan Examiner" and
as Field Inspector for the Regional Agricultural Credit Corporation of Sioux
City, Iowa, since March, 1988.

JACOB SUESS

Jacob Suess, now a resident of Herreid, South Dakota, but a pioneer of Eureka,
was born in Wurms, South Russia, on January 31, 1866. His wife, Katharina
Berreth, born July 14, 1867, was also a native of Wurms.

In October, 1892, Mr. Suess and his bride came to McPherson County, settling in
Eureka. His first employment was in a warehouse owned by Jacob Maier. In 1893,
he established himself in a little feed store located where Carl Bareither's
shop now stands. Several years later he worked in a general merchandise store
operated by Caspar Hatz; this store was located on the site where Mehlhaff's
Meat Market now stands.

In 1900, he removed to a farm, where he built a comfortable frame house. He
lived there until 1915, when he and his family moved to Herreid.

Mr. and Mrs. Suess have two children, Albert Suess, Ellensburg, Washington, and
Henry Suess, Herreid, South Dakota.

MRS. BARBARA OPP

Mrs. Barbara Opp is one of the pioneer women whom the Eureka community
remembers with delight, as it prepares to make its Golden Jubilee. She and her
husband and family, bore their share of the burdens that made possible the
growth and progress now everywhere in evidence. She was born in Glucksthal,
South Russia, in 1857, where she married George Opp, also a native of Russia.
The Reverend Mr. Becker performed the ceremony. Seventeen children were born to
this union, ten of whom still survive. They are: George Opp, Streeter, N. Dak.;
Mrs. Barbara Martin, Streeter, N. Dak.; Fred Opp, Isabel, S. Dak.; Christ Opp,
Eureka; Mrs. Rosa Wetzler, Ellendale, N. Dak.; Philip Opp, Eureka; Mrs.
Christina Steffan, Eureka; Mrs. Kathrina Mitzel, Eureka; August Opp, Timber
Lake, S. Dak.; and Henry G. Opp, Eureka. All except Henry live on the farm.
Henry is the manager for the Eureka Farmers' Dairy Association.

The Opp family arrived in Campbell County on November 24, 1890, from the
southern part of the state by team. Financially they were poor, but they were
determined to make good. They located seven miles southwest of Eureka. Their
farm equipment consisted of two oxen, one hand plow, and a stone boat. They,
too, suffered many hardships. Although in later years they erected a farm house,
their first house was built of sod, as was the barn.

Some years ago, Mr. and Mrs. Opp moved from their home in the country to the
city of Eureka. Mr. Opp died several years ago, and his wife lived alone in her
home for some time. Recently she moved to Streeter, North Dakota, where she now
lives with a daughter, Mrs. Barbara Martin.

PHILIP OSTER, SR.

Philip Oster, a representative of the sterling element of people who came to
America during the eighties, was born October 30, 1872 of German parentage. He
came to this country with his parents in 1886, locating at Freeman, South
Dakota. In the spring of 1889, they came to Campbell County, filing on a
preemption claim, and later on a homestead located seven miles west of Eureka.
As a boy, Mr. Oster stayed at home assisting his father on the farm.

        Mr. and Mrs. Philip Oster, Sr.

In the year 1890, Mr. Oster left for Groton seeking employment. He was unable
to find work immediately, and as he had no money to pay for lodging, he and
another man also seeking work, walked a mile out into the country every night
where they dug a hole in an old straw stack and slept. Upon being employed, Mr.
Oster remained at Groton all summer and fall and during the year of 1891.

Mr. Oster was married in 1895 to Katharina Zeller, her birth date being August
20, 1876. She, too, was of German parentage, and her parents were Christian
Zeller and Katharina Heine Zeller.

When Mr. and Mrs. Oster were married, their property consisted of 160 acres
homestead land, two horses and two cows. During the first years of their
marriage, Mr. Oster was in partnership with his parents and after their passing,
assumed full charge of the farm. Later he entered the live stock raising and
feeding business, and today is one of the leading live stock buyers of this
territory.

The people of this community have displayed their confidence in Mr. Oster by
electing him to different positions of trust. He was school treasurer for six
years, was a member of the City Council from 1929 to 1933, is a director of the
Eureka State Bank, and has held other offices.

Mr. and Mrs. Oster retired from the farm in 1923 and moved to Eureka. At that
time, Mr. Oster's land holdings had increased from 160 acres at the time of his
marriage to 5,120 acres in Campbell and McPherson Counties.

A comfortable home for his family was always Mr. Oster's great desire. In 1930,
this ambition was realized and they now reside in one of the finest homes in
the city.

In 1930, Mr. Oster purchased the F. W. Homeyer Grain Elevator. His son, Philip,
Jr., is the manager.

Twelve children were born in the Oster family. They are: Maria, Henry, Katie,
Emelia, Ida, Philip, Jr., Frieda, Albert, Alma, Gotthardt, Emil, and Lillian.

Today Mr. Oster takes active part in public affairs, and continues his live
stock and shipping business.

CHRISTOPH BECKER

While the Franco-Prussian war raged in 1871, Christoph Becker was born on
December 4th, in southern Russia, not far from Odessa. As a small boy he
dreamed of being a teacher. In 1890, when he came to Eureka the dream came
true, for he taught German school in this community for one year. But he
decided learning a trade would be more to his liking.

Accordingly, he began his apprenticeship in 1891, under J. A. Melicher, who had
a shop where the Northwest German Farmers Mutual Insurance Company office now
stands. Here he made ox harnesses -- a trade in which Melicher was an expert.

Three years later, he set up a business of his own in a shop on south Market
Street near John Smith's saloon. At the present time Mr. Becker owns a harness
shop on Main Street, west of J. D. Harr's barber shop. He still makes and mends
horse harnesses, although that line of work has decreased considerably since
the horse and buggy days.

Mr. Becker was married to Miss Albina Frey in 1899. The Reverend H. Reinhardt,
pastor of the Zion Lutheran Church, performed the ceremony. Two sons and two
daughters are living. A baby died in infancy. The names of the children are:
Emma (Mrs. Rev. Karl C. Meyer), Nauvoo, Illinois; Arthur Becker, manager of the
local creamery, Eureka; Edwin Becker, instructor in a rural district, Eureka;
Frieda Cartwright, Milwaukee, Wisconsin.

Mr. Becker served one term as alderman from the second ward, where he and his
family reside.

        C. Becker and J. Schatz Harness Shop

ANTON F. WALD

Strasburg, South Russia, was the birthplace of Anton F. Wald. He was born March
25, 1868. When he grew to manhood he had the choice of either entering military
service or teaching school. He chose the latter, and taught for three years at
Sulz, South Russia. He was not as interested in teaching as he might have been,
and knowing that he had two more years of teaching ahead of him he decided to
leave Russia. At the age of 25, he arrived in McPherson County, August 24,
1892, going directly to Eureka. He was already married, having united in
marriage to Julianna Kraft, in Russia, November 24, 1887.

The first four months in Eureka were spent in the employ of Doering Brothers
general merchandise store. Later he worked for John M. Dietrich, in a lumber
yard.

The Walds became the parents of the following children: Ida Schumacher,
Zeeland, N. Dak.; Frand A. Wald, manager of lumber yard, Houghton, S. Dak.;
Martha Wandler, New England, N. Dak.; Elizabeth Weber, St. Paul, Minn.; Andrew
Wald, Strasburg, N. Dak.; Regina Blum, New England, N. Dak.

He remained in Eureka seven years, and during his stay here worked also for
Caspar Hatz in a general merchandise store.

He went to Bowdle, South Dakota in 1894, and took up a homestead five miles
northeast of Bowdle. The family lived on the homestead and Mr. Wald found
employment in the town of Bowdle. His employer's name was Harry Gidle and he
owned an implement shop. In 1903, Mr. Wald entered the lumber business. For the
past thirteen year he has been employed by the Atlas Lumber Company in various
towns in North and South Dakota.

His wife died in 1930, at Bowdle, South Dakota, and was buried at Zeeland,
North Dakota. In 1931, he was married to Mrs. Johanna Wentz of Bowdle.

For the past five years he has been in the employ of the local Atlas Lumber
Company and is making his home in Eureka.

JOHANN SCHMITT

Johann Schmitt, one of the later arrivals in the pioneer period, was born in
Bergdorf, South Russia, on January 19, 1860. He was married to Fredericka
Laemmle in 1887, Rev. Biernbach performing the marriage ceremony in the
Lutheran church of Bergdorf.

In 1892, with his wife and two children, he joined a party of friends and
acquaintances who had decided to try their fortunes in America.

At Menno, South Dakota, Mr. Schmitt and his family stopped to visit his
brother, Gottlieb. Here Mrs. Schmitt and the two children remained for three
weeks, while her husband came to Eureka in search of land. Since his financial
circumstances were comparatively good, consisting of about $1,300.00 cash, he
was well prepared to buy land and equipment. After looking the country over, he
decided to locate in Bergdorf Township, about 25 miles east of Eureka. He filed
on a homestead and bought 160 acres from Valentine Bittner, for which he paid
$500.00. He then went back to Menno and returned with his family.

Since the quarter section he had bought was already equipped with buildings,
consisting of a sod house and small barn, it was necessary only to buy
equipment in order to begin farming operations. He purchased two oxen, a cow, a
wagon, a plow and harrow, and on May 11, 1892, sowed 20 bushels of wheat.
Weather conditions being favorable, his wheat field yielded 397 bushels,
despite late sowing.

Eight children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Schmitt, all of whom are living except
a daughter, Margaret, who died in childhood. Those surviving are: John J., a
farmer living 25 miles east of Eureka; Jacob, operating a shoe repair shop in
Long Lake, S. D.: Friedericka, married to Fred Mohr, a farmer near Hosmer, S.
Dak.; Henry B., farming 25 miles east of Eureka; Edward, a farmer 25 miles east
of Eureka; Lydia, married to Jacob J. Kuszmaul, a farmer nine miles east of
Eureka; Edwin, engaged in the drug business in Eureka.

Mr. Schmitt eventually acquired 1,760 acres of land, all located in McPherson
County. He retired in 1923 and moved to Eureka, where he passed away four years
later. Mrs. Schmitt, with her son Edwin, still resides here.

CASPER VETTER

This is the story of an orphan boy, who started life on his own at the age of
seven, working for the munificent sum of two rubles (about $1.00) a year and
subsistence. He labored for seven years at that rate of pay. But better things
came, even in South Russia. When he was 14 years old, the orphan boy sought a
new employer and found a job in the village of Selz, where he had been born on
December 1, 1872.

Here he became opulent, for he earned 125 rubles a year and saved all but 25
rubles. Then instead of working for others, a friend advised him to invest his
savings in materials, and worked at wagon making for some time.

But the young boy didn't relish the idea of serving in the army of the Czar so
he somehow managed to finance the journey to America, where military service
was not required and where there was free land to be had for all who would have
it. So it was, that Casper Vetter started for the new land in 1892, leaving
behind him the grave of the mother whom he scarcely remembered since he was
only seven when she died. The father had died when he was a child less than two
years old. The parents were Anselm Vetter and Margareta Aberlach Vetter. The
father was a native of Selz, and the mother of Kantel.

        Mr. and Mrs. Casper Vetter

Steamers and railroad trains carried him far away from Russia and finally
landed him in Eureka, South Dakota. Here, with a dollar and a few smaller coins
in his pocket, he began his career in the adopted country.

Free land then wasn't so plentiful, in fact near the city it had all been taken
up by early comers. So Casper found a job with John Stickel in his blacksmith
shop at $10.00 per month. Later he got a job with Fred Schamber in the machine
business at $30.00 per month. He felt his fortunes were going ahead at
satisfactory pace and remained in the employ of that business for more than
seven years. Later he was employed by Strobel Brothers and Jake Schall, who
also dealt in farm machinery.

By 1896, he felt himself able to provide for a wife and was married to Miss
Christina Knodel, the Reverend Father Lamish performing the ceremony. To this
union have been born five sons: Frank, now a member of the grain firm, Vetter &
Wolff in Eureka; Casper, Jr., Yakima, Washington; Herbert, Backus, Minnesota;
Eugene, Lodi, California; and John, residing in Yakima, Washington.

Mr. Vetter has been active in church and community life in Eureka, and for a
number of years has held public office. Being a member of the Catholic church,
he was active in the organization of the Catholic church in Eureka and took a
prominent part in the erection of the church edifice here.

He was a member of the city council, as alderman from the third ward, for eight
years and served as city marshal for a period of twelve years.

In 1936, he was appointed to the county commission upon the resignation of
Commissioner Fred Schanzenbach, serving two months of the unexpired term. In
the following election he was chosen commissioner from this district, an office
he holds at the present time.

Reminiscing on old times and his career in his adopted land with a Jubilee Book
writer, Mr. Vetter said he has no complaint to make. He feels that fortune has
been kind, even if it has frowned a bit at times. He has found it possible to
raise his family and provide a home without the necessity of other aid than his
own efforts, despite the difficult times of the past few years. His philosophy
of life, in this respect, is without doubt colored by the resolute courage
characteristic of the pioneer period of the history of the community as well as
the entire county. "Life is hard sometimes," he said, "but there is always a
way out, if you look long enough and hard enough."

FRED NETZER

Fred Netzer was born in South Russia, as were thousands of other pioneer
settlers of Dakota Territory. Old Arzis, Bessarabia, was the village where he
first saw light on October 1, 1869. He came to America at the age of 18, with
his parents and seven brothers and sisters, Martin, Andreas, Friedericka,
Christian, Johann, Sophia and Wilhelm. The family arrived in what is now New
Salem, North Dakota, in December, 1887, the father filing on land in that
vicinity. Here Fred Netzer lived with his parents until he was 22 years of age,
working on the farm and doing various other odd jobs.

In 1892, he was married to Emilia Isaak, the ceremony being performed by the
Reverend Wilhelm Knapp. After the death of his father, he helped his mother on
the farm for another year, after which he and his wife came to Eureka. Here he
worked at day labor with a team of horses until the year 1901. He then bought a
quarter section of land one-half mile south of Eureka on which he erected frame
buildings and commenced farming operations.

        Fred Netzer

Seven children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Netzer, of whom only one survives --
Ida, married to Adam Wunner, marshal of the city of Eureka. Mr. Netzer's wife
died in 1914, and several years later he was remarried to Mrs. Margaretha
Aldinger. In 1916, he removed to Eureka, where he now resides.

DR. O. H. GERDES

When a quiet-spoken, unassuming country doctor achieves a place as the First
Citizen of his town, his life story cannot be told by a recital of the facts
usually employed. Dr. Otto Henry Gerdes, who spent virtually his whole
professional life in the Eureka community, was such a country doctor. He was a
skilled physician, highly respected by the colleagues of his profession and
considered by them an unusually accurate diagnostician. The people of the
Eureka community knew him as most faithful and sympathetic in their physical
ills. They knew him as always ready to answer calls, day and night, in bitter
cold and storm, in scorching heat of summer. But none of these facts explain
Dr. Gerdes, the physician and the man.

Indeed, the esteem in which he was held by all, and love and devotion he
inspired in those who were privileged to know him intimately, were as unusual
as his personality. The secret of his hold upon the community, which was his
home for more than 40 years, must be sought in the unseen realities of the
human spirit. He was, apparently, one of those rare souls cast in a highly
individual mold, which, while they move and have their being in the midst of
us, are somehow definitely apart from the mass of humanity.

If one would understand Dr. Gerdes, that understanding must he sought in the
realm of that indefinite and yet real thing called personality. That something,
which cannot he seen, touched, weighed nor measured, and still the greatest,
most substantial and lasting of all human values.

        Dr. and Mrs. O. H. Gerdes

Little may be hoped for by way of arriving at a clear understanding of this
unusual and interesting man in this brief and necessarily superficial inquiry.
A few highlights of his career, a few glimpses of him in his few hours of
relaxation, provided the writer by his children and his friends, may, however,
provide a suggestion of what sort of human being Dr. Gerdes was. These
vignettes of his character, when he ceased being the dignified professional man
and was husband, father, friend, neighbor and citizen, will surely prove among
the more cherished memories to all who knew him.

It is interesting, then, to recall that he was the product of two great
cultures. Born in Kleinmargens, 0st Friesland, Germany, on January 25, 1868, he
received his early education In the excellent schools of his native land.
Before coming to America he had been "absolved," as graduation is known in
Germany, from the "Gymnasium," a division of the German educational system
about on par with the American Junior College. He was 17 years old at that
time, thus having been under the influence of German culture during his most
impressionable and formative years. It is also of importance to remember that
Germany was then at the outset of the great period of development in all lines
that was to reach its climax at the beginning of the World War. The spirit of
that time can hardly have failed to leave a deep impression on his sensitive
spirit.

About this time his father died, so together with his mother and a brother, he
immigrated to America to take up his residence in Manson, Iowa, with an older
brother who had emigrated two years earlier. Soon after arrival he secured
employment in a drug store in that city -- perhaps the best place he might have
found to come in touch with the real heart-beat of American life. One may well
conceive this thoughtful youngster observed and learned much at this
cross-roads of the community.

Almost taciturn by nature, he said little, if anything, of this period of his
life nor of his impressions as a medical student with a Dr. Martin and later in
Rush Medical College in Chicago. But wherever the 24-year-old doctor learned
it, he knew human nature when he emerged from the famous Chicago school with
his degree in 1892. That he, also, had absorbed the spirit of American ideals
is very evident. Coming to a community, populated almost exclusively by German
immigrants, most of them unschooled, a less sturdy spirit might have drifted
complacently on the tide of the primitive conditions of life. He might have
allowed Americanization of his town to seep in gradually from the outer edges.

One may recall, also, that he had arrived in America about the same time as the
majority of the people among whom he lived and worked. Apparently he took his
superior training and gifts as a challenge that went beyond his duty as a
professional man. He ministered not only to their physical ills but assumed an
attitude of vigorous leadership in their struggle to adjust themselves to the
new conditions of life and to achieve qualifications for American citizenship.
In his quiet way, he did this by example and precept rather than by direct and
obvious persuasion.

The exacting requirements of his large medical practice made impossible for him
to take as active part in public life as one might feel he would have done had
he had a bit more leisure. But he did serve for more than a score of years as a
member of the board of education and here he was admittedly a power in shaping
the policies which have brought about the excellent schools now enjoyed by
Eureka. It was not until toward the end of his career (1930-32) that he
consented to be elected mayor of this town, a circumstance that is generally
felt to have been more an honor to the town than to him.

He was, however, during his entire life vitally interested in all things that
might make the community a better and more pleasant place to live and gave his
financial support generously and willingly to those ends. He was, naturally, a
staunch supporter of the Eureka community hospital and, when it was
established, served as its chief-of-staff for a period of years. His
professional status, also, made him a natural selection for the county board of
health, of which he was also a member for a long period.

More versatile than the average professional man, he was an astute business
manager, acquiring interest in at least two of the banks in Eureka and was at
the time of his death a majority stockholder in the Eureka State Bank, a
position now occupied by his daughter, Mrs. Irene Olson. He was, also,
conversant with the problems of the farmer and at one time set up his son in
farming in the community. Much more might he said of this phase of his
activity, but this will be omitted for the more delightful and less known side
of his character, his home life and his hours off duty. The most reliable
sources of information indicate he was little less than idolized by his family
and to his cronies and friends, he was a most delightful companion.

He loved, in quiet hours at his office, to sit with his hat on, feet on his
desk smoking his pipe, reading either a medical tome, or for relaxation, a
mystery story -- a characteristic of many another active mind. He was a great
lover of dogs and nearly always owned one. He was especially partial to the
Irish setter, but owned other breeds as well. During a large part of his busy
life he also kept a Jersey cow upon which he lavished a great deal of time and
attention.

His most enduring hobby, however, was poultry and he was unusually successful
in breeding prize winners, exhibiting not only at local fairs but in many
eastern shows as well. His collection of blue ribbons, won by his chickens,
were among the most highly prized of his possessions. As with dogs, he
experimented with various breeds, finally settling on Silver Laced Wyandottes
as best suited to his fancy.

Being what might have been called a German of the old school, he could be
imagined to have been stern and exacting as a husband and father, but on the
contrary, he was considerate of his wife and, his family, looking back upon
their childhood, say rather indulgent toward them. He was greatly interested in
all their little problems and watched over their physical and educational
development in minute detail.

Dr. Gerdes was married a short time after establishing his practice in Eureka,
in fact, the following year having opened his office in the town in 1893. His
wife was Miss Bertha Bryan, a native of Woodstock, Illinois, and born there
November 17, 1874. She was thus only 19 when she came to Eureka to be assistant
to Postmaster Frank Hooper for a brief interval, after which she planned to
continue her journey westward to the Pacific Coast. However, meeting the
handsome young doctor altered her plans and they were married after a courtship
of six months, on October 22, 1894.

Of Scandinavian extraction, she brought into the home a new note, being of a
happy, jovial disposition with an outlook on life characteristic of the
traditions and traits of her race. She proved a sympathetic, congenial mate and
became the mother of four children, Mrs. Irene Olsen and Mrs. F. K. Mehlhaff of
Eureka; Maude M. Gerdes, M. D., of Minneapolis; and Martin H. Gerdes, now
residing in California.

Mrs. Gerdes held a high place in the affections of her husband and during a
long period of illness preceding her death, she was the object of his tender
and deep concern. Her death on October 14, 1939, was a hard blow to him. He had
done everything known to medical science to combat the disease which finally
took her life, even bringing her to Germany in the hope that the ocean voyage
or perhaps German specialists might bring her relief.

Mrs. Gerdes was a member of the Presbyterian church, being unusually active in
all departments of its activity. She was, also, a charter member of the Eastern
Star and vitally interested in all movements in the community looking toward
progress.

The strenuous routine of Dr. Gerdes' life and his unsparing use of his strength
and energy without a doubt wore down his robust body prematurely, and his health
failed gradually until 1935, when he suffered a cerebral hemorrhage, from which
he never fully recovered. A second similar attack ended his life on June 29,
1936. The funeral service was held in the Zion Lutheran Church with the
Reverend Mr. Schmitt and the Reverend J. F. Graepp officiating. The largest
concourse of people ever to gather in Eureka for a similar occasion came to pay
him tribute as he was laid to rest after a long life of outstanding service to
his community.

There are hundreds of interesting anecdotes which might well be told here since
they shed much light on his rich personality, if space permitted. However, one
highly significant circumstance may suffice. The generation now active in the
community is largely composed of the babies he helped usher into the world.
Their veneration of the pioneer doctor's memory caused them to cast about, long
and earnestly for a suitable memorial. Many methods of honoring him were
suggested, but none seemed entirely suitable.

        Koch and Gerdes Drug Store

        Dr. O. H. Gerdes

The solution of the problem apparently came from what may be termed, for want
of a better name, the mass mind of the community. Theo. Straub who first
suggested the idea, brought the decisive result. The "Big Stone" was to be Dr.
Gerdes' memorial. The suggestion met with general and enthusiastic approval.

"Big Stone" was historic and beloved of all who had grown up in Eureka.
Children went there in spring to picnic and to pick May flowers that grew in
profusion around it. Pedestrians and hikers often found it a convenient place
to rest. And in the pre-automobile era, lovers perhaps found it an inviting
place to whisper sweet nothings that ever thrill young love. In later years,
another generation, the third since Dr. Gerdes first came to Eureka, learned to
love "Big Stone." They, too, picked May flowers at its base and they placed
small stones at the border of the path leading to it for a considerable
distance.

Yes, "Big Stone" was to be Dr. Gerdes' memorial. It was massive in size,
suggesting the proportions of his heart and soul. It was rough as to exterior,
but its heart was sound and as strong and fine as its exterior. It seemed
permanent and a part of the life of the generation who loved it, even as the
good doctor's memory with them would be permanent, and as they loved "Big
Stone" so they would revere his memory.

The immense boulder was, therefore, moved to the city park near the lake. There
it was given place, and there it will stand for all time. As this is written
plans have been completed to dedicate the great boulder and a bronze plate,
properly inscribed, to the memory of Otto Henry Gerdes, the country doctor, who
became the First Citizen of this town and the most beloved man of his time. The
country doctor, who was not only physician, but "Father confessor," trusted
confidante, and steadfast friend to the great majority of his
contemporaries. -- L. E. F.

JOHN STOEBNER

John Stoebner was born in Kassel, Russia, on August 17,1870. He lived there for
23 years. While yet in Russia he was married to Magdalena Herr. The Rev. Eberle
performed the ceremony.

The Stoebners had relatives and friends near Eureka. In 1893, they decided to
come to America and find homesteads in the same vicinity. Having but little
money of their own, they had to borrow $175.00 to make the trip. Arriving in
Aberdeen, they faced the necessity of changing trains before proceeding to
Eureka. The west-bound train did not leave until the next day, so they had to
find a place to stay for the night. They had but 50 cents saved for an
emergency, and had gone without food for two days. Finding an unoccupied
platform on one side of the depot, they made a bed there for themselves and
their one child. Some time after dark, a watchman came by, swinging his
lantern. He saw the Stoebners and called to them, telling them that they could
not sleep there. Knowing no language but their native tongue, the Stoebners
tried to explain with difficulty. Sensing their plight, the watchman motioned
for them to follow him. He took the timid family to the Conrad Hotel, a wooden
structure not far distant. He spoke to the manager and left.

After a few minutes, the manager motioned to the Stoebners to follow him. He
led them to the dining-room, and served a meal to them. But, hungry as they
were, they could eat but little. They were too frightened. Soon the manager led
them up a flight of stairs to a bedroom and explained, as well as he could,
that they were to spend the night there. Then he left them alone.

With his wife crying, and his own anxiety at fever-pitch, Mr. Stoebner spent a
long, sleepless night full of torture as he tried to imagine what might happen
to them in the morning. However, at the first sign of daylight, they were
merely told to come downstairs to breakfast. They were able to eat more for
breakfast than they had eaten for supper the night before.

They were then escorted to the Milwaukee train, which was bound for Eureka. Mr.
Stoebner's pronunciation of "Eureka" was somewhat different from that of the
English-speaking conductor, so they did not understand his announcement of
their arrival at Eureka. An attempted persuasion on his part to have them leave
the train met with a stolid refusal. Relatives of Mr. and Mrs. Stoebner were
waiting for them on the platform. When they did not appear some of the
relatives entered the coach. Only when the Stoebners saw their relatives would
they believe that they had really arrived at their destination.

Mr. and Mrs. Stoebner filed on a homestead three and one-half miles south of
Eureka. They purchased a plow, a wagon and two oxen. Mr. Stoebner traded a fur
coat he had brought from Russia for a milch cow.

The house on the Stoebner homestead was built of sod, as was the barn. The
house, fourteen by sixteen feet in size, had only one small opening. Mr.
Stoebner gathered rushes from a nearby slough, tied them in a bundle, and
stuffed this bundle in the opening to serve as a door. One cold winter evening
after his wife and children were in bed, Mr. Stoebner left the house to visit a
neighbor. He carefully pressed the bundle of rushes into the doorway so that
but little heat could escape. The temperature was lower than he had
anticipated, so he soon returned to his own home again, fearing that his family
would freeze if he stayed away too long. But his fears were groundless when he
pulled the rushes from the doorway, the house was almost comfortably warm.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Stoebner

The country around Eureka was well populated at that time, so any willing
person could find plenty of work. Mr. Stoebner worked for others for 50 cents a
day, after he had taken care of his own crop. The hours were long and the
earnings meager, as already indicated, but content with only simple food and
clothing, the family managed to live. After years of hardship, the Stoebners
acquired a considerable amount of farm land. When their sons were able to
manage the home farm, Mr. and Mrs. Stoebner retired. They now live in Eureka.

Their children are: Mrs. Magdalena Pietz; Fred Stoebner, farmer; Emil Stoebner,
superintendent of schools for McPherson County; Edward Stoebner, farmer; Mrs.
Martha Tribble; Adolph Stoebner, principal of Artas city schools; Emma
Stoebner, teacher; William Stoebner, laborer in California; Eugene Stoebner,
teacher; and Otto Stoebner, farmer.

JOHANNES KLEIN

Johannes Klein came to Eureka in 1893, with the ebb tide of the great
immigration from Russia. He had come to America in 1875, from Teplitz where he
was born in 1855, settling on a homestead, a part of which is now occupied by
the city of Tripp, South Dakota. On his arrival in Eureka, he purchased a
blacksmith shop operated by Johannes Stickel. Together with his son, John, he
conducted a general blacksmith business in Eureka until 1900, when he moved to
Artas, engaging in the same business.

Three years later he removed to a farm four miles west of Eureka, which was his
home until 1908, when he moved to Eureka again, entering the plumbing and
heating business, and continuing until his death on August 9, 1920.

Johannes Klein was married in 1879, to Regina Isaak, a native of Kulm and born
in 1856. Six children were born to this union, live of whom survive. They are:
Jacob J., Gottlieb, John, Theresa (Mrs. Andrew Stickel), Maggie (Mrs. Ed
Stickel), all residing in Eureka; and Ed Klein of Aberdeen.

Johannes Klein, while a modest and unassuming man, will long be remembered by
the generation who knew him. Not only was he an ardent church man and a
faithful member of the Lutheran church, but an amateur musician. Early in life
he may have had a desire to develop an innate musical genius, but circumstances
of his life made this impossible.

When he had reached middle age, however, he went to work in earnest. From the
Reverend H. Reinhardt, who was then pastor of the local Lutheran church, he
learned the rudiments of music, being entirely ignorant of the art of reading
musical score. It was not long until he was able to play simple music on the
organ. Natural genius and faithful practice enabled him in a short time to play
church music and he served the church as organist and choir leader for a period
of years.

The story of this accomplishment is one of those glimpses of pioneer life which
must needs be an inspiration to all who contemplate it thoughtfully. Here was a
man whose destiny led him to the forge and the anvil, but whose ambition,
natural gifts and industry opened for him a new world of pleasure and
accomplishment.

CHARLES BOYD FONCANNON

Primarily connected with the educational and political development of the city
of Eureka is the life of Charles Boyd Foncannon. Mr. Foncannon was born on the
22nd of April, 1869, at Millard, Missouri. His parents were Michael B.
Foncannon and Julia S. Foncannon, nee Beatty, both of whom were born in
Fairfield County, Ohio, the former tracing his lineage to the sturdy Holland
Dutch stock which settled in the State of New York, while the mother was of
Scotch-Irish extraction, her ancestors having come to this country in the
middle of the eighteenth century and having settled in Pennsylvania. Young
Foncannon's parents moved to Missouri and were residents there during the Civil
War. The father served in the army in defense of the Union as a member of the
Seventh Missouri Volunteer Cavalry.

Charles B. Foncannon received his early education in the public schools of his
native country and later attended the North Missouri Normal School at
Kirksville, from which he graduated as a member of the class of 1890.
Thereafter, he took a special course in the Missouri State University. After
leaving college, he was superintendent of schools at La Plata, Missouri, for
two years. In 1894, he came to Eureka, where he was principal of schools for
four years. In 1898, he was the successful candidate for the office of
Superintendent of Schools for McPherson County, which position he held for four
years. He was president of the State Association of County Superintendents of
Schools from 1900 to 1902, and was very instrumental in writing the first
school code of laws which was adopted by the State Legislature in 1901. In 1902
he was elected to represent the Fifth District on the Board of County
Commissioners of McPherson County, which office he held for four years. He also
served one term in the legislature as a representative of McPherson County.

        Mr. Charles Boyd Foncannon

From 1902 to 1910, he entered the active real estate business in Eureka and
made a specialty of financing loans on real estate security. In 1910, he moved
to Aberdeen where he was also engaged in the real estate business for a
considerable length of time. Since 1923, he has been engaged in the coal
business, making his headquarters at Aberdeen. On the 18th of June, 1900, he
was united in marriage with Miss Ottilia M. Hinz, who was born in Manchester,
Wisconsin, on the 12th of November, 1879, a daughter of Louis and Minerva Hinz.
The Foncannons have one daughter, named Vivian Maurine, who is now known in
Aberdeen as Mrs. A. L. Ward, Jr.

ADOLPH EHRMANN

A young adventurer embarked on a voyage into a strange land and landed in New
York on the 16th of July, 1898, with only $5.25 in his pocket. This young man
was Adolph Ehrmann, pioneer blacksmith of Eureka. Mr. Ehrmann was born in
Odessa, Russia, on the 1st of October, 1867. As a boy he worked for his father,
who was a carriagemaker, and by the time young Adolph was 25 years of age, he
had mastered the trade.

Being alone and practically penniless in the city of New York is no ideal spot,
even for a young adventurer. However, young Adolph did not lack courage so he
immediately purchased a railroad ticket to Philadelphia with the balance of his
money. A former apprentice of his father lived at this point, and Mr. Ehrmann
hoped that by contacting him, he might be able to find employment.
Disappointment was his, however, when upon arrival he found out that
Philadelphia had an over-supply of skilled workers, and few jobs were to be
gotten. Adolph stayed in Philadelphia for about a month, and experienced great
satisfaction viewing historic points such as Independence Hall and the Old
Liberty Bell. Though his funds were practically exhausted, he was again spurred
by the desire for adventure, Adolph Ehrmann left for Eureka, South Dakota,
where an uncle of his lived. His friends unsuccessfully tried to dissuade him
from this trip, fearful that he might be scalped by the Indians, or meet some
similar fate. He arrived in Eureka on the 5th of September, 1893, and was met
by relatives and friends from the old country. Work was plentiful in Eureka, and
he soon had his debts paid, and even had laid aside a few dollars for his
future needs.

Concerning crop conditions at that time, Mr. Ehrmann states that the yield of
1893 was not very large. Drouth hit the Eureka country in 1894, and a feature
of that year was the heavy frost that fell on the night of the Fourth of July,
turning everything black on the following morning. In 1895, the yield was again
limited, and the prices of small grain were exceedingly low. Mr. Ehrmann states
that in that year the price of wheat ranged from 25 cents to 45 cents per
bushel. Barley was sold for from 5 cents to 6 cents per bushel. Potatoes from
25 cents to 35 cents per sack; eggs for 5 cents a dozen; butter for 5 cents a
pound; flour at $1.60 per 100 pounds; and two chickens could be purchased for
25 cents. In spite of these prices, the people were able to make a living and
were happy and contented. In 1895, Adolph Ehrmann opened his own blacksmith
shop in Eureka, and has operated the same continuously down to the present day.
His experience as a carriage maker in the old country proved invaluable to him
in this line, and today he is recognized as an expert in this trade.

Being asked about sleighs in the early days, Mr. Ehrmann replied that for the
most part they were of home-made construction. The old pioneers took pride in
being able to make their own tools and equipment, and would often assemble to
discuss the merits of their inventions.

On the 3rd of February, 1898, Adolph Ehrmann was united in marriage with
Barbara Schlaht. The marriage ceremony was performed by Reverend H. Reinhart,
the German minister in Eureka. This union was blessed with the following
children: Pauline Mehlhaff, who is the wife of Dr. W. H. Mehlhaff, a practicing
dentist at Wishek, N. Dak.; Emma Ehrmann, a rural school teacher close to
Eureka; Albert Ehrmann, employed by the Dakota Central Telephone Company at
Aberdeen; and Barbara Ehrmann, a telephone operator at Wishek, N. Dak.

After a short illness, Mr. Ehrmann's first wife died on the 17th of September,
1908. The following year he married again; this time to Anna Wenzel. The
marriage ceremony was again performed by Reverend H. Reinhart. To this union
one son was born, namely, Henry Ehrmann, who is now employed by the Dakota
Central Telephone Company at Parkston, S. Dak.

Today, Mr. and Mrs. Ehrmann reside in Eureka, happy and contented, and greatly
enjoy relating their experiences in pioneer Eureka.

JOHN G. NEUHARTH

Though only six months old when he came to America from Kassel, in South
Russia, John G. Neuharth grew up in time to become a part of the real pioneer
period of the Eureka community. He was born in 1873, in Kassel, the village
where so many of the earlier arrivals first saw light of day. His childhood and
early boyhood were spent in the vicinity of Yankton. Arriving in McPherson
County in 1892, he located on a farm 15 miles northeast of Eureka. The
following year he was married to Katharina Opp, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Daniel
Opp, the Rev. Ulrich Reue performing the ceremony. Fifteen children were born
to the Neuharths and twelve of them survive. All reside in the Eureka vicinity.
They are: John J., George, Christ J., Rose (Mrs. Christ Fischer), Martha (Mrs.
Albert Meidinger), Henry J., Edward J., Arthur J., Hulda (Mrs. Julius
Wittmayer), Gust J., Elmer and Lillian, twins.

While the new settlement had progressed a bit as compared with the primitive
wilderness that greeted the trail blazers of 1883, it was still primitive
enough when John G. Neuharth took up farming. The rails had come to Eureka, but
ox teams were still much in evidence and there were hundreds of farmers to the
north and west of the town, who still were so far away from their markets and
source of supplies that a trip to town required from two to four days. From his
own farm a trip to town was a pretty stiff day's traveling even after horses
became the principal means of transportation.

Mr. Neuharth took active interest in matters of public concern in his community
