and was chosen county assessor, a position he held for 12 years in all. Later
his son, George J., held the same office for six years. The elder Neuharth has
also been active in the Northwest German Farmers Mutual Insurance Company for
27 years.

REV. PETER BAUER

Neuberg, South Russia, was the birthplace of the Rev. Peter Bauer, for many
years pastor of the Reformed church in Eureka. He was born on the 21st of
January, 1856. When he grew to manhood, he chose teaching as his

        Rev. and Mrs. Peter Bauer

vocation and taught in the schools at Worms, South Russia. He taught both the
German and Russian languages.

At the age of 37, he decided to take his small family to America. They arrived
in McPherson County in October, 1893. He accepted the pastorate of the Reformed
church in Eureka shortly after his arrival. He was the congregation's second
pastor.

Fourteen children were born to the Rev. and Mrs. Bauer. Twelve of these are
living, namely: Edward Emanuel Bauer, printer, Minneapolis, Minn.; Lydia Barby,
Bismarck, N. Dak.; H. I. Bauer, manager of the Home Lumber Co., Eureka, S.
Dak.; Emma Louise Golden, instructor in Normal, Hayes, Kansas; Natalia
Wilhelmenia Workman (deceased), Denver, Colorado; Flora Englehart, Billings,
Montana; Martha Nichol, Helena, Montana; Lillian Bauer, instructor in school at
Jamestown, N. Dak.; Antonia Wahrer, Des Moines, Iowa; Elsie Varnson, Pensecola,
Florida; Hildegarde Hoffer, Minneapolis, Minnesota; Otto Bauer, Omaha, Nebraska.

Mr. Bauer's son, H. I. Bauer, of Eureka, has held various public offices in the
city of Eureka. He was city treasurer in 1912-1913. He served as alderman from
the first ward in 1914. When the new public school was established, Mr. Bauer
was a member of the school board, sparing no efforts to see the plans
materialize. He was elected city mayor in April, 1926, and served to 1928.

A great deal of credit should be given him for the erection of the Eureka
Community Hospital, as he was one of the most ardent workers to see the
movement brought to a successful issue.

The Rev. Peter Bauer resigned the first of the year from his duties as pastor
of the Reformed church in Leola, South Dakota, where the family has been
residing for a number of years. They are still living in Leola at the present
time.

MICHAEL HAFTL

Michael Haftl was born in Hungary on September 25, 1886. At the age of 20 he
was united in marriage to Juliana Judt. Fourteen children were born to this
union.

In 1892, Mr. Haftl and his then small family came to McPherson County and
settled in the newly organized village of Eureka, where the majority of the
family was born and raised.

While in Eureka, Mr. Haftl engaged in the livery business and owned the livery
stable located on Pfeffer Street. He has told many interesting facts concerning
the people that brought their horses, the only transportation facilities at
that time, to be housed for several days. On many occasions not only the horses
sought shelter in the stable, but the people themselves as well.

In 1900, Mr. Haftl became manager of the local branch of the Hamm Brewing
Company, which position he held until his retirement in 1912. His wife died in
1905, leaving a family of eight children, the three youngest being girls with
ages ranging from seven to nine. Mr. Haftl never remarried, but, with the aid
of an older daughter, devoted his entire time to his motherless family.

He passed away at the Good Samaritan Hospital in Aberdeen, South Dakota, on
August 14, 1936, after an illness of five days. Interment was made in the
Lutheran cemetery in Eureka by the side of his wife.

Of the 14 children born to the Haftls, eight are living. They are: Mrs. Juliana
Stevens, Bowdle; John Haftl, Eureka; Mrs. Ann Johnson, Sioux Falls; Mrs. Karl
Pleissner, Baker, Montana; Charles Haftl, Miles City, Montana; Mrs. George
Colbo, Baker, Montana; Mrs. Henry Ottmar, Eureka; and Mrs. J. D. Harr, Eureka.

        Sietzick Store -- Haftl Dray Line

FRIEDRICH F. OBERLANDER

Born in Petersthal, South Russia, on March 10, 1863. Friedrich F. Oberlander
was destined to pioneer in the wilds of Dakota Territory. He married Christina
Brose in 1889, and five years later they left Russia for America. They arrived
in Eureka by rail on March 28, 1894, Mr. Oberlander was then 31 years old.
Others in the party were his brothers, Peter and Philip, Gottlieb Eisemann,
Friedrich Wanner, Philipp Keszler and George Martel (Hosmer), all with their
families. It may be mentioned in passing that the ship they came over on sank
two years later with loss of life and property.

Mr. Oberlander took up land in Campbell County, 11 miles southwest of Eureka.
Buildings were put up of sod, this being the cheapest building material. He
borrowed money to buy farm equipment consisting of a header, mower, rake, hand
plow, wagon and two oxen, valued at about $300.00. The only means of
transportation to town was the oxen hitched to a stoneboat. Farm products
brought very little, wheat being 35 cents per bushel, butter 3 cents per pound
and eggs 3 cents per dozen. Since there were very few or no grasshoppers, and
the weather was more favorable than in recent years, the crops were usually
good. He was thus enabled to buy more land from time to time, in spite of low
prices for what he produced.

Mr. and Mrs. Oberlander brought up a family of 10 children, all living except a
boy, George, who died several years ago. The others are: Margaret, born
November 18, 1890: Christina, born February 20, 1891, married to Jacob Eckman
of Eureka; Katharina, born July 27, 1893; Karolina, born December 3, 1895;
Elizabeth, born February 12, 1898, married to Jacob Bauman of Java, S. Dak.;
Emilia, born October 24, 1899, married to Jacob Heiser, a farmer living west of
Eureka; Fred, born June 4, 1902, now living on a farm west of Eureka; Ernst,
born December 15, 1908, residing in Ashley, N. Dak.; Otto, born March 28, 1911,
s farmer living southwest of Eureka.

Mr. Oberlander retired from farming in 1923, and in October of that year took
up residence in Eureka. After the death of his wife on March 13, 1925, he lived
for some time with his daughter, Christina, later going to the Good Samaritan
Home in Eureka, where he is employed as janitor.

FREDERICK L. KLEIN

Frederick L. Klein, son of Ludwig and Rosina Jutze Klein, was born in Dumowa,
South Russia, on June 20, 1873. His father died when Fred was six years old. His
mother remarried and never came to this country. He had four brothers and two
sisters, all of whom came to this country. They are: Jacob L., of Eureka, S.
Dak.; Ludwig, formerly of McLaughlin, now deceased; William, of Lehr, N. Dak.,
now in California; Carl, of Hebron, N. Dak. His sisters, Mrs. Jacob Reich, of
Hebron, N. Dak., and Mrs. Andrew Schauer, of Hosmer, S. Dak., preceded him in
death.

        Mr. and Mrs. Frederick L. Klein

In June, 1893, when 20 years old he came to Eureka, South Dakota, arriving on
an immigrant train with $2.00 in his pocket. He had $20.00 until he fell asleep
on the train at Bridgewater, South Dakota, and was robbed of all but $2.00.
Upon his arrival in Eureka he labored for two years as a farm hand. The first
year he worked for John Strobel at $90.00 a year. The second year he contracted
with Philipp Schmidt for $100.00 a year, but due to the crop failure he
received only $90.00.

In 1895, he homesteaded seven miles north of Eureka, South Dakota, on Spring
Creek. January 8, 1896, he was married to Magdalena Stoebner, eldest daughter
of Mr. and Mrs. Henry Stoebner, Sr., by Reverend Bauer. They had six children,
three of whom died in infancy and Herbert, who died in November, 1931. The two
remaining children are Emma, wife of Edward Rau, of Ashley, and Gideon Klein,
White Eagle agent at Eureka, South Dakota. Mr. and Mrs. Rau have two sons,
Clifford and Myron, age five and eight. Gideon was married in October, 1927, to
Alma Wolsky of Nome, North Dakota, and they have one son, Douglas, six years
old.

He rented his farm in 1908, and came to Eureka, where he went into business
with the firm of Keim, Kiesz & Klein, which partnership lasted five years. Mr.
Klein then bought an elevator which he sold a few years later to Edward Bohle.
This elevator stood where Fiechtner's elevator now stands. The last few years
of his life were spent in retirement due to a heart ailment which had also been
the reason for his leaving the farm. Along with other interests, he paid some
attention to real estate from 1917 to 1922. In 1910, he built the home in
College Addition that is now the home of Mr. and Mrs. Gideon Klein and son.

When the elder Kleins homesteaded on Spring Creek, seven miles north of Eureka,
the creek was filled with water. In some places there were holes 8 to 10 feet
deep. The creek was filled with bass, perch and bullheads. After a few years
these disappeared and only buffalo fish and carp remained. However there were
always a lot of young fish and minnows.

Gideon was born in a sod house on this creek and lived there the first eight
years of his life and tells how, when his parents both worked in the fields, he
played all day on the banks of the creek with his big, black dog. His favorite
pastime was to sit by a culvert and catch the little fish in his hands as they
came through. During all the years the Kleins lived there, the creek flowed
every summer and after a rain it was a torrent. Spring Creek was a waterfowl
paradise in those early years, the Kleins recall. There were thousands of ducks
of all kinds. They nested along the creek. The wild ducks came into the farm
yard with the tame ducks. In the spring hundreds of wild geese fed in the
fields near the buildings. Other birds nesting along the creek were cranes,
herons, snipe and killdeers. On the upland were robins, meadow larks, prairie
chicken, grouse and many others.

Wild animal life was prolific along the creek. Badgers, mink, skunk, weasel,
coyote and gophers. Antelope were seen occasionally. The coyote serenaded them
nightly in the hills. The only hunting that was done by Mr. Klein was for
weasel, mink and gophers. The weasel and mink on several occasions killed
thirty or more young ducks and chickens in one night.

In 1896, Mr. and Mrs. Klein experienced a severe winter. The sod shanty they
lived in was covered with snow. Their sod barn was attached to the house but
the only entrance was from the outside and on one occasion they couldn't tend
the stock for three days. In March of that year came another bad blizzard. It
came up so fast that the cattle were unable to get home and were lost several
days before the pioneers even dared to venture out to look for them. By that
time the cattle had frozen to death. Many pioneers lost cattle in this
blizzard. Kleins found theirs all huddled together in a depression along the
creek frozen to death. That spring and in every spring after a heavy snow the
ice broke up, threatening to wash away the buildings. Ice blocks six to eight
feet long and four feet thick came tearing down the creek.

About the first of May in 1902, it rained so hard that the creek bottom was a
swirling rapids and rose so rapidly the Kleins had to flee to higher ground.

Frederick L. Klein died of heart ailment on May 18, 1922, at the age of 48
years, 10 months and 28 days. Mrs. Frederick Klein was remarried to J. D.
Wittmeier of Avon, South Dakota, in August of 1929, and at the present time is
living in Avon.

CHRISTIAN VORLANDER

Able, conscientious and philanthropic, such is the early pioneer's conception
of the man, Christian Vorlander. A native German, he was born in Hessen-Nassau,
Germany, on the 2nd of December, 1862. As a young man he came to Eureka, and
for more than 30 years played an active part in the industrial, economic and
political development of the city. Kind of heart and eager to help the
unfortunate, his business sagacity in many cases was instrumental in paving the
way to success for the pioneer farmers and business men in and about Eureka.
His ability as a banker was unquestioned, and he held the confidence of all
people who dealt with him. He served as president of the German Bank of Eureka
for many years, and later, when the name of that institution was changed to the
First National Bank, he served as president of that institution.

        Christian Vorlander and daughter, Irene

Mr. Vorlander was very active in civic affairs. In republican party circles, he
was recognized as one of the leaders in McPherson County. He was city treasurer
for many years, and in 1906 was elected to the office of mayor of the city of
Eureka. He held that office for one term, and in 1916 was again re-elected. His
second administration was characterized by many modern improvements being made
in the sewage and water works system of Eureka. Mr. Vorlander was very active
in the organization of the Eureka Commercial Club and was president of the
group for a time.

His untimely death occurred on the 11th of August, 1920, when he was slightly
over 59 years of age. Surviving him were his wife and four children. His wife
died in 1936. The names of the children and their residences are as follows:
Irene Ziesmer, Mankato, Minnesota; Norman Vorlander, Mankato, Minnesota; Edward
Vorlander, Minneapolis, Minnesota; Carl Vorlander, Minneapolis, Minnesota.

CHRISTIAN SCHELL

Christian Schell's life story is a part of the theme of hardship, privation and
final triumph which makes up the symphony of the winning of McPherson County to
civilization. He was born in Gluckstal, South Russia, on August 18, 1875. He
was married to Rosa Schell, the ceremony being performed by the Reverend Mr.
Biernbach. He emigrated from Russia in 1899, coming to Eureka in May of that
year.

He was then 26 years old and penniless. He homesteaded on land located about 18
miles southeast of Eureka. The usual sod buildings were erected and equipment
purchased. This consisted of an old wagon, grass mower, rake, hand plow and
harrow, all bought on terms. Since the crops did not always turn out well, it
was sometimes necessary to look for work, and occasionally he had to go many
miles from home before finding employment. In spite of many hardships and
disappointments, however, he prospered and eventually acquired 920 acres of
land, all free of encumbrance.

Mr. Schell served on the school board of his district in the capacity of
president, chairman and treasurer at various times. His son, Christian, also
served as treasurer from 1933 to 1936.

The Schells had two children, John, born May 23, 1906, and Christian, born
August 18, 1909, both farmers living on or near the homestead, southeast of
Eureka.

Mr. Schell retired from farming a few years ago, but remained on the old
homestead where he resides at present.

CHARLES MIX

Charles Mix, a native of South Russia, was born in 1872. At the age of nine he
emigrated to this country with his brothers, John, Fred, Gottlieb and Christ.

The five brothers first settled in Douglas County, in the southern part of
Dakota Territory. In 1888, they located on land southwest of Bowdle, while one
of them, Christ, came to Eureka and opened a tinshop and hardware store in
partnership with Gottlieb Doering. The following year Charles also came to
Eureka, where he was employed in his brother's tinshop.

In 1890, he went back to Douglas County, where he worked on a farm owned by a
relative until the latter part of 1891, when he returned to Eureka. Here, in
partnership with Peter Wentz, he bought out a harness shop and began conducting
that business. After several years, he sold his interest in the harness shop
and began buying grain and live stock for the market, continuing in the live
stock business until the time of his death in 1925. He was also active in
politics at one time and was elected sheriff of McPherson County for two terms.

In 1896, Mr. Mix was united in marriage to Sophia Glaser. There were four
children born to them, namely: Olivia, married to M. Wiederaenders, an
instructor in the Lutheran college at Waverly, Iowa; Alma, married to E. A.
Nelson of Wendell, Minnesota; Milbert, a member of the teaching profession,
residing in North Dakota; Karl, at present in the United States navy.

Some years after the death of her husband, Mrs. Mix was remarried to John
Lauck, and now resides in Alta Vista, Iowa.

EDWARD G. KENNEDY

One of the natural born Americans whose personality impressed itself vividly
upon the Eureka community was Edward G. Kennedy.

While he is remembered in Eureka primarily as an implement dealer, at one time
he had extensive ranch interests in the vicinity of Dawson, North Dakota. He
was a skillful horseman and always as much interested in live stock on the
farms.

He was a genial man, kind and considerate and left a host of friends in the
community when new duties called him away.

Edward G. Kennedy, a native of Pennsylvania, was born in Hollidaysburg on the
17th of December, 1884. He obtained his education in the common schools of that
state. After the Civil War, in which he took part, he secured a clerical
position in Pittsburgh, which he kept until 1889, when he determined to cast
his lot with the new territory of Dakota. He first located in Potter County,
then a part of Walworth County, where he remained until 1898, when he located
in Eureka, McPherson County, where he established himself in the grain and
agricultural implement business. In 1897 he was appointed to the office of
United States marshal for the district of South Dakota by President McKinley.
In 1902 he was reappointed by President Roosevelt. That year he closed out his
business in Eureka in order to devote his undivided attention to his official
duties.

On December 15, 1891, he was married to Miss Mary B. Brundage, of Bismarck,
North Dakota, who died on the 5th of April, 1900. Two children were born to
them, Ruth and Donald. Both Mr. and Mrs. Kennedy have died.

EMIL DIERENFELDT

One of the later arrivals in Eureka is Emil Dierenfeldt, born in Pommern,
Germany, on September 10, 1866. He came to this country when he was 17 years
old. For some time he farmed in the state of Missouri. He worked in packing
plants in Chicago, Illinois, and in several other states.

He came to Eureka in 1899, and purchased a meat market and grocery store.

On November 4, 1907, he married Katharina Reichmann in Germany. Five children
were born to them, as follows: Fred and Herman, who assist their father in the
meat market and on the farm; Otto, rancher and dry farmer, Ovid, Idaho; Alice,
assistant postmaster, Eureka; and Gertrude, attending college in Jamestown,
North Dakota. Mr. Dierenfeldt operated a feed lot and deals in live stock, in
addition to the meat market and grocery store. Now 70 years old, he still
manages his store.

                General James B. McPherson

President James Buchanan signed an act of Congress which created Dakota
Territory March 2, 1861. Lincoln came into office two days later. He appointed
William Jayne, who had been his family physician and neighbor at Springfield,
Illinois, the first governor of Dakota Territory.

Jayne's first official act was to count all white people in the territory.
There were 2,402. That was less people by 1200 to 1500 than voted for governor
in the 1982 election in McPherson County. This was near the beginning of the
Civil War.

Near the end of the same war, during Sherman's Atlanta Campaign, General James
B. McPherson, commanding the Army of the Tennessee, was fighting at Atlanta.

From this General who fell on the battlefield there in 1864, this county got
its name.

                McPherson County Officers

C.B. Keim       Jacob Maier, Jr.        L.E. Balliet            C.C. Hilgeman

                Organization of McPherson County

        Pioneer -- Court House -- New 1928

In 1884, there were scattered settlers in McPherson County, but no government
had been organized. There were no railroads, but immigration preceded them and
the fatal shooting of a young settler as the result of a quarrel emphasized the
need for government. Several men, who had squatted in McPherson County, were
spending the winter of 1884 in Ordway, then an important and flourishing town.
Among them were: Chas. N. Herreid, later governor, Henry Z. Moulton, John H.
Darlington, W. W. Cornwall and S. P. Hardenbrook. These five decided the county
should be organized, and upon petition to Governor Nehemiah Ordway, Moulton,
Darlington and John W. Dow were named commissioners.

It appears the organization preliminaries were started early in the year, but
slow communications dragged the time out until March 6, 1884, when a party of
five men driving two teams started from Ordway with Darlington's shack in
McPherson County as their objective. The day was bitterly cold with deep snow
on the ground. Arriving at Darlington's shanty, the party first were obliged to
shovel snow out of the barn so that their weary teams might be put in shelter
while they went about the business in hand. They had brought with them a sheet
iron stove which they set up in the shanty and after building a fire, the
business of organizing the new county was taken up.

Henry Z. Moulton was made chairman and Chas. N. Herried clerk, and thereupon
the other officers were appointed. At midnight the five declared the county
organized. Early historians give the location of Darlington's shanty as
Northeast Quarter Section 36, Township 125, Range 67. Officers pointed
according to the written report of Chas. N. Herried included: Register of deeds
and clerk, Chas. N. Herried; sheriff, Shannon P. Hardenbrook; assessor, Joseph
Worthy; judge of probate, Wendell W. Cornwall; surveyor, F,. D. Haynes;
treasurer, E. W. Howell; coroner, Z. Spitler; superintendent of schools, Wm. A.
Broadhurst; justices of the peace, Henry Hoover, Andrew Williams, Bela E.
Dieter; constables, John M. Hooker, Wm. Roberts, and George Kemp.

The organizers then went to bed, two in the bunk build into the shanty wall and
the others dressed in furs and high topped shoes bivouacked on the floor in a
position that allowed each of them to use a pair of feet as a pillow, a fact
vouched for by the late Governor Herried's own account of the incident. At
daybreak, the party breakfasted on cold victuals and started the weary return
trip to Ordway through the deep snow.

For reasons not entirely clear there was another organization meeting on June 6
of the same year. The same officers were named, however. The reason may have
been that while Governor Ordway named the commissioners, he failed to send them
credentials. At the first meeting they were merely equipped with certificates
of their appointments on the authority of the secretary of the territory.

Bibliography: McPherson County official records. Newspaper articles, L. E.
Falk. Interviews: James Clayton, Leola, S. Dak.; Amos Hoffman, Leola, S. Dak.;
Mrs. C. N. Herried, Aberdeen, S. Dak.; Gage Scrap Book, Aberdeen, S. Dak.

                HISTORY OF EUREKA PUBLIC SCHOOLS

        First School - 1888, School Group - 1890, Mrs. McKeever, Teacher

EUREKA SCHOOLS ... FROM ACORN TO OAK

August 80, 1888, nine months after the extension of the Chicago, Milwaukee and
St. Paul Railroad tracks to a point eight miles north of Hillsview, known as
"End of Track,' marks the beginning of the Eureka Public School system. At this
time the district bonded itself for $700.00 to provide school facilities for
their children.

The school bell tolled for the first time in the newly founded Eureka in the
late fall of 1888. The excitement that the tolling of the bell caused can
scarcely be imagined. Many children, though some had almost grown to maturity,
had never yet been in an American school, and lacked the fundamentals of
American A, B, C's.

Among the many young women who pioneered on these prairies of the Dakotas, I
think those who deserve a spot in the pages of history are the school teachers.
Think of the difficulties that this pioneer educator, Mrs. McKeever, the first
public school teacher, encountered. Although she had 47 pupils enrolled, we
find no classified classes, nor suitable books, and most of the children
possessed no knowledge of the English language. Small or large, 6 or 16 years
old, a]l were enrolled in the primary department, where their daily routine was
as follows: reading, grammar, geography, and spelling, after which they put
their books away, folded their arms and were dismissed.

Prior to the organization of the Eureka Independent School District, April 18,
1893, the town children attended school "in the country Districts to which the
territory that composed the new City belonged; that is, those north of the
Highway Number 10 attended school in the Gluecksthal Township (127-73), those
south of that line attended school in the Bauer School District, (twp. 126-73).
They had two small school houses in the former part of the town and one in the
southern part. After the creation of the Independent District these small
houses were moved up right west of the new two-story frame school house which
had been built late in 1892. The other one from Bauer District was moved up
just across the street south from the two-story frame school house and used
both as a church and school house."

The first Independent School Board was composed of the following members: John
Stoller, Christ Wentz, Geo. Bippus, Jos. Worthy, Jos. Stoullil and Geo. Tammin.

        Mrs. McKeever -- 1888 Eureka's First Teacher

Other pioneer educators and co-laborers of Mrs. McKeever, who can not be passed
without mentioning were: Bessie McAssey, Ida Michael, and D. B. Strait, who
will be remembered as the first Public School Principal. His period of service
began in the fall of 1892 and ended in June, 1894. It was during this period
that Eureka organized independently, and built the two-story frame building for
$2,500.00.

The school opened in the fall of 1894, with C. B. Foncannon as principal. Mr.
Foncannon resides at present at Aberdeen and I quote from his recent letter the
following: "At the beginning of my term we graded the school as best we could.
Most of the pupils were not able to speak any, or very little English, and the
lower grades, of course, were the crowded ones."

"The large two-story frame building at first contained but two large rooms, but
they were finally divided into two upstairs and two downstairs. However, the
room downstairs was not divided the first year as the primary grade was there
and had a large enrollment." It was a rather common occurrence to find an
enrollment of from 70 to 85 pupils in the primary room. "I think there were 280
enrolled the first year. During most of the winter season, night school was
held to teach language to adults."

Mr. Foncannon served as principal until November, 1897, when he resigned on
account of ill health. He was elected County Superintendent of Schools the
following fall. It was while serving in the latter capacity that he made the
following statement: "Five hundred eighty children of school age in McPherson
County have not attended school a single day, 200 children have attended only a
few days, 100 children of school age out of 2,438 total eligible children
attended the 12 weeks required by law."

November, 1897-May, 1898 (W. D. Moffatt, Principal)

Mr. Moffatt came to Eureka to take over Mr. Foncannon's principalship of the
Eureka Public School. The school records reveal to us the first pupils who had
reached the distinction of completing the eighth grade in the Eureka Public
Schools. All five were duly graduated May 27, 1898. They are: Flay Rathman,
Edward Stoullil, James Parker, Peter McSnany, and Mary Worthy.

September, 1898-May, 1900 (Ed. W. Maloney, Principal)

The school years of 1898-1900 brought forth two important events, at least from
the pupils' point of view, namely: the closing of the school on October 5, so
that both pupils and teachers could attend the Grain Palace Exhibit in
Aberdeen, and again in January, when a scourge of scarletina was passing
through Eureka and community. The grim reaper snatched away three primary
pupils, of whom the record reads, "Of such is the Kingdom of Heaven."

In the following year we find an enrollment of 201 pupils, of whom James Finlay,
a visitor wrote, "Very pleased with classes, teacher and pupils; they are
bright and energetic."

School was closed on September 17, in honor of President McKinley's burial, and
again during the month of January, when a severe siege of measles waylaid school
activities for the month.

September, 1900-May, 1902 (L. A. Weatherby, Principal)

It is during these years that the grade classification takes definite form. The
enrollment was as follows: 8th grade, 12; 7th grade, 6; 6th grade, 6; 5th grade
A, 18; 5th grade B, 18; 4th grade A, 23; 4th grade B, 9; 3rd grade, 18; 2nd A
division, 25; 2nd B division, 29; 1st grade A, 29; 1st grade B, 26; 1st grade
C, 21; 1st Primary, 6.

One can see very readily that the primary classes had the greatest enrollment.
The record states that, from this first grade and the primary group, 17 were
promoted and 24 withdrew during the term. Rather irregular attendance. All
non-tuition pupils were charged $2.00 per month, tuition payable in advance.
The annual tax levy during these years read as follows: Tuition, $4,000.00;
General, $1,200.00; Sinking, $300.00; Interest, $224.00; the total being $5,774.

Included in the records we find the following resolutions of the Board: A
teacher marrying during the school term is considered a breach of contract. The
average teacher salary is $55.00 per month. "The Eureka Post" was the official
organ of the Board of Education.

The School Board consisted of seven members who were elected in connection with
the regular city election, electing two members for each ward, and one member
at large for two-year terms. Hard coal was their favorite fuel, as they bid for
30 tons at $10.50 per ton.

September, 1902-May, 1904 (M. M. Guhin, Principal)

This period marks an important forward movement in Eureka's Educational System.
The Board considered with Principal Guhin the adding of the ninth grade and the
issuing of diploma to those finishing this course. So, in April, 1903, the
clerk was requested to order four blank diplomas for commencement. In due
course, after many hours of diligent search and inquiry, we find listed the
first four proud ninth graduates (May, 1903), namely: Clara
Clayton, Nettie Smith, Edwin Koch, and Willie Guhin.

        Pioneer Grade and High School -- 1894

        Grade School -- 1909

Due to the high cost of hard coal, and not knowing what to do about it, the
Board was called upon to ask community opinion at a mass meeting. Here it was
decided to use soft coal and purchase hot blast stoves. This, however. proved
unsatisfactory, and the next year we find that Janitor Klipfle was ordered to
again set up the hard coal stoves.

In August, 1903, the Board felt no further need of their First School House; so
it was offered for sale. All bids offered were found unsatisfactory and were
rejected; later, this property was sold to the Lutheran Church Society for
$175.00. This I understand, was later converted into a dwelling house.

Grammar High School Attendance Summary:

Number of boys enrolled ....... 111
Number of girls enrolled ......  95

Total ......................... 206

In answer to Professor Guhin's resignation, we find the following resolution:
"Resolved: that the Board tender Prof. Guhin its thanks for the efficient work
he has done in our public school, and that it regrets that he has decided to
engage in other business.

"The Board extends its best wishes for his success in whichever field he may
work."

September, 1904-May, 1905 (Emmet McKenna, Principal)

An educational monument, the present grade building, was launched in April,
1905. The Board resolved to build a suitable school not to exceed $10,000.00.
Upon counting the votes the bond election had carried by a majority of three
votes.

Another group of four students received diplomas in May.

The Board sought a $3,000.00 loan from Commissioner Bach. His communication was
filed with the clerk. It seems, from my investigation, this loan never
materialized, and nothing further of this large school building project was
heard of.

September, 1905-May, 1907 (H. B. Collins, Principal)

This is the first notice of the local teaching staff appearing in the
"Northwest Blade":

"HIGH SCHOOL AND GRAMMAR DEPARTMENT REGISTRATION, SEPTEMBER 9

School opens Monday, September 11, 1905.

Corps of Teachers

Herbert B. Collins, Principal of City Schools.
Miss Cecelia A. Emmons, Principal of Grammar Dept.
Miss H. Mae Jefferson, First Intermediate Department.
Miss Mabel Little, Second Intermediate Department.
Miss Mabel Murray, Primary Department.
Miss Emmons, Librarian.
John Klipfel, Janitor."

The October 5, 1905, minutes allowed a bill of $40.00 to W. E. Kelly for school
building plans, and a bill of $462.00 to Hollandsworth & Hart Lumber Company.
This, evidently, was for an addition to the school house standing to the west
of the two-story building.

Tuition for non-resident children was advanced to $12.00 per month to be paid
in advance.

The closing of the school on May 31, 1906, meant that another round had been
reached in Eureka's Educational climb. The record reveals that the tenth grade
class was presented with diplomas. A well chosen and fitting topic,
"Preservation of the Niagara Falls," was given by the first and only tenth
grade graduate, Edward Isaak.

September, 1907-May, 1909 (Harold D. Wright, Principal)

Upon opening the schools, it was found that the primary rooms had insufficient
room and seats. A temporary relief for this was found by having one group of
the children attend in the morning, another group in the afternoon. This,
however, proved not very satisfactory. So another teacher was employed, and the
offer of the Lutheran Church to use their school house was accepted. However,
the church later found the need of their own school house, and our primary
class marched down town and occupied a room in the City Hall for the remainder
of the school term.

In April, 1909, the Board met to canvass the returns of the election held
Tuesday, April 20, 1909, for the purpose of issuing bonds for a suitable school
building. Total votes cast, 122; of which 97 were cast in favor of issuing
bonds.

The Board advertised for bids for the sale of school bonds as follows:
$3,000.00, payable in 10 years; $3,000.00, payable in fifteen years; $3,000.00,
payable in 20 years from date of issue.

The Board met in May for the purpose of conferring with Architect George
Issenhuth, Huron, regarding plans for the new school building. It was decided
to award the contract for the plans and specifications to the above named
architect. The public was notified that the Board would receive bids for the
construction of a two-story brick school house. The Board found it necessary to
sell their two old school houses to make room for the new two-story brick
building. Martin Stickel's bid of $650.00 for the old two-story school was
accepted; so was Daniel Mettler's bid of $310.00 for the small school house.
Both houses had to be removed from the school grounds by June 15, 1909.

The whole block of $9,300.00 bonds was sold to The Farmers and Merchants State
Bank of Eureka, paying $1.03 premium for the entire block of bonds, bearing 5
per cent interest rate to be paid semi-annually. The contract for the proposed
school building was awarded to Mr. C. J. Parks of Alexandria, Minnesota, for
the sum of $14,345.00.

On July 14, 1909, a resolution was formulated requesting the Mayor to call an
election for the purpose of ascertaining public opinion of this school district
on the question of incurring an indebtedness of $6,000.00 for providing proper
ventilation, heating, sewage and water facilities for the proposed school.

At the election the bond issue carried by a 50 vote majority.

The bid of the German Bank of this city to pay $6,000.00 for the entire bond,
bearing 7 per cent interest, was accepted.

Dow & Dow's bid for heating, ventilating, and plumbing at $4,000.00 was also
accepted, while W. H. Miller received the contract for the sewer and water
system for $410.00.

School Board

O. H. Gerdes, President. Other members were: H. Reinhardt, J. Schall, George
Knickerbocker, G. J. Zenk, Ed. Maag, clerk, and G. G. Klein, treasurer

September, 1909-May, 1910 (J. F. Watschke, Principal)

School opening was delayed this year until September 20. because the new school
building was not completed. However, in order to carry on school the Board
leased the former two-story school building, now owned by John Smith, for
$25.00 per month, as well as the two east rooms of the City Hall, and the
Lutheran school house, all three to be used until the new school building was
completed.

The December 23rd minutes read, "School house erected by C. J. Porter is
completed and accepted as satisfactory."

September, 1911-May, 1913 (C. W. Gruver, Principal)

In order to provide soft water for the boiler, the Board had Mr. Casper Spries
make a cistern on the northwest corner of the building. But this seemingly did
not furnish sufficient water. The Board then hired Adam A. Deibert to dig a
well. This well was later fitted with a pump and engine which gave the school
an independent water system.

The tax levy for 1911 contained the following: $2,400 general fund, $2,500
tuition fund, $2,000 sinking fund.

Six large wall clocks were bought in September, one for each room. At present
these clocks still keep good time. Scarlet fever visited the community. The
school was not closed but fumigated every Saturday during the epidemic.

The Board moved to have five and one-half hours school in all grades every day,
except the first grade.

No substitute teachers were permitted except in case of sickness.

The Board resolved to continue granting diplomas to students passing the tenth
grade at the close of each school year.

September, 1913-May, 1916 (C. S. Suddarth, Principal)

The Board felt the need of some lighting fixtures in the entrance of the school
house. Janitor Pietz was ordered to purchase two or three gasoline lights and
install them in the entrance of the school house, so that the school could be
used evenings to hold meetings and evening school.

It was moved and carried (March 28, 1913) to abolish kindergarten.

The Board also felt the need for more play room. With this in mind they
purchased the vacant lot from the German Reformed Church for $175.00 and the
Philip Oster property adjoining the school to the west for $1,500.00. The Oster
house was later sold to John Ladner for $625.00.

The minutes of January 30, 1915, read: "On account of the direct draft that
enters the school house door every time the door is opened, the board deems it
necessary to build a vestibule." This was to be done as soon as possible. Well,
it must have been impossible, for we are still waiting.

Visitor Wm. J. P. Giedt reports "Good Work" in all classes.

September, 1916-May, 1918 (R. Smith, Principal)

School had entered into a period of rapid expansion. Mr. Smith was asked to
draw up the eleventh grade course of study, which conformed to the state
course. Upon studying the same it was adopted and ordered to be installed in
September, 1917.

The Board invested in $3,000.00 city bonds, payable in 1917-1918.

The first eleventh grade graduation in Eureka took place on the 24th of May,
1918. Diplomas were granted to the following: Florence Giedt, Calma Schamber,
Lillian Schamber, Emma Schock, Matthew Smith, and Hildegard Stoller.

September, 1918-May, 1921 (J. Jones, Jr., Superintendent)

Due to the cold weather driving into the windows, George Hauenstein was offered
a $417.00 contract to weather strip the windows in the school house and the
double doors at the entrance.

Superintendent Jones urged the building of a laboratory, and to this end the
Board purchased $271.00 worth of equipment. Much of this equipment is used at
present.

The Dakota Utilities was paid $107.00 for wiring the school house.

Again the minutes state, "On motion it was decided to repair or build an
entrance to the school house." It is too bad, but it seems that the Board
failed again in providing adequate protection from the cold.

Tax levy was raised to $7,600.00 and tuition rates advanced to $24.00 a month
in advance.

The beginning of our basketball activities dates back to September 6, 1919. The
Board ordered Janitor Louis Papendick to erect a basketball structure on the
grounds.

The minutes again state that grades four, five and six are over crowded, and
that an extra room and teacher are needed.

The clerk read a communication (January 31, 1920) received from the State
Department of Education, stating that the Eureka High School had met the needed
requirements and had become a fully accredited twelve grade high school; in
which status Eureka High School still continues. The first students to be
graduated from a full four-year high school course are: Florence Giedt, Lillian
Schamber Emma Schock, Hildagard Stoller, Otto Isaak, and Mathilda Isaak.

The Board again felt the need for expansion, and bought Louis Papendick's
property for $3,800.00 and A. Sprich's property for $4,600.00, and extended an
invitation to Architect Foss to come to Eureka to plan an enlargement to the
school system.

A. W. Schumacher bought the buildings from the A. Sprich property for $781.00.

Tax levy raised to 15 1/2 mills.

The Board seems to have had considerable trouble in hiring and keeping a full
teaching staff. During the three years that Mr. Jones acted as Superintendent
of Schools his and the teaching staff's salaries were raised each year.
Superintendent (1918-1919) $155.00, (1919-1920) $188.00, (1920-1921) $220.00
per month. High school teacher salaries ranged from $155.00 to $166.00. The
grade teachers' salaries ranged from $110.00 to $135.00 per month.

Tuition for non-resident students was raised from $24.00 to $36.00 per month.

September, 1921-May, 1922 (E. M. Pfutzenreiter, Supt.)

Tuition was raised to $50.00 per year.

In August, 1921, a motion was made to exclude all resident pupils from
attending the Eureka city schools because of over-crowded conditions; later the
Bauer district pupils were admitted because there were only a few children.

No school on September 21-22, because of County Fair days.

Thanksgiving week with pay was granted to all the teachers provided they attend
the Institute.

The Board took up the need of expansion again, and submitted to the electors
the question of issuing $50,000 bonds at 6 per cent for constructing and
equipping a high school for the Eureka Independent School District on January
27, 1922.

On May 2, 1922, the special bond election favored the issuing by a 55 vote
majority.

The entire block of $32,000.00 bonds was sold at a $1,495 premium to Drake
Ballard. The last of these bonds maturing in 1942. Board: Fred Hepperle, Phil
Schamber Gust Mauser, H. I. Bauer, and Solomon Isaak.

The Board bought the Pietz & Schamber lot for $100.00 on which to move the
Warner house. For this work Mr. Quast was paid $480.00.

J. W. Henry was awarded the architectural work and D. D. Johnson the
construction work, both of Aberdeen. C. F. Blackman received the contract for
electric fixtures at $2,030.00.

September, 1922-May, 1923 (H. H. Wilson, Superintendant)

Superintendent Wilson changed texts and courses to form to the State Department
of Education.

Resolution: "Be it resolved that Public School teachers be prohibited to attend
dances during school sessions on the following nights: Sunday, Monday, Tuesday,
Wednesday, and Thursday. This action was not taken because of a prejudice
against dancing, but because of the best interests of the school."

Mr. D. D. Johnson constructed a chimney for the old school house to remedy the
defective heating system caused by a faulty flue.

Walter Broich was appointed truant officer for the year. The Warner house
underwent considerable repair and remodeling to put in shape for living
quarters.

The matter of drying out the new building was taken up on December 2, 1922. It
seemed that the new High School building had neared completion.

Mr. Henry Kundert was hired as janitor for $80.00 per month (August 16, 1923).

The Board purchased a New International Encyclopedia for $168.00, which is
still in use.

The Board decided to dedicate the new High School on Lincoln's Birthday,
February 12, 1923.

"A well rendered and appreciated dedication program was held Monday evening,
February 12, 1923, in the new High School gymnasium. The opening program
consisted of several well rendered overtures by the Eureka City Band, under the
direction of E. L. Fisher.

"Superintendent Wilson gave the address of welcome, which was followed by
Frieda Mettler's (Kelle) reading of `Lincoln's Gettysburg Address.' Edwin S.
Isaak, Eureka's young talented tenor, rendered a solo, accompanied by Elsie
Kiesz at the piano.

"Thomas Ringsrud, clerk of the School Board, spoke on Lincoln's life, after
which Walvin Giedt gave a speech in behalf of the students.

"To close the ceremonies, the audience was treated to n fast and furious
basketball game between Ashley High School and Eureka's stars: Jerome Carr, O.
Elster, Bones Hepperle, Ed. Serr, and Edwin Siegele.

"As good as our local boys looked, they lacked the ability of letting the ball
fall through the loop. The score 31-7 may seem lopsided, but it was no one's
game till the whistle blew at the close."

September, 1923-May, 1925 (O. G. McMillan, Supt.)

Tax levy 15 1/2 mills for running expenses and 7 mills for over-due bonds and
interest for sinking fund purposes.

Henry Kundert was rehired as janitor and appointed truant officer.

New sidewalks were built for the new school house by Louis Papendick.

The Board sanctions a full 12 unit High School course as follows:

9th Year--English, Algebra, Ancient History, Latin.
10th Year--English, Geometry, Modern History, Latin.
11th Year--English, American History and Government,
  Algebra and Solid Geometry, German.
12th Year--English, Physics, Sociology and Economics,
  German.

September, 1925-May, 1930 (Ferdinand Hochhalter, Supt.)

Eureka's forward Educational desire was again seen in the Board's action!
Requiring first grade certificates of all teachers employed in the Eureka
Public School.

Mr. Pfeffer, a local business man, donated a considerable sum of money to the
High School to be used in buying books for the library.

The tax levy for 1925 was $20,700 00.

Superintendent Hochhalter drew up a set of rules for the High School students,
which set forth proper conduct in and out of school.

The Board grants Superintendent Hochhalter permission to build a portage stage
in the gymnasium and to install showers in the wash rooms.

The Lutheran Church received permission to use the gymnasium for church
meetings and confirmation exercises, during the time the Lutheran Church was
being built. However, the school house was not to be used for religious
purposes by out of town sects or denominations.

        Henry Kundert, Custodian

Resolved: "Dancing is prohibited at all times in the High School!" (May 11,
1927).

The 1927 tax levy had dropped to $17,000.00.

September 30, 1929, brings the completion of our present curriculum. The Board
added the domestic science department for the girls and shop facilities for the
boys.

Dr. Gerdes, who had served the Eureka community as a board member for 23 years,
resigned, and, with his resignation presented a set of Nelson's loose leaf
Encyclopedias to the school. The set is in constant use and kept in an up to
date edition.

The local banks were closed in December, 1930, and all bills were tabled in the
regular meeting.

The 1931 tax levy was reduced to $16,000.00.

September, 1931-May, 1933 (B. L. Berhow, Superintendent)

The High School gymnasium floor was taken up and relaid with new sub-flooring.

Superintendent Berhow consulted the Board regarding building a balcony on the
east side of the gymnasium, which provided additional seating space and a place
for the boys' lockers.

Tuition rates were increased to $50.00 for the grades and $85.00 for the High
School students.

The "E Club" was granted permission to use the High School gymnasium on nights
when it was not in use by the High School, at the rate of $1.00 per night,
provided they appoint some one to have general charge and see to it that
everybody be out of the building and lights out by 10:30.

Resolutions: "All school children must be off the school grounds by 8:00 p. m.
and all minors be prohibited from loafing in pool halls and places of
questionable nature."

"Grade teachers must have at least two years of teaching experience before
becoming eligible for the Eureka school system."

"A married woman with an able bodied husband shall not be employed as teacher."

"Women marrying during school year, shall be suspended upon 30 days notice."

"No teacher shall influence or give special instruction to any grade pupil for
advancing said pupil more than one grade in one year."

"All large orders are to be handled through local merchants and be approved by
purchasing committee."

"Each teacher must supervise his or her own children on playground."

"Playground activities should be planned in advance the same as classwork."

"Problems of discipline, which teachers are unable to cope with, must be
reported and disposed of at once by superiors."

The petition of the High School boys asking the Board to buy football equipment
was tabled for the year.

September, 1933-May, 1935 (M. A. Midthun, Supt.)

The Board advertised for bids on a stoker, and hired J. B. Harris, as
consulting engineer, to check the present heating system and draw plans for
stoker installation.

E. R. Schulkoski's bid for a "Bros Stoker" for $1,367 was accepted and ordered
to be installed.

The Board decided that they no longer needed the old Warner house which had
been moved from the school grounds to provide more play ground and offered the
same for sale. Amelia Kolpacoff's bid of $1,000.00 was accepted. The Board felt
that the children needed more play room. Mr. Bertsch's property which joined
the school play ground was purchased for $1,725.00.

The old Papendick house and barn were sold to David Kurle for $190.00.

September, 1935- (W. O. Lenz, Superintendent)

The sinking fund had accumulated to such an extent that the Board felt, since
they could not take up any of the outstanding bonds in advance, it would be
better to invest some of this money in interest bearing securities. This was
accomplished through the purchase of $7,000 city sewer bonds at 4 per cent
interest.

        Eureka High School Basketball Team
        District Champions -- 1929
        Walter Kolpacoff, Henry Ehrman, Herbert Stickle, John Bride,
        Coach. Fred Homeyer, Harold Heilman, Edwin Schmitt, Captain,
        Werner Straub, Helmut Bertsch.

The Board authorized the purchase of a set of Compton's Encyclopedias for the
grades and a senior set of Britannica for the High School.

The last of the old double seats in the fourth grade room were replaced with
new single seats.

For some years the School Board, as well as the Eureka community, had felt that
their present educational curriculum needed overhauling to make it more
selective and offer greater opportunities in self expression and in music
appreciation.

This desire was partly realized through funds obtained from a High School
Carnival. A mimeograph was purchased, and under the able direction of Mr.
Pitkanen, a monthly school periodical, "The Trojanite," was issued, thus giving
the students an opportunity to develop their writing talents and enabling the
community to know of school doings.

From this same fund football equipment was purchased, enabling the school to
place the boys under a more complete program of supervised physical development
and culture.

An upper and a lower grade chorus were organized and placed on a regular
practice schedule. To follow up this foundation music laid in the grades, boys'
and girls' Glee Clubs were organized in the High School, which led toward
granting both credit and honor upon completion of the courses.

On November 11, 1935, a High School Band was organized and practice begun, much
to credit of the city council, which donated to the school what old band
equipment the city still retained from the former city band, and to the School
Board, which authorized the purchase of two Bass and two French horns, street
drums and sundry equipment amounting to $600.00.

The honor and credit of having the Eureka High School Band appear in uniform,
goes to the city council and School Board who jointly pledged the necessary
funds enabling their purchase.

The High School Glee Clubs under the direction of Miss Helen Engebretson, and
the High School Band under the direction of Principal Case, proved at Mobridge,
where they were entered in competition with similar organizations that they had
made splendid progress by bringing back three trophies. The band, as well as
the several solo and ensembles, received superior ratings.

This community's 50-year ever increasing desire for a more liberal education
and for more avenues of self expression will largely be materialized and
consummated when its present authorized addition to the High School building
will become a reality. -- Supt. W. O. Lenz

EVANGELICAL LUTHERAN ZION CHURCH

Along with the growth of Eureka, came the development and need of religious
institutions, and in January, 1889, sentiment became so strong that Lutheran
believers were encouraged to form a congregation.

The Reverend August Zimmerman, a missionary, who had come to this section the
previous year, 1888, was doing some very fine work in the Hoffnungsthal and
Hoffnungsfeld congregations west and northwest of our city. It was John J. Harr
and a few faithful workers, who took it upon themselves to invite the Reverend
Zimmerman to attend a meeting held in the schoolhouse to ascertain whether or
not it would be advisable to organize a congregation of the Lutheran faith.
Much to the dismay of the presiding pastor, there were some twenty heads of
families present to express their desire for the organization of a Lutheran
congregation. The amazed Reverend Zimmerman retaliated with these words: "I
thought there were perhaps six or seven who were really interested in
organizing a congregation in Eureka and now, to my surprise, there are many who
have already expressed their willingness to become n part of this religious
organization."

Again on February 9, 1889, the Reverend Zimmerman was called to take charge of
a special meeting. Much work had been done toward increasing the number on the
membership roll. The name of the newly organized group was chosen, and was to
be known as the Evangelical Lutheran Zion congregation. This was the first
official step to be taken and with the acceptance of the constitution and
bylaws of the Lutheran Iowa synod, the framework of a strong religious
institution was laid. It was at this gathering that men were selected to act as
deacons. Those chosen were as follows: Christoph Schumacher, John Wittmayer,
Johann Schlaht, and Gottlieb Doering, the latter to act as secretary.

The following is a list of the charter members of the organization, many of
whom are still living: Christoph Schumacher, Gottlieb Doering, John Wittmayer,
Johann Schlaht, Gottlieb Mix, Jacob Liedle, Friedrich Mix, Christian Wolff,
Conrad Schmidt, Adam Kuebler, Christoph Wittmayer, Martin Stickel, Gottlieb
Kraenzler, Wilhelm Christmann, Peter Wentz, William S. Siegele, Michael
Schlaht, Jacob Bieber, Sr., Lorenz Preszler, Jacob Rau, Christian Kuebler,
Philip P. Martell, John Stoller, Jacob Aldinger, Karl Layer. Johannes Stickel,
Christian Doering, Gottlieb Klein, George W. Christmann, Gottlieb Hellwig, Fred
Hepperle, Gottfried Quast, Philip Wiedmeier, Johannes Siegele, Henry Frey,
Emanuel Reiner, Fred Beck, John J. Harr, Christ Wentz, Friedrich Holwaegner,
Christoph Hezel, David Isaak, Michael Schaeffer, Friedrich Schamber, Julius
Schamber, Philip Schamber, Emil Schamber, and Christoph Mix.

At this same meeting on February 8, 1889, it was decided to erect a church
building some time during the coming year. After much discussion the following
members were elected to the building committee: Jacob Liedle, John Stoller,
Christian Wolff, Gottlieb Mix, Jacob Bieber, Sr., Christian Wentz, with the
Reverend A. Zimmerman to act as chairman and Gottlieb Doering as secretary.

On March 17, 1890, a meeting was held for the purpose of providing a place of
abode for the minister, with the understanding that the pastor take up his
residence in the city. John Stoller was selected to work with the church board
in the solution of this problem, with the understanding that the cost was not
to exceed $500.

The members of the entire parish were called together again on May 11, 1890,
when the group agreed to pay their pastor $400 for the ensuing year's work, and
to hire an assistant pastor to take charge of the parochial school at A salary
of $200 yearly. The latter sum of money to be paid by parents of children
attending the school.

On October 5, 1890, the committee working on the parsonage reported that the
structure was complete and ready to he occupied. This building was located on
the lots now the property of John Schlichenmayer, across the street from Jacob
Nehlich. At this meeting, the Evangelical Lutheran Parochial Society was
incorporated and included the following congregations: Evangelical Lutheran
Zion, St. Petri, Friedens and Trinity. Officers to act for the new
consolidation were: John J. Harr, secretary; the Reverend August Zimmerman,
treasurer; and the following as trustees: Martin Stickel, Christ Wentz,
Christoph Schumacher, Philip Miller, Franz Weisenburger, Conrad Pfeifle and Carl
Fischer Application was made by the Hoffnungsthal congregation to join the
newly organized society and on March 15, 1891, at a special meeting, this group
was taken in.

        First Lutheran Church -- 1889

At a meeting held on August 23, 1891, the Eureka congregation met and elected
members to solicit funds for the erection of a church building. These men were:
John J. Harr, John Stoller, Christ Wentz, Christ Doering, Emanuel Reiner, John
Wittmayer and David Isaak. It was not until February 7, 1892, that a report was
received from the bui1ding committee. The committee had been quite successful
and had collected $774.91 and told the members of the church that there would
be no further delays in the erection of the building.

A special meeting of the parish was held on August 30, 1891, at which time
Hoffnungsfeld congregation was included in the parochial society. The Reverend
F. Walter was employed as assistant pastor to have charge of the school at a
salary of $1.00 per family. On April 10, 1892, the St. John's congregation was
included in the growing parish with a total of seven congregations, all under
the supervision of the Reverend A. Zimmerman. In the fall of the same year the
Neudorf congregation, also, was accepted into the society, making eight
separate congregations.

Great progress had been made with the building of the new church building and
on June 26, 1892, the structure was formally dedicated. About a year later,
June 21, 1893, the congregations were called together to consider the
resignation of the Reverend Zimmerman, who was compelled to change climate
because of the ill health of his wife. On July 9, 1893, a call was issued to
the Reverend Heinrich Reinhardt, who accepted the work at a salary of $500 per
year, serving all eight churches.

On March 14, 1894, the Lutheran cemetery of Eureka came into existence with the
grant of an acre of land for this purpose by John J. Harr. A committee
consisting of Fred Hepperle, John Wittmayer and John J. Harr, was chosen to
plat the new cemetery into plots 16 1/2 x 16 1/2 feet and to offer them for
sale at $3.00 each, the money derived from such sales to be used for
maintenance. Charles Pfeffer was contacted in December, 1894, relative to
securing a road to the newly plotted cemetery by a committee consisting of David
Isaak, Christian Wentz and Christian Doering.

The Evangelical Lutheran congregation had grown to such an extent that in
November, 1896, they severed their connections with the Evangelical Lutheran
parochial society and established a parish of their own. The Reverend Heinrich
Reinhardt accepted the call as their first pastor.

        Pioneer Zion Lutheran Church -- 1889

In April, 1902, it was decided to purchase the lot east of the church building
from Friedrich Gall, and move the parsonage to that location. Those in charge
of this work were: Johannes Klein, Gottfried Quast and Jacob Thurn.

In the year 1903, the Reverend Reinhardt solicited funds to be used in buying a
school building which was used in future years by the German Parochial
confirmants.

Not until the meeting on May 13, 1906, were any plans made for the sorely
needed addition to the church building. It was decided to build wings to the
east and the west of their present structure. The Reverend Reinhardt again
volunteered to lead a group in the solicitation of the funds, and in the spring
of 1907, with the aid of Julius Schamber, John Stoller and Jacob Thurn, was the
work completed, and, it was then found necessary to assess each member to raise
the $4,000. The work was completed in 1908.

In 1910, the church board, Jacob Hoffmann, Jacob Thurn and John Liedle,
supervised the erection of the present parsonage at a cost of approximately
$5,000. The old parsonage was sold to the Lutheran college to be used for a
home for one of the instructors.

After the resignation of the Reverend Reinhardt in April, 1913, a call was
issued to the Reverend L. Seehase, Clinton, Iowa, who accepted and arrived in
Eureka on August 23, 1913 During the absence of a minister caused by the
change, various ministers from the college served the Lutheran congregation.

During the past few years, it had become apparent to the members that they
would soon be faced with the problem of expanding the church building. However,
nothing was done until 1920, when it was decided some action must be taken. A
committee consisting of John Doering, Johannes Klein, John Stock, Gottfried
Quast and Heinrich Straub, were instructed to get plans and the approximate
cost for the proposed addition. This committee reported on February 8, 1920,
that the approximate cost to build a suitable addition would be $5,385, which
it was believed, would increase the seating capacity to 120 people. This
proposal was rejected by the church members. Another committee composed of
Henry Pietz, Karl Kafer, Jacob Bieber, Johannes Klein and Christian Kuszmaul,
was elected for the purpose of getting figures on a new church building. The
findings of the committee were reported and it was pointed out that a frame
structure, as planned, would cost $26,000. The vote was again taken and the
proposed structure lost by only two votes. The idea of building was temporarily
abandoned and not until September, 1922, was the old housing problem brought to
the foreground. Another committee was elected consisting of Heinrich Straub,
Fred Hepperle, Philip Schamber, Jacob Bieber and William Boettcher, and they
immediately contacted the firm of Buechner & Orth to secure figures on a brick
structure, the size they believed to be adequate for the congregation. Again
the building of a new church was dropped, because the estimated cost of the
building, $40,000, was believed to be more than could possibly be raised and
the congregation did not want to go in debt.

At a meeting in April, 1925, the group decided to attack the building problem
from a different angle. A committee consisting of Christian Kuszmaul, Christian
Goehring and Jacob Bieber, were instructed to make a canvass of members for
subscriptions for the new building, and in August, 1925, it was found that this
group had raised the sum of $210,055. Henry Ottmar was elected building
treasurer.

It was on November 28, 1925, that definite steps were taken and the erection of
a new brick structure was assured. Due to the death of one member of the board,
Fred Hepperle, Christian Kuszmaul was selected to fill the vacancy. On February
7, 1926, the committee reported to the congregation that a building, as
proposed, would cost $60,000. A vote favored the continuance of the plan by a
73 to 19 margin. An equalization board was elected consisting of John Liedle,
Gottfried Quast, Heinrich Straub, John Wolff and Fred Koenig. This board
reported that a sum of $53,600 should be raised and at this time the building
committee was instructed to enter into an agreement with A. J. Clocksin,
contractor.

The last service, with communion, was held in the old church on March 7, 1926.
On the following Monday a large delegation gathered at the church to assist in
tearing down of the old building. But, before actual work was started, a bid of
$600 was received for the old church and this was accepted by the board, and by
March 20 the lot was cleared, ready for the new church. During the building of
the new church all services were held in the school gymnasium. The money for
the beautiful decorations on the two large windows was donated by the Sunday
School and the Ladies Aid. The latter organization, also, donated funds for
other furnishings, such as rugs.

The laying of the cornerstone occurred on June 28, 1926.

Following the suggestion of the Reverend Seehase a group of young folks, who
had been confirmed by the local pastor, headed by Theo. Straub, were organized
to raise funds to defray the cost of a pipe organ. It was but a short time
until approximately half the funds needed for this purpose were on hand.

        Zion Lutheran

Friedens and Neudorf congregations voluntarily gave $2,000 toward the new
building. By November 1, 1926, this fine structure was completed and on
November 7, it was dedicated. The building and equipment complete, cost
approximately $67,000, which was paid for in full at the time of its completion.

The Reverend Seehase, who had served the congregation for the past twenty-three
years, suffered a paralytic stroke in March of 1936, and was forced to
discontinue his work. Neighboring pastors were called upon to fill the vacancy
for a time, and on July 1, 1936, the Reverend Fred Schnaible was called to
serve temporarily and take charge of the parochial school. He remained here
until August 1, when he found it necessary to answer a call to Port Huron,
Michigan.

        Zion Lutheran Church -- 1926

On August 23, 1936, it being just twenty-three years since the arrival of the
Reverend Seehase to take charge of this parish, a farewell service was given in
his honor. Ministers assisting in the service were: The Reverend H.


Haase, of Hosmer; the Reverend John Schmitt, of the Hoffnungsthal congregation,
and the Reverend Orville Boettcher. The service ended with a brief talk by the
Reverend Seehase, who managed to say a few words of appreciation. Shortly after
this time, the Reverend Seehase moved with his family to Clinton, Iowa.

On November 23, 1936, it was voted to secure a student pastor to take charge
until the called minister, the Reverend Paul Hinrichs, could arrive. Karl
August Becker, Dubuque, Iowa, led the Lutheran congregation during the holiday
season of 1936. On February 9, the Reverend P. A. Hinrichs and his family
established their home in our midst and he took up his duties as pastor.

The membership of the present congregation totals more than 150 families. The
present church board consists of the following members: John G. G. Neuharth and
Gideon Klein, deacons; Jacob Mauch, Sr., secretary; Jacob M. Goehring,
treasurer; Michael Jost, Adam F. Obenauer and Emanuel Heilman, trustees;
Josephine Isaak, organist; and Jacob J. Goehring, janitor.

                                -- Compiled by A. W. Schumacher

THE EVANGELICAL LUTHERAN ZION CHURCH LADIES AID

The first Lutheran Ladies Aid at Eureka was founded during 1900, under Rev.
Henry Reinhardt. For three years this organization met twice a month in the
different homes of its members and carried on its activities. In 1913, Rev.
Reinhardt left Eureka, and, for lack of a spirited leader, the Aid disbanded.

The present organization was organized in April, 1920, and met for years in the
basement of the girls' dormitory at the Eureka Lutheran College. The purpose of
this organization, according to Article 2 of the by-laws, is to encourage
Christian and social fellowship among its members, to support the congregation
in its efforts to raise the necessary funds for the upkeep of the church, and
in furthering other sets of Christian benevolence.

The first members of this organization were: Mrs. Philip Schamber, Miss Margaret
Mussgang, Mrs. Gus Mauser, Sr., Mrs. Jacob Bieber, Mrs. Otto Froh, Miss Emma
Becker, Mrs. John Stickel, Mrs. Philip Flemmer, Mrs. Fred Klein, Mrs. Fred
Homeyer, Christina Kapp, Mrs. Fred Orthwein, Mrs. L. Seehase, Mrs. George
Sandrock, Miss Clara Eckman, Mrs. Fred Hepperle, Mrs. Julius Schamber, Mrs.
August Kleinhans, Mrs. Charles Mix, Mrs. C. F. Liefeld, Mrs. Henry Ottmar, Mrs.
Henry Pietz, Mrs. Henry Straub, Mrs. Gottfried Quast, Mrs. Christ Scheuffle,
Mrs. Henry Meyer, Mrs. John J. Harr, Miss Emma Straub, Mrs. Sol. Isaak, Mrs.
Ludwig Orthwein, Mrs. August Martell. Of these early members, Mrs. Henry
Straub, Mrs. Sol. Isaak, Mrs. Philip Flemmer, and Mrs. August Martell have
continued as active members uninterruptedly up to the present time.

The Aid has at all times tried to uphold its purpose. During the 1920-21 famine
in Russia, a carload of old clothes was gathered and sent there. Hundreds of
dollars donated by benevolent friends or raised by the Aid through bazaars and
dinners, were also sent to help relieve suffering.

During the existence of the Eureka Lutheran College the Aid helped, at
different times, in furnishing rugs and other necessary articles for the
college as well as for the girls' dormitory. When it was desired, the ladies
even helped in interior decorating of the rooms.

When the new Zion Church was built in 1926, the organization furnished all the
carpeting, two sets of altar cloths, a crucifix and two altar lights, and one
of the large colored windows.

When the Good Samaritan Home was opened here in 1933 by Rev. Hoeger, the Aid
again was of service. They sponsored a shower, attended by most of the ladies
in the city, which helped supply many needed things for the home. At the
request and expense of the Aid, the parsonage was rewired in 1935.

In October, 1936, it was decided to sponsor a "Food Shower" for the Good
Samaritan Home. The first of these was held the following November.

Conference groups have been housed and fed at different times by the
organization. Annual donations are sent to needy institutions of the synod and
to the mission fund.

A Ladies Aid Chorus was organized in 1933. This group of thirteen singers has
been actively engaged in singing for various meetings and for the "shut-ins" of
the congregation. Caroling for these unfortunates at Christmas time has been an
annual pleasure.

        LUTHERAN LADIES AID

        Top Row: Magdalena Neuharth, Rose Ottmar, Della Greiser, Eva Heilmam,
        Margaret Straub, Emma Heer, Gladyce Straub, Erna Wenzel, Reva Isaak.
        Second Row: Katharina Haag, Lydia Obenauer, Ann Nelson, Margaret Wenzel,
        Christina Schock, Rose Schumacher, Rosa Golz, Rosina Zeller, Maria
        Stabler.
        Third Row: Eva Vetter, Rose Schaible, Matilda Schumacher, Emilie
        Goehring, Christina Wolff, Elizabetha Weller, Magdalena Eckman,
        Margaretha Stock, Katharina Ottmar.
        Fourth Row: Geneva Martell, Elizabeth Schamber, Katharina Flemmer,
        Hulda Isaak, Margaret Flemmer, Alma Klein, Ann Finkbeiner, Albina
        Straub, Maria Isaak, Katherine Pietz.

At present the Aid has a membership of forty-two.
