Goehring.

Worthy of mention is the long service rendered the organization by Mrs. Emilie
Goehring, who has served six and one-half years as treasurer and five years as
president of the Aid.

        Reformed Church

THE REFORMED CHURCH OF EUREKA

Many of the early Germans who settled in the Eureka territory were desirous of
carrying their religious faith from the old country into this new home of
theirs. Consequently, in the fall of 1887, the Petersburg Reformed Church was
organized by the Rev. U. Reue. The services for that year were more or less
held in the homes of the members of the congregation, but in the spring of 1888,
their first church was built. The dedication sermon for this church was
delivered by the Rev. U. Reue, who at that time made his permanent home in
Menno, Dakota Territory.

The charter members of the Petersburg Reformed Church were: John Bauer, who
served as elder; Joseph Werre, who served as deacon; John Kemmet, Fred
Preszler, Jacob Mehlhaff, Christoph Mehlhaff, Jacob Sauter, Philip Neumiller,
John Ladner and Philip Schaeffer.

On the 1st of January, 1913, the old church was sold to the Baptist
congregation, and was moved to a location about 10 miles northwest of Eureka.
However, the Reformed congregation at the same time purchased the old
Presbyterian church of Eureka. This building was then standing on its present
location. The newly purchased building was almost completely renovated. A new
floor was put in, new seats were added, and a balcony constructed. The first
services in this church were conducted on Easter Sunday, the 23rd of March,
1913, and were under the charge of the Rev. G. J. Zenk.

The pastors of the Petersburg Reformed Church of Eureka were: the Rev. U. Reue
from 1887 to 1892; the Rev. Peter Bauer from 1892 to 1902; the Rev. J. F.
Jungblut from the spring of 1903 to November 7, 1904; the Rev. G. J. Zenk from
July 9, 1905, to May 16, 1921; the Rev. H. Treick from June 30, 1921, to April
30, 1932; and the Rev. W. J. Krieger who came November 24, 1932, and is pastor
at the present time

The members of the consistory are: the Rev. W. J. Krieger; Henry D. Opp and
Henry G. Hieb, elders; August Bender and Wm. Mehlhaff, deacons. The present
membership of the church is 152.

Among the truly and really pioneers of this territory were the ministers of the
gospel. These men, getting salaries of only $300 to $500 a year, often times
traveled from 10 to 50 miles to visit a sick patient. The trip was often made
by wagon and sleigh with the thermometer registering as low as 35 degrees below
zero. One minister would often find it necessary to serve as many as six
congregations to earn his very meager salary. The complete circuit of his
churches in good weather would often take him three days. The hardships,
privations and accomplishments of these pioneer ministers in consoling the
people in their darker moments, and giving them courage to carry on, were such
that words can not express it.

THE GLUECKSTAHL REFORMED CHURCH

The Glueckstahl Reformed Church is located approximately 14 miles northeast of
the present city of Eureka. It was organized in conjunction with the Petersburg
Reformed Church of Eureka by Rev. U. Reue, in 1887. The charter members of this
church were: Daniel Opp, who served as elder; Simon Heupel, who served as
deacon; George Neuharth, Valentine Mettler, John Knapp, George Hieb, Philip
Mueller, Friedrich Preszler, Friederich Neuharth, Jacob Meidinger, Christ
Maier, Jacob Hoff, Henry Schnabel, Henry Ladner, Jacob Ladner, Johann Preszler,
George Junkert, Karl Hoff and Daniel Schanzenbach.

Rev. W. J. Kreiger became the pastor of this group in May, 1896. During his
early stay with the congregation, church services were conducted in a granary
on the Philip Mueller farm, now occupied by Jacob Koerner. During the winter
months, services were held in the home of Daniel Opp, Sr. In the spring of
1897, under the progressive guidance of Rev. Krieger, a new church was
constructed. This church was dedicated in October, 1897, and the dedication
sermon was delivered by Rev. J. H. Stark of Scotland, South Dakota. From 1896
to April, 1901, the Glueckstahl Reformed Church was served by Rev. Krieger,
who at that time also had charge of the Presbyterian Church at Roscoe, South
Dakota.

Many of the charter members of this organization are still living, making their
residence in Eureka. They are: George J. Hieb, George G. Neuharth, John Knapp,
and Grandmother D. Opp, who is now nearly 90 years of age.

The pastors who served this church are: the Rev. U. Reue from 1887 to 1892; the
Rev. W. J. Kreiger from May, 1896 to April, 1901; the Rev. J. F. Jungblut from
1903 to November, 1904; the Rev. G. J. Zenk from 1905 to May, 1921; the Rev. H.
Treick from 1921 to April 30, 1932; in November, 1932, the Rev. Krieger again
took charge of this church and is their pastor at the present time.

The members of the consistory are: the Rev. W. J. Krieger, Jacob D. Opp and
John J. Opp, elders; and George J. Neuharth and Henry H. Schnabel, deacons.

        Baptist Church

FIRST BAPTIST CONGREGATION

The Baptist Church of Eureka was organized June 21, 1886. The following were
charter members: Fred Weisz and wife, Justina; George Bippus and wife,
Elisabetha; Karl Schenkenberger and his wife, Margaretha; Jacob Huber and his
wife, Johanna; Jacob Renz and wife, Barbara; Johann Krueg and wife, Margaretha;
Johann Renschler and wife, Katharina; Christian Benz and wife, Julianna;
Andreas Hoff, Samuel Grena, Louisa Shock nee Fischer, the Reverend Berthold
Matzke and his wife, Ernestine. The first officers elected were Jacob Huber and
Karl Schenkenberger, deacons, and Johann Krueg, secretary.

The Reverend Mr. Berthold Matzke was the first pastor and served from
1886-1888. During his pastorate the 51 members were added to the church
membership. Reverend Mr. Englemann's pastorate (1889-1890) added 53 members;
the Reverend Mr. Tenske (1891-1893), 52; the Reverend Mr. Golling (1894-1897),
153 members. The Reverend Mr. L. Wiesel served from 1897-1900, and the
congregation was increased to 304 members, divided up in the following groups:
Eureka, Mound City, Herreid, Venturia and Ashley. Members formerly belonging to
the Eureka Congregation organized the above mentioned congregations.

The Reverend Mr. Hilzinger was the next pastor of the Eureka Congregation and
served from 1900-1904, with a gain of 32 members; the Reverend A. Heringer from
1904-1910, 70 members gained; the Reverend Mr. Roth served only one year with
four members gained. The Reverend Mr. F. W. Schaeffer served four years and
gained 29 members. The Reverend Mr. Bauer served only three months. His
successor was the Reverend E. Brockel, who served two years with a gain of nine
members. The Reverend D. Klein served from 1920-1924, adding 76 members. The
Reverend J. L Schmidt served from 1925-1930, gaining 51 members. During the
pastorate of the Reverend E. S. Fenske, 1931-1934, the congregation added 57
members. Student J. E. Gunst served the past two years during summer vacations.

During the 60 years the congregation raised $70,127.26; $56,919.85 were for
local purposes and $13,207.51 for missions and charities.

EUREKA BAPTIST LADIES AID

The Baptist Ladies Aid of Eureka South Dakota, was organized on the 3rd February
1911. At the time of the organization there were 22 active members. The first
officers of the Aid were: Mrs. Agnes Roth, president; Mrs. Mary Koerner,
secretary; and Mrs. Julia E. Giedt, wife of the late Theodore J. P. Geidt,
treasurer. The objectives of the Aid are to promote the interests of the
Baptist Church and its members in Eureka and vicinity. All meetings are held in
the basement of the Baptist Church and the order of business consists of
singing, praying, the reading of the Scriptures, and discussion of projects
whereby the group might be of benefit to some needy person. The meetings are
held on the second Tuesday of every month, and lunches are served every third
meeting. The membership of the organization has varied from a maximum of 25 to
the present minimum of 13.

The officers of the Baptist Ladies Aid have been as follows: 1912-1913, Mrs.
Gottlieb Eisemann, president; Mrs. Mary Koerner, secretary; and Mrs. Julia E.
Giedt, treasurer; 1914-1915, Mrs. Agnes Shaeffer, president; Mrs. Julia E.
Giedt, secretary and treasurer. The Aid was inactive from the years 1915 to
1920. In 1920, they were reorganized and the following officers were elected:
Mrs. Paulina Klein president; Mrs. Rosa Frieh, secretary; and Mrs. Martha
Vetter, treasurer. The officers for the years 1926 to 1930 inclusive, were:
Mathilda Schmidt, president; Mrs. Rosa Frieh, secretary; Mrs. Emma Hoff,
treasurer. Since that time the officers have been as follows: 1930, Elizabeth
Keszler president; Rosa Frieh, secretary; and Emma Hoff, treasurer; 1931-1933,
Mrs. Ludwig Fenske, president; Martha Bitz, secretary; and Martha Vetter,
treasurer; and 1935-1936, Rosa Frieh, president; Martha Bitz, secretary; and
Martha Ladner, treasurer.

HISTORY OF THE: EVANGELICAL CHURCH IN THE EUREKA TERRITORY

Our early settlers were, as a rule, Christians who required religious services
from cradle to grave. To supply these, the pioneer ministers followed them into
the trackless wilderness to minister to the spiritual hungers there. And so it
was, that in the early '80's, when members of the Evangelical Church from
Indiana and Illinois and southeastern South Dakota, moved to what is today
Walworth, Campbell, and McPherson Counties, Evangelical missionaries followed
to build up their religious groups. Privations were many, the rewards small. At
first there was some difficulty in getting the right men with enough spiritual
stamina to reach these pioneer people.

In 1884, the Reverend A. Knebel, the nearest superintendent at that time, sent
as missionaries to this Eureka territory, Pastors J. Fandrey, A. Geyman, H.
Loewen, A. Berreth, and C. Mueller, who, with Reverend Knebel, took turns in
coming here for religious sessions.

        Evangelical Church -- 1923

Because of the difficulties encountered in living and traveling, there were
long periods during which many of those Evangelically inclined, had no soul
sustenance. In 1886, after Reverend H. Wolf's short stay in this vicinity, the
Reverend A. Berreth was definitely assigned to the Eureka Mission. Eureka
Church was organized with Phil. Kallenberger, Christ Kurle, Friedrich Aman,
Christian Enzi, Jack Becker, John Handel, A. Wagner, Ludwig Heuter, and some
others, as charter members. Services were held at A. Wagner's residence until a
church was built in 1888. Bishop Thomas Baumann conducted the dedicatory
services. In May, 1889, Reverend H. Baumann succeeded the Reverend Berreth, and
in 1890, the Reverend G. W. Heilsher with G. W. Nicolai as assistant, relieved
the Reverend Baumann.

The Reverend Hielsher, needing a parsonage, straightway went to building one

In 1891-92, this part of the country suffered greatly from crop failure. Many
did not have enough to eat and found difficulty in keeping themselves decently
clothed during the trying winter months. The Reverends Hielsher and A. Knebel
did fine missionary work in appealing to the eastern branch of the church for
aid to their needy parishioners and friends. Their call was answered by a "help
fund" of over $5,000 in cash and over 100 boxes of clothing. Many were clothed
for the winter, and over $3,100 worth of flour and $1,000 worth of groceries
were distributed.

In 1893, after accomplishing splendid missionary work, the Reverend Hielsher's
term of service expired and he was moved elsewhere. At that time a preacher's
term in the Evangelical Church was three years in one locality before being
stationed to another charge.

Since the Reverend Hielsher's time the Eureka church was so organized that a
regular succession of the following preachers occurred: Reverends S. Brown and
Rekow, 1893-1894; Reverends H. Loewen and F. Loewen, 1894-1897; Reverend C.
Werthenbach, 1897-1899; Reverend B. H. Fisher, 1899-1900; Reverend R. E.
Strutz, 1900-1902; Reverend D. Bitner, 1902-1905; Reverend E. Melzian,
1905-1910; Reverend G. Heuer, 1910-1911; Reverend W. Butschat, 1911-1914;
Reverend A. Ermel, 1914-1918; Reverend E. Melzian, 1918-1920; Reverend S.
Finkbeiner, 1920-1923; Reverend W. Krueger, 1923-1924. At this time the need for
a new church building was imperative and the present church was then built.
Bishop S. P. Spreng conducted the dedicatory services. The Reverend Krueger was
forced to leave his pastorate here because of ill health. The Reverend G.
Kowalke served during 1924-1925; Reverend Hochstatter, 1925-1926; Reverend G.
Kowalke, 1926-1930; Reverend W. Juedes, 1930-1933; and Reverend R. Bloedau,
1933 to the present, 1937.

        Top Row: Mrs. E. Kowake, Mrs. Jake Kiesz, Mrs. D. Mehlhaff, Mrs. Susie
        Humann. Bottom Row: Mrs. J. Aman, Mrs. R. Bloedau, Mrs. Jacob Keim,
        Mrs. W. Bauer, Mrs. J. Dalke.

EVANGELICAL LADIES AID

On November 26, 1929, a meeting of the ladies of the Evangelical Church of
Eureka, South Dakota, was called for the purpose of organizing a Ladies Aid
Society. The meeting was conducted by Rev. G. H. Kowalke, pastor.

The constitution and by-laws were read and adopted. The name selected for the
organization was "The Ladies Aid of the Evangelical Church of Eureka, South
Dakota." The purpose of the organization was to support the local church and
other benevolent institutions of the church, and conduct themselves according
to the discipline of the Evangelical Church. The meetings are held on the first
Wednesday of each month. The first officers elected to serve the organization
were as follows: Mrs. Susie Humann, president; Mrs. John J. Schaeffer, vice
president; Mrs. Fred Schanzenbach, secretary; Mrs. E. C. Kowalke, treasurer.

The dues for the upkeep of the Aid were set at 25 cents per month. At each
meeting the members, in order, were called upon to serve a lunch to which
guests could be invited. A charge of 25 cents per luncheon plate was made.

Four meetings each year are conducted as Missionary meetings, and the
membership dues, for that particular meeting, are used for missionary purposes.
During the month members of the Aid call on the sick, and such calls are
reported at each meeting. In many cases flowers have been distributed to the
sick. The Aid has been instrumental in many of the latest improvements of the
church.

In 1930 they bought a piano for the church for which they paid $225.00. In 1932
they paid for putting a ceiling in the basement of the church. In 1934 they had
the entire of the church repainted and redecorated.

The present officers of the Aid are as follows: Mrs. R. Bloedau, president;
Mrs. John Kiesz, vice president; Miss Edna Kurle, secretary; Mrs. Martin Weber,
treasurer.

GERMAN CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH

The Congregational Church of Eureka was organized on January 19, 1904, at a
meeting held at the home of Mrs. Maria Hetzler, with the following present: The
Rev. C. F. Finger, Mr. and Mrs. John Klundt, Mrs. Hetzler, Mr. and Mrs. Jacob
L. Klein, Mr. and Mrs. F. G. Mehlhaff, Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Hirsch, Mr. and Mrs.
John Strobel, Sr.

After a thorough discussion, it was decided to name this newly organized church
the German Congregational St. Paul's Church, and it was also decided to accept
the constitution and by-laws as prescribed by Superintendent M. C. Eversz. The
first officers elected were: Deacons, John Strobel and John Klundt; secretary,
John Klundt; treasurer, John Strobel; trustees, John Klundt, Philip Miller,
John Strobel, Christian Grosz, and Jacob Gruebele.

The church services were held in a store building bought from Jacob Isaak,
which was later moved to its present location and rebuilt into what is now the
present church. The rebuilding of the church, and also the old parsonage, was
done by Carpenter Jacob Thurn. The lot upon which the church now stands was
donated by Mr. Fred Schamber. Donations were also given by the businessmen of
Eureka, which made it possible to complete the church and parsonage.

The new church was dedicated on the second Monday in October, 1904 The speakers
were Superintendent Dr. Eversz, Rev. H. Seil, who spoke in English, and Student
Warkentien. All meetings were well attended.

The first pastor called for the newly organized church was the Rev. Jacob
Morach, who served faithfully for four years. It was through his efforts that
the first chorus was organized. He left in the fall of 1908. His place was taken
by the Rev. F. A. Willman, who resigned in December. 1910, to become a
traveling missionary. In May, 1911, Rev. Aumann of Chicago, accepted the call
and served the church as its pastor for a little more than two years. He was
followed by the Rev. H. Ollech, who resigned after five years of service. In
1919, the Rev. Bock accepted the call, but served only one year and six months
before tendering his resignation. The Rev. Weiskopf served as pastor from 1921
until the spring of 1922.

In May, 1922, the Rev. Knaus, then a student at Redfield College, was called.
He arrived in June, 1922, and still serves the congregation at the present
time. It was during his service here that the church became free of all debts
and built a new, modern parsonage. It was through his work and efforts that the
church grounds were beautified by the planting of a lawn and trees.

Of the charter members only three are living, namely, F. G. Mehlhaff, John
Strobel, Sr., and Jacob L. Klein.

The parish now consists of six churches, as follows: St. Paul's Congregation at
Eureka; Friedens Congregation at Greenway, South Dakota; Jesus Congregation at
Herreid, South Dakota; Hoffnungsfeld Congregation at Long Lake, South Dakota;
Bethel Congregation No. 1 (known as Aman's Church); and Bethel Congregation No.
2 (known as Gross' Church). Altogether, they have a membership of 168 families.

The parish records show the following statistics: Baptized, adults 15, infants
182, total 197; confirmed, 301; marriages, 115; deaths, adults 46, children
under 10 years 40 (1, ninety years old; 2, eighty years; 10, seventy years; 12,
sixty years; 8, fifty years; 5, forty years; 3, thirty years; 4, twenty years;
1, ten years; 40 infants), total 86.

The total amount of Mission money collected during the 33 years existence of
the parish, is $25,893.94, which was divided or paid out for foreign and home
mission work.

Some of the first baptisms in the newly organized church were: Emma Mehlhaff,
Daniel G. Strobel, Albert E. Strobel, Edna Wittmayer, Jacob J. Hirsch, Paul
Hausauer, Martha Klein, Rheinhold Aman, and Gertrude Mehlhaff.

The present officers of the parish are: Trustees, Karl Hausauer, chairman,
Emanuel Levi, Gottlieb Bertsch, F. K. Mehlhaff, treasurer, and Robert Jahraus,
secretary. Church officers of the St. Paul's Church at Eureka are: Trustees,
Karl Hausauer, Christ Kurle and Julius Reiner; deacons, Henry H. Kundert and
David Kurle; treasurer, Karl Bareither; secretary, F. J. Job; Sunday School
superintendent, John Jundt; assistant superintendent, Robert Jahraus; school
organist, Eleanor Kundert; ushers, John Schutz and Julius Hagel.

        Congregational Church -- 1904

THE CATHOLIC CHURCH

The Catholic congregation of Eureka was organized in March, 1888, as a mission,
under Rev. Jos. A. Stephen from Ipswich, with a membership of eight families
and four or five single persons. He held services in the school house, depot,
and private residences. In September, 1890, Rev. Father Wulff, of Hoven, took
it as his mission. In the summer of 1893, the congregation built the church, 24
x 36, 14-foot posts. The church was dedicated July 16, on the feast of St.
Mary of Mount Carmel, for whom it was named, the Rev. Bernard Strassenmeier, of
the Catholic Indian Mission at Standing Rock Agency, being the master of
ceremonies, with Rev. Father Wulff as assistant, there being then about 33
families and active members. New Year, 1894, Rev. John Lamesch, of Ipswich,
succeeded Father Wulff, and took Eureka as one of his missions, where he did
much good and was well liked by his entire flock. He died July 3, 1896, at his
parsonage in Ipswich.

        Catholic Church -- 1893

In October, 1893, Eureka was visited by Rev. Father Nic. Dahlman.

In 1893, Chas. Pfeffer gathered funds for a bell and succeeded in buying one
weighing 1,800 pounds for $132.00, which stood in front of the church until
1897, when the congregation built a steeple and placed the bell in position,
from where it rang the Angelus every morning and called the Catholic to his
worship, or tolled for dead members of the parish.

Some of the early members included: Casper Vetter, Anton Wald, James Guhin,
John Dietrich, Mike Klein, Raymond Volk, Jacob Schall, John A. Smith, Daniel
Smith, Frank Hammerschmidt, John Sahli, Frank Sahli, Joe Voeller, August
Usselman, Frank Schumacher, Pat McInney, George Fullehan, Frank Ell, and John
Ell.

Father Wolloch, residing in Hillsview, has charge of the local church at the
present time.

EUREKA COMMUNITY HOSPITAL

        Eureka Community Hospital -- 1928

From the memory of hazardous emergency operations, performed under the most
trying conditions in private homes, from the agonizing miles of train or road
travel to get to adequate medical care in the city hospitals of Aberdeen,
Bismarck, or Mobridge, has come the fulfillment of a long-wanted community need
-- the Eureka Hospital. We, of the present generation, know little of the
hardships and sufferings endured by our fathers during those ear]y years when
doctors rode horseback, equipped with only a simple set of instruments, to
venture operations under conditions unthinkable today.

For years Eureka and vicinity had felt the need of adequate hospital
facilities. Too many emergency cases had been lost in the 60 to 130 mile race
with death to the closest modern-equipped hospitals, to allow the issue of a
local medical institution to become dormant. But, during the years previous to
1929, whenever the expense and cost of an adequate building was brought into
the hospital discussions, the matter would cool and drop -- the project
appeared too vast and forbidding financially.

In 1926, Mr. George G. Neuharth, one of Eureka's most enterprising pioneers,
became vitally interested in the hospital project. He discussed this matter
with other business men and farmers, in and around the city, until more and
more became hospital-minded. The subject became a constant topic for discussion
at business men's meetings and at other civic gatherings. During the fall of
1927, the Eureka Commercial Club appointed a committee to gather information
regarding public opinion on the project. On November 2, 1927, P. W. Treick,
president of the Eureka Commercial Club, called a meeting of the Hospital
committee, consisting of Christian Kusmaul, Theo. J. P. Giedt. Dir. Geo.
Sandrock, Jacob Keim, George G. Neuharth, P. W. Treick, H. I. Bauer, Wm.
Boettcher, and A. W. Schumacher, who when informed of details, voted unanimously
in favor of the hospital project and appointed H. I. Bauer as chairman, and A.
W. Schumacher as secretary of this committee.

After preliminary plans had been investigated and discussed, this group was
incorporated under the laws of the State of South Dakota on December 20, 1927,
as the Eureka Community Benevolent Hospital Association. The first Board of
Directors, elected on December 20, 1927. were the following: George G.
Neuharth, Christian Kusmaul, John J. Keim, Theo. J. P. Giedt, Henry D. Opp.
Philip Schamber, Henry Straub, August Bender, Henry Bieber, John B. Wittmayer,
and Christian Grenz. The Board elected as their officers: George G. Neuharth,
president; Henry D. Opp, vice president; Philip Schamber, treasurer; and Theo.
J. P. Giedt, secretary. The Board agreed that this Association should be
non-sectarian and non-profit sharing. The matter of raising the necessary funds
was discussed and it was decided to procure members at $100 per membership and
accept any other donations, large or small.

Various locations were considered for the site of the building. The Board
finally induced Henry O. Opp to consent to sell his former residential site,
which seemed best suited for the new hospital.

Buechner & Orth, Minneapolis, Minnesota, architects, were employed to draw up
plans and specifications for the construction of the building. Mr. A. J.
Clocksin, Aberdeen, was employed as the main contractor.

        FIRST STAFF OF NURSES - 1929: Miss Elizabeth Neuharth Mehlhaff, Supt.,
        Mabel Satrum, Mrs. Maxine Siltman Neuharth, Mrs. Nellie Davis Zimmerman.

Operations for the construction of the hospital were begun in the spring of
1928. On August 26, 1928, the laying of the cornerstone with the usual public
ceremonies accompanying it, showed that work was progressing. The speakers for
this occasion were: Dr. O. H. Gerdes, Rev. J. L. Schmidt of the Baptist Church,
and Rev. G. W. Kowalke of the Evangelical Church.

The furniture and equipment of the hospital, except the elevator and the X-Ray
machine, were donated by business men, fraternal organizations and churches of
Eureka and its vicinity. The cost of the building and the equipment, as it now
stands, is about $75,000. The hospital has room for 28 beds and is considered
as one of the best equipped hospitals in the Northwest. The Board has employed
at all times a competent corps of registered nurses and the institution has
enjoyed a fine professional reputation among medical men of this section of the
country.

The hospital was opened for public inspection and admission of patients on
March 18, 1929. The staff doctors at that time were Dr. O. H. Gerdes and Dr. E.
E. Stephens. Miss Elizabeth Neuharth was the first superintendent, serving
efficiently and faithfully until her resignation June 1, 1934. Miss Johanna
Deibert succeeded her and served faithfully until her resignation March 1, 1935.

Mrs. H. Treick was the first patient admitted to the hospital. Miss Elsie
Roemmich of Eureka, was the first surgical patient. The first baby born at the
hospital was a son of Mr. and Mrs. Henry Kaul of Ashley, North Dakota. Ardyn
Ardyl Thurn, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Fred Thurn of Wishek, North Dakota, was
the first girl born at the hospital.

The dedication of this noble civic achievement was held June 2, 1929,
out-of-doors, before an audience of about 3,000 interested citizens.
Interspersed with singing and band music, speeches were given by Rev. H. Treick
of the Reformed Church, Rev. R. Knaus of the Congregational Church, and the
staff doctors, O. H. Gerdes and E. E. Stephens.

Up to January 1, 1937, 2,473 patients have received hospital care. Records show
that 288 babies were born in the hospital from 1929 to 1937.

        Ardyn Ardyl Thurn, Wishek, N. D.
        First living baby, born April 12, 1929.

Dr. E. E. Stephens and Dr. Robert C. Ray are the present staff physicians.

Miss Martha Clausen, present superintendent, has already made an enviable
record both as regards efficient and economical management, and the maintenance
of a high standard of professional service for the institution. A staff of able
registered nurses is on duty to assist her at all times.

The present officers and directors of the Board are: Henry D. Opp, president;
John J. Keim, vice president; Robert Jahraus, secretary; Henry Ottmar,
treasurer; Geo. G. Neuharth, Henry Straub, Henry G. Hieb, Rudolph Hilgemann,
George Zeller, Rudolph Schott, John E. Ottenbacher.

The Eureka Hospital is truly a great asset to this community, and all those who
have labored for its achievement can look back upon their efforts with
justifiable pride.

        Ella Bieber, Winifred Vander Vorste, Lois Gloege, Ella Schutz,
        Dr. Bobert C. Ray, Martha Clausen, Dr. E. E. Stephens.

THE EUREKA COMMUNITY HOSPITAL AUXILIARY

The Eureka Community Hospital Auxiliary was organized October 18, 1933. The
purpose of this organization is to aid in the development and maintenance of
the local hospital, and to create an interest for this institution among the
public. Any woman who is interested in the Eureka Community Hospital, its aims
and purposes, is eligible for membership.

The original membership totaled 28; at present this number has increased to 39
members.

The following are the original officers: President, Mr. E. E. Stephens; first
vice president, Mrs. Christ Flemmer; second vice president, Mrs. Albert Isaak;
secretary, Mrs. J. D. Harr; treasurer, Mrs. Theo. Straub.

The present officers are: President, Mrs. E. E. Stephens; first vice president,
Mrs. H. I. Bauer; second vice president, Mrs. Leland Olson; secretary, Mrs.
Alex Mehlhaff; treasurer, Mrs. Theophil Mehlhaff.

The regular meetings of the Auxiliary are held on the third Wednesday of each
month, at 2:30 p. m., in the City Council rooms. The time before and after the
business meeting is devoted to sewing for the hospital, when there is such to
be done.

Each member pays an annual membership fee of 60 cents. After each meeting a
light lunch is served by three members, who are selected in alphabetical
order. Everyone, including the public, is invited to this luncheon; a small
charge of 10 cents helps pay expenses.

        HOSPITAL AUXILIARY

        First Row: Mrs. R. Jahraus, Mrs. Wm. Dickhaut, Mrs. Gideon Klein,
        Mrs. E. R. Isaak, Mrs. Andrew Stoebner, Mrs. J. G. G. Neuharth.

The annual meeting takes place in May of each year, at which time new officers
are elected.

The Auxiliary has purchased many things for the hospital in order to help
beautify the building, and to add to the comfort of the patients. This list is
too long to mention in detail, but includes the following: curtains, blankets,
bedside table lamps, rugs, linoleum floor covering for the lobby, dishes and
utensils. Besides these, the group has done much sewing of hospital linen and
towels. The ladies were also able to present a fine electric coffee urn to the
hospital by collecting Nash coffee can covers. Assistance was given to the
hospital superintendent last year during National Hospital Day. In May, 1935,
Mr. F. W. Junker gave a hand painted picture to the Auxiliary who, in turn,
presented it to the hospital. Many similar donations have been presented from
time to time. These courtesies are greatly appreciated and help make our Eureka
Community Hospital Auxiliary a worthwhile organization in its efforts to
improve hospitalization in this vicinity.

FRED WELLER POST NO. 186

The first American Legion meeting in Eureka was held in the spring of 1920, for
the purpose of organizing a Legion Post. A charter was granted April 28, 1920,
with the following charter members: Andrew Martell, Fred G. Wenger, R. A. Harr,
R. H. Isaak, H. O. Hepperle, John J. Strobel, Peter H. J. Hennecke, C. D. Harr,
W. H. Dennis, and Fred K. Schock.

The Post, numbered 186, was named after Fred Weller, the first man in this
vicinity to sacrifice his life for his country during the World War. The first
officers elected were: Andrew Martell, post commander; Gust M. Mauser, vice
commander; Ed. S. Isaak, post adjutant; M. J. Pufahl, finance officer; R. A.
Harr, chaplain; P. W. Treick, historian. Of the charter members, seven are
still members in good standing, namely: Andrew Martell, R. H. Isaak, John J.
Strobel, P. W. Treick, Ed. S. Isaak, J. M. Goehring, and Fred Schock. One of
the charter members, R. A. Harr, is deceased; others have moved to other parts
of the country.

The monthly meetings, the second Tuesday of each month, were first conducted in
the City Council rooms, before being held in the basement of the Farmers &
Merchants State Bank, later moved to the City Hall, and to the Civic Club
rooms. A passenger coach, purchased in January, 1936, and built solidly into a
permanent location near the center of the city, has been the present quarters
of the Post.

The Fred Weller Post, considered one of the outstanding posts of the 14th
District, has been very active. November 11th is always a day of celebration.
Each year, except 1932 and 1986, the Post has sponsored an Armistice Day Party
which has drawn people from all over this Dakota territory. Each year the Post
puts on a membership drive. In 1981, with 117 members enrolled, the high point
was reached. We were honored in the spring of 1982, by having Wm. H. Dennis,
then commander of the Post, elected District Commander of the 14th District, in
which capacity he served for one year. In March, 1936, the 14th District held
its annual meeting in our city, but, due to stormy weather, the attendance was
not as large as expected. Nevertheless, by evening the banquet hall was filled
to hear the Department Commander give an inspiring talk. Again, the Fred Weller
Post No. 186, had done well and had made new friends and strengthened old
acquaintances.

        Fred Weller

The officers elected and appointed to serve the Post for 1936-37 are: Andrew
Martell, post commander; W. F. Volzke, first vice commander; Fred E. Merkel,
second vice commander; E. H. Buckenberger, post adjutant; John J. Strobel,
finance officer; J. M. Goehring, service officer; Christ Fischer, sergeant of
arms; P. W. Treick, welfare officer; R. H. Isaak, chaplain; Arthur Bjork,
historian; Daniel Mehlhaff, athletic officer.

AMERICAN LEGION AUXILIARY

The American Legion Auxiliary of the Fred Weller Post No. 186, was organized on
the 3rd of May, 1922; the first meeting being held in the Wenger Hotel in
Eureka, South Dakota. The charter members of this organization were 17 in
number and consisted of the following women: Myrtle Wenger, Della Greiser,
Elizabeth Isaak, Lorchen Isaak, Eva Isaak, Lillian Treick, Mathilda Isaak,
Bertha Savage, Mathilda Hepperle, Katharina Froh, Emilie Goehring, Clara
Hoffman, Grace Bjork, Emma Quast, Rose Schumacher, Annette Martell and Augusta
Merkel. The first officers were: Clara Hoffman, president; Myrtle Wenger, vice
president; Annette Martell, secretary; Eva Isaak, treasurer; Mathilda Isaak,
historian; Bertha Savage, sergeant-at-arms; and Mathilda Hepperle, chaplain.

The women eligible to the American Legion Auxiliary, the largest body of
patriotic women in the world today, are the mothers, wives, sisters and
daughters of members of the American Legion. The American Legion Auxiliary was
formed for the purpose of aiding the American Legion in carrying out the great
program of peace time service in America to which the Legion is dedicated.
Although often working independently on the projects of its own, the Auxiliary
has no purposes that are apart from the aims of the Legion, whose slogan is,
"We serve as they served."

In May, 1922, the unit started its first poppy drive. Four hundred poppies were
sold, and of the proceeds $10.00 was sent to the relief of the Japanese, and
the balance used in decorating the graves of the veterans and cleaning up both
cemeteries. The "poppy drive" is now sponsored by the Auxiliary every year and
the proceeds have been used for the following purposes: Contributions made to
the Battle Mountain Sanitarium at Hot Springs, South Dakota; Christmas presents
and groceries given to needy families; donations to the Girl Scouts
organization of Eureka; donations to Relief Agencies; beautifying of the
Lutheran and city cemeteries in Eureka. At various times candy has been made
and clothes and garments knitted and sent to Veterans Hospitals. Special
donations have also been made to the Eureka Community Hospital. Food and candy
sales have been held as a means of raising additional funds to carry on their
worth-while work.

In 1928, Mrs. Emilie Goehring represented this district at the National
Convention of the American Legion Auxiliary held at San Antonio, Texas.

The past presidents of the American Legion Auxiliary are as follows: 1922,
Clara Hoffman; 1923, Myrtle Wenger; 1924, Mabel Dennis, 1925 and 1926, Kathryn
Jahraus; 1927, 1928, 1929, 1930, Emilie Goehring; 1931, Edna Wolff; 1932, 1933,
1934, Irene G. Olsen; 1935, Cecelia Harr; and 1936, Emilie Goehring.

The district officers of the Auxiliary at this time are: Marie Kelly of
Aberdeen, South Dakota, district President, and Emilie Goehring of Eureka,
South Dakota, district vice president.

GIRL SCOUTS

Girl Scouting, an organization devoted to fostering healthy guidance of growing
girls, was promoted here in September, 1932, by Mrs. Henry Ottmar. At that time
Mrs. Ottmar organized the first Girl Scout troop of 24 girls, which is now
known as the Meadow Lark Troop.

The Lions' Club (now called Civic Club), furnished a meeting place for the
scouts by letting them use the club rooms every Tuesday of the week for their
meetings.

Mrs. Henry Ottmar has been leader of this troop since its organization.
Assistance in this work was given by the American Legion Auxiliary and the
Parent-Teacher Association, who provided funds for the annual national dues.
This aid was much appreciated as the scouts had local dues and registration
fees to pay.

In April, 1933, the scouts had acquired the Tenderfoot rank and joined the
Nationals. They had now become first class scouts, and the next step was to
become Golden Eaglets.

On May 12, 1933, the scouts made their first public appearance in uniform at a
Mother's Day program, which they prepared in honor of all mothers. They have
sponsored since then many more such programs besides having taken part in
patriotic programs, Parent-Teacher programs, and parades. The girls have given
Christmas parties for the needy children, and brought cheer to the sick and
shut-ins by their carol singing. In return the scouts have enjoyed hiking,
theatre parties, picnics, camping, and fireside meetings on the Island.

A number of girls had the benefit of attending camp several seasons at Pickerel
Lake, where they enjoyed outdoor life, especially swimming, which had been
denied them here.

        TOP ROW - Viola Obenauer, Marjorie Mutschler, Olive Bauer, Verna
        Hieb, Saralee Frohlichman, Elaine Dahms, Viola Bareither.
        Center Row: Scout Leader Mrs. Henry Ottmar, Eileen Schanzenbach,
        Lila Preszler, Freida Mauch, Darnelda Flemmer, Alice Isaak, Olivia
        Bareither, Kathleen Olsen.
        Bottom Row - Saralee Bender, Eleanor Kundert, Verona Bender, Martha
        Schlichenmeyer, Drummer Scout Gerald Smith, Alma Klein, Freida
        Schick, Francis Ottmar, Phylis Weber.

Scouting teaches the girls many important things concerning good conduct which
they otherwise would be apt to pass by. Proper respect to flag and country
helps in building citizenship. "Be Prepared," the scout motto, strengthens
character.

One of the worth while undertakings, with the help of their fathers, was the
beautifying of one of the islands, known as the Girl Scout Island, on the north
of the lake.

Our latest deed of kindness was to aid in the Gerdes Memorial project. Dr.
Gerdes loved the Girl Scouts and always enjoyed giving his assistance whenever
called upon. The girls shall always feel thankful for his having been their
friend.

The Honorable Theo. J. P. Giedt, who had been a scout master a number of years
previous, also lent a helping hand in scout promotion.

About two years ago a second troop, known as the Pocahontas Troop, was
organized. This group has had as their leader Mrs. Irene Olsen. These girls are
a younger group and therefore have many more years of scouting to look forward
to.

The parents and many other citizens who have helped the scouts in various ways
have our sincerest appreciations. The scouts are looking forward to doing their
bit to help make this Golden Jubilee Celebration a huge success.

The city of Eureka should be proud to boast of the Girl Scouts, and the scouts
are proud to be citizens of Eureka.

THE EUREKA PARENT-TEACHER ASSOCIATION

The Parent-Teacher Association, whose purpose it is to create a better
understanding between the parents and the public schools and to foster
educational betterments in the community, was organized May 3, 1929. The first
meeting was called to order by Mrs. Golden, who explained the purposes of such
an organization to the group gathered. At this time the following officers were
elected: Mrs. H. I. Bauer, president; Mr. Hochhalter, vice president; Mr. H. I.
Bauer, treasurer; and Mrs. A. F. Isaak, secretary. Due to ill health, Mrs. H.
I. Bauer was able to serve as president only one month. After her resignation,
Mrs. Kowalke was elected president. Since that time five members have served as
president of the P. T. A. These were: Mrs. J. M. Goehring, 1931-32; Mrs. Henry
Zenk, 1932-33; Mrs. E. R. Isaak, 1939-85; Mrs. Walter Schonbrod, 1935-36, and
Mr. Wm. Dickhaut, 1936-37.

In 1931 a P. T. A. chorus was started. Today this organization, consisting of
twenty-five women's voices, is one of the best singing groups in the city.

The P. T. A. has at different times given aid to the poor and helped support
other civic projects. The most outstanding project of the association is the
city library which was organized in 1932.

In order to meet the requirements of a standard organization and to offer
information of interest to our members, study groups were introduced in 1933.

The Eureka P. T. A. was honored during the spring of 1934 by being chosen the
host of the District Convention. At present ninety members are enrolled and the
organization will be able to meet the requirements essential for full standing
in the national affiliation of Parent-Teachers.

THE EUREKA FIRE DEPARTMENT

The Eureka Fire Company, later known as the Eureka Fire Department, organized
July 25, 1896. The first officers were as follows: John Schmidt, Chief; John
Pfeifle, First Assistant Chief; A. Schenkenberger, Second Assistant Chief; Wm.
Siegele, Secretary; John Doering, Treasurer. The following men were active
members of the organization at that time:

R. Nickish              John Parrot             Mike Henne
James Clayton           Ed Dickman              William Enze
Frank Blessner          Gottlieb Pietz          Salomon Isaak
Matt. J. Mergines       D. Nickish              M. W. Richards
J. W. Youkim            J. J. Klein             C. J. Parker
Jack Aldinger           John Siegele            H. G. Kusler
Fred Werre              Gottlieb Aldinger       Henry Ernst
John Werre              J. C. Guhin             John Weisbeck
Jacob Springer          John Stickel            Fred Henrick
John Stoller, Sr.

The Eureka Fire Department drafted by-laws and set their meeting dates for the
first Thursday of each month. The first Tuesday in January is reserved for the
annual meeting when officers for the coming year are elected and important
annual plans are made.

At this first meeting in 1896 it was decided to sponsor an annual Firemen's
Ball on New Year's Eve. This feature has always been one of the city's
outstanding social events. The fortieth Firemen's Ball was held December 31,
1936.

The first equipment, two hose carts and a few lengths of hose, was rather
inadequate. At times fire-fighting was a discouraging and hard experience,
especially so in winter when the snow hindered the moving of man-powered carts.

In the spring of 1935 the Department was determined in their desire to obtain a
fire truck. The organization sponsored a car raffle on August 15, 1935, the
winner receiving $600 credit on a car; $75.00 in cash was given for the second
prize, $50.00 for the third, and the fourth winner received $25.00. This
project was very successful and the Department purchased a Chevrolet chassis
truck. The City equipped the truck in tip-top shape and presented it for
service March 8, 1936.

The Department now has 1,000 feet of 2 1/2 inch fire hose and 300 feet of 1 1/2
leader line hose, a Tanno spray, two gas masks, and a first aid kit, plus the
1936 Chevrolet 1 1/2 ton fire truck.

ACACIA LODGE, A. F. & A. M., No. 108

This lodge was organized under a dispensation granted on the 12th of June,
1890, by the Most Worshipful Grand Master of the Grand Lodge A. F. & A. M., of
Dakota. The charter members and first officers were: Howard G. Elliott, W. M.;
E. L. Hopkins, S. D.; A. G. Bernard, J. W.; C. W. Johnson, treasurer; L. T.
Boucher, secretary; Chester L. Pratt, S. D.; Frank H. Hooper, J. D.; C. N.
Gardner, S. S.; Thomas Irgens, J. S.; F. Frankhauser, tyler; John C. Williams,
Jowie W. Proctor, Warren P. Wilson, Lewis H. Moulton, Edward Braddock.

The lodge received its charter from the Grand Lodge of South Dakota on June 10,
1891. The following officers were duly installed: Edgar L. Hopkins, W. M.;
Frank H. Hooper, S. W.; John E. Reagan, J. W.

The first member raised after the lodge received its charter was J. F. Wilson.

The lodge met upstairs in the two-story building known as "The Lodge Hall" for
many years. Some of the members came long distances to the meetings over bad
roads and in cold weather. The lodge had a steady growth in membership from
year to year.

In the spring of 1923, the building occupied by the lodge for its meeting
quarters was sold, and the organization was given notice to vacate. The
question of obtaining suitable quarters was raised and discussed by the
members. They finally came to the conclusion that if the lodge members wanted
to carry on as an organization, they must procure their own meeting hall, as
there was no suitable hall or building available. Committees were appointed and
worked hard to formulate plans for building and means of raising the necessary
funds. It so happened that the former Evangelical Church building was for sale.
This building was bought and a lot was also acquired.

The Acacia Building Association was incorporated, each member paying a
membership of $50.00. The funds so raised were used to pay for the building,
lot, and the moving of the building to its present location. Now the Masonic
Lodge and its Sister Lodge, the Order of the Eastern Star, has its own
permanent meeting hall. The property as it stands cost about $2,500.00. The
Eastern Star helped finance the undertaking. The Royal Neighbors also hold
their meetings in this hall.

The hall was dedicated December 12, 1923, in accordance with the ceremonies
prescribed by the Grand Lodge of South Dakota by the following acting Grand
Officers: R. S. Lockhart, Grand Master; Frank H. Hooper, Deputy Grand Master;
E. A. Folley, Grand Chaplain; P. W. Treick, Grand Secretary; L. C. Shockey,
Grand Junior Warden; E. Overholzer, Grand Senior Warden; C. N. Burr, Grand
Senior Deacon; H. W. Knutson, Grand Junior Deacon; O. E. Jackson, Grand
Architect; R. A. Ulray, Grand Marshal.

About 200 visitors from Selby, Java, Bowdle, Leola, Aberdeen, Sioux Falls, and
Pollock, South Dakota, and Linton, North Dakota, attended the dedication.

The present officers of the lodge are: F. K. Mehlhaff, W. M.; E. J. Serr, S.
W.; R. A. Dahms, J. W.; Joseph Bender, treasurer; R. Jahraus, secretary; A.
Bjork, S. D.; S. Finkbeiner, J. D.; E. R. Schulkoski, S. S.; Julius Bender, J.
S.; L. Papendick, tyler.

The following poem from the Masonic Advocate was read after a public
installation held on Monday night, December 3, 1894:

THE LEVEL, PLUMB AND SQUARE

We meet upon the Level, and we part up on the Square:
What words sublimely beautiful those words Masonic are;
They fall like strains of melody upon the listening ears,
As they've sounded hallelujahs to the world three thousand years.

We meet upon the Level, though from every station brought,
The Monarch from his palace and the Laborer from his cot,
For the King must drop his dignity when knocking at our door
And the Laborer is his equal as he walks the checkered floor.

We act upon the Plumb--'tis our Master's great command,
We stand upright in virtue's way and lean to neither hand;
The All-Seeing Eye that heads the heart will bear us witness true
That we do always honor God and give each man his due.

We part upon the Square for the world must have its due;
We mingle in the ranks of men, but keep the Secret true,
And the influence of our gatherings in memory is green,
And we long, upon the Level, to renew the happy scene.

There's a world where all are equal -- we are hurrying toward it fast;
We shall meet upon the Level, there where the gates of death are past
We shall stand before the Orient, and our Master will be there
Our work to try, our lives to prove by His unerring Square.

We shall meet upon the Level there, but never thence depart,
There's a mansion bright and glorious set for the pure in heart -
And an everlasting welcome from the Host rejoicing there,
Who in this world of sloth and sin, did part upon the Square.

Let us meet upon the Level, then, while laboring patient here
Let us meet and let us labor, tho' the labor be severe;
Already in the Western Sky the signs bid us prepare
To Rather up our Working Tools and part upon the Square.

Hands round, ye royal craftsmen in the bright, fraternal chain,
We part upon the Square below to meet in Heaven again;
Each tie that has been broken here shall be cemented there.
And none be lost around the Throne who parted on the Square.

EUREKA CHAPTER No. 27, O. E. S.

The real origin of the Eastern Star, like Masonry, will always be shrouded in
mystery. History shows that it may have had a French origin as early as 1730.
To Dr. Rob. Morris, Poet Laureatte of Masonry of Kentucky, goes the honor for
the real origin of the Eastern Star degrees in its initiatory form. He
organized the Order of the Eastern Star about 1850, and in 1860, with the help
of Robert Macoy of New York, the ritualistic work was perfected and beautified.
From a few members, the order has grown to be the largest women's organization
in the world. There are chapters all over the world, and in many languages. Not
only are the wives, daughters, mothers, sisters, and widows of Master Masons
eligible to its membership, but Master Masons are admitted by petition and
initiation to the order.

EUREKA CHAPTER NO. 27, O. E. S.

        First Row: Edna Serr, Alma Klein, Lillian Mehlhaff, F. K. Mehlhaff,
        P. W. Treick, Jenny Turner, Alice Dierenfeldt.
        Second Row: Ida Dahms, Grace Bjork, Ann Buckenberger, Alma Bender,
        Katharina Dierenfeldt, Margaret Pietz, Beryl Martell.
        Third Row: Bertha Kretschmar, G. Klein, Ruth Stephens, Emma Schatz,
        Art Bjork, Irene Olsen.

The greatest constructive work is done in benevolent and educational programs.
Much effort and money have been given generously by chapters and their members
to all charitable fields. Contributions are given to Homes for the Aged,
Hospitals, Schools and Homes for Orphans, Tubercular Sanitariums and Nurseries.
Wonderful and extensive beyond imagination is the work done in the name of
education. Scholarship and student loan funds and various other means of
encouraging education are being developed by Grand Chapters in practically
every state. Eureka Chapter No. 27, O. E. S., was organized on January 1, 1895,
by F. A. Brown, Grand Patron of Aberdeen, with 10 charter members, namely: Mrs.
C. N. Herreid, Mrs. Corwin Johnson, Mr. J. E. Reagan, Mrs. Wm. Matthews, Mrs.
Joseph Worthy, Mrs. John Parrott, Mrs. G. N. Fiddler, Mrs. A. Erne, Mrs. J. E.
Reagan, and Mrs. O. H. Gerdes. The first officers were Mrs. C. N. Herreid,
worthy matron; Mr. J. E. Reagan, worthy patron; Mrs. Corwin Johnson, associate
matron; Mrs. Wm. Matthews, secretary; Mrs. Joseph Worthy, treasurer.

To Mr. J. E. Reagan goes the honor of being the only charter member on our
membership roll today. Many have affiliated with other chapters, while others
are affiliated with that chapter on high, in the city not made with hands.

The membership today includes 39 members.

The present officers are: Mrs. J. H. Schatz, worthy matron; Mr. Arthur Bjork,
worthy patron; Mrs. E. E. Stephens, associate matron; Mr. Gideon Klein,
associate patron; Mrs. Irene G. Olsen, secretary; Mrs. Gideon Klein, treasurer;
Mrs. Paul O. Kretschmar, conductress.

EUREKA PUBLIC LIBRARY

There is no record of any citizen or group of citizens of the early days of our
city, who were sufficiently interested or showed foresight enough to perpetuate
their names through the establishment of a library. Neither does it seem to
have occurred to anyone to apply to Andrew Carnegie in his palmy days to favor
our city with a library such as he was donating by the score to cities in the
nineties. In those days Boston was the center of culture of our country and
Wisconsin was the "woolly west." The people of Eureka had not gotten around to
look for the better things in life. The farmer was planning to raise more
wheat, to plant more corn and to buy more land. Likewise, the business men in
town, ever alert for new deals, were endeavoring to enlarge their places of
business and to provide for material things. Culture had to wait and the days
of public libraries were not yet.

The Eureka Public Library is of comparatively recent origin. The city was not
without libraries as such. Both the public school and the Eureka Lutheran
College, established in 1910, boasted of good libraries; however, these were
not open to the public and their use was restricted in a sense to the students
and faculty.

To Miss Joanne Giedt and her father, the late Senator Theodore J. P. Giedt,
belongs the credit for making plans to establish the library as we know it
today, and while the institution does not boast of a building of its own and is
still housed in very modest quarters generously donated by the Civic Club, our
people have every reason to be proud of this, our library. Under competent
management, new books have been added either by purchase or donation each year
and patronage on the part of the public, especially the junior element, has
been most gratifying. There are at present over 1,400 books on its shelves.

The library dates back to the year 1932, at which time an appropriate
resolution was adopted by the local Parent-Teacher organization to establish
and to sponsor a library to be known as the Eureka Public Library. A committee
of organization consisting of Miss Joanne Giedt, Mrs. Edwin G. Neuharth and
Mrs. Oscar Elster, with two officers of the P. T. A., Mrs. Henry Zenk and Miss
Helen Von Lehe, as ex-officio members, drafted plans and made a thorough study
of methods and procedure. The Public Library Commission was called upon for
their free service and donations of books and periodicals by our citizens were
solicited. The drive netted 250 books.

No funds were available the first year to pay a librarian and volunteers gave
unstintingly of their time and services to make the new library a success.
Credit, in this connection, is duly given to the Misses Flora Grinde, Verna
Schamber, Martha Homeyer and Lydia Frey, and to Mesdames Rieger and Bjertness,
for the work performed. Miss Frey is now in her fifth year of faithful and
competent service as a member of the board.

The Lions Club, now Eureka Civic Club, donated the use of their club
headquarters to the library. The splendid spirit of cooperation on the part of
this club proved a big factor in enabling the Parent-Teacher Association to make
the library a success.

In 1934, the city council voted to pay a small salary to a librarian and the
appointment of Mrs. Anne Buckenberger to this position has met with the
approval of all concerned.

It was a sad day for the community when the Eureka Lutheran College pulled
stakes to return no more. However, there was Balm in Gilead -- the college
donated munificently and the library was increased to 850 volumes.

In the summer of 1936, the Reverend L. Seehaase, looking back on 25 years of
service in the ministry in our city, decided to retire. As a result the library
was remembered by him with 180 volumes of his own collection of books.

The Parent-Teacher Association makes an annual donation of the major part of
its income, as does the Civic Club. Other organizations of the city have always
contributed when called upon. Dr. J. O. F. Kraushaar of Aberdeen, has donated
liberally.

The present board consists of: Miss Frey, Mrs. Zenk, Walter Schonbrod and J. H.
Schatz. Mrs. Buckenberger, librarian. There are those among us who are "lovers
of good books." Your scribe, as one of these, wishes to join Mrs. Celeste
Barnes of the South Dakota Free Library Commission, in complimenting the board
on the high quality of work done with the means at hand. "A workman, that
needeth not be ashamed."                        -- H. Z.

MODERN WOODMEN OF AMERICA

The Eureka Camp, Number 2593, of the Modern Woodmen of America, one of the
city's pioneer fraternal societies, was organized December 12, 1894, and had

F. W. Boettcher, P. H. McEnany, Daniel Mettler, Henry Frey, O. H. Gerdes, Fred

clerk, B. F. Puckett; escort, P. H. McEnany; watchman, Daniel Mettler; sentry,
Henry Frey; camp physician, O. H. Gerdes. The first Board of Managers consisted
of F. W. Boettcher, Fred Schamber, and John Klein.

The Eureka Woodmen Camp has not been regularly active during the past six
years, but is still alive in spirit. The officers at the present time are:
