GOOD SAMARITAN HOME

The Good Samaritan Home of Eureka is one of many homes operated by the
organization of the Good Samaritan Homes. Rev. August Hoeger of Fargo, North
Dakota, the founder of the organization, is the president and an active worker
in the interests of the homes. Theo. Straub, one of Eureka's leading business
men, took an active interest in the organization of the Good Samaritan Home in
Eureka.

The home in Eureka was opened November 1, 1934, with Hattie Bunkowski as
superintendent. The organization purchased the Eureka Lutheran College
buildings and converted all buildings for the purpose of housing inmates. The
home in Eureka is occupied at the present time by 40 old folks and five
children.

The purpose of the Good Samaritan Home in Eureka is to provide a home for the
old who find it impossible to take care and provide for themselves. Every
possible effort is made at this home to make the old folks comfortable and to
provide a place which they enjoy. Everyone is welcome at this home, regardless
of color or creed.

EUREKA STATE EXPERIMENT FARM

Establishment

The South Dakota legislature at its 1907 session passed an act empowering the
State Board of Regents of Education to select one of several sections of public
school land for establishment and use in conducting agricultural experiments,
(Session 1907, Chapter 229, H. B. 275). The location which was chosen under the
foregoing act by Professor Clifford Willis, then Agronomist of South Dakota
State College and Experiment Station was Section 36, Township 127 N., Range 73
West of the 5th P. M., McPherson County, South Dakota.

This land is located exactly on the east side of the city of Eureka, which is
fortunate because it is thus altogether accessible for visitors and others who
come to the place either for business errands or on excursions to study
agricultural problems of the area, many of which are demonstrated on permanent
fields of the experiment farm.

The buildings were located on the experiment farm directly at the east end of
McPherson Street, thus affording a pleasing view of a part of the town from the
residence. The building site was planted with a number of species of trees
including Russian Olives, Ash, White Birch, Caragana, Cottonwood, Elm, and
Boxelder, Lilacs and Blue Spruce. All of the species of trees indicated have
survived from the time of planting over a period of 25 years, and now
constitute one of the attractive features of the area in which they are
located. They also serve as a demonstration of the possibility of utilizing
tree plantings where soil type and other conditions are found to be favorable.

Progressive Citizens Joined in Promoting the Experiment Farm.

No account of the establishment and maintenance of Eureka Experiment Farm would
be adequate without noting the endeavors in its behalf carried out by some
Eureka citizen residents of that time.

Previous to the year 1907, Mr. Fred Hepperle, Senator 1907 session, had voiced
the opinion that an experiment farm at Eureka might and would make it possible
to find an answer to questions about better farming, which could, in turn, be
applied to farms over a radius of 100 miles, which comprise a typical area of
north central South Dakota. Mr. Hepperle and many others were well aware of the
farming possibilities of this great area which, up to the time indicated, had
been rather exclusively a wheat growing country. His attention had been called
to the fact that 3,300 cars of grain were hauled out of Eureka in 1892, valued
at two million dollars. He reasoned that such a performance would indicate the
possibility that other crops such as corn and sorghum, and no doubt alfalfa
would do as well, and that more corn and other cultivated crops might even help
keep up the production of wheat.

The enterprise of Mr. Hepperle thus creditable to him was in no wise
characteristic of him alone. It was typical of a large group of citizens with
whom he worked, and his action and theirs was typical of the enterprise of the
community, and was characteristic of representative citizens. Mr. Julius
Schamber, Eureka pioneer and business man, and Senator from McPherson County in
1909 session, introduced bill to set aside $6,000.00 for improving the farm and
the erection of buildings. These were stipulated to be house, barn, granary,
etc.

The Eureka Experiment Farm was thus legislatively established by the session of
1907, and the site located by Director J. W. Wilson, and Professor C. Willis,
then Agronomist and Superintendent of Substations under the direction of the
Regents of Education. A. E. Hitchcock, Mitchell, and T. W. Dwight, Sioux Falls,
were members of the board who completed the location of the farm and buildings.

In later years the Honorable Theo. J. P. Giedt, lawyer of Eureka and member of
the South Dakota legislature in 1911 and 1913, was a staunch supporter of the
idea of Eureka Experiment Farm and worked continuously in its behalf until the
time of his death in 1936.

Mr. Giedt sought the advice of Dr. N. E. Hansen of South Dakota State College,
and also Director J. W. Wilson of South Dakota Experiment Station, and of the
late President R. L. Slage of the foregoing institution. On the basis of their
judgment, he induced citizens of influence to secure the necessary legislative
action.

        Dwelling -- Seed House -- Implement House.
        State Experimental Farm Buildings at Eureka -- July, 1928.
        Photo by L T. Skinner.

Soil and Crop Experiment Fields Were Laid Out Permanently

Plots of ground or small fields were laid out for conducting soil and crop
experiments by Professor Willis; and various kinds of crops were planted for
experimental trial in the first summer, in 1908. The experiment fields were
marked off permanently with metal stakes. Among the different kinds of crops
put down in the handwriting of Mr. Willis for the first year as found in the
permanent record are: Corn (Dakota No. 86 and Minnesota No. 13); Wheat
(Blue Stem, Durum, Arnautka and Kubanka); Barley (Hanna, Hannchen and Swan
Neck); Oats (60 da. and Swedish Select); Millet (Kursk and Black Voronizh); Flax
(Common and Primost No. 25).

        Eureka Experiment Farm. Barley on Acre No. 501-10 (1914). Corn on
        Adjoining Acre in the Background. Photo by Champlin.

It is a matter of historic interest to note how large a number of these crops
that proved well adapted in Eureka territory had been first successful under
similar conditions of soil and climate in Russia. The now well known
explorations of N. E. Hansen brought in geographic strains of durum, oats, and
millet, that found a new home at Eureka Experiment Farm where they excelled
either as they were, or furnished a basis for further improvement by selection
and crop breeding. Hansen has emphasized Dr. Candolles' law of hardiness which
says in effect that, biologic forms as crop plants which are able to survive
are those which may be introduced from nearly similar conditions in other parts
of the world.

Improvement of Crop Varieties Has Been Continuous.

During the time since the first year when the foregoing crops were put in for
trial at Eureka Experiment Farm, numerous new varieties and strains of all kinds
of crops have been introduced and tried out. In all cases it has been possible
to find better varieties which would increase the quality and amount of crops
produced. Introduction and improvement of various crops at Eureka Experiment
Farm has been a process of development through successive seasons. Among the
varieties that appear to be suitable for Eureka area in later years are Ceres
Wheat, Bison and Red Wing Flax, Odessa Barley, and Richland Oats. Many other
varieties of the various crops have been tried out and found fairly well
adapted, but it is impossible to put them all down here. The foregoing will
serve as illustrations of the way in which the best ones have been picked out
from among the many at Eureka Experiment Farm, and recommended for the farming
area round about.

Eureka Corn for the Northern Corn Belt.

At the time when Eureka Experiment Farm was established, corn was not very
commonly produced on the farms of the area. The need for including a row or
cultivated crop in rotation with others became more and more apparent. It was
desirable or necessary to find some kind of early maturing corn that would be
worth raising on its own account, and that would likewise leave the land where
it might be cultivated in good condition for a succeeding small grain crop.
Flint corn had been produced with some success over small areas, and later the
variety Northwestern Dent was utilized by some corn growers. These varieties
were found to produce ears very close to the ground, and also to have branching
qualities that were objectionable.

About 1914, Alta Corn, which had been developed at Highmore, was introduced at
Eureka. Alta is an early variety of yellow corn with fairly deep kernels, small
cob, with ears growing at good height on the stalk. Before many years the corn
thus introduced at Eureka was disseminated over the northern part of the state
and it became one of the leading varieties. It proved impossible for a long
time to develop any variety of early corn that would combine as many desirable
qualities for growth in the northern area as this Alta.

The variety Alta and a number of other early varieties were utilized along with
Northwestern Dent, to furnish a basis for a combination and selection of a new
variety that might be even a little more successful under the conditions than
any of the varieties named. An ear-row corn breeding system was installed at
Eureka in 1925, and it has been continued ever since.

        Corn on an experimental Acre at Eureka in 1913. Since then corn has
        advanced in relative importance in crop systems.

A number of corn growers were consulted about kinds of corn which might
contribute desirable factors in the new ear-row breeding plot. Among those who
helped secure one or more single ears of corn for this regional planting, and
the kind of corn now put down in the original records are:

J. W. Parmley, Ipswich -- Average Yellow Dent.
J. W. Parmley -- Wimples Yellow Dent, with 15 years selection for earliness.
J. W. Parmley -- White Corn early like Flint of 35 years standing in Purell
        Township, Edmunds County -- farm of A. H. Germain.
J. W. Parmley -- Flint, such as the Rees raised when Lewis and Clark bought of
        them in 1804.
Jacob Ottenbacher -- Golden Jewel.
Lorenz Lippert -- Tom Engelbretson's Silver King.
Emil Dierenfeldt -- Flint and Dent.

The outcome has been the development of Eureka corn which is named after the
town and experiment farm where it has been developed. Eureka corn has been
tried out practically by numerous growers. In the past season it was introduced
into a corn variety trial adopted in Oregon by County Agent J. J. Inskeep. The
Eureka corn was evidently successful in this trial as evidenced by the statement
by County Agent Inskeep:

"I am sending you herewith a report of the corn variety trial which was
completed in Clackamas County this last year."

"This test entailed quite a lot of time and work but I got a great kick from
it, especially from seeing your little yellow dent lead the field in seed
production. I would like to inquire whether or not this Eureka dent is strictly
a hybrid corn. Please let me know. Please also let me know whether or not I
could secure some seed for next year, where I could secure it and what the
price would be."

"This variety showed the results of excellent breeding and selection, and I
want to congratulate you as I feel that the performance of this variety here is
quite an achievement, and really a feather in your cap."

Sweet Clover Was Added.

Another crop which was introduced into the area by Eureka Experiment Farm about
1914 was sweet clover for forage. That was an important step, especially because
sweet clover is a legume and it could supplement the native legumes of the area
in returning nitrogen and organic matter to the soil removed by other kinds of
crops.

It was also found important at Eureka to choose the right varieties and strains
of the different crops but likewise important to arrange these crops in proper
system or crop rotations. The most successful system at Eureka proved to be
apparently: (1) Row crop (corn, sorghum), followed by: (2) Small grain (wheat,
oats, barley, rye), followed by: (3) Legume (alfalfa or sweet clover). For
instance, it was proved at Eureka that wheat raised continuously on the same
land yielded -- bushels per whereas wheat which followed corn yielded --
bushels per acre.

Space permits only this one illustration of the fact Eureka Experiment Farm has
not only found the right of crop, but likewise profitable arrangement of crops
crop systems or rotations.

The investigations here described at Eureka Experiment Farm deal fundamentally
with (1) Soils, and (2) Crops. During the years since 1911 they have been
carried out with the supervision of A. N. Hume, as Agronomist and Superintendent
of Substations for South Dakota State College and Experiment Station,
Brookings. Problems of soil, including types, technology, and management have
been referred to J. G. Hutton, Associate in Soils. Crop problems, and details
of field management have been supervised in detail successively by Manley
Champlin, A. T. Evans, K. H. Klages, and Clifford Franzke. Recent direct
supervision of small grain breeding is in charge of S. P. Swenson.

The first resident foreman at Eureka Experiment Farm beginning before the
dwelling was constructed, was Mr. Walter Broich, who was also a student at
South Dakota State College. He was succeeded by Mr. Frank Sutherland, and he in
turn by Mr. Joseph Hoffman. The latter was succeeded by the present foreman,
Mr. Walter Schonbrod. The terms of service of these foremen collectively have
covered more than one quarter of a century. With no undue sentiment, it may be
stated that they have been faithful in more than a few things. The details of
experimental work on the land at Eureka Experiment Farm require fidelity without
which none of the other work would be worthwhile.
                        --A. N. Hume, Agronomist and Supt. of Substations.

                EUREKA - The World's Greatest Wheat Market

"Wo gehst due hin ?"
"Eureka !"
"Ich meine due warst dort letzte woch!"
"Yah, sure, but I got to go dere agin dis week . . . wheat, weizen and weizen
. . . wheat . . ."

Eureka, during that 15 year period between 1887 and 1902, was the greatest
primary wheat market of the world. Here in this pioneer city, the world's wheat
basket was being filled with the best grade of grain obtainable anywhere. Its
32 grain buying concerns -- 10 of these were elevators, 22 were large flat
grain storage houses -- were kept busy day and night storing and shipping out
the wheat that the hard-working German-Russian farmers steadily brought in by
oxen or horses. For 15 years farmers from a radius of 50 to 75 miles
laboriously hauled grain to this terminus of the branch line of the Milwaukee
railroad leading north from Roscoe, out into the open prairie region. Year after
year, 200 men worked in day and night shifts handling the 3,000,000 to
4,000,000 bushels of sacked golden grain plucked from a bounteous land.

"Wheat--weizen--weizen--wheat!" Thirty-three hundred freight carloads of it or
a train of cars 30 miles in length in 1892. "Wheat--money--money--wheat!" Two
million dollars worth of prosperity in 1892.

Let us follow that heavily-laden, slow-moving ox cart into Eureka. The quiet,
lonesome prairie is left waving behind us; a hubbub of shouts, whistles,
creaking of carts, bellowing of oxen excites the tender ears accustomed to
silent prairie vastness. People and colors, strangers and foreign babbling,
English, German, Russian, mixed together into a symphony of excitement.

"Wait here with the oxen, son; I'll go look for a place to unload." Hours would
go by leaving the barefooted boy dancing near the tired oxen, trying to keep
himself warm from the night chill. Lines of ox carts, hundreds of boys tending
loads, hundreds of buyers and sellers estimating, weighing, dickering over
wheat sacks--forty wagons trailing into Eureka in one long string. "Why, here
are the wagons from all over the world," thought the wide-eyed farmer lads.

        Market Scene -- 1892

The fact that the farmers could not unload quickly did not mean that there was
not plenty of opportunities for him to sell his wheat. Sometimes dozens of
buyers were after him, all over his load, each trying to offer a better price.

This "coming to town" was a joyous affair. Farmer called farmer; news held back
for weeks was given out; feet thumped along the high board walks over which hung
large awnings which extended out from buildings of frame construction. Glasses
clinked and bottles danced over the counters of 16 saloons while bartenders
hurried to wait on thirsty customers and to make a good profit above the $1,000
annual city tax each establishment had to pay. Restaurants and rooming houses
did boom business. Business men and merchants of all sorts were voicing
bargains and gorgeously displaying their wares, hopeful for a generous share of
the $1,000,000 to $2,000,000 brought into Eureka annually.

And so, with the farmers, the town did prosper and business places took in
their share of the money. Machinery men usually sold about 50 threshing
machines a year. One year 73 were sold to eager farmers who had greatly
extended their productive acreage in order to raise fare wheat -- more wheat,
more money. Eight livery stables did a thriving business. Hotels could not
accommodate all the people who wished to stay overnight. Many tired travelers
slept in stables and on store floors. Often clerks, pressed hard during a day's
selling, were compelled to stay awake all night guarding his merchandise while
the floor space, the counters, were covered with snoring, bearded farmers. Hay
was often brought into the stores, spread on the floors, slept on, and in the
morning swept out into the street as we now do with sweeping compounds.

Grain men were always busy with the tremendous task of buying, storing and
shipping millions of dollars worth of wheat. In 1897, two-thirds of the world's
wheat crop, 1,136,480 bushels, was shipped from Eureka. On October 14 of that
year, 30,000 bushels of this precious grain was shipped out.

        Pioneer Grain Buyer -- 1897

While some flat houses and elevators were conveniently located near the
railroad, many of them were scattered about town and extra handling of the
grain was required in order to get it to the trains. These flat houses were
large storage bins, divided into various compartments. The sacks of grain were
carried from the farm wagons into the houses by the workmen and then rolled up
cleated planks and dumped into the bins. In the elevators there were no dumps
as now. Sacks were emptied into the weighing hopper by hand. From the hopper a
bucket elevator raised the grain to where it could be distributed into the
different bins.

Buyers received grain day and night, but the usual time for loading it into box
cars was at night when there was a slight lull in the buying activities. Buyers
paid on an average from 36 cents to 50 cents per bushel for the wheat which was
always of number one quality and never required testing for weight or dockage.
Each of these grain concerns received, on an average, 10 times as much grain a
year as the average elevator does now.

For the most part local buyers were honest and got more business than did the
line-company buyers, who had a reputation for shifting weights and stealing. A
favorite trick of theirs was to offer more per bushel and then short change the
farmer in pounds. Only very rarely was a flat house operator accused of
stealing from the farmer.

The railroads profited greatly by this tremendous wheat traffic. During the
months of October and November, 1892, the Eureka station's earnings exceeded
those of any other station on the entire Milwaukee system by reaching the
enormous figure of $100,000 per month.

With all its hustle and bustle, Eureka of the '90's was a decent little town.
The grain haulers, composing by far the largest percentage of the visiting
population, were peaceable and law-abiding. When staying all night in Eureka
they would lunch and beer, visit with friends and cronies until one or two in
the morning before "hitting the hay."

        Horse-Power Pioneer Threshing

It was not an uncommon sight to see six or eight ox teams leaving town tied each
behind the other. The men, homeward bound, would be in the front wagon enjoying
their keg of beer with songs, stories, and "man" talk. Trips to town and back
took days and weeks according to the distances covered, and so it is not
difficult to picture the amazement of one farmer when asking the question,
"Where you going?" to reply to the Eureka-bound grain hauler, "But I thought
you were there last week!" Or last month. Perhaps some far-distance farmer
would visit the town semi-annually and consider that often; but, we know that
if it happened between 1887 and 1902, he was heavily loaded with that golden
wheat which, in turn, made Eureka the Greatest Primary Wheat Market in the
World.

The Soo Railroad's entrance into the territory west to Pollock in 1902, forced
the Milwaukee Railroad to extend its tracks northward into Linton, North
Dakota. This extension of the two roads distributed a large percentage of
Eureka's business among the towns that sprang up along their tracks and ended
the wheat boom days in this territory.

In 1902, Eureka lost its grain market prestige, but is still one of the most
important towns between Aberdeen and Bismarck.

        McPherson County Wheat Scene, 1935

EMERGENCY AGRICULTURAL ADJUSTMENT ADMINISTRATION

To the early settlers of the county, the thought of receiving benefit payments
from the government for adjusting the crop acres on their farm may seem rather
strange, and as though something very definitely is wrong with the farmers of
today, who have to adopt such methods in their farming practices. They are
right, for there have been many very definite changes taking place in the
county the last 50 years, and this is just as true in the field of agriculture
as it is in the fields of medicine, science and engineering developments. Fifty
years ago, farmers sold their steers at the age of three or four years,
weighing 1,000 to 1,200 pounds. Today farmers put 800 to 1,OOO-pound baby
beeves on the market in half that time. Then it was an all-winter job to market
the grain crop from the previous harvest; now, with the modern truck, an entire
season's harvest can be put on the market in a week's time. Then, the best one
agricultural worker could do during a season was to produce food for seven city
workers. Today, with modern machinery, one agricultural worker can produce food
for 25 city workers. Yet the ratio between urban and rural population has not
changed anywhere near that ratio.

Any thinking person who stops to consider these facts, must come to the
conclusion that if the farmer is to maintain his position in the American
economic life, he must start thinking about what his neighbors are doing and
make plans accordingly. The Agricultural Adjustment Administration offers the
farmers of the county an opportunity for collective thinking and action,
whereby they can solve many of their problems and help maintain their present
position in our economic life, and above all else leave something for future
generations, who will have to live on our soils as we are doing today.

In another 50 years, it is safe to predict that the farmer who deliberately
exploits his soil from year to year for his own immediate economic benefit will
be considered a social outcast.

Since the Agricultural Adjustment Administration started operating in 1933,
McPherson County farmers were paid $1,252,073.52 in way of benefit payments up
to January 1, 1937. This represents all payments actually made in the wheat,
corn and hog, cattle, sheep, and agricultural conservation programs. During the
same period, the county commissioners budgeted $275,122 to pay county expenses,
and of this amount they had to raise in tax levies only $147,192.11, which is
only 11.75 per cent of the money paid to farmers in benefit payments.
                        --Henry L. Leonhardt, County Extension Agent.

THE SOD SHANTY

The sod shanty of pioneer days has passed from the scene almost entirely in
McPherson County. The early settlers who still survive, speak of it with
apparent affection. It was invariably the first home the immigrants built in
the new land. A breaking plow, a team of oxen, about ten dollars' worth of
timber for the roof and door, and a window or two, was all that was required,
except for the labor of laying the sod.

        Pioneer Sod House

The sod shanty had its drawbacks, but it also had decided advantages. Carefully
built, it was warm in winter and cool in summer, in fact much more so than well
built frame houses.

Very few sod shanties remain in the county now. Most of them have been razed or
allowed to wear away by the action of the elements.

The building of a good sod shanty, however, entailed some skill. Wherever
possible, sod with heavy grass, usually from a lake bottom or low place was
used. Real gumbo sod was best of all. With a supply of sod broken came the
cutting it into strips about two feet in length, The plows usually cut the sod
about 12 inches wide. Two rows of sod strips were laid close to one another --
making the walls two feet thick. The first layer was laid length-wise to
outline the desired size of the structure. The next layer crosswise and so on,
until the desired height was reached. Door and window frames were, of course,
put in place at the proper time

The wall finished, rafters either of sawed lumber or in some instances timber
hewn along the Missouri River, were rested upon them. The earlier shanties had
sod covering the roof. These roofs were warm but not waterproof, a decided
disadvantage when spring and summer rains fell. Shingles usually supplanted sod
on the roof as soon as possible.

With a good supply of sod close at hand the erection of a sod shanty was a
matter of only two or three days.

Sometimes a stone foundation was laid for the walls, a most desirable feature,
since moisture from the ground would not affect them. When the walls were
finished, they were generally stuccoed with mud, clay if available, inside and
out, to make them tight.

        The Sod House of Pioneer Days

Tamped clay floors were mostly used in the earlier shanties and were very
satisfactory. Properly made, they were smooth and hard and could even be swept
with a soft broom. Later, board floors were generally laid.

The size of the shanties varied, but among German immigrants in McPherson
County, families were usually large and structures about 30 feet long and 20
feet wide, outside measurements, were the most common. They were generally
divided into two rooms with the kitchen about 10 x 16 feet in size. The outside
door almost invariably opened into the kitchen, which had one window as a rule,
with two in the living room

Perhaps the most interesting of the furnishings of sod homestead shanties in
the McPherson County German settlements were the home-made stoves. Lacking
money to purchase steel ranges and the conventional heaters, the settlers built
their own. They were of ingenious construction generally fired with straw,
since wood was lacking.

Many of the settlers had two of these stoves, one for heating and the other for
cooking. Should the heating stove have been used for cooking in warm weather,
the shanty would have been uncomfortably hot all the time. Both stoves were
built of small prairie boulders and clay.

They varied in size, but the proportions were the same in nearly all cases.
Heating stoves or "Stubofens" were perhaps five feet six inches long, a bit
over two feet wide and perhaps five feet high. Often one end was built into the
wall dividing the kitchen from the living room with the firedoor in the kitchen
so that straw could be fed into it without littering up the living quarters. In
many of these "stoves" a steel plate extended from the firedoor end to within a
few inches of the farther end, effecting a sort of return-flue principle for
the fire. An opening above this plate made it possible to put roasts or other
foods to be cooked on it. When it was thus employed, the stove would first be
fired and when of the right temperature the ashes would be swept out and the
food placed in it. The housewife could put her dinner into place in the morning
and when she returned at noon everything would be done to a turn. Many people
in the Eureka territory sigh regretfully when they talk about the bread baked
in these old fashioned "stoves."

        Modern McPherson County Farm

It required but little fuel to heat the room, a couple of bushels of straw
generally being sufficient for the day, even in cold weather. Once heated
through, the rock and clay would radiate a gentle heat for several hours.

Many shanties also had cook stoves. These were placed in the kitchen and were
of course smaller. A typical "Kochhart" was two feet long, 16 inches wide, or
so, and three feet high. In some cases there was a steel plate for the top, but
others had rock and clay top with holes over which kettles were placed. The
cook stove was also fired with straw. The fuel problem was thus of no great
worry. In case there was no flax or wheat straw, slough hay or rushes would
serve quite as well

Cast iron cook stoves were of course better, but for heating many of the
pioneers parted with their hand-made apparatus with regret. It was fine, even
heat and the cost for fuel was negligible. But, of course, one could not have a
clay and rock heater in the fine homes many of the settlers built for
themselves when they gained a competence.

        Stone House with Sod Roof

THE EUREKA LUTHERAN COLLEGE

In the years to come the words "Eureka Lutheran College" will mean very little
to the younger generation other than the two structures on the former college
site; to those who have seen Eureka grow fro 1909 through 1933, and who might
have attended the college, these same words bring back fond memories of school
days.

        Eureka Lutheran College -- 1910

This school was a typical denominational institution. When the pioneers broke
into new territory, a church and a school were the first public institutions
usually erected. The German immigrants, accustomed to the schools of Christian
higher education in their mother country, desired to give their children the
same opportunities they themselves had enjoyed, and, before they had realized
the advantages afforded by publicly owned institutions elsewhere, built college
buildings at a cost of some $25,000.

The question relative to an institution of this character for this Dakota
region was first propounded in 1908. The idea was received with much
enthusiasm; both Parkston and Eureka, South Dakota, bid for the honor of
fathering the school, and Eureka, because of its more central location, won.
The generous offer of Emil Dierenfeldt to donate four blocks of ground free of
charge for a building site from his 37-acre tract of land east of the railroad
was accepted and a building 80 x 120, three stories high, with a full basement,
was soon in the process of construction.

On September 11, 1910, the institution was dedicated. District President
Eisenberg read the dedicatory services. A great throng of people assembled.
Bands from Eureka and Leola entertained at the depot and on the campus. In the
afternoon double church services were held at the church and in the city hall.
During the last services of the eventful day a grateful people laid $465.06 on
the collection plate in order to further n dream come true. Before the college
opened practically all the building fund had been collected.

The first school year opened on September 14, 1910. Three professors, Mr.
Schaefer, Mr. Van Arman, and Mr. Braulich, had been engaged to assist
Reverend-Director Taubert tutor 91 students. Three courses were offered: the
preparatory, the academic, and the commercial. The students were a free,
unrefined, and unhampered bunch, ages varying from 14 to 24 years. The first
year had many unexpected disappointments caused by various misunderstandings.

On September 12, 1911, the second year opened for even greater discouragements.
Collections did not materialize and debts mounted. The opening enrollment was
45, but at the end of the term only 17 students were in attendance; Dr. Taubert
resigned.

Reverend Bunge accepted the presidency in 1912 at a most critical time --
Eureka had lost interest. Fifteen students enrolled the first semester. During
this year 1,500 trees were planted on the campus.

Professor Liefeld, joining the faculty in 1913, brought new life to the
institution by organizing outside interests for the students -- a band and an
orchestra, a mixed and a male chorus. A student paper, the "Trommel," was
started. It was a year of thorough progress.

In 1914, 114 students registered. More instructors came to foster more
activities. The first "outflies" were had and the musical organizations made
their first tours.

The first graduation exercise was held in 1916 in the city hall. Mrs. Olivia
Wiederaenders (nee Mix) was the first graduate. Director Bunge was compelled to
resign because of ill health.

Professor Schaeffer succeeded director Bunge. The gymnasium was built and
dedicated. A class of seven, four of whom are now ministers, was graduated in
1917.

Dr. Rodemann and Professor Liefeld divided administerial duties between
themselves when Director Schaeffer resigned, due to ill health, in 1918.

Reverend Sandrock began his twelve years of service for the College when he
took over the duties of Director in 1920.

Though many instructors came and went during the last years of the school's
existence, the names of Professors L. S. Sheldahl and O. Elster should be
mentioned in regard to their faithful service.

Many improvements were made on the campus and school buildings -- there was an
ornamental fountain and four brick light posts donated by the class of 1922;
the president's residence was built in 1924; two residences were constructed in
1926; a portable building used for commercial training quarters was erected in
1928.

Eureka College suffered a great loss in the departure of Professor Liefeld in
1929. He had been in charge of the music department for 16 years and had
greatly contributed to the building up of interest and spirit toward the school.

Director Sandrock left in 1931 and Professor Sheldahl was made acting president
until Reverend H. J. Kuhlmann accepted the position in 1932.

The financial burden connected with college administration became too heavy
during the last depression years and the American Lutheran Church felt
compelled by the circumstances to merge Eureka Lutheran College with the St.
Paul Luther College in 1933.

Though E. L. C. has come and gone, we feel that it was a vital part of the
upbuilding of the city and that many, with heavy hearts, saw its influence
taken away by forces beyond control.

        Watermelon Club -- 1930 A. Schaedler, J. D. Harr, Jac. Harr, A. F.
        Obenauer, Theo. Straub, Ed. Serr, John G. G. Neuharth, Ed. Maier,
        Dr. J. E. Mehlhaff.

WATERMELON CLUB

Back in 1929 and 1930 there was one organization in Eureka that boasted of its
perfect attendance. That organization was the Watermelon Club. These boys got
together just once a day and every member was there promptly at the stroke of
three, for it was then that the juicy taste-tickler was sliced. First come,
first served; and if you weren't on time chances are you would NOT be served.
Those pictured are: Adam Schaedler J. D. Harr and daughter, Adam F. Obenauer,
Theo. Straub, Ed. Serr, John G. G. Neuharth, Ed. Meier and Dr. J. E. Mehlhaff.

PRAIRIE FIRES

Among many other threats to life and property of the pioneer period in this
section was prairie fire. Before the sod was broken, a growth of high thick
grass was a real threat after the heavy frosts of the early fall. Once on fire
and fanned by a strong wind, the flames swept on and on, burning thousands of
acres as there were no plowed fields nor in most places rivers or creeks to
stop them.

Except for the first year or two, there is little record of any serious loss by
prairie fire in the community immediately adjacent to Eureka. The few pioneers
who had taken up their claims in 1883 and 1884, were the only ones reporting
prairie fire losses. It is probable that the Russian-German immigrants, coming
from the steppes, were familiar with the danger of fires in the tall grass and
exercised more than ordinary precaution in preventing them.

The most disastrous fire in the county history was the one which swept over the
eastern part of the county on April 2, 1889, when the city of Leola was
practically wiped out. The fire swept down on the little town from the hill
country to the northwest, fanned by a wind of hurricane nature and everything
in its path was destroyed.

The only protection against prairie fire was a fire-break, made by breaking the
sod with a plow around buildings or hay stacks. These fire-breaks, in order to
be safe, must be no less than two rods in width. If travelers chanced to be in
the path of a prairie fire, their only protection was to set another fire ahead
of them and then take refuge in the burned-over area. The speed of the prairie
fire was almost incredible.  The fastest horse would sometimes be unable to
keep ahead of it, depending on the speed of the wind.

        Eureka Lake

THE HYSTORY OF EUREKA LAKE AND PARK

The possibilities of making a park and recreational center out of the lake and
surrounding ground which lies directly to the west of the city of Eureka were
long apparent to the people of this community. Approximately 25 years ago,
under the guidance of August Martell, boating and fishing were done here to
some extent. A ball park was located close to the lake, and soft drinks were
sold to the general public. Soon after this time an outlet for the recently
constructed city sewers was needed and the lake was used for that purpose. A
very severe winter and scarcity of water killed many of the fish in the lake,
and for many years afterwards the lake was often thought of, but nothing much
was done. The late Theo. J. P. Giedt was one of the chief agitators for making
a recreational park out of this body of water, and in 1920 and 1921, was
instrumental in having many trees planted along the lakeshore, most of which
failed to grow.

In the summer of 1933, the lake question was again brought out of its lethargy
by the Civic Club of Eureka President Roosevelt had only been in office a few
months when he organized the Public Works Administration, and the people were
clamoring for municipal sponsorship of governmental projects as a means of
tiding them over the depression years. At one of the meetings of the Civic Club
during that time, a committee consisting of F. K. Mehlhaff, John G. G. Neuharth
and J. J. Pietz was appointed to look into the matter, draw plans, and see if
they could make a PWA project out of the lake. These men worked on the matter
for approximately two months, and even made a trip to Pierre, South Dakota, to
investigate the matter and submit their plans, when they thought it feasible to
act in conjunction with the city council in going ahead with the matter.

Toward the end of the summer of 1933, the following men and the organizations
which they represented, were designated as "The Lake Committee," to foster a
PWA project for the purpose of making "the lake" a recreational center, namely:
John J. Pietz, representing the Izaak Walton League; John G. G. Neuharth,
representing the Civic Club; F. K. Mehlhaff, representing the School Board; J.
H. Schatz, representing the City Council; and A. Bjork, representing the
American Legion. This group met and elected the following officers: J. J.
Pietz, chairman; John G. G. Neuharth, vice chairman; A. Bjork, secretary; and
F. K. Mehlhaff, treasurer. J. H. Schatz was designated as the official contact
man between the committee and the city council.

The first major problem that confronted the committee was the removal of the
sewage outlet from the lake. In cooperation with the city council, the
committee was successful in having a special election called for the purpose of
voting on the question of issuing bonds on the credit of the city for the
purpose of financing the cost of changing the sewage outlet. The people
responded whole-heartedly and voted in favor of the bonds by a large majority.

Soon afterwards, the city purchased a new dumping ground and the site of the
old dumping ground was changed from the west side of the lake to a point about
a mile north of the city. Much work and labor was necessary to move old junk
and debris from the lake shore to the new dumping ground. However, PWA funds
were available and when the winter of 1933-1934 was over, practically all of
this work had been done.

At the same time it was necessary to have a plan of the proposed project
submitted and maps drawn. The city council again responded benevolently and
employed engineers Jepson and Yost of Brookings, South Dakota, to do this work.
This was soon done and a project submitted to the PWA officials, which was soon
approved by that group. The cost of the project was apportioned between the
city, the sponsor of the project, and the Public Works Administration, with the
understanding that only relief labor could be used on the project. The city
council responded with many donations, and the people of Eureka and various
social organizations made individual donations and sponsored public
entertainments as a means of raising funds.

The next thing was to beautify the lake grounds. This was done by building a
road around the lake. Much excavating and filling in was necessary and a first
class road was finally completed. The shore line was changed to some extent,
and all marshes and mud flats were removed. Almost the entire lake shore was
rip-rapped with rock, bridges were built, an island in the lake was built up,
and two peninsulas were beautified. A baseball diamond was laid out, and plans
drawn for an athletic field for the school and city. The part of the shore line
which was not rip-rapped was covered with sand, and the Eureka Lake now
possesses one of the finest bathing beaches in northern South Dakota. The roads
around the lake have all been graveled, guard-rails have been erected in many
places, a boat dock constructed, and a bridge now connects the mainland and
island. Thousands of trees were planted and have been kept up since that time
by funds appropriated by the city. City water has been extended to the beach
grounds. The land on which the park and lake are located is owned by the city
of Eureka.

The park as yet is not complete. The committee still plans on making many
additions to it. Plans for the erection of a bath-house have already been
submitted; more trees are intended to be planted, and various other
improvements have been talked about and apparently are in the process of
realization. A huge stone has been taken to the park grounds and it is the plan
of the community to have a plaque attached to this stone in honor of the late
Dr. O. H. Gerdes, pioneer Eureka physician. This rock is to be known as the Dr.
O. H. Gerdes Memorial.

The present condition of the city lake and park is such that the people of
Eureka and surrounding country can well be proud of this project, and the work
and industry which was required to make it such, can never be fully appreciated.

THE EUREKA RADIO STATION -- 9PI

Eureka was in the forefront in the development of radio. Long before the
airways were full of "chain programs" Station 9PI was sending music out on the
ether waves and listening to broadcasts from other lands. Its own programs were
received from coast to coast and from Canada to the Gulf of Mexico. So well
developed was the receiving powers of 9PI that, at the outset of the World War
in 1914-17, war messages direct from Germany were received over its aerial.

Owners of radio sets in those primitive days of the wireless were often
thrilled by the programs received from 9PI, but there was surely an equal
thrill and satisfaction for three men, who had made the equipment largely with
their own hands. It was in 1910 that Edward Isaak and J. A. Gardner, then
manager of the Dakota Central Telephone office in Eureka, began experimenting
with "gadgets" they hoped to assemble into radio receiving apparatus. It wasn't
so long until they found an enthusiastic co-worker in the person of Dr. J. O.
F. Kraushaar, now of Aberdeen. "Doc", as he was called in the radio work room,
wound coils by hand, slowly and laboriously, and performed a hundred other
tasks requiring infinite patience. Isaak and Gardner and the volunteer helper
often worked until the small hours of the morning, completing some piece of
apparatus necessary or decided upon for trial at long drawn-out conferences.

The work shop was located on the rear platform of the Isaak Variety Store and
when the amateur radio engineers had completed their apparatus, the studio was
established. None of the "engineers" will perhaps experience another thrill to
equal the one they got when their first broadcasts were traveling out into
space and landing in New York, on the Pacific coast, south to Texas and north
into Canada. They were still full of plans for further development and
improvement of their station when the United States entered the World War and
an unsympathetic government ordered the apparatus dismantled.

After the war a new license was obtained and the station again operated for
some time with excellent results. But soon after, Edward Isaak moved to
Chicago, Gardner had left, and Dr. Kraushaar was practicing medicine in
Aberdeen and so the career of 9PI was over. But, it was one of the first
wireless stations in this section of the northwest.

THE GREAT BLIZZARD

The year 1888 will always be borne in mind by Dakotans, as well as by the
people of the entire west, as the year of the great blizzard. It fell
unannounced on January 12th, with a ferocity before undreamed of by the
denizens of the plain. In the northern portion of the territory, it began early
in the morning, but in the central and southern portions it did not arrive
until later in the day when people were scattered in their avocations, children
were in school, farmers out with their stock, or enroute to or from market, and
the resulting fatalities were fearful. It was a beautiful winter morning, warm
and gracious, with soft, variable breezes -- just such a morning as was
calculated to draw the people into the open. One moment it was bright, warm,
glorious; the next moment, and without the slightest premonitory warning, the
terror fell with unexampled rage. All attempt at description must be weak and
inadequate. For 15 hours it continued, blinding, impenetrable, intensely cold,
the atmosphere filled with needles of ice driven by a furious gale, accompanied
by a deafening roar; then it was gone and nature smiled out again as bright and
innocent as a morning in May. One hundred twelve citizens of South Dakota
perished in the storm and many others suffered extreme peril. Minnesota,
Nebraska, and western Iowa suffered equally.

GOLF IN EUREKA

"Ye ancient and honourable game of golfe" broke in on Eureka first on July 7,
1926, when a few enthusiasts for the game organized themselves into a club. The
first course was entirely improvised, even as to greens, a tin cup sunk into
the earth being the sole provision. The game could flourish only until the
grass was too high, since the club finances at the outset permitted no
expenditure for mowing the course.

This first brave advance guard of golfers included Henry Isaak, who was
president, William Boettcher, John Pietz, Otto A. Herzog, secretary-treasurer,
Joseph Bender, Dr. P. W. Treick, vice president, Edwin Isaak and Gideon Klein.

As the popularity of the game grew in the community, the course was developed
on the plot originally leased from the Farmers and Merchants State Bank, north
of the Fair Grounds. It was laid out by Leonard Seamann of Faulkton, and is now
a nine hole course, accounted one of the better in this section of the state.

As in many another golf club, the heaviest duties in maintaining the
organization devolved upon the secretary. The men serving in that capacity
succeeding Mr. Herzog are as follows: Dave Bauer, 1927-28; E. G. Neuharth,
1929-30; Edwin J. Serr, 1930-35. Mr. Neuharth was again named secretary in
1936, and is the present incumbent of the of office.

The local club has often entertained tournaments during recent years, the most
important of which was the Northern States Tournament in 1935.

Golf continues to hold the interest of a large number of Eureka people and even
during the recent depressed times has flourished to a gratifying degree.

                REMINISCENCES By Rev. Henry Reinhardt.

Eureka will soon celebrate its Golden Jubilee. It is only fitting and proper
that we recall the events of the past 50 years. I was asked to relate my
experiences during the 20 years that I have lived and worked in Eureka. I am
glad to comply with this request, even though I realize that writing of one's
own experiences is a delicate task. That which is written is often
misunderstood. When it concerns one's own works and deeds, it may be mistaken
for egotism. But the fact that I have since grown to be an old man, and that
those for whom this is written are for the most part of the second generation,
makes the task easier. I sincerely hope that those people of Eureka who
remember the old days, will accept this in the spirit in which it is written;
and that the second generation will see that the work of their fathers was
worthy, and will follow in their footsteps.

In June, 1893, after I had finished my studies, I received word that I was to
start my work in Dakota Territory, in and near Eureka, with a friend and
fellow-student, who was already there. The things I had heard of Dakota were not
good. Reports said that the winter was very long and terribly cold; a
succession of blizzards such as were unknown elsewhere. Summer, on the other
hand, was unbearably hot; hot drying winds blew continuously, crops were poor.
That was my impression of Dakota then. Can you understand when I say that I
approached Eureka with mixed feelings? Would my faith be strong enough?

I stopped in Aberdeen on my way to Eureka. A friend at home had asked me to
look up a man there, which I did. My meeting with this man was the beginning of
a long friendship. He gave me the first good advice I received in Dakota: He
told me never to speak of the people in this territory as Russians, but to call
them Germans, for they were as German as he and I, even though they had migrated
to Russia before coming here. I have always followed his advice, and have often
passed it on.

My friend, K., met me at the station when I finally arrived, and took me to the
parsonage. I delivered my first sermon in Eureka the next Sunday. At that time
the parish was comprised of eight congregations. An older and more experienced
minister should have been in charge of it. But after I spoke in several
congregations, the people called me as their pastor. After some correspondence
with the District President, etc., I was allowed to accept the call.

Until fall, friend K. and I worked and lived together. Then, in the fall, I
married and became head of my own household. K. followed my example a few
months later.

With God's help, conditions in the parish improved. Of work there was plenty.
On every Sunday and holiday there were two or three services, each one 20 or 30
miles away from the previous one. A horse and buggy were used to cover the
distance between congregations. During my first year here, the members of the
congregation met with me for Bible class every Wednesday evening. In addition,
there were visits to the sick, marriages, funerals, and so on. One thing gave
me great pleasure and the strength to go on. That was the fact that the divine
services were always well attended. One could feel that these people did not
attend from habit, but that they wanted to hear the word of God.

Eureka was the end of the railroad at that time -- the only terminal station in
northern South Dakota and southern North Dakota. Practically all immigrants
coming into this territory came through Eureka. I remember one Sunday as I
stepped into the church that it was crowded, yet to my surprise most of the
people were strangers to me. A few days before, an immigrant train had arrived
in Eureka. The people, for the most part, came from South Russia. Many of them
had friends and relatives here. Thankful for their safe arrival, they all came
to church to hear the word of God. I did not use the sermon I had planned for
that Sunday; instead of that, I thanked and praised the Lord for the safe
journey of the people. In closing I reminded my listeners that God was with
them in this new land; that they must be faithful; to acknowledge with Joshua
that "I and my house will serve the Lord." I felt that my words, coming from my
heart, went straight to their hearts.

Eureka, as the terminal of the railroad, did much in the religious field.
Through Eureka those people who went on, some of them as far as Canada, kept in
touch with their church. Later the church followed them.

Eureka was the biggest wheat-shipping center in America then. Farmers came from
as far away as 70 miles to bring in their products. They had to travel with
wagons and sleds. During the early years, oxen were frequently used. The
elevators and other business places in Eureka were often open day and night to
accommodate the people who arrived at all hours. Do you ask, why didn't Eureka
grow into a big city? The answer is, "Terminal station."

Generally, after the farmers had attended to their business, they came to the
parsonage, often in groups. Sometimes they wanted some church service. At other
times they wanted to start another congregation. I was often called upon to
settle arguments. Until late into the night these negotiations lasted. My dear
wife suffered much. Coming from Illinois, she was very homesick. She used to
lie awake and listen to the arguments and negotiations for hours, unable to
sleep. She bore everything without a complaint. It all helped the growth of the
church, and the Kingdom of God. How often we now sit during the twilight hour
and remember those days! How often we say, "Do you remember this?" "Do you
remember that?" But that is a sign of old age. Youth lives in the future, middle
age lives in the present, but old age lives in the past -- in remembrance.

In the meantime, three years had passed. The work had become too much for me. I
became sick, but, with God's help, I recovered. The doctor, however, advised me
to seek a milder climate, and to rest from my work for a time. I called a
meeting of the congregation and laid my troubles before them. But they refused
to let me go. Instead, they said they would give me a long vacation, and get
somebody to assist me. With tears in my eyes I left the meeting -- and stayed
in Eureka. For a year a student-minister assisted me. At the end of that time
the parish was divided.

The darkest hours of our life in Dakota followed. A terrible diphtheria
epidemic broke out, covering a territory of at least 30 miles. Hundreds of
children died. The anti-toxin for diphtheria was not generally used at this
time. But in the end this anti-toxin stopped the epidemic. Many people did not
believe that the disease was contagious. They visited the sick, took their
children along, and as a result the disease kept on spreading from farm to farm.
Dr. Gerdes, the pioneer doctor in this territory, did more than it seems
possible for one man to do. I worked with him. I did all he told me to do to
guard against contagion. I received permission from the Board of Health, of
which he was president, to visit the sick and bury the dead. Two or three
funerals in one day were not unusual. In about two months I had forty funerals.

I had a confirmation class of about 20 children in the house, and was always in
fear that they, too, would get sick. But the Lord be thanked, none of my
household nor any of those children caught the disease.

But our darkest hours did not come until the next year. My wife became sick.
For ten months she could not leave her bed. Twice she had to go to Minneapolis
to a hospital. Just at this time, God took our beloved little boy, our only
child, from us. On the 10th of February, 1899, with the thermometer at 42
degrees below zero, we buried him. On the way back from the cemetery I froze my
face, ears, and hands. I had to be rubbed with snow to get my blood circulating
again. The people were very kind to us, and helped us to bear our burden. Even
today, in our thoughts, we silently press the hands of those who were so good to
us then.

Thinking of Eureka brings back many memories, both joyful and sorrowful. How my
thoughts wander! I remember the first funeral service I conducted. A boy, about
14 years of age, had fallen from a haywagon, which passed over him and killed
him. The family was just building a new house -- I believe it was the first
frame building in that part of the county. During the sermon I stood in the
doorway between two rooms. I believe that the house never again saw as many
people in it as on that day. Blizzards, too, often made things rather
unpleasant for us. From many such instances, I will mention one. During a
blizzard I was called to conduct a funeral. Luckily, we drove with the wind,
and arrived at the place where the funeral was to be held without any trouble,
although the blizzard was continuously growing worse. A number of neighbors
were already there. The funeral services were held in the house. It was
impossible to even think of having any service outside. Three or four men
carried the small coffin to the grave, which had been covered with boards to
keep the snow out. After the services we were all forced to stay in one room
for more than 30 hours -- there were about 20 of us. Not until late the next day
could any of the neighbors leave for their homes. There were humorous
incidents, too. Once I was to perform a marriage ceremony. I had traveled five
or six miles toward the place where the ceremony was to take place, when I saw
a rider coming in my direction. The horse was bedecked with ribbons, so I knew
the rider was one of the wedding party. I thought he had been sent to meet me,
as was done at big weddings in those days. He stopped, and instead of turning
around and accompanying me, told me that I could turn back. The groom had
changed his mind! Here we have the opposite of the royal wedding feast. There
the guests refused to come. Here the guests came, but the groom was unwilling.

Another instance was similar, but in this instance I was already in the house
when the bride-to-be refused to go through with the ceremony. When somebody
asked me what I thought of it, I said, "Rather now, than after the wedding."
Many marriages take place in too much of a hurry.

The tide of immigrants continued. Many of them settled here for a while, then,
either because they did not like the country, or because they had lost all they
had, moved on into North Dakota and Canada. Later I made mission-inspection
trips into North Dakota, and met whole congregations that had belonged to the
congregations in and near Eureka, and had known me there. I say again: Eureka
was for years the center of the Dakota missions, and as the terminal station
did much to carry on the work of the church.

I often said, and I say again, that Eureka was the important business center
for a large territory. Farmers for miles around brought all their products to
Eureka to sell, and bought the things they needed there. At that time Eureka
had many business places of all kinds, and very few homes. The homes were all
small and poorly built, as though they were intended for temporary use only.

There were seven churches in Eureka then, and it often seemed as if there were
not enough. I always felt that everybody should have the opportunity to believe
what he wanted to believe.

The people seemed to feel that they were not building on a firm foundation. Men
asked, "How long will things stay this way?" Everybody realized that Eureka
would not always be the terminal station; that the railroad would some day go
on; that new towns would spring up farther on. What would happen to Eureka
then? Everything was temporary. Nobody wanted to make things permanent. Who
knew how long they would last? Time would tell.

Often, when the train did not come for days because of a blizzard, we would sit
around the warm stove in one of the stores and build railroads. From Aberdeen
to Leola, to Eureka, and to Mound City. From Eureka to Bismarck, or from Leola
to Ashley. All dreams, of course, but many of them were to come true. First, a
railroad was built from Bowdle west to the river, then from Eureka north to
Bismarck; from Aberdeen to Leola, and from Ashley west to the river. Eureka
ceased to be the terminal station. Overnight a migration began. People left
Eureka to move to some other town. Furniture, houses, and even stores were
moved. New towns sprung up along the line; Greenway, Zeeland, Hague, Strasburg,
and Linton on the Milwaukee road; Venturia, Artas, Herreid, and Pollock on the
Soo Line, Java and Evarts west of Bowdle. Many business firms moved from Eureka
to the new towns. This, however, did not harm Eureka. For the first time the
people had a chance to build permanently. Several business firms combined, and
the Eureka Bazaar was built. Soon other buildings were being erected, not
false-fronted frame buildings, but substantial buildings of stone and brick.
The old buildings were renovated. Soon a telephone system was installed,
electric lights and running water followed.

Eureka began to look like a city. New, fine dwelling houses were erected.
Eureka began to grow. No longer was it the temporary stopping place for
thousands of people, but a permanent home for those who remained. The healthy
growth of Eureka showed itself in the school system. A large, new, modern brick
building was erected. A two-year high school course was added to the elementary
grades. The Lutheran College, too, was located in Eureka. I was indeed sorry to
hear that this school was closed during the recent depression. It hurt me that
this small child, the Eureka Lutheran College, never had a chance to grow up
into manhood.

Many years have passed since we left Eureka. The boys and girls I knew then
have grown up into men and women. A new generation has grown up. The reins are
now in their hands. I am sure they are good hands. They are of the same sturdy
stock as the pioneers who once migrated to South Russia, and there built their
homes, churches, and schools; who broke up the ground, worked it, and forced it
to give up crops. Here, too, they have built their homes, churches, and
schools. It was all hard work, of course, but they did it. They make their own
living, many of them through their farms. I do not fear for the future of
Dakota and of Eureka, despite the poor crops and the long years of the
depression. I believe in the present generation, and in the generation that is
now growing up.

And now: May that spirit of the past and the present continue to he the same in
the future, so that the generation that will celebrate the 100th anniversary
can and will look backward to their ancestors with the same pride as you do.

With the best wishes to all of you, dear people of Eureka, and for your Golden
Jubilee.

                                        Sincerely yours,
                                        Henry and Rosa Reinhardt.
