A HUNTER'S PARADISE

During the time when Eureka was the world's greatest wheat shipping terminus,
there also existed in this territory a haven for the hunters. Pictured above
are a few of Eureka's sharpshooters in the year 1894. Included in the group
are: Dan Nickisch, Steve Fisher, Fred Frankhauser, Sr., Jake Schall, Frank
Blessner and John A. Smith. This day's hunt totaled 351 ducks, 28 chickens and
4 snipes.

THE GERMAN PILGRIMAGE TO RUSSIA AND THE UNITED STATES

The people in and around the city of Eureka, South Dakota, are commonly
referred to as "Russians" by the uninformed in face of the fact, that while
they were perforce able to speak and understand the Russian language, they were
Germans, having maintained their racial purity throughout the generations that
had sprung up since their forefathers settled on Russian soil. True, they came
from Russia to South Dakota, but their native language was German. Even to the
present day they have maintained that language and customs and traditions that
identify them as Germans.

As Germans, also, lived their ancestors in South Russia, the Ukraine, Kriem
peninsula and Bessarabia through more than three generations before political
and social dissatisfaction -- but mainly the hunger for land, innate to the
German race -- induced many to leave the land their forefathers had adopted as
their home.

They had been invited to this land by its ruler, Czar Alexander I, who, toward
the end of the 18th century and the beginning of the 19th, granted a number of
privileges to induce prospective immigrants from Germany to settle the Steppes
of South Russia. It may be explained that Czarina Catherine II, a former German
princess, had earlier, in 1765-76, issued a similar invitation to her former
countrymen. This invitation had been accepted by a group rather less adapted to
colonize a new land, though they did settle on the Volga River. Most historians
agree this venture was one of only indifferent success.

The privileges Alexander offered were mainly religious freedom, tax exemption
for 10 years, exemption from military service, and to each family was allotted a
portion of "Crown land," in the average about 100 acres, for his use. Only
families with children were admitted and the colonist must have 300 gulden (a
gulden was equal to 40 cents) in money or property. He was required to be
either a farmer, or adept in some trade, and of good standing in his community.

Thus, these German colonists in South Russia, who came largely from
Wuertemberg, entered their new home by no means as paupers, but brought with
them a civilization of higher standard than was prevalent. The typical colonist
built up, in not many years, farms and communities that served as models to
settlers from other lands and to the natives of the region. In this way, they
fulfilled the hope of Alexander I, who had called them for that purpose.
Besides settling those "Crown lands" which an easy-going autocracy failed to
develop to the full extent of the possibilities that lay hidden in the fertile
soil of the Steppes.

The German colonist was equal to the task. In three generations he built an
empire by hard work, thrift and tenacity of purpose. He clung to the traditions
of the home land and the inborn respect for law and order. He also clung to his
German language. That was used exclusively in home, church and school. Vastly
changed living conditions and the desolation of the far-flung Russian Steppes,
no doubt, provoked a fierce homesickness for the wooded hills of his home land.
But he carried on.

In spite of the many adversities, inherent to the development of a new country,
and bearing up under the disappointments that are a matter of course with the
farmer-settler of a strange soil in a new climate, the German peasant, who had
come to subdue the wild Steppes, won out. Even his government, in which he
enjoyed the greatest autonomy for many years. A peasant, he had, in less than a
lifetime, become a land holder -- maker of his own laws.

During the second generation, that is, the first one born on Russian soil, a
great expansion occurred beyond the land allotted to the original colony. Many
went out to settle on land belonging to the landed nobility. Renters first,
they soon became owners, so that the third generation was spread in countless
farmsteads, villages and colonies all over the plains of South Russia from
Caucasus Mountains far into Bessarabia. For well nigh 100 years, this work of
peaceful conquests had been going on, and in these years many changes had taken
place.

Land prices had gone up with the development of the Steppe. Farms grew smaller
while population increased. The number of land-less increased, and too, the
number of the poor, as conditions of existence grew more difficult. An element
of dissatisfaction and unrest made itself felt more and more in the hearts of
many. This was aggravated by the attitude of the Russian government toward the
colonist. In 1874, compulsory military service in Russia, including the
colonists, became law and with it they lost the one cherished privilege -- that
had been granted to their forefathers.

This law, and the never ceasing desire to own land, started toward the end of
the 19th century a great immigration from Southern Russia. The colonists became
colonizer. Well toward a million souls poured out through the harbors of Europe
to find new homelands -- across the seas -- mostly in the two Americas. One
group, roughly speaking, from the neighborhood of Odessa, found its way to the
Great Plains, the Dakotas, where besides other locations the greatest number
made their home in the territory around the present city of Eureka, in South
Dakota.

Here on the fertile, but untamed, prairies they did what their forefathers did
on the Steppes of Southern Russia -- by hard word, thrift and tenacity of
purpose, carried them victoriously through all hardships and privations. They
built up one of the best farming communities on the plains, one that withstood
the onslaught of drouths and depression as well as any and better than most.
Farms, schools and churches throughout the Eureka territory and the towns
around it, give testimony that the seed those pioneers sowed bore good fruit,
and ample assurance is given that the spirit of the pioneers is alive in the
coming generation, who clearly see their duty to continue the work their fathers
have begun. Most encouraging for the future is that the new generation, while
preserving as far as possible the best of the traditions of their forebears, is
accepting American ideals of government, culture and social philosophy. Their
contribution to future progress of our great land may, as a result, be expected
to add new and enduring values. Das Walte Gott!         --Walter Schoenbrod.

        Arrival of Seven Coaches of Russian Emigrants -- November 13, 1892

THE MILWAUKEE ROAD

The railroad, a many-fingered explorer and opener of the untracked areas of the
world, brought life blood to this territory It brought that eager army of
immigrants, which in turn, helped reap the golden harvest from an ever-rich
soil. It brought the means of progress and took to the markets of the world
that which we produced. The Milwaukee road pioneered in the building of Dakota
Territory in like fashion with those few hardy pioneers traversing empty
prairie stretches by ox team or afoot. Without the Milwaukee, Eureka would not
have enjoyed 15 years of unparalleled prosperity; without the road, Eureka would
not be a key city between Aberdeen and Bismarck.

Mr. F. H. Johnson, a Milwaukee road executive assistant, personally forwards
this information concerning the history of railroad building in this territory:

"In 1880 the C. M. & St. P. Railway Company located the Hastings and Dakota
Division of its railroad from Ortonville, on the state line of Minnesota, in a
westerly direction to Aberdeen, and also located its Whetstone Branch, running
in a northwesterly direction from Milbank into the Wahpeton and Sisseton
Reservation, and during the years 1880-81 completed the construction of the
same, opening its station at Aberdeen on July 14, 1881. This line was
subsequently continued in a westerly direction by Roscoe to Bowdle, and
completed to that point in September, 1886. The line from Orient to Eureka was
built in 1887, and stations opened at those points in June and July of that
year."

So it was in that eventful year of 1884 that the C. M. & St. P. Railway Company
extended the James River division of their system north and south of Roscoe
with the apparent object in view of eventually connecting the two capital
cities, Pierre to the southwest and Bismarck to the northwest. In June of that
year, grading was discontinued eight miles north of Hillsview. It was generally
understood that a terminal station would be established near the end of the
grade. This location was at first known as just "the end of the line."

In the latter part of July, 1887, the first regular time card was issued for
the new extension. No name had yet been given the new terminal station, but was
merely designated on the card as "end of track." This it remained until the
city was platted and named Eureka three months later. Imagine the excitement of
the isolated settlers in this "end of the track" region when the first railroad
train whistle heralded the entrance of better times on July 27, 1887. This
first regular train north of Roscoe was in charge of Conductor John Green and
pulled by engine No. 14, piloted by Engineer Jasper Richardson. On the same day,
Auditor J. N. Button checked in J. W. Guhin as agent of the local station. A
month later, August 25, 1887, the Western Union Telegraph line was completed to
Eureka and wire communication with the outside world was established.

The first great step of settlement was completed. What followed is known
history. In the spring and summer of 1888, trainloads of immigrants poured in
from Iowa, Illinois, Minnesota, and southern South Dakota. Special trains of
from 20 to 30 cars of immigrant movables were of common occurrence. In addition
to this local movement, many extra coaches were needed to transport the
immigration coming direct from Russia and Germany. It was the Milwaukee road
that brought these workers in and took back to the world that which enabled
them and others to live -- the wheat.

During the $2,000,000 wheat shipping year of 1892, it was estimated that over
165 trains of 20 cars each were necessary to bring to markets the wheat that
made Eureka the world's greatest primary wheat market.

It was, also, during this same year in the months of October and November that
the Eureka station surpassed the earnings of any other station on the entire
Milwaukee system by reaching $100,000 per month.

Hand in hand, the Milwaukee road has prospered with the community. It has
always been, that, without one, the other cannot survive. The road has helped
build communities, aided in the support of the public institutions, and, just
plainly, furthered progress. During the peak of the drouth years, 1933-34, the
railroads of South Dakota refunded $8,000,000 to the farmers.

It is interesting to mention that the local station is the original one built
in 1887. The careful upkeep of the structure has camouflaged its age.

The station agents who have all done good service for the road are listed as
follows: J. W. Guhin, 1887-1896; John Rathman, 1896-1901; Amos Lott, 1901-1904;
E. J. Scott. 1904-1920; Art Bjork, 1920 to present. Mr. Bjork, the present
agent for the railroad, has the longest record of service in Eureka. He is,
also, an active man in the civic interests of Eureka. He is a member of the
Lake Park Committee, Legion, and a Mason.

The section foremen responsible for the upkeep of the road have been: James
Mundy, 1887-; Johann Schmitt, -1893; J. J. Johnson, 1893-1896; Chas. Becker,
1896-1903; Carl Stein, 1903-1905; Jacob Teske, 1905-1936; Theo. Teske, 1936 to
present date. Jacob Teske, the section foreman, holds the record of 31
continuous years of service in the Eureka division. He retired last year after
a severe illness befell him while in active service. His record stands
unblemished as no major accident occurred to himself or men under his
supervision in those many years. Mr. Teske has four sons employed by the
railroad, namely, Jacob, Jr., foreman at Java, South Dakota; Theo., foreman at
Eureka, succeeding his father; Edward and Arthur, Eureka. The writer will take
this opportunity to pay tribute to the late Freight and Passenger Division
Agent O. F. Waller, who gave much to the Eureka railroad. He passed away in the
year 1936. He is succeeded by R. A. Burns of Aberdeen, South Dakota. H. M.
Gillick, Aberdeen, South Dakota, Division Superintendent, has also aided much in
the betterment of railroad service in the Eureka territory.

THE INDIAN SCARE IN THE LATE FALL OF 1890 By Henry Bieber

One morning just before sunrise, a wagon drove up to our home, informing us
"that the Indians had broken out," and advised us to rush to town at once "as
they may be upon us before long." Then the excited man drove on. I was not
quite 12 years old, and dad had intended to clean flax in a warehouse at Eureka
that day. We received the weeds (wild buckwheat) for our labor, as crops had
been poor that year.

After a short talk, dad decided we would drive in to Eureka and see. If there
was danger we would come back and get the others, otherwise we would clean
flax. Just as we had hitched up, our neighbor came over, a tall,
broad-shouldered heavy muscular man, muzzle-loading gun in arm, pipe in mouth.
He came over to notify us of the outbreak and to get dad's idea about it.
Learning of our intention, he calmly pulled out his tobacco pouch and stuffed
his pipe with the remark, "Ah, there's somebody whose heart has not dropped
through his shoes. I thought the soup would not be eaten as hot as it was
cooked." We climbed into the wagon, and he turned to shuffle it back in his No.
13 boots, his pipe well on the go.

On reaching town, we found it full of teams and wagons, the latter piled high
with hay and bedding, some women and children on them, as some had come almost
in the middle of the night. The sidewalks and part of the streets were full of
people. There was confusion almost everywhere Some wailed, "They'll come and
massacre us all." A well set middle-aged man drew my attention, evidently a
veteran of the Russo-Turkish war, which was fought 13 years before. He had
plans for defense. "Let us gather all able-bodied men in companies," he said,
"distribute guns and ammunition among them. We'll use the wagons for breast
works, and fight them." He talked loud and sonorously, but his idea had little
support.

"We'll wire the government for militia," some old timers from the southeastern
part of the state said. And wiring they tried, but lo, there seemed to be
something wrong. No answer came. Were the wires cut? Was the garrison at Fort
Yates, 400 or 600 men, massacred as had been, 14 years before, General Custer
with his gallant seventh cavalry? But some claimed the operator was too drunk to
wire. Some thought the whole thing was bunk.

However, I had little time to hear the various opinions, as I soon found myself
turning the crank on the fanning mill in the warehouse.

Early in the afternoon mother and the others came in, except brother John.
Another party had driven up, claiming they had a message to send all people to
town, probably for better protection. John, however, stayed, keeping the two
best horses and the single-barrel muzzle-loading shotgun. Thinking if Indians
did come, he would make his getaway. With two fresh horses he could change
mounts, while they had but one mount. And should one come too close, he still
had the old gun.

Early in the afternoon or so, the mist at the depot had cleared away, and
messages could be sent and received. Both Bismarck and Fort Yates knew nothing
of an Indian uprising. An hour or so later there occurred an exodus, wagon after
wagon rumbled out of town heading homeward. It was decided that I should go
with a relative about 13 miles to the northwest. We started in middle afternoon.
There were three or four wagons in the train, neighbors. Earlier in the
forenoon six young men on good horses had ventured out to the northwest to
reconnoitre. I remember, as two of them rode up the street, nosing their horses
clear up to the sidewalk several times, taking farewell of some friend or
relative, as there was no knowing lest they fall in the hands of the crafty
Indian. This accomplished, they rode off with their comrades to the may-be
Indian infested northwest. They carried no packages or guns that I could see,
but certainly revolvers in their pockets, and as it appeared later, a goodly
supply of fire-water. There was quite a feeling of safety as they rode out, a
dauntless, stalwart bunch.

Coming up a little hill about three-fourths of a mile outside town on our
homeward journey, there a short distance ahead came those six gallant men,
happily singing. On meeting us they assured us in chorus that everything was
safe. One even turned his horse, rode several rods back with us to assure us of
our safety. Then he turned and joined his happy comrades, as men are happy when
they have accomplished a great deed.

Their news and happiness spread over to us, and in a high and cheerful spirit
we drove on, until we came to the home of Mr. Schmidt (the
Eureka-LaGrace-Winona road led through his yard) when somehow we all stopped.
Mr. Schmidt went in the house and quickly coming out, asked the others to come
in and look. There opposite the door. on the rear wall of the kitchen, hung a
large sized dishpan, with several bullet holes in it. Close around it and at
some distance from it were a lot of bullet holes in the plastered wall, with
about an even amount of empty revolver shells on the floor. On the cook stove
was the frying pan with some "wurst" (sausage) in it, remnants of a meal. The
coffee pot also was in evidence. Then it dawned on us why those men were so
extremely happy and their fine horses so remarkably fresh. They had come that
far, and there had held a frolic and then returned to town. Right there our
high and cheerful spirits sank to naught. And more, what if those telegrams
were false? What if the operator had just written them out to satisfy the
people? It was plain, that we were in great danger of getting killed that night.
Mr. Schmidt, realizing the gloomy prospect, invited the others to spend the
night with them, which invitation was quickly accepted. The horses were cared
for, while the women started the cook stove. After supper, as it was dark by
that time, guards were put out. After a while, another lad or two, and I, were
put on guard. We were to keep our eyes and ears open, especially to the west and
northwest for any approaching Indians riding or crawling. Next we were told to
keep an eye on the horizon in that direction for any sign of fire, as it was
thought when Indians go out on a massacre they also would set fire. Last, but
not least, watch for the barking of dogs -- and we watched the dogs.

Having received our instructions we retired to the north of the house. Turning
our heads now and then to the left to see about crawling Indians, we mostly
relied on our ears and the dogs. And if dogs ever had a concert, they certainly
had one that night. There were four or five sets of them, from the northwest
clear around to the southeast. It was a still clear night, and as we were farm
lads, and well acquainted with the nature of that faithful servant of men, we
knew that no dog would join or continue an idle concert while there was an
enemy near his master's place.

It must have been full moon or nearly so, as the moon was well up and quite to
the east. We were sitting there in the full moonlight, realizing that an Indian
peeping over the low ridge to the west and northwest could easily see us, while
we could not see him, so we moved our seating apparatus to the west or gable
end of the house, as we were by no means to be outdone by an Indian. We had
bettered our stand quite a bit, as we had our field of observance in plain view
before us, and didn't have to turn our heads every time we wanted to take a
look.

Just then it was that I almost received a shock. Sitting quietly, shaded from
the light of the moon, talking a little now and then and listening to the dogs
in the distance, I had completely forgotten our own dogs, when suddenly with a
coarse, powerful voice they fell in as leaders, the others following. The
closeness and quick, powerful racket almost sent a stunning shock through me.
We informed them of their impoliteness and obediently they moved over near the
barn to fall in again a little later, though somewhat more polite at the start,
but we let them have their way.

We had no guns while on guard, but were supposed to report anything unusual.
One party had bought a single-barrel breechloader that day. He had five or six
shells, as they were practically out. There was besides that, one double and
one single barrel muzzleloader in our party. As we reported nothing, one of our
superiors would be around now and then to see whether we are massacred already
or still wore our hair. I do not remember how long we were on duty, neither
where, or how I slept. About sunrise my superior rolled me out to finish our
journey. A new day ahead of us, and still wearing our hair, we began to think
that those Indians were not so bad after all, and those telegrams were most
likely original. Reaching the farm, everything seemed all right. There were no
fences at that time, and with the family gone, the cattle (not many in number
that time) had free access to the haystack. But it didn't seem to have hurt
them any, except they seemed a little dry that morning, as they had no water
the day before. There were no big water tanks then. The next few nights there
was a little uneasiness, but we soon realized that there was nothing to the
report. Some thought a Yankee had sprung a dirty joke on "those green Russians."

I do not remember whether it ever was found out how that scare started. One
story was that a couple of whites were on the Missouri, and seeing a band of
Indians dance and act queerly while coming toward the river, believed they were
on the warpath.

The younger generation might think the pioneer people were cowards, but I think
they miss their target as much as those six sharpshooters did that dishpan. If
they consider that many of them had come to this country in the fall, and had
stayed the first winter in the southeast part of the territory, which was then
well settled mostly by their countrymen, and heard the stories on those long
winter evenings of that horrible massacre of whites by the Indians 10 years
before over in Minnesota, they, too, might have been uneasy. Moreover, people
there, especially the hardware dealers, told them how dangerously close they
would come to those Sioux Indians that had wiped out General Custer and his
men, when they went out in the spring, to their northwest home. The hardware
dealers sold many a gun to the greenhorns on the strength of those stories, that
they would not have sold otherwise.

It was also known among the settlers that fall, that the Indians were restless,
with their ghost dance and Messiah craze. Even old timers on the river and men
knowing Indian nature gathered in small bodies at some good loghouse for a
number of nights, with guards out. Only a short time later, the government
officers at Fort Yates deemed it their duty to step in, and they ordered Sitting
Bull's arrest, who was thought to be the main figure in that unrest. We all
know how sad that undertaking ended, and it was especially sad, as we know now,
that neither Sitting Bull nor his followers or any other Indian, had any
intention of crossing the river and molesting the settlers. Fear and distrust
have caused many a sad occurrence among mankind, of which the latter was one.
If left alone it is with certainty that the whole agitation would have spent
itself harmlessly.

But, who knew?

THE EUREKA HOME EXTENSION CLUB

The Home Extension Club of Eureka was organized July 30, 1935, and was given
the name "The Rainbow Home Extension Club." The membership is as follows:
Mesdames Henry Bitz, vice president; August Harr, Theo. Isaak, secretary;
Gideon Klein, president; Paul Kretschmar, treasurer; Eugene Liedle, Russell
Nelson, Edwin Neuharth, Edwin Serr, Werner Straub, Otto Wenzel and Rheinie
Werre.

As a matter of historic interest this short statement of their objective and
creed is appended:

Our aim: Well informed, progressive, prosperous, efficient, happy families.

Our creed: To maintain the highest ideals of home life; to count children the
most important of crops; to so mother them that their bodies may be sound,
their minds clear, their spirits happy, and their characters generous;

To place service above comfort; to let loyalty to high purposes silence
discordance notes; to let neighborliness supplant hatreds; to be discouraged
never;

To lose self in generous enthusiasms; to extend to the less fortunate a helping
hand; to believe one's community may become the best of communities; and to
cooperate with others for the common ends of a more abundant home and community
life.

This is the offer of the Home Bureau to the homemaker of today.
                        -- Ruby Green Smith.

        The Day Eureka Boasted of 42 Grain Houses.

WHEN WHEAT WAS KING

A story on another page of this volume relates the principal facts of the days
when Eureka was the greatest primary wheat market of the world. The handling of
that flood of wheat was something of a problem with the equipment then in use.
At the present time with modern elevators, dump scales and grain hauled in
tight grain boxes, the labor of loading grain into railroad cars has been
reduced to a minimum.

But when Eureka was the wheat capital, it was in small flat houses the grain
was stored until it could be loaded in railroad cars. With more than 30 of
these markets doing business, few of them were located at the tracks. Farmers
hauled the grain in sacks and it was wheeled on hand trucks into the flat
house, the sacks dumped and returned to the farmer. Then there was the job of
hauling it from the house to railroad cars. All of it had to be shoveled by
hand. With a volume of grain, counted in hundreds of thousands of bushels, it
does not require much imagination to realize the tremendous amount of manual
labor involved. At the busiest seasons, several hundred men were required to
load out grain and assist in the unloading at the house. The work proceeded
night and day, the men working in shifts. One special wheat train after another
steamed out of Eureka with capacity load on the way to the mills to be ground
into flour or perhaps exported to foreign lands. Compared to that era, the
present day volume of wheat, even in a good year, would seem insignificant, but
with modern equipment a few dozen men could handle the golden stream with but
little effort.

LOST IN A BLIZZARD

Mrs. Henry Pietz of Eureka, relates the following gripping story of her mother,
Mrs. J. J. Harr, whose experience in a blizzard has often been told in after
years. The incident occurred 44 years ago. Mrs. Pietz's story follows:

It was on a bright warm day in winter, late in the month of February, 1893,
when Mrs. Harr had finished her family washing and hung it on the clothesline
about 100 yards from the house. A sudden snow storm came up and the wind had
whipped one of the sheets from the line and Mrs. Harr went outside to pick it
up. The storm gained such force that she was unable to see the house and began
to walk in search of it.

She was dressed in a house dress, wore bedroom slippers, and had a shawl thrown
over her shoulders.

Mrs. Harr walked blindly, all the while realizing that she was utterly lost,
but also remembering the blizzard of 1888, when people had frozen because they
had stopped to rest, so she kept on her feet, weary and exhausted. Her mind was
constantly on the five small children who were left at the farm house with no
one to care for them but the oldest sister, Emelia, who was 12 years old. She
prayed that she might be returned to them.

Meanwhile, the anxious little brood was growing more alarmed at the delay in
their mother's return and one of the older boys begged to go out and look for
her. The elder sister was firm in her command that they all stay inside, even
though she too knew that her mother was lost. The mother trudged through the
snowstorm hour after hour, occasionally stopping for a minute, throwing the
shawl over her head and extending it out beyond her face to shield her from the
wind and to aid her in catching her breath. Suddenly, she heard a train
whistle, and began stumbling toward the welcome sound. She knew if she could
reach the track, she would reach either Hillsview or Eureka. It was with a sigh
of relief that she found the tracks as dusk was fast approaching. Following the
track, Mrs. Harr soon saw the outlines of the Frankhauser slaughter house, now
belonging to the Dierenfeldt Market, and found shelter and rest there until she
felt able to continue on home. She was able to find a fence that she knew would
lead her to the gate of the Harr homestead which was then located where the
Mehlhaff slaughter house now stands.

The joy of her family in seeing their mother return was almost indescribable.
Their father was employed in town by Martin Schamber, Sr., and was not aware of
the tragedy that had threatened his wife until he arrived home that evening and
all were safe.

Mrs. Harr was confined to her bed for two or three weeks and suffered the loss
of her speaking voice for several months following the ordeal. Five weeks after
the time of the frightful experience her daughter, Martha, now Mrs. Phil
Tempel, of Marion, South Dakota, was born.
