ADVERTISING IN 1914

Back as early as 1914 Eureka had its musical organizations. At the time this
picture was taken this group had been doing a bit of advertising for the
Firemen's Eighteenth Annual Ball. Reading from left to right are: Emil
Schenkenberger, Otto Isaak, Francis Wenger, Maude Gerdes, Heinie Gerdes, Ella
Schamber, Edwin Schenkenberger, Clair Warner, and the last in line is unknown.

THE GRASSHOPPER PLAGUE

Eighteen eighty-nine, the year of statehood, was probably the hardest year in
the history of South Dakota, not excepting the great grasshopper year of 1875.
With that year began the period of reverses which cut our population, destroyed
our credit and for a time made Dakota a name of ill repute. Several causes
contributed to this situation. A great drouth came upon the land, practically
destroying the crop. In some localities, the people who were depending solely
upon the wheat crop, were left absolutely destitute and dependent upon public
charity. They were beginners in a new land, had not yet accumulated a surplus,
and on the reverse were as a rule deeply in debt. Many were forced to mortgage
their personal property and their homesteads. Their sole dependence was the
crop, and when that failed their situation was truly pathetic. Thousands
abandoned their homesteads, surrendered their mortgaged chattels, and left the
state, carrying with them a tale of woe which was disseminated throughout the
country. The opinion everywhere was that Dakota was a failure. Governor
Mellette finally went to Chicago and other eastern points and appealed for
assistance for the many destitute. The response was generous and all actual
suffering was averted.

        Eureka Wool Market -- 1897 53,000 pounds were purchased in single day
        by local merchants.

CIVIC ORGANIZATION

Some of the earliest business men recall that as early as 1900 there existed an
active civic organization in the city of Eureka. The exact date of the
beginning of this group cannot be found and it appears that the club was
discontinued for lack of support. Informants state that in 1914 a commercial
club was organized, but no definite information remains.

In June, 1920, an attempt to organize was made for the third time. A group of
lenders assembled in the city council rooms to elect a temporary chairman,
Christian Vorlander. On July 12 of this year, he called a meeting of the
businessmen for the purpose of organizing. The name Eureka Commercial Club was
adopted. Nine directors were elected at this meeting of whom Carl Vorlander,
Otto H. Froh and A. F. Isaak were elected for a term of one year; Fred
Hepperle, Phil. Schamber and Karl G. Kafer for two years; Christian Vorlander,
Fred G. Wenger and H. O. Hepperle for three years. Immediately following this
meeting the directors met to elect officers which resulted as follows:
Christian Vorlander, president; Philip Schamber, vice president; H. O.
Hepperle, secretary; and Carl Vorlander, treasurer. Each of the five remaining
directors were made chairmen of the following respective committees: Highways,
Railroads, Entertainment, Internal Affairs, and Miscellaneous; Karl G. Kafer,
Fred Hepperle. Fred G. Wenger, Otto H. Froh and A. F. Isaak.

This club was very active from then on and can take credit for many
accomplishments within our city. One of the many movements that it assisted in
putting across was that of promoting the building of the new hospital, which
credit can almost be wholly given to the Eureka Commercial Club for repeatedly
bringing it up at meetings until so well established that stock was subscribed
and the hospital association organized.

Records show that the support of this club was good until about 1930, when the
attendance apparently was not as regular as it should have been. However,
meetings were held regularly until the meeting in June, 1931, at which time it
was voted to discontinue the Commercial Club and organize a club that
affiliated with a state or national organization. It was believed that more
interest could be created if about once a year a representative from the
headquarters would meet with the local members.

On June 19th, a state representative of the Lions International arrived in
Eureka and met with a group of business men for the purpose of organizing. The
Eureka Lions Club was organized on that date and the following officers were
elected: Dr. E. E. Stephens, president; Theo. Straub, first vice president; F.
K. Mehlhaff, second vice president; William Blair, secretary-treasurer; H. B.
Wolff, lion tamer; and R. H. Isaak, tail twister. There were 28 charter
members. This club continued its function until June 17, 1935.

At this same meeting on June 17, 1935, the members of the Eureka Lions Club
voted to organize a new club. It was at this time that the Eureka Civic Club
came into being with the following officers and directors: Emanuel Heilman,
president; Theo. Straub, vice president; A. W Schumacher, secretary-treasurer;
Henry Ottmar and Walter Schonbrod, directors. This club has operated
successfully and has grown to nearly 50 members. It has as its present officers
and directors: Gideon Klein, president; J. H. Schatz, vice president; A. W.
Schumacher, secretary-treasurer; Edward R. Isaak and George J. Kelle. Jr.,
directors.

BUSINESS HISTORIES

EUREKA EQUITY EXCHANCE

In June, 1915, a small group of farmers decided to form a farmers'
organization, where the farmers could buy and sell their own products. A board
of five directors was elected to go on with the building of a Farmers' Elevator.
Those elected were: Jacob Mehlhaff, president; John Hoff, Jr., secretary; and
Adam Weller, John J. Staebler, and Alex Hamilton. This board drafted a set of
by-laws, and obtained a charter and articles of incorporation necessary to own
and operate a business of this type.

On August 13, 1915, the contract for the erection of a 20,000 bushel cribbed
and iron clad elevator was awarded to D. F. Hoage & Co., of Minneapolis, Minn.,
for the sum of $5,500.00.

On October 1, 1915, the board of directors accepted the newly constructed
elevator. They were then ready to receive grain and serve the members of the
organization. John Ottenbacher was engaged to manage the business. He served in
that capacity for six years. Fred J. Herr, of Tripp, South Dakota, served two
years, and A. M. Klein one year. Since July, 1924, Henry Ottmar of Eureka, has
been the manager.

New equipment has been added from time to time. Coal sheds and a flour
warehouse were built. The elevator has always been kept up-to-date.

In 1935, the organization was changed to a cooperative corporation. This
increased the membership materially and reduced the overhead expense. The
Eureka Equity Exchange has more than 250 members at the present time, and has
paid over $200,000.00 in dividends since 1915.

In October, 1936, the stockholders decided to add a bulk and service gas and
oil station. The bulk and service stations of the Sterling Oil Company were
purchased, and the Exchange took possession of their new property on January
15, 1937.

The present directors include Andrew Kallenberger, president; J. B. Wittmayer,
secretary-treasurer; Ludwig Schumacher, John E. Ottenbacher, and John A.
Schick. Henry Ottmar is manager.

THE NORTHWEST GERMAN FARMERS MUTUAL INSURANCE COMPANY

During the fall of 1896, a number of farmers and business men of Eureka and
vicinity gathered to organize a Mutual Insurance Company. A committee was
chosen to draw up a constitution and by-laws, with instructions to report on
January 29, 1937, at which time a general meeting was called to complete the
organization. The first board of directors included Jacob Zeller, president;
Fred Aman, vice president; John J. Harr, secretary; Christian G. Wolff,
treasurer; and Martin Stickel, John Moser, Matthias Schatz, Emanuel Reiner, and
Karl Hausauer. The original name of the company was the Evangelical Lutheran
Mutual Insurance Company of Eureka, South Dakota. It operated only in the state
of South Dakota, writing insurance against loss or damage by fire, lightning
and tornado on buildings, and against hail loss to growing crops. The latter
was soon discontinued, and the name of the company changed to its present form,
the Northwest German Farmers Mutual Insurance Company.

Through the efforts of A. Schatz of Zeeland, North Dakota, who served as
director and official for 33 years, the company extended its field of
operations to North Dakota. Incorporation was effected in that state in 1903.
The company has accumulated a reserve of $135,000.00.

Farm business was written exclusively up to two years ago, when city property,
consisting mainly of dwellings, personal property, schools, and churches, was
added.

The first secretary, John J. Harr, acted in that capacity until 1911, when John
Stock took over his duties. In January, 1927, A. Stoebner was elected as
secretary, and is acting in that capacity at the present time. The members
serving on the board of directors in 1936, were as follows: A. Bender,
president; Paul Welder, vice president; Henry G. Hieb, treasurer; A. Stoebner,
secretary; and Christian Schumacher, Rudolph Hilgeman, Fred Scherle, Adolph
Boschee, and Wm. Funk.

ISAAK'S VARIETY STORE

Isaak's Variety Store, originally a farm machinery and hardware business, was
started in 1890 by David Isaak and C. H. Mewing. In 1892, they erected the
two-story building now occupied by the business.

In 1893, Adam Sailer became a partner. The next year Solomon Isaak and Gottlieb
Isaak also became partners. In 1898, Mr. Sailer withdrew from the partnership,
and soon thereafter, Solomon Isaak and C. H. Mewing followed his example.
Gottlieb Isaak retired shortly thereafter, leaving David Isaak as the sole
owner. In September of that year, J. E. Hetzler became a partner of David
Isaak. A grocery department was added. Three years later, in 1901, Mr. Hetzler
severed from the partnership. At this time David Isaak's children, Josephine,
Albert, and Edward, began helping him. For a time they bought cream in the rear
part of the store. As time passed, a dry goods department was added. Business
improved, and in 1913, the west half of the present store building was built.
The firm name at that time was David Isaak and Sons.

In December, 1917, David Isaak died, shortly after Albert had entered the
service. Edward Isaak now operated the store alone until 1919, when Albert
Isaak returned from France. The stock in the store was changed to a variety
line.

From 1926 to 1930, Mr. and Mrs. Albert Isaak managed the store. In 1930, Mr.
and Mrs. Edward Isaak took over the home goods department, and the Albert
Isaaks managed the dry goods department and added a ladies' ready-to-wear line.

In 1935, Mr. and Mrs. Albert Isaak purchased a store in Cedar Falls, Iowa.
Since that time Mr. and Mrs. Edward Isaak have been managers of Isaak's Variety
Store, as it is now called. The building was remodeled and redecorated in 1935.

H. STRAUB AND SONS

H. Straub and Sons, pioneer firm in the furniture and funeral directing
business, have given Eureka and surrounding community 43 years of service. In
1894, Henry Straub purchased the Chas. Lyher interests in the Lyher & Frey
Furniture and Carpenter firm. Frey & Straub continued operating the furniture
factory in what is today known as the Emanuels Church building. In 1898, the
building was sold to a group of lodge organizations. In June of that year the
Mathews building, located beside the Koch & Gerdes building, was purchased from
Julius Schamber and Christoph Hebel, and business was resumed.

In 1900, Henry Straub purchased the interest of Henry Frey, and took over the
entire business. In 1904, he purchased the Koch & Gerdes Drug Store building
from Ed and John Maag, and added it to his other building. In 1912, the two
buildings were wrecked and the present building was erected. In 1932, a portion
of the basement was transformed into a modern funeral parlor.

Theo. Straub, the oldest son of Henry Straub, a licensed funeral director and
embalmer, became a partner in 1925. His younger brother, Werner M. Straub,
entered the business in 1931, with the same qualifications as those of his
brother.

THE NORTHWEST BLADE

In the early days of our county seat, Leola, there were two publications
existing -- one the Northwest, and the other the Blade. After a time these two
newspapers consolidated under the name of the Northwest Blade. The owner of the
consolidation is unknown, but we have found the Northwest Blade as a newspaper
was established in 1884 in Leola.

In October, 1893, E. A. Warner, Paul D. Kribs, L. T. Boucher and Charles N.
Herreid purchased the Northwest Blade with the understanding that the plant be
moved to Eureka. The moving of the equipment was no small job, the trip being
made cross-country. It was not until November 3, 1893, that the first
publication of the Blade was made at Eureka.

In October of 1894, E. A. Warner purchased the holdings of his partners and
became the sole owner of the Blade. This paper prospered under the guiding hand
of Mr. Warner and in 1907 he erected the building now occupied by the newspaper.

At this time there was another newspaper plant in our city which issued two
newspapers weekly -- The Post, an English newspaper, and the Die Eureka Post,
an entirely German publication. In 1911, E. A. Warner, publisher and owner of
the Blade, English, purchased the plant of the Eureka Post and Die Eureka Post
and immediately discontinued the English publication in favor of the Northwest
Blade. From that time until April, 1917, Mr. Warner issued two papers weekly,
on which date he sold his entire holdings to Mauser and Froh. This partnership
existed until June of 1927, when the reins of the Northwest Blade were turned
over to Otto H. Froh as sole owner. During the time Mauser and Froh were in
charge, the German newspaper was sold and only the Blade was continued. Otto
Froh remained at the helm of the newspaper until August, 1936, when he
relinquished his holdings to F. C. Shankland, your present owner and publisher.

DAKOTA CENTRAL TELEPHONE COMPANY

The first telephone exchange, known as the Eureka Telephone Company, was built
in Eureka in 1889, by E. S. Kennedy, C. N. Herreid, L. T. Boucher, F. W.
Boettcher, F. H. Hopper, J. E. Regan, Chas. Pfeffer, I. Parkhurst, Wm. Guhin,
and E. A. Warner, each of whom invested an equal amount of money to buy
material and telephone equipment.

The office was located in the Parkhurst restaurant, then known as the Siberian
Cafe. Ida Soper was the first operator, and Frank Guhin the relief operator.
Wm. Crawle was the first lineman.

In 1889, J. L. W. Zietlow of the Dakota Central Telephone Company, which was at
that time a very small telephone company, built a grounded line along the right
of way from Roscoe to Eureka, to be used for long distance service. This line
was used until 1900, when, due to the increased number of long distance calls,
a line having two copper metallic circuits was built from Bowdle. In the same
year the Dakota Central Telephone Company purchased the Eureka telephone
exchange from the Eureka Telephone Company, giving the stockholders of that
company stock in the Dakota Central Company in exchange.

In 1900, the telephone office was moved from the Parkhurst restaurant to the
city hall. Years later the office was moved to the second floor of the Farmers
Investment Company building, where it is located at the present time. Sam
Finkbeiner is local telephone manager.

THE EUREKA CREAMERY

In the fall of 1924, the then closed Eureka Creamery, located south of the two
main business blocks, was purchased by Fernholz and Schultz. They began
operations the next spring, and have been operating continuously.

A new building was erected along the railroad siding just west of the depot in
1933. Built of brick, it is two stories high. Its two large cold storage rooms,
cooled by a large refrigeration machine, will hold four carloads of butter. The
creamery has two large churns and four vats, and other equipment of sufficient
capacity to manufacture one million pounds of butter manually. All machinery is
operated by electricity.

The building also houses a large egg handling plant. A separate building is
used for the live poultry division.

The present officers of The Eureka Creamery, Inc., are: A. C. Schultz,
president; E. R. Schultz, vice president; F. J. Fernholz, treasurer; J. J.
Fernholz, secretary and general manager; and Arthur Becker, acting manager.

HOME OIL COMPANY

This plant was built during the summer of 1936, and was then known as the East
River Oil Company. A year later the name was changed to the Home Oil Company.
It has always handled Phillip's 66 products. Reinhold Ladner is the manager.

PHILLIP'S 66 SERVICE STATION

The building was erected in 1930 by Ed Schauer and Ernest Arlt. They operated
the station until 1932, when they sold it to Ed Rische of Herreid, South
Dakota. In 1934, the present proprietor, Chris Ladner, purchased the station
from Mr. Rische. It is now known as the Phillip's 66 Service Station.

J. H. DALKE, CONTRACTOR

In the fall of 1922, J. H. Dalke, who was then manager of the Interior Lumber
Company, left the lumber business and entered the contracting business. He has
been operating as a general contractor ever since.

HIEB AND OPP

The firm of Hieb and Opp was started by Edward G. and Franklin H. Kennedy on
April 29, 1892, under the name of Kennedy Brothers. In November, 1896, Edward
G. Kennedy took over the business and changed the name to Kennedy Implement
Company. At this time the company already handled the well-known John Deere
line of farm implements and machinery. Hieb and Opp are still the agents.

On November 7, 1907, John N. Brundage and Gottlieb B. Stadel bought out Mr.
Kennedy, but continued to operate under the old name. It was at about this time
that the present up-to-date building of cement blocks and concrete was erected.

Later, in 1916, Mr. Stadel sold out, and John J. Hieb and Henry D. Opp became
partners of Mr. Brundage for a few months, when he also sold his interest.
Jacob J. Weixel was the new partner. The firm continued under this management
for five years, when Mr. Weixel released his interest to his partners, John J.
Hieb and Henry D. Opp, the present owners. They changed the name of the
business firm to Hieb and Opp.

THE EUREKA FARMERS DAIRY ASSOCIATION

The Eureka Farmers Dairy Association was organized July 1, 1924. The first
officers were as follows: Peter Junker, president; John J. Staebler, William
Ottenbacher, Alexander Hamilton, and Christ Schumacher, directors; and Jacob
Mauch, Sr., manager.

The first location was in the old creamery building. It is now located in a
building belonging to the Humann Mercantile The purpose of the association is
to pay its members the highest possible price for their cream and dairy
products, and pay a dividend each year.

The present officers are John J. Staebler, president; Henry Fauth and Gottlieb
Rau, directors; and Henry G. Opp, manager.

VETTER AND WOLFF

In 1925, Frank Vetter and John Wolff bought the old Jacob Strobel elevator.
They immediately tore down the old elevator and built a new one, covered with
sheet metal siding. Frank Vetter continues to act as manager of the firm.

LYRIC THEATRE

This business was originally started by Kent Shaw and Chas. Lee in the building
formerly occupied by the Orthwein general merchandise store. Later, Chas. Lee
purchased the interest of Kent Shaw in the business. In 1935, the Lyric Theatre
was purchased by W. C. Turner, and was under the exclusive management of V. C.
Turner until the fall of 1936. Since then, W. C. Turner has been in charge of
the theatre. The building was purchased in the early winter of 1936, from Mrs.
Frieda Orthwein.

QUALITY HARDWARE COMPANY

On January 1, 1920, George G. Neuharth and his son, John G. G. Neuharth,
bought the hardware department of the Eureka Bazaar from Julius Schamber.
In 1923, they moved the stock into the Golden Rule building, which they
purchased from Wm. Bader. The firm name of Quality Hardware Company was
adopted at this time.

George G. Neuharth sold his interest in the business to John Kallenberger
at about this time. Several years later, in 1927, Gottfried Neuharth
purchased Mr. Kallenberger's share.

John G. G. Neuharth and Gottfried Neuharth are the present owners of the
Quality Hardware Company. John G. G. Neuharth is the active manager.

The business known as Mehlhaff's Market was started on April 1, 1914, by F.
G. Mehlhaff. Prior to that time the building housing his market was used by
Casper Hatz, Emil Dierenfeldt and Jacob Braufman, for various purposes. In
1910, it was purchased by F. G. Mehlhaff from the German Bank, and for
several years he rented it to different parties. During the first two years
of the business, F. G. Mehlhaff handled only the sale of meat. The sale of
groceries fruit and vegetables was later added. In August, 1927, Mr.
Mehlhaff purchased the Grosz and Keszler meat market, formerly the Bader
meat market located on Main Street, and used the same for a period of four
months, while the new building for the market was being built.

The new building was ready for occupancy on the 27th of December, 1927, and
is a brick and tile building, 25 x 80 dimensions. It is now affiliated with
the Red & White Food stores. F. G. Mehlhaff retired from active
participation in the business in 1931, and since that time, F. K. Mehlhaff,
his son, who has been with him during the entire life of the business, has
become its manager.

THE HOME LUMBER COMPANY

The Home Lumber Company was organized on June 1, 1920, by Herbert I. Bauer,
on the site just east of the Milwaukee depot. After several years of
prosperous business, the Home Lumber Company purchased the buildings
formerly occupied by the Interior Lumber Company, in the heart of the city.
Their stock of merchandise was moved to the new location in the spring of
1927.

EUREKA MEAT MARKET

In 1899, Emil Dierenfeldt and August Schmidt bought the F. D. Frankhauser
Meat Market, then located on the present site of the Quality Hardware
Company. About a year later, Mr. Schmidt sold his interest to Mr.
Dierenfeldt.

Emil Dierenfeldt has operated the meat market and grocery store for the
past 37 years, except for about one year. In 1907, he rented it to L. Lampe.

In 1909, after having moved about from one location to another for several
years, Mr. Dierenfeldt erected the brick building which now houses the
Eureka Meat Market. It is located on the corner of Main and Market Streets.

His sons, Fred and Herman, assist Mr. Dierenfeldt in the management of the
store.

JOHN WOLFF

John Wolff started in the cream-buying business in 1921, in the same
building in which he is now located. At that time he bought cream for the
Eureka Creamery.

At the end of two years, he bought the building in which he operated, from
Emil Dierenfeldt. For eight years after that he bought cream for the
Beatrice Creamery Company of Aberdeen, South Dakota. Since that time, in
about 1931, he has operated as an independent buyer.

He also buys poultry, hides, eggs, wool, all kinds of furs, scrap iron, and
dry bones; and sells sugar, salt, feed, and seed.

FORSTER & NELSON

On July 1, 1925, Peter M. Forster and Russell E. Nelson started business as
a partnership, doing automobile repairing in the Wenzel Garage, which had
just been built.

About nine months later, on April 1, 1926, they secured the agency for
Chevrolet cars and trucks, and moved to the Gideon Klein building situated
at the north end of Market Street.

They continued in the general garage business at this location until
January 1, 1929, when they purchased the garage building, fixtures, and
stock belonging to John P. Kiesz. This building is located east of the post
office.

NU-WAY CLEANERS

For many years Eureka had only intermittent pressing service, operated
usually as a side line to some other business. The first complete cleaning,
pressing and tailoring service was established in 1930, by Otto Wenzel. The
firm operated under the name of The Nifty Cleaners.

Three years later the equipment was purchased by Walter and Reuben Reich,
and the name changed to The Nu-Way Cleaners. Call and delivery service was
inaugurated at this time. In 1934, Walter Reich, the present proprietor,
took over the firm. Power equipment was installed during the same year.

The Nu-Way Cleaners are located in Wenzel's Garage building, at Main and
Second Streets.

EUREKA GAMBLE STORE AGENCY

The Eureka Gamble Store Agency was purchased by H. M. Rickmeyer, the
present owner, in 1935, from John Wahl. who had opened the store about a
year before. At that time the store was located in the Bareither building.

The store was moved to the Philip Schamber building by the new owner. He
operated there until 1936, when he purchased the old pool hall building,
then occupied by the 132 Club. In June, 1936, the store was moved to this
location. Since that time the store has been remodeled. Merchandise handled
by this firm includes light hardware, auto accessories, washing machines,
radios, and cosmetics.

RED OWL STORES, INC.

A Red Owl grocery store was opened in Eureka in the fall of 1935. E. R.
Pfeiffer was the first manager of this store, and served in that capacity
until the winter of 1936-1937. The present manager is Wallace Schimke. The
business is located in the old Frolichmann store on the corner of Market
and Pfeffer Streets.

GOEHRING AND BECK

The firm of Goehring and Beck was started on September 1, 1919, by L. E.
Balliet and J. M. Goehring, who, at that time bought the John H. Schmidt
saloon building. The firm was then known as Balliet and Goehring.

At first the store sold only groceries, but after a few years, shoes, dry
goods, notions, and hats were added to the merchandise handled by this
store. In January, 1927, L. E. Balliet sold out to John Kallenberger, and
the firm name was changed to Goehring and Kallenberger. About a year later,
in March, 1928, Reuben Beck bought John Kallenberger's interest, and the
name was again changed, this time to Goehring and Beck.

In 1928, Gust G. Beck also bought an interest in the store. The three
partners operated two stores, one at Eureka and one at Hillsview, until
October 1, 1929, when the store at Hillsview was closed. That year Reuben
Beck sold his interest to J. M. Goehring and Gust G. Beck, the present
owners and operators.

MEHLHAFF FURNITURE STORE AND MORTUARY

William Blair and Alex Mehlhaff established the Blair and Mehlhaff
Furniture and Undertaking Parlors in March, 1931, at the present location
on Market Street. In 1935, Alex Mehlhaff took over the business and changed
the name to Mehlhaff Furniture Store and Mortuary.

During the past year the funeral parlors were modernized and the furniture
display rooms were enlarged.

H. H.GROSS

H. H. Gross is the owner and manager of the Gross Dray Line. He began
business in 1936. His place of business is in the old Schamber and Hennecke
Livery Barn.

EUREKA BAKERY

The Eureka Bakery is under the exclusive ownership of A. E. Strobel, who
began the business in the spring of 1936. The firm sells bread in the
neighboring towns of Leola, Hosmer, Roscoe, and others. It is located in the
basement of the J. J. Strobel building. Emil A. Weller is the baker.

TOM LOHNER

Tom Lohner operates a "candy service" in Eureka and neighboring towns, as a
wholesale distributor. Since 1935, his stock of merchandise has been
located in the Farmers Investment Company building in Eureka.

SCHOTT BROTHERS

The Schott Brothers, a firm consisting of Ed. and Rudolph Schott, partners,
own and operate a motor sales and repair shop on the Fred Schott, Sr., farm
nine miles northwest of Eureka. They sell Plymouth and DeSoto cars.

PARK HOTEL

Lillian Gardner purchased the interest of Gottlieb Keim in the Eureka Hotel
in the fall of 1935. She then changed the name to the Park Hotel and Cafe.
The building in which she maintains her place of business is owned by Jacob
Strobel.

THE EUREKA LIQUOR STORE

The Eureka Liquor Store, owned and operated by the City of Eureka, opened
for business in the Municipal building on the 4th of May, 1935, with an Off
Sale license. E. H. Buckenberger and R. A. Dahms were managers. In June,
1936, E. H. Buckenberger was appointed manager by the city council and has
been in charge since that time.

The store has enjoyed an ever-increasing business and has netted the City
of Eureka an average net profit of $300 a month. The return of normal
conditions promises more business than has been done in the past.

HEILMAN'S STUDIO

In February, 1911, Emanuel Heilman, the present owner of the Heilman
Studio, entered into a partnership with H. C. Chaffee, who at that time
operated three Photo cars (movable studios on wheels) at Herreid, Zeeland,
and Eureka. In May, 1911, E. Heilman sold his interest in the photo cars to
H. C. Chaffee, with the agreement on the part of Chaffee to discontinue the
photo profession in Eureka. At that same time Mr. Heilman bought out the
Hemmrich Studio, then located on the present site of the Gottlieb Eiseman
residence. He operated there for about a month, and then bought the
Golinski lot and building, located west of Isaak's Variety Store on Main
Street, from E. W. Hinsdale.

Until 1915, the Golinski building served Mr. Heilman as both living
quarters and studio. At that time the old building was torn down, and two
buildings, one a house, and the other the studio, were erected.

MARTHA'S BEAUTY PARLOR

On April 20, 1931, Miss Martha Roemmich opened a beauty parlor, now known
as Martha's Beauty Parlor. Miss Roemmich operated the shop alone for one
year, and then procured the help of Miss Jessie Schmitt, who remained with
her for two years. Three years ago Miss Elaine Isaak entered the employ of
Miss Roemmich, and is still a member of the staff of Martha's Beauty Parlor.

The beauty parlor is located in the J. J. Keim building on Market Street.
In 1936, the entire shop was remodeled and refinished, and made into a
modern shop. New equipment has been added from time to time.

JOE'S BARBER SHOP

Joe's Barber Shop was opened in 1931 by J. K. Fischer, who at that time
purchased the Ideal Barber Shop from N. D. Harr, renovated the shop
completely, and changed the name. It was then located south of the Eureka
Drug Store.

J. K. Fischer operated the shop at that location until 1935. He was
assisted by Emanuel Kary and Rheinie Werre. In 1935, he was forced to close
the shop because he was unable to find a new location. The building in which
he had been operating was taken over by the Gamble Store. In August, 1935,
Mr. Fischer reopened the shop in the J. J. Pietz Hardware building.

THEOPHIL MEHLHAFF

Theophil Mehlhaff has been engaged in the carpenter and contracting
business in and around Eureka since 1916.

PAUL O. KRETSCHMAR

Paul O. Kretschmar, a native of McIntosh County, North Dakota, came to
Eureka in 1927, from the firm of Sullivan, Hanley & Sullivan, attorneys, of
Mandan, North Dakota, and for a time was a partner in the firm of Giedt &
Kretschmar. From 1928 to 1932, he was states attorney of McPherson County.
From 1932 to 1934, represented McPherson County in the state house of
representatives. In the fall of 1935, he was appointed County Judge to fill
the vacancy caused by the death of Theo. J. P. Giedt. Mr. Kretschmar held
that office until 1937, when he was elected State Senator from McPherson
and Campbell Counties.

Paul O. Kretschmar received his legal education at the University of
Minnesota.

EUREKA MOTOR SALES

The Eureka Motor Sales has been operating under this name since September,
1935. This business was long known as Keim & Kiesz, consisting of the
partners John Keim and Jacob Kiesz. Jacob Kiesz became a partner of the
firm in 1916, when he purchased the interest of J. P. Kiesz. For many years
they held the Ford agency in this community, and sold implements. The
implement business was discontinued in 1928. In 1935, the firm of Keim &
Kiesz was dissolved, and Jacob Kiesz purchased the garage building and
continued in business under the name of the Eureka Motor Sales.

ED'S BILLIARD PARLOR

This business was started in April, 1934, By Ed. Schumacher, when he
purchased the Ed. Kunz equipment in the old Martell pool hall building.
Since that time, he has continued the business under its present name.

DR. P. W. TREICK

Dr. P. W. Treick, dentist, began the practice of his profession in Eureka,
July 5, 1919, purchasing the practice of Dr. E. E. Snow. The office was
located in the building then known as the German Bank building. The
building is now known as the Farmer's Investment building and Dr. Treick
still maintains his offices there. He is a graduate of Chicago Dental
College.

J. F. OBENAUER

The J. F. Obenauer Land Co., Inc., was established in 1910, and
incorporated in the year 1928. The officers of this firm are J. F.
Obenauer, president; A. F. Obenauer, vice president; K. G. Obenauer,
secretary and treasurer; and H. J. Obenauer, assistant secretary. This
corporation deals in real estate in the states of North and South Dakota,
and also Montana and California. John F. Obenauer commenced his real estate
dealings in Eureka during 1914. For a short time he was in partnership with
Theo, J. P. Giedt, and the firm name was known as Giedt and Obenauer Land
Company. During these years he was also connected with a garage and
threshing machinery business. For a number of years Mr. Obenauer was
Immigration Agent for the Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul Railroad Company.

JOHN J. KEIM REPAIR SHOP

John J. Keim began his business career in Eureka in 1908 as a partner of Keim,
Keisz & Klein, dealers in farm implements, flour, feed, and grain. In 1913, the
business was continued by John J. Keim and John P. Kiesz under the name of Keim
and Kiesz. In 1916, John P. Kiesz sold his interest to Jacob Kiesz, and the
Keim & Kiesz firm continued in the garage business until September, 1935.
During all this time they handled the sale of Ford cars in Eureka. In 1935, the
Keim & Kiesz partnership was dissolved; Jacob Kiesz retaining the motor sales
department and the building, while John J. Keim took over the repair building
and repair department. His share of the business today is known as the John J.
Keim Repair Shop.

JULIUS BENDER

Julius Bender, wholesa1e distributor of products for the Hamm Brewing Company
of St. Paul, Minnesota, maintains his place of business in the former August D.
Harr garage building, which he purchased in 1936. Mr. Bender was formerly
manager of the Eureka Creamery Co., accepting that position in 1925 and holding
it continuously until the spring of 1936.

J. J. WEIXEL ELEVATOR

In 1915, J. J. Weixel moved to Eureka from a farm 10 miles northeast of Eureka,
and purchased the interest of J. N. Brundage in the elevator and implement
business operated as a co-partnership under the name of Brundage, Hieb and Opp.
In 1921, he released his interest in the implement business, and assumed the
complete ownership and management of the elevator. Mr. Weixel is acting in this
capacity at the present time.

EUREKA NATIONAL FARM LOAN ASSOCIATION

The Eureka National Farm Loan Association was organized in the year 1934, by
Henry Zenk, who was formerly connected with the Eureka State Bank.

BAREITHER ELECTRIC SHOP

Carl Bareither started business in Eureka in 1919 as a partner of John Reich,
coming here from a farm. The business then consisted of a tin shop, and was
located on the site of the present Quality Hardware Store and later on the site
of the Adam F. Obenauer machine house. In 1920, the Reich-Bareither partnership
was dissolved and Carl Bareither became the sole owner of the business under
the name of Eureka Furnace Co. In 1924, Mr. Bareither located his business at
its present site on Market Street, and also added the handling of electrical
supplies and Delco light plants. In 1927, Fred Greiser purchased the furnace
department, and about the same time Mr. Bareither began the sales and servicing
of Frigidaires. In late years, the business has consisted chiefly of the sale
and servicing of electrical equipment.

SLIM's PLUMBING COMPANY

Emil Schulkoski, better known as "Slim," came to Eureka in 1917 and secured
employment with the J. J. Pietz Hardware Co. In 1922, he and Henry Pietz opened
up a plumbing shop of their own. "Slim" purchased the interest of Henry Pietz
in 1924, and has operated the same in his own name since that time.  His
offices are in the basement of the Farmers Investment Company Building.

        Doering Bros. General Merchandise of Pioneer Days

FIECHTNER GRAIN COMPANY

About 45 years ago, a Mr. Pine erected a flat house on the present site of the
Fiechtner Grain Company elevator. A short time later he sold it to Doering
Brothers, who in turn sold it to Jacob Keim, John Keim, and Solomon Isaak in
1895.

Keim, Keim, and Isaak operated under the firm name of Flour, Feed and Grain
Company until 1916, when they sold the flat house to Ed Bohle.

The next year, in 1916, Mr. Bohle wrecked the old flat house and erected an
elevator in its place. He operated the elevator until 1926, when he sold it to
the present owners, Christ and Jacob Fiechtner. They operated under the name of
Fiechtner Brothers until 1936, when they incorporated. The firm name is now
Fiechtner Grain Company. Christ Fiechtner is the manager.

HENRY BITZ

Henry Bitz began a plumbing and heating business in Eureka in 1935. His place
of business is located on Main Street, in the building formerly used by the
Farmers Dairy Association.

STERLING OIL CO.

The Sterling Oil Company of Minneapolis, Minnesota, opened a bulk station in
Eureka in 1926. Geo. Kelle was appointed manager and has served in that
capacity down to the present time. In 1928, they purchased the Kallenberger
service station on State Highway No. 10, and have operated the same until
January of 1937, when they sold both the bulk and service station to the Eureka
Equity Exchange. From 1928 to 1937, Matt. Mutschler was in charge of the
service station.

DR. E. E. STEPHENS, M. D.

Dr. E. E. Stephens came to Eureka in the spring of 1929 and was immediately
placed on the staff of the newly constructed Eureka Community Hospital.
Prior to his coming to Eureka, Dr. Stephens was associated with the Aberdeen
Clinic, at the time operated by the Drs. R. L. Murdy and E. A. Pittenger.  He
spent a year internship in the University Hospital at Minneapolis, Minnesota,
and then spent three years in Sioux Falls, South Dakota, as an assistant
doctor, specializing in surgery.  Dr. Stephens is a graduate of the University
of Minnesota Medical College.

DR. R. C. RAY

Dr. R. C. Ray, physician and surgeon, came to Eureka in the summer of 1936,
from Garrison, North Dakota. His offices are located in the Bockenheim building.

J. J. PIETZ HARDWARE CO.

In 1916, J. J. Pietz and John Smolinsky purchased the Fred Hepperle store
building on Market Street. They Immediately tore down the old structure and
built a fine, new brick building on the present site of the J. J. Pietz
Hardware Co. It was originally intended to only engage in the plumbing and
heating business, but because of extra space, hardware supplies were added. At
various later dates, the firm purchased the hardware stock of the Golden Rule
Store, Humann & Sons, and F. Honoroff. The plumbing and heating stocks were
subsequently sold to Henry Pietz and E. Schulkoski, and the firm engaged solely
in the hardware business. In 1918, J. J. Pietz purchased the interest of John
Smolinsky in the firm, and has operated the business as sole owner down to the
present time. In 1926, an addition was made to the building, and in the fall of
1936, the basement was renovated, and now houses a business men's club and
barber shop.

        John Pietz, Sr. General Store, in the '80s

THE LUNCHEONETTE

The Luncheonette was started in 1928 by Gertrude Mehlhaff, now Kortan, and Mrs.
Emma Schatz, in the old Kafer Clothing Company building. Since 1930, "Emma and
Jake" Schatz have been running this place of business. In the spring of 1936,
many modern improvements were made, and an addition made to the building.

SOCONY-VACUUM OIL CO., INC.

In January, 1925, the firm known as the Eureka Oil Company was purchased by
Gideon Klein from J. E. Zoerb, the new owner retaining the old firm name. Mr.
Klein operated this business as sole owner until 1930, when he sold his
complete fixtures, supplies, stock, and a newly installed hydraulic lift, to
the White Eagle Oil Corporation It was about this same time that the White
Eagle Co. merged with the Socony Vacuum Oil Co., under which the business is
now known in Eureka. Gideon Klein was retained as manager of the bulk station,
and serves in that capacity at the present time. In 1936, this corporation
erected a modern service station at the corner of the intersection of Market
and McPherson Streets, and the old service station at the end of Market Street
was discontinued, and is now used only as a headquarters for the bulk station.
C. Flemmer is in charge of the service station.

THE EUREKA DRUG COMPANY

The Eureka Drug Company building was erected in 1907 by John and Edward Maag.
The Maags operated a partnership business until 1913, when it was purchased by
H. D. Opp. In 1915, Mr. Opp sold one-half interest to R. H. Isaak, and in 1916
Mr. Isaak became the sole owner, purchasing the remaining interest of Mr. Opp
in the firm. R. H. Isaak continued as the owner and Manager of the business
for 14 years, when he hired a new manager and devoted his entire time to his
optometry business. In May, 1936, Mr. Isaak's optometrical equipment was moved
into the Farmers Investment building, and he placed the management of the store
in the hands of Edwin Schmitt and Andrew Olson, the present managers of the
store.

THE EUREKA DRAY LINE

The Eureka Dray Line is operated by George Nagel. Mr. Nagel became interested
in the business in November, 1919, when he and Christ Oster purchased the same
from J. M. Serr. In 1923, Oster sold his interest to Nagel, and the latter has
continued the business as sole owner since that time.

HUMANN MERCANTILE COMPANY

The Human Mercantile Company is operated by G. G. Humann. He purchased the
interest of Fred D. Schanzenbach in the store in the fall of 1936. The firm of
Schanzenbach & Humann started in the dry goods business in the Eureka Bazaar
Department Store in 1916. In 1921, they purchased the Fred Hepperle store and
have operated the same continuously under the name of Schanzenbach & Humann
until the fall of 1936, when G. G. Humann became the sole owner as hereinbefore
stated.

F. J. HOMEYER

F. J. Homeyer began the practice of law in Eureka in June of 1935, immediately
after his graduation from the law school of the University of South Dakota, as
an associate of Paul O. Kretschmar. Since November, 1935, he has maintained a
separate office. His office at the present time is in the Farmers & Merchants
State Bank building in Eureka.

FRED'S TIN SHOP

Fred's Tin Shop is owned and operated by Fred Greiser and is located in the
basement of the Farmers Investment building in Eureka. This business was
started in 1928 by Mr. Greiser, and he has been actively engaged in the same
since that time. Before engaging in a business of his own Fred Greiser was
employed by Pietz and Smolinsky, and then by the J. J. Pietz Hardware Company.

NATH D. HARR BARBER SHOP

From 1918 until 1925, Nath D. Harr worked for his brother, J. D. Harr, in his
barber shop. He rented the shop from J. D. Harr and operated it alone until
1928, when he started his own barber shop in the Northwest German Farmers
Mutual Insurance Company building on Market Street. Two years later he moved
into the Farmers and Merchants State Bank building, where his shop is still
located.

ISAAK GRAIN OOMPANY

In 1897, Solomon Isaak and John Keim started buying grain in a flat house. Five
years later, in 1902, they bought an elevator from William Rapp. They operated
as a partnership for several years, and then Solomon Isaak bought John Keim's
interest and started in the grain business by himself. In 1910, he wrecked the
elevator and built a new one, which is still used.

In 1919, Mr. Isaak rented the business to Fred Strobel. A year later, Walter
Isaak became a partner of his father. A few years later Solomon Isaak withdrew
from the firm, and Walter operated it alone until his death in 1935. The
company known as the Isaak Grain Company was incorporated that year, and
operates elevators at Eureka, Hillsview, and Long Lake. Theo. Isaak and Edwin
S. Isaak are the general managers.

HAAG SHOE REPAIR SHOP

The Fred Haag Shoe Repair Shop was started by Fred Haag in 1923, in the old
store building of L. Orthwein & Son on Market Street. About five months later
the shop was moved to the John Wolff cream station building, where it operated
for eight years. In 1931, Mr. Haag moved his shop to the J. J. Keim building on
Market Street. He has an up-to-date shop with modern equipment.

AUGUST BENDER

The coal business which is now operated by August Bender was started in 1902,
by Solomon Isaak. He owned the business until 1920, when it was taken over by
Walter Isaak. On January 1, 1931, August Bender took over the coal business,
and now operates in the Isaak Grain Company elevator.

ADAM F. OBENAUER

Adam F. Obenauer started in business for himself in 1916, when he built a
garage on Lot 4, Block 3, Original Plat, and dealt in automobiles, tractors,
and threshing machinery and supplies. He sold the garage in 1919, and since
that time has handled only heavy machinery, repairs, and supplies. For 16 years
he has sold the J. I. Case machinery.

          WENZEL'S CARACE

After selling auto accessories for several years, William Wenzel built a brick
garage building on Main Street, in 1925. He obtained the agency for the Nash
car. Mr. Wenzel has equipped his shop with up-to-date tools, and with the help
of several trained mechanics, continues to do business in his own building

           JOSEPH BENDER

Joseph Bender established his general merchandise store in Eureka in 1915, in a
building owned by Joseph Bell. A few years later he purchased the building,
which now houses his store, from Fred Wenger. Since that time the store has
grown in size, and the building has undergone various improvements. Joseph
Bender is the sole owner and manager.

DRS. OLSON AND OLSON

Dr. A. B. Olson and Dr. O. P. Olson, dentists, opened a dental office in Eureka
on November 1, 1936, coming here from Bowdle. The office is located on the
first floor of the Farmers Investment Company building.

DR. A. R. MARTELL

Dr. A. R. Martell opened a dental office in Eureka on January 8, 1936, after
practicing in this part of the state for five years. His office is located
across the street from Humann Brothers store.

STROBEL COAL YARD

The Strobel Coal Yard was established in 1917 by Fred Strobel and operated
under the firm name of Fred Strobel, Dealer in Coal and Wood. When Fred Strobel
passed away in 1933, Daniel F. Strobel took over the business, which he still
owns and operates.

SCHAEFFER BLACKSMITH SHOP

John J. Schaeffer, the owner and operator of this business, came to Eureka in
1924 from Ashley, North Dakota. The building which houses the business was
built in 1917 by John D. Friske, who used the same for a blacksmith shop until
1924, when he sold out to the present owner. Mr. Schaeffer specializes in
welding and machine work.

J. D. HARR BARBER SHOP

J. D. Harr began his barber career in April, 1912, as an apprentice of W. O.
(Bill) Davis, who at that time had his place of business in the basement of the
Farmers Investment Building. After one and one-half years of service, his
employer left Eureka, and Mr. Harr opened up a shop of his own in a building
located on the present site of Strobel's grocery store. After two years of
service here, he purchased his present shop on Main Street from D. B. Strait in
1916, and has operated a shop there since that time, except for short intervals
of time when he sub-let the business to other parties.

PHILIP OSTER AND SON

The firm was established in 1896, by Hezel and Schamber. Five years later the
elevator was sold to E. S. Woodworth Grain Commission Co., of Minneapolis,
Minn. In 1907, the present owners purchased the elevator. Philip Oster, Sr.,
and his son, Philip, now operate under the name of Philip Oster and Son. They
deal in grain, coal, flour and seeds.

HAROLD OPP

In June, 1933, Harold H. Opp and Edward Neuharth as co-partners, assumed
control of the Standard Oil bulk and service station in Eureka. This
partnership continued in business until August, 1934, when Harold Opp purchased
the interest of Edward Neuharth in the enterprise. In January, 1936, the
service station part of the business was sold to H. K. Fischer, Harold Opp
retaining the bulk station, which he operates at the present time.

ALBERT BERRETH

In January, 1936, the interests of Henry K. Fischer estate in the Standard
Service Station in Eureka were purchased by Albert Berreth. Mr. Berreth has
been in charge of the business since that time.

EDWARD DRENKHAHN

In the year 1914, Edward Drenkhahn arrived in the United States from the place
of his birth in Germany. It was. not until 1920 that he took up his abode here
in the city of Eureka, where he worked for F. W. Junker until 1924. From that
time on Mr. Drenkhahn has been in business for himself as a painter and
interior decorator.

NORTHERN POWER & LIGHT COMPANY

The Northern Power & Light Company maintains a substation in Eureka, South
Dakota. They are affiliated with the Northwestern Public Service Corporation,
as a subsidiary corporation. The local employees of the corporation are: J. V.
Wilhelm, Karl Kuhn, Andrew Ries, and Hilda Kusler.

THE STYLE SHOPPE

An exclusive ladies' ready-to-wear firm, known as the Style Shoppe, was
established on February 1, 1935, by Mrs. Martha Quast and Mrs. Alma Bender. On
July 1, 1936, Mrs. Bender withdrew from the firm. Mrs. Quast is now the sole
owner. The Style Shoppe is located in the Farmers and Merchants State Bank
building on Market Street.

HUMANN BROTHERS

The Humann Brothers general merchandise store was started in 1912, when Jacob
Humann and his sons, Andrew and Gottlieb, purchased the building which at
present houses the store, from Jacob Schutz. The building was erected in 1908
by Michael Wenzel. The firm name at this time was J. Humann and Sons.

In 1916, Gottlieb Humann withdrew from the firm, and the name was changed to
Humann and Son. In 1922, Emil Humann became a shareholder in the business and
the name was changed back to its original form. In 1928, Jacob Humann sold his
share to his sons, Emil and Andrew, the present owners, and the name Humann
Brothers was adopted.

EUREKA'S GOLDEN ANNIVERSARY

The idea of properly marking the Golden Anniversary of the City of Eureka
apparently sprang from the nebulous and yet very real thing called the mass
mind. It was discussed casually by business and civic leaders of the city for a
year or two before concrete action was taken. But before 1936 had progressed
far, organized effort took the matter in hand. A series of Civic Club meetings
were held, committees appointed and definite objectives were set up. A group
of 125 business men of the city joined heartily in boosting the project. As the
movement got under way Theodore Straub was chosen general chairman; J. G. G.
Neuharth, vice chairman; A. W. Schumacher, secretary; and Fred K. Mehlhaff,
treasurer. The members of the executive committee were: Philip Schamber, Russell
Nelson, F. C. Shankland, Gideon Klein, Casper Vetter, and Andrew Martell. The
balance of the group was divided up into 18 committees, headed by the
following: Edwin Neuharth, finance; Theodore Bauer, publicity; J. H. Schatz,
advertising; Henry Ottmar, pioneer; Emanuel Heilman, historical; Edward
Schumacher, parade; Henry D. Opp, prizes -- judges; Henry Jundt, sports; John
J. Pietz, exhibits; Christ. Flemmer, whiskers; Julius Bender, shows; Frank
Vetter, old-time dances; Theodore Isaak, new-time dances; John J. Schaeffer,
parking; R. H. Isaak, entertainment; Edward R. Isaak, decorating; Harold Opp,
hospitality; A. F. Obenauer, concessions.

There was to be not only the usual historical parade, reunion of surviving
pioneers and suitable programs interspersed with band music, but a definite
attempt at preserving the history of the half century that has passed since the
city was organized.

A collection of biographies of pioneer settlers, not only in the city but the
surrounding community, the jubilee planners reasoned, would present a valuable
and interesting picture not only for the present but for all time to come. The
collection of data for these biographies was naturally a formidable
undertaking, but it was accomplished by a fine organization of effort and
painstaking and careful labor of many workers.

Simultaneously, or nearly so, came the important part of financing the
publication of a book of sufficient proportions not only to present a picture
of the past but of the contemporary scene as well. Preliminary estimates
indicated that this would cost a substantial sum of money. But the jubilee
planners were not dismayed. They had a small fund of donations as a nucleus and
went resolutely to work to finance the publication of the historical book by
selling advertising space. In this enterprise their effort was characterized by
excellent business methods and the result was a gratifying success. By early
January the chairman of the advertising committee could announce ample funds for
the publication of the book.

Tremendously encouraging to the jubilee committee was the fine endorsement it
received on every hand, of the idea underlying the publication of the volume.
Even before the preparation of the copy was complete, inquiries and orders
arrived virtually from coast to coast from persons who had at one time resided
in Eureka or its vicinity. When time came to let bids for publication, the
committee felt justified in placing an order for 4,000 copies and was pleased
to be able to announce a selling price of one dollar per copy.

Under the chief editorship of Fred J. Homeyer, material was gathered not only
of a biographical nature, but a complete history of the city and brief
histories of all churches, schools, and organizations as well were included.
Brief paragraphs, also, record the business story of commercial enterprises of
the city at the present time.

In order to create an old-time atmosphere a whisker club was organized,
memberships costing a dollar. Release; from the club, on the other hand, cost
three dollars. Prizes of $25, $15, and $10 were planned for the most successful
cultivation of whiskers.

Not to be outdone the women organized a shawl club whose members pledged
themselves to wear the traditional "Kopftuch" or head-shawl which was worn by
the women of the community in pioneer times. Membership in this club was fifty
cents but releases cost two dollars. The members wore their shawls certain days
each week under penalty of fines. Mrs. Irene Gerdes Olsen was chairman of this
club.

A three-day celebration WQS planned for June 16, 17 and 18, 1937. The first day
to be devoted solely to the pioneers and the historical parade as well as a
banquet to the pioneers. The second day was named Governor's Day and Governors
Leslie Jensen of South Dakota, and William Langer of North Dakota, were invited
to be present. The boundary line between North and South Dakota was to be let
down symbolically as the people from the two states met their Governors at
Eureka.

The entertainment feature of the second day was to be horse racing and a water
carnival. The third day was designated Guest Day with a special train from
Aberdeen bearing the Elks Band, the Aberdeen High School A Capella Choir, and
the American Legion Drum and Bugle Corps. An elaborate electric parade was
planned for the evening of the final day. During the three-day celebration two
free street performances of various kind of entertainment were planned with a
baseball tournament to provide sport for the baseball minded during the entire
period. Carnival shows and rides were to be available on the streets for
persons who enjoy this form of entertainment.

As a fitting beginning to Eureka's Golden Anniversary, the churches of this
community set aside the Sunday preceding the celebration, June 13, as a day of
reverence, at which time appropriate services were conducted in each of the
religious institutions.

It was the earnest desire of the entire jubilee organization to make this
three-day festival as joyous as possible for the pioneers. With this thought in
mind, every minute detail was carefully attended. Sleeping quarters, lounging
rooms and a banquet were only a few of the niceties which were arranged for the
"Old Timers." The committee did not lose sight of the fact that this was a
celebration for the pioneers and was held solely in their honor.
